\title{Chaos and bifurcations in continuous\\ language-model feedback}
\author{Timothy D. Jones\\ Praxagent}
\date{Private working draft, 18 October 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\noindent\textbf{Status of this draft.} Unless marked otherwise, results on the soft-token map are numerically
validated in float64 (rung 1 or 2 of the evidence ladder in Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence}). Float32 maps are labeled
as such. Huginn's spectra are float32 and its accuracy runs bfloat16. \planned{Red} marks planned work and \inprogress{orange} running work. The only
computer-assisted proofs are those of the period-8 orbit of Table~\ref{tab:orbits} and of the coexisting attracting
period-2 and period-3 orbits at one parameter cell (rung 3, Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence}).

\begin{abstract}
Fed back through its own soft next-token distribution over the full vocabulary, a smooth full-distribution variant
of Soft Thinking, a pretrained language model defines a
discrete-time map with continuous states, whose dynamics can be mapped, predicted from derivatives at selected
transitions and in places proved, and whose consequences for inference depend on the feedback architecture and the
numerical regime. In Qwen2.5, OLMo-2 and Llama-3 models of 0.5B to 72B parameters, temperature and
steering move the loop between periodic dynamics, which write repeating text, and regimes with positive finite-time
Lyapunov exponents that automatic-differentiation tangents reproduce. We call these regimes chaotic as a numerical
label, not a proof of an asymptotic chaotic set; 74 to 92\,\% of sampled labels persist to $10^5$ steps. Finite-time
expansion has direct consequences for reproducibility. Rerun with only the batch size changed, chaotic float64 loops
separate until their most probable tokens differ, on the schedule the Lyapunov exponent sets, while periodic loops
mostly stay together, in four models up to 70B; in Qwen2.5-0.5B the text changed in 173 of 192 chaotic loops but in
only 7 of 192 periodic ones. In a control fixed in advance in Qwen2.5-0.5B (448 cells), with
the matrix shapes held fixed the runs are bitwise identical, and a displacement injected instead grows on the same
schedule, although one secondary criterion failed (from a displacement of $10^{-9}$ on, more than 10\,\% of the
periodic loops also separated). In arithmetic tests designed before the runs, changing only the batch
layout, which alters the floating-point execution while the intended computation stays the same, changed the answers
of bfloat16 soft-thinking inference on 56\,\% of 96 problems in a 0.5B model and 55\,\% in a 7B model, far more often
than with greedy decoding, at temperatures where the pipeline answers poorly (none changed where it answers well); an
exploratory reset of the fed-back state at every step removed most of these changes. In
tests fixed before their data, Soft Thinking with its published settings changed 21\,\% of its answers to GSM8K
problems not used before in this study with the batch layout alone, and reasoning models that answer 86 to 90\,\% of
them correctly (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, QwQ-32B) changed 14.5 and 19.5\,\%, the two layouts disagreeing on
correctness for 11.7 and 14.5\,\% of the problems. Ordinary sampled reasoning with the same random numbers changed about as
many, so this sensitivity is not unique to continuous feedback. Nearly all of these changes came from long runs that
reached the thinking or answer budget in at least one layout. Two mechanisms contribute, depending on the model. In
float64 the loops of Qwen2.5-7B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B mostly contract, so their answer changes are bfloat16
rounding carried by long feedback to answers that sit near ties, while the loops of QwQ-32B and
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B grow slowly in more than half of the copies, and a relative displacement of $10^{-10}$
alone changes about 14 and 18\,\% of their answers, respectively. Exactly repeating text can hide a state with a
positive finite-time exponent: the text records only the most probable token, which can repeat while the fed-back
probabilities do not repeat over the run. In Qwen2.5-0.5B, along recorded orbits, the growth that steering adds comes mainly
from the readout, where next tokens compete; a logit bias alone suffices for chaotic cells. Earlier injection carries chaos to lower temperature, and steering at two depths creates
chaos neither depth causes alone. Whether a prompt's loop is chaotic does not predict the accuracy of ordinary sampled
answers beyond what the model's uncertainty about its first answer token predicts, and within the precision of the
tested designs we detect no edge-of-chaos advantage. The loop shows quantitative signatures of low-dimensional maps:
late finite-order doubling ratios approach Feigenbaum's constant numerically (4.6688 against 4.6692, at the float64
floor of the computation), periodic windows accumulate on folds with distances close to a $1/N^2$ law, passage times
predicted in advance from fold normal forms came within one iterate of the return map in 42 of 48 cases at four new folds, over two frozen stages (24 and 18 of
24) (the six
misses, at a period-6 fold, 3 to 5\,\% short), and interval arithmetic proves an attracting period-8 orbit and, at one parameter cell, two coexisting
attracting orbits of periods 2 and 3. In the recurrent-depth model Huginn, a frozen test confirmed a predicted accuracy loss. The loop is not ordinary sampled
generation.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}
\begin{figure}[!t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{fig-cross512-lyapunov.png}
\caption{Chaos and repeating text in a language model fed its own output: the soft-token map of
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, steered along the philosophy direction at layer 12, on $512\times512$ float64 cells over the
steering strength $\alpha\in[0.0625,0.0775]$ and the temperature, $\log_{10}T\in[-0.17,-0.125]$. Colored cells are
chaotic by the finite-horizon criterion of Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}, and the color gives their largest Lyapunov
exponent (left color bar). In gray cells the model settles into a repeating loop of text, shaded by its largest
exponent, which measures how strongly the loop contracts (right color bar; light gray: close to losing stability);
4.4\,\% of the cells, also gray, have neither a detected period nor an exponent above $10^{-3}$. Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} names the loops.}
\label{fig:teaser}
\end{figure}
Several recent inference methods feed a language model its own continuous output. Soft Thinking replaces each sampled token by the expected embedding under the next-token distribution
\cite{zhang2025softthinking} (its published implementation first keeps only the most probable tokens and renormalizes;
the map studied here uses the full distribution, which keeps it smooth), latent-reasoning methods feed
hidden states back \cite{hao2024coconut}, and recurrent-depth models iterate a block toward a fixed point
\cite{geiping2025recurrent}. Each turns inference into an iterated map, and an iterated map can be chaotic. We show
that the expected-embedding loop of released language models is chaotic, in the finite-horizon sense defined below,
over wide ranges of temperature and steering strength, trace where the growth that steering adds comes from, and
measure what the chaos does: in a chaotic regime an irrelevant change of batch layout can change what the loop
writes (in Qwen2.5-0.5B it changed the text of 173 of 192 chaotic float64 loops, but of only 7 of 192 periodic
ones). In bfloat16, where nearly every loop diverges, the batch layout
changed the answers of a short soft-thinking pipeline on 56\,\% and 55\,\% of 96 arithmetic problems in two
models, at temperatures where the pipeline answers poorly; where it answers well, no answer changed. The same loops show quantitative signatures of low-dimensional dynamics, with late doubling ratios that
approach Feigenbaum's constant numerically. In short, fed back through its own soft next-token distribution, a
pretrained language model defines a discrete-time map with continuous states whose dynamics (periodic windows,
period-doubling cascades, chaotic regimes and shrimp-shaped windows, the ``shrimps'' named by Gallas
\cite{gallas1993structure}) can be mapped, predicted from derivatives at
selected transitions and in places proved; their consequences for inference depend on the feedback architecture and
the numerical regime.

Three consequences follow for the use of such loops. The first concerns reproducibility: in a chaotic loop, differences far
below anything a user controls, such as the batch layout or the numerical precision, can change the output: a chaotic
loop amplifies them, and long low-precision feedback can carry them to answers that sit near ties even where the
float64 loop contracts. In an
exploratory test resetting the fed-back state before every soft step removed nearly all such changes (Section~\ref{sec:consequences}).
Ordinary numerical nondeterminism can thus become irreproducibility of the output, because the feedback amplifies or
carries it. The second concerns steering:
practitioners choose steering strengths, and here the strength is a bifurcation parameter, so near a boundary a small
change switches the attractor or brings chaos, and a smooth dose-response cannot be assumed (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
The third concerns prediction: where the loop is periodic, normal forms computed from the model's derivatives predicted
passage times fixed before they were measured: at four new folds the normal form's passage integral (the secondary
predictor of the first test, the primary of the second) came within one iterate of 42 of 48 passages, and the other
six were 3.1 to 5.2\,\% short (Section~\ref{sec:mechanism}), so the quantitative tools of dynamical systems apply to the
inference loops of released models, and larger models do not simply leave the chaos behind (Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}). We also report a negative result: beyond the model's first-token uncertainty, the loop's regime carries no detectable information
about the accuracy of ordinary sampled answers.

Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} shows a small piece of the parameter plane of one
such map, built from Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct. Cells with a positive finite-horizon Lyapunov exponent lie side by side
with windows in which the model repeats a fixed loop of text. Parts of this picture have been seen
before. Recent work has measured chaos-like sensitivity in language-model generation
\cite{li2025quasilyapunov,jaca2026chaos}, temperature-driven changes of output statistics
\cite{arnold2024phase,nakaishi2024phase,sun2025phase,ruan2026criticality,johnson2026temperature}, periodic
attractors of paraphrasing loops \cite{wang2025attractor}, attractor states of model-to-model conversations
\cite{ko2026attractor}, and, through their Jacobians, the stability of looped and recurrent-depth models
\cite{bai2019deq,bai2021stabilizing,tomihari2025recurrent,yang2026stars}, and finite-depth readout dynamics have
been used to predict the answers of looped models \cite{luo2026prediction}. Besides Soft
Thinking, probability-weighted vocabulary embeddings are fed back in training \cite{zeng2025ponderlm}. In a small task-trained looped transformer, fold coefficients
computed from derivatives predict passage times and spectral gaps without fitting \cite{kim2026phase}.

We study the expected-embedding (``soft-token'') map of released pretrained language models, defined exactly by
their stored weights and run with a fixed chat prefix at a fixed position. We follow it in fifteen models, from
Qwen2.5-0.5B to Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B (three dense families and a mixture of experts, with three further models as controls for the latter), in most detail in
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, and in two variations: two models writing one text, and a recurrent-depth model that iterates
its own block. We chose these families because each releases open weights at several sizes, so that size can be varied within a family, and because they come from three developers with different training data and recipes; OLMo-2 also releases its training data, and Llama-3 has public sparse autoencoders, which we use for feature controls. The map is smooth. Its
Jacobians come from automatic differentiation, and its periodic orbits can be computed and continued like those of
any smooth map. Chaos and periodic windows occur in many nonlinear feedback systems. Random-network theory, in which
a specified ensemble of recurrent networks becomes chaotic above a critical gain, in continuous time
\cite{sompolinsky1988chaos} and in discrete time by a quasi-periodic route \cite{doyon1994bifurcations}, and the
period-doubling cascades and shrimp-shaped domains of low-dimensional maps \cite{feigenbaum1978quantitative,gallas1993structure},
and the cascade to chaos of fixed-step gradient descent, studied as a map rather than through its gradient-flow limit, in
exactly solvable models of deep networks, where the edge of stability is the first bifurcation of the training map
\cite{hofmann2026map}, motivate looking for such structure here, but they do not show that a trained network realizes it. We test this
directly. We locate chaos and periodic windows in the models' parameter space, read the texts the loop writes there,
and check whether bifurcation theory, applied to derivatives computed from the model, predicts its behavior before
the behavior is measured. The models realize this structure, and the predictions largely hold. We also show what the loop's derivative says about where chaos can occur and where the growth that steering adds comes from (Section~\ref{sec:theory}; Section~\ref{sec:picture} assembles the pieces into one picture), and that larger models do not simply leave it behind (Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}). Ordinary sampled generation with a growing context is a different
system; Section~\ref{sec:beyond} also measures how much of the mechanism survives sampling.

\paragraph{Terms and scope.} Throughout, \emph{chaotic} is a numerical label: after a transient, the largest
finite-time Lyapunov exponent exceeds $10^{-3}$ and no period up to a cutoff is detected (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}).
It is evidence of finite-time exponential separation along the measured trajectories, not a proof of an asymptotic
chaotic set, and some labeled cells are long transients that a periodic orbit eventually captures: in random samples,
74 and 92\,\% of the labels of two maps persist to $10^5$ steps (Section~\ref{sec:maps}). We call the computed
exponents finite-time exponents and reserve ``chaotic attractor'' and ``chaotic set'' for cases with stronger evidence.
Where we draw such sets (Figure~\ref{fig:strangeattractor}) we call them numerically observed attractors: sets that the
tested trajectories approach and then stay near over the recorded horizon. Their invariance and attraction are
finite-time numerical evidence, not certified; the interval-certified cycles are attractors without qualification.
The loop itself is the soft-token feedback map, iterated in discrete time, not a Poincar\'e return map; by a return map
of a scalar observable $s$ we mean the graph of $s_{n+k}$ against $s_n$.
Each claim is stated for the system on which it was measured. The autonomous soft-token map (a fixed prefix and one
fed-back token), its versions with a longer fed-back memory, growing-context Soft Thinking, sampled thinking with
common random numbers, ordinary generation and the recurrent block of Huginn are different systems, and results do
not transfer between them without a test. Table~\ref{tab:claims} lists the main claims with the kind of evidence behind
each.
\begin{table}[tbp]

were fixed and pushed before its data. Exploratory: found by search or by an analysis designed after seeing data;
structures selected this way are not estimates of how common they are. Details, amendments and failures are in

\paragraph{Contributions.} The results fall into four groups.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Chaos in released models, and what it does to inference} (Sections~\ref{sec:maps}
  and~\ref{sec:consequences}; Appendix~\ref{sec:scale}). Temperature and steering move the loop between repeating text and chaos with embedded windows in all three model families, Qwen2.5, OLMo-2 and Llama-3. In float64, whether rounding differences between runs grow depends on the regime. Run twice with only the batch layout changed, 190 of 192 chaotic float64 loops of Qwen2.5-0.5B grow past
  a relative distance of $10^{-2}$ after a median of 102 steps, in line with the Lyapunov exponent (median ratio
  0.91), and at 173 of them the most probable readout token comes to differ; 182 of 192 periodic loops stay within
  $10^{-2}$, and at only 7 of them does the most probable token come to differ. Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B without steering (24 of 24 and 20 of 20 chaotic loops diverge, 0 of 24 and 1
  of 20 periodic ones) and Llama-3.3-70B with steering (24 of 24, none of 24) behave alike. In bfloat16 nearly every
  loop diverges, and in a frozen test on 96 additions the batch layout changed the answer of a soft-thinking pipeline
  in 56\,\% of problems in Qwen2.5-0.5B and 55\,\% in Qwen2.5-7B, against never and 10\,\% for a forced greedy
  baseline. An exploratory causal test shows that the loop carries this: resetting the fed-back state before every soft step
  removes nearly all of it (on an H100, 47 against 0.8\,\% of answers changed in Qwen2.5-0.5B, and 41\,\% against
  none in Qwen2.5-7B), and accuracy changes by at most 5 points. These changes occur where the pipeline answers poorly
  (23 of 96 problems correct in the 0.5B model at $T=1$); at $T=10^{0.4}$, where both models answer 90 to 96 of the 96
  correctly, the layout changed no answer. In a test frozen before its data, Soft Thinking with its published
  settings changed the answers to 21\,\% of 256 GSM8K problems not used before in this study in Qwen2.5-7B with the batch layout alone, on an
  H100, mostly between wrong answers; replaying the fed-back tokens cut this to 6\,\%; with reasoning models that answer 86 to 90\,\% of the problems correctly through soft thinking (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and QwQ-32B), 14.5 and 19.5\,\% of the soft-thinking answers changed, and a similar share with discrete sampled thinking. A chaotic loop's text reuses the phrases of
  neighboring loops in no fixed order, and a copy started $10^{-10}$ away writes the same text for 9 to 150 steps,
  then a different one (Table~\ref{tab:chaostexts}). Exactly repeating text can hide a state with a positive finite-time exponent: the text records only the most
  probable token, and the same token can win at every step while the probabilities behind it, which the loop feeds
  back, do not repeat over the run (Section~\ref{sec:hostile}). Beyond the model's
  first-token uncertainty, the loop's regime carries no detectable information about the accuracy of ordinary sampled
  answers, and within the precision of the tested designs we detect no advantage at the edge of chaos.
\item \emph{Why the loop can be chaotic, and where the growth that steering adds comes from}
  (Section~\ref{sec:theory}). The loop's derivative
  factorizes into the inverse temperature, the covariance of token embeddings under the model's own next-token
  distribution (a cross-covariance for untied embeddings), and the network's Jacobian; with tied embeddings and without the network the map would be a modern Hopfield update, which
  cannot cycle. The factorization proves contraction at sufficiently high temperature and, at fixed steering strength
  and sufficiently low temperature, excludes chaos on invariant sets whose margin between the two leading tokens stays
  bounded away from zero as the temperature falls; the zero-temperature limit is eventually periodic, and chaos in our
  sweeps occupies intermediate temperatures. Stability belongs to the loop, not
  to its steps: along a period-8 orbit every step expands some direction while the cycle contracts. Along the recorded
  orbits, the growth that steering adds comes mainly from the readout, where next tokens compete. Steering into chaos
  raises the readout factor of each step's growth by
  2.58 nats per step relative to nearly unsteered cells, while the network factor changes by only $-0.07$ (interval
  $[-0.23,0.12]$); a logit bias alone, with the network unsteered, makes 3.5\,\% of its grid chaotic; and an
  orbit-preserving crossover, which apportions the growth along the recorded orbits, assigns it to steering's change of
  the readout ($+0.81$ nats per step in the 0.5B model), not of the network, whose change damps it ($-0.49$). Random directions also steer
  into chaos. Earlier steering carries chaos to lower temperature, in Qwen2.5-0.5B and Llama-3.3-70B, and two vectors
  at two depths create chaos where neither alone does (Section~\ref{sec:consequences}).
\item \emph{Low-dimensional organization, predicted from derivatives} (Sections~\ref{sec:adding}
  to~\ref{sec:organization}). The windows carry concrete texts: a period-adding family gains one ``is X'' pair per
  period step. A fold's normal form, computed from derivatives, predicts without fitting how long trajectories linger
  near the vanished orbit: in two frozen tests at four new folds, 42 of 48 forecasts fell within one iterate, and
  both all-row criteria failed (the second because passages from the entrance of a period-6 fold's channel came in 3
  to 5\,\% short), and windows accumulate on the fold with distances close to a $1/N^2$ law
  (log-log slope $-2.00$; fitted with an offset, the free exponent ranges from 2.13 to 2.45 with the fit range). On the eleven fold curves we continued,
  13 of the 17 tangent reversals are located cusps (the four found on 18 October are not located), and near them local unfoldings, frozen before solving, matched the
  orbits that Newton solves recovered at twelve parameter offsets in number and at eleven in stability. A doubling
  cascade has superstable points through period 768 and finite-order Feigenbaum ratios that approach $\delta$ numerically (the
  last, $4.6688$, against $4.6692$, at the float64 floor of the computation). A period-5 window of a plane steered at two depths is a numerically verified shrimp:
  followed cell by cell, its attractor fills a main cell with legs and doubling shells bounded by fold and flip curves,
  a trace-zero loop replaces the head of a one-dimensional shrimp, and twice the fold curve has two cusps of opposite kinds and a
  self-crossing. Around one of these junctions an orbit census fixed in advance matched the predicted counts at 9 of
  13 points and found two coexisting attracting orbits at all five points inside it. An interval-arithmetic proof shows that a primitive period-8 orbit exists and is locally attracting, to our
  knowledge the first proof of a periodic orbit of a full transformer language-model map. A second proof of
  the same kind shows that at one parameter cell a period-2 and a period-3 orbit coexist, each unique in its box and
  locally attracting, each with a certified, if tiny, neighborhood of its basin: two different attracting behaviors
  under identical settings (Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence}).
\item \emph{Beyond the one-token loop} (Sections~\ref{sec:beyond} to~\ref{sec:dialogue}). The chaos is not an
  artifact of the one-token window: with a memory of 8 or 16 soft tokens Qwen2.5-7B is chaotic at more temperatures
  than with one, and in Soft Thinking's growing context a tiny early perturbation grows exponentially inside its
  one-token chaotic band, though not in every model. The organization recurs across families, and size does not set
  the chaos: across fifteen models from 0.5B to 72B, a larger model's chaos can move to higher temperature instead of
  vanishing. Resolved through the stack, the network damps a change of the fed-back token more strongly in the larger
  Qwen2.5 and Llama-3 models, because their blocks write a growing residual stream that depends little on the
  fed-back token, so a change of that token becomes an ever smaller fraction of the state; a mixture of experts with
  3B of its 35B parameters active per token damps more than a dense 9B model of its family. Two loops writing one text lock each other into periodic orbits: 60\,\% of pairs of differently steered
  0.5B agents that are each chaotic alone are periodic together (twelve such orbits verified by Newton's method). In a
  second system, the recurrent-depth model Huginn, a spectral estimate ranks held-out questions by the size of their
  residual under relaxation, and a frozen generation test confirms the predicted accuracy loss at relaxation 1.20 and
  noninferiority at 1.05.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Related work.} Chaos-like sensitivity of generation \cite{li2025quasilyapunov,jaca2026chaos,verazuniga2026selffeeding},
temperature-driven changes of output statistics
\cite{arnold2024phase,nakaishi2024phase,sun2025phase,ruan2026criticality}, repetition loops
\cite{holtzman2019degeneration,xu2022loop} and behavioral attractors of paraphrasing and conversation
\cite{wang2025attractor,ko2026attractor} have been measured in generated text, and one study fits a noisy effective
map to a hidden-state coordinate \cite{johnson2026temperature}, but none of these computes the orbits, multipliers or
bifurcations of the model's own map. Equilibrium, looped and recurrent-depth models have been analyzed through fixed
points and Jacobians \cite{bai2019deq,bai2021stabilizing,geiping2025recurrent,tomihari2025recurrent,yang2026stars},
and the closest methodological precedent, a derivative-based fold analysis of a small task-trained looped transformer, predicts
passage times and spectral gaps without fitting and derives from the same bottleneck an $N^{-2}$ tail of relaxation
times over inputs \cite{kim2026phase}. We know of no earlier prospective test of quantitative predictions from a
pretrained language model's own derivatives (here passage times at four new folds, where 42 of 48 forecasts fell
within one iterate and both all-row criteria failed, by one row and at the entrance passages of a period-6 fold, and
the orbits recovered and their stabilities at frozen offsets near cusps), of no earlier frozen held-out test of a
spectral prediction of a pretrained language model's response to relaxation, which we give for Huginn, and of no
earlier computer-assisted proof of a periodic orbit, or of coexisting attractors, of a full transformer
language-model map. These statements rest on targeted searches (12 and 15 October 2026) that found no prior work
combining these elements for the feedback map of a pretrained language model: an absence of evidence within the
search, not a proof of priority. Appendix~\ref{sec:related} discusses this work, expected-embedding feedback, and
bifurcations, Feigenbaum ratios and shrimps in neural models in detail.

\section{The inference map}
\label{sec:map}
\paragraph{Definition.} We describe the map for Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, the model studied in most detail; the other
models enter the same construction with their own embeddings and steering layers (Appendix~\ref{sec:scale}). Let
$E\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times d}$ be the input embedding matrix ($V=151{,}936$, $d=896$). The model reads a fixed chat prefix, with the system prompt ``You are an AI talking with another AI. You
have complete freedom. Feel free to pursue whatever you want.'' and the user turn ``Hello!'', followed by a single
input embedding $x$, and produces next-token logits $z(x)\in\mathbb{R}^V$. The soft-token map feeds the expected
next-token embedding back in place of $x$:
\begin{equation}
  F_{T,\alpha}(x) = E^{\top}\operatorname{softmax}\!\big(z_\alpha(x)/T\big),
  \label{eq:map}
\end{equation}
where $z_\alpha(x)$ are the logits computed with $\alpha v$ added to the residual stream at layer 12 at every position,
prefix included; the steering vector $v$ is described in the next paragraph. Positions are fixed and the window does not grow, so $F$ is an
autonomous map of $\mathbb{R}^{896}$. As $T\to0$ it reduces, where the argmax is unique, to greedy decoding of the
same fixed-prefix, one-token-window system. It is not ordinary generation with a growing context.

\paragraph{How we steer.} Steering adds one fixed vector to the network's internal state while it runs; no weight is
changed. The state it changes is the residual stream, the $d$-dimensional vector carried at each token position, to
which every attention and MLP block adds its output. Our main direction, philosophy, is built in three steps from 16
short texts (\repo{tools/explore_llm_phase_map.py}, \texttt{CONCEPTS} and \texttt{NEUTRAL}): eight philosophical
sentences, such as ``What is the nature of consciousness and subjective experience?''\ and ``Is there something it is
like to be me?'', and eight neutral ones, such as ``The meeting is scheduled for nine in the morning.''\ and ``The
package weighs two kilograms.'' First, each text is run through the unsteered model, without the chat template, and the
residual-stream vector after decoder layer 12 (of 24, counted from 0) is read at its last token. Second, the direction
is the mean of these vectors over the philosophical texts minus their mean over the neutral texts. Third, it is
rescaled to the mean residual norm at that layer over the 16 texts, $\lVert v\rVert=189$, so that $\alpha$ is the push
as a fraction of a typical residual vector: $\alpha=0.07$ adds 7\,\% of one. The raw difference has norm 8.1, so
$\alpha\approx0.043$ reproduces the natural contrast between the two groups of texts, and the values of
Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}, 0.0625 to 0.0775, push 1.5 to 1.8 times as far. In every forward pass of the loop, $\alpha v$ is added to the residual stream after layer 12 at every position: at
each token of the prompt and at the fed-back soft token. The later layers therefore also see a steered prompt, since
the keys and values they compute for its tokens carry the push (Figure~\ref{fig:loop}). The other
concept directions (gratitude, hostility, kindness and cooking, each with eight sentences) are built the same way at
their own layers, and the specificity controls use norm-matched random directions, single MLP neurons and
sparse-autoencoder features (Appendices~\ref{sec:origin} and~\ref{sec:size}).

\paragraph{State space and regularity.} Attention is causal, so the keys and values of the prefix do not depend on
$x$, and $F$ is an autonomous map of $\mathbb{R}^{896}$. For the dense models studied here it is real-analytic, because
RMS normalization (with its positive regularizing constant), the SiLU activation, attention, rotary embeddings at fixed
positions and the softmax readout are analytic; with top-$k$ routing, a mixture of experts is smooth only within each region of fixed routing, and its map can be
discontinuous where the routing changes. The continuity and differentiability arguments below apply to the dense
models. Every soft token is a convex combination of embeddings, so $F$ maps $\mathbb{R}^{896}$ into the
compact convex polytope $K=\operatorname{conv}\{e_t\}$, and $F(K)\subset K$. Every orbit therefore enters $K$ after one
step, $F$ has a fixed point in $K$ by Brouwer's theorem, and, being continuous on a compact set, it has invariant
probability measures there. Because $\log^+\lVert DF\rVert$ is bounded on $K$, Oseledets' multiplicative ergodic
theorem applies, in its version for non-invertible maps, which allows exponents equal to $-\infty$: for every
invariant measure the Lyapunov exponents exist at almost every point
\cite{oseledets1968multiplicative,eckmann1985ergodic}. The exponents we report estimate them over finite horizons
(Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}).

\paragraph{Why feed a model its own output?} Feedback is not an addition to language-model inference; it is how
text is generated. Autoregressive decoding appends each chosen token to the input and conditions the next step on it,
and as $T\to0$ the map studied here becomes greedy decoding wherever the most probable token is unique; the familiar
repetition loops of greedy decoding are then the cycles of the limit. What the loop changes is what is fed back: the expected embedding instead of one chosen token. That is the
step of Soft Thinking \cite{zhang2025softthinking}, and related methods feed back hidden states
\cite{hao2024coconut} or iterate a block to a fixed point \cite{geiping2025recurrent}; continuous feedback of this kind
is studied because it can carry more than one token's worth of information. We also hold the prefix and the window
fixed. That simplification is deliberate: it makes the loop an autonomous map whose orbits, exponents and bifurcations
can be computed, and in one case proved, much as low-dimensional maps made the routes to turbulence tractable.
This fixed-prefix, fixed-window soft-token map differs both from ordinary autoregressive generation, whose context grows,
and from recurrent-depth (looped) transformers, which repeatedly update hidden states through a shared block
\cite{dehghani2019universal,giannou2023looped,geiping2025recurrent}; dynamical methods apply to both kinds of loop, but
their update operators, and therefore their derivatives and stability conditions, differ. We then
test which effects survive in the fuller systems: Soft Thinking's growing context, sampled decoding, longer fed-back
memories and two models writing to each other (Appendices~\ref{sec:bridge} and~\ref{sec:dialogue}). The
answer-level consequences reported below were measured in a soft-thinking pipeline with a growing context, not in the
reduced loop. Deployed systems feed outputs back as well, when agents read their own earlier steps and when pipelines chain
model calls.

\paragraph{Steering as a probe.} Steering is an established way to control a language model without retraining: a
vector added to the residual stream, typically the mean difference between activations on contrasting prompts,
strengthens or weakens a behavior, such as sycophancy or refusal \cite{rimsky2024steering}, sentiment or topic
\cite{turner2023activation}, or a personal style of writing \cite{zhang2025steering}, and representation engineering
uses such directions to read and control concepts \cite{zou2023representation}. Our recipe is the standard contrastive
one \cite{rimsky2024steering}, added at every position, and our pushes are moderate: at most 12\,\% of the mean residual
norm per vector in every map (the continued curves reach 13.1\,\%), and 6.25 to 7.75\,\% in Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}.
Pushes of this size do change what a model writes, which is what steering is for: in Qwen2.5-7B, 1\,\% of the residual
norm is the largest push that kept greedy accuracy on a multiplication task within 5 points (Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour}). They are
far smaller than activation additions reported as effective in steering work, one of which has nearly ten times the
norm of the residual stream it is added to \cite{turner2023activation}. We do not steer to make the model behave in a
particular way. We use steering as a probe: $\alpha$ is a control parameter, like the amplitude of a periodic forcing,
that moves the model through a one-parameter family of nearby maps without retraining it, so that bifurcation curves
can be traced. The chaos does not depend on it: at $\alpha=0$, temperature alone gives chaos with periodic windows in
Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B and OLMo-2-7B, and in a narrow band in Qwen2.5-0.5B (Appendix~\ref{sec:origin}). Nor, in the 0.5B and 8B
models, does the direction's meaning: random, sparse-autoencoder and single-neuron directions of the same norm make the
map chaotic about as often as the concept directions (at 70B the directions differ; Appendix~\ref{sec:size}).

\paragraph{Precision.} Hugging Face RMSNorm layers and rotary-embedding tables compute in float32 even when the
model is cast to float64, so we patch both. A float32 map is accurate to $3\times10^{-6}$--$2\times10^{-5}$ per
step. That is enough for phase maps (245 of 256 test cells agree with float64) but not for derivatives of composed
maps: one-step float32 Jacobian-vector products are accurate to $5\times10^{-5}$, yet the 8-step monodromy of a
period-8 orbit is off by 3\%. All orbits, multipliers and test functions below are therefore computed in float64.

\section{Why the loop can be chaotic}
\label{sec:theory}
Chaos in the sense used here, a positive largest Lyapunov exponent with no period found (the operational label of
Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}), requires that nearby states drift apart: a small difference $\delta x$ between two states must, on average, grow
from one step to the next, which is what a positive largest Lyapunov exponent measures (such growth alone does not
make an attractor chaotic). To first order, one step multiplies the difference by the derivative of the map, so the
question is decided there. The soft-token map $F_{T,\alpha}$ of \eqref{eq:map} has a simple derivative with respect to
the state $x$. With $p=\operatorname{softmax}(z_\alpha(x)/T)$,
\begin{equation}
DF_{T,\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{T}\,E^{\top}\bigl(\operatorname{diag}p-pp^{\top}\bigr)J_{z_\alpha}(x).
\end{equation}
Qwen2.5-0.5B ties its output matrix to the input embedding, so $z_\alpha=E\tilde h_\alpha(x)$, where $\tilde h_\alpha$
is the final normalized hidden state of the steered network, and the derivative factorizes:
\begin{equation}
DF_{T,\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{T}\,\Sigma_p\,J_{\tilde h_\alpha}(x),\qquad
\Sigma_p=E^{\top}\bigl(\operatorname{diag}p-pp^{\top}\bigr)E=\operatorname{Cov}_{t\sim p}(e_t).
\label{eq:gain}
\end{equation}
\input{figures/tikz-loop}
The derivative of the loop (Figure~\ref{fig:loop}) is the covariance of the token embeddings under the model's own next-token distribution,
amplified by the inverse temperature and composed with the derivative of the network. The network factor
$J_{\tilde h_\alpha}$ is the derivative of the whole transformer, with every attention layer, MLP block, normalization
and residual connection and the steering included (attention depends on $x$ through the query, key and value of the
fed-back position; those of the fixed prefix do not change); the factorization only separates it from the readout.
The covariance form is the standard identity for the Hessian of the log-partition function of an exponential family
\cite{wainwright2008graphical}, the Jacobian of the softmax \cite{gao2017softmax}, and it appears explicitly in the
Jacobian of the modern Hopfield update \cite{ramsauer2020hopfield}; here it is composed with the derivative of the full
transformer. For tied embeddings, placed between a network Jacobian and its transpose and divided by $T^2$, the same
covariance is the Fisher information of the next-token distribution pulled back to the network's input (for untied
embeddings the covariance of the output vectors takes its place), a metric used to attribute
predictions to tokens and to describe steering geometrically \cite{liu2026jacobianscopes,wang2026fishback}; in the
feedback map it enters once, multiplied by the Jacobian.
Each factor has a plain meaning. The network Jacobian $J_{\tilde h_\alpha}$ says how much the transformer's final
hidden state moves when its input moves, through all of its attention and MLP blocks. The factor $\Sigma_p/T$ is the
derivative of the fed-back expected embedding with respect to that hidden state. Its matrix $\Sigma_p$ is the variance
of the token embeddings under the next-token distribution: it is large when tokens with different embeddings share
the probability, so that a small change of the logits moves the expected embedding far, and it tends to zero as one
token takes all the probability. The scalar $1/T$ multiplies the whole derivative.

At a state of the period-8
orbit of Table~\ref{tab:orbits}, \eqref{eq:gain} agrees with forward-mode derivatives of the full map to a relative
error of at most $1.35\times10^{-13}$ over eight random directions (Figure~\ref{fig:derivcheck};
\repo{tools/softmax_gain.py}). Without tied embeddings, as in the 7B and 8B models, the logits are $z_\alpha=W\tilde h_\alpha(x)$ with an
output matrix $W\neq E$, and $DF_{T,\alpha}=T^{-1}E^{\top}(\operatorname{diag}p-pp^{\top})W J_{\tilde h_\alpha}(x)$:
$\Sigma_p$ becomes
the cross-covariance $\operatorname{Cov}_{t\sim p}(e_t,w_t)$ of input and output embeddings, which is not symmetric in
general. The temperature limits below do not change. The rest of this section uses the factorization in four ways: it
shows where steering and temperature enter; it shows that, with tied embeddings, the transformer between input and
output is what makes cycles and chaos possible at all (untied embeddings can cycle even without it); it proves that
the loop contracts at sufficiently high temperature and, at fixed steering strength and sufficiently low temperature,
excludes chaos on invariant sets whose margin between the two leading tokens stays bounded away from zero, while
stability belongs to the composed loop rather than to single steps; and three measurements show that, along recorded
orbits, the growth steering adds comes mainly from the readout, where next tokens compete.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{fig-derivative-check.png}
\caption{Formula \eqref{eq:gain} is exact up to rounding. At a state of the period-8 orbit of Table~\ref{tab:orbits}
(Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12, $\alpha=0.071674$, $\log_{10}T=-0.115$, float64), in eight
random unit directions $u$, each derivative is compared with the network's forward-mode derivative $DF(x)\,u$.
(a) Central differences $(F(x+hu)-F(x-hu))/(2h)$. For large steps $h$ their error is truncation, which falls like
$h^2$ as $h$ shrinks (dashed line, slope 2; fitted slopes 1.98 to 2.00 for $10^{-4}\le h\le10^{-2}$); below
$h\approx3\times10^{-6}$ rounding takes over and the error grows like $1/h$ (dotted line). (b) Each direction's best
central difference (open circles, $1.7\times10^{-9}$ to $4.8\times10^{-9}$) and the factorized derivative
$T^{-1}\Sigma_p J_{\tilde h}(x)\,u$ of formula \eqref{eq:gain} (filled, $3.8\times10^{-14}$ to $1.35\times10^{-13}$),
which has no step size: the formula agrees with forward mode more than four orders of magnitude more closely than
any finite difference.}
\label{fig:derivcheck}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Where steering and temperature enter.} Temperature appears in \eqref{eq:gain} twice: explicitly, as the factor $1/T$, and inside $p$. As
$T\to0$ the two pull in opposite directions and $p$ wins: where one token leads, $p$ concentrates on it, $\Sigma_p$
vanishes faster than $1/T$ grows, and the readout factor tends to zero, so the loop approaches greedy decoding; only
near ties between leading tokens does the factor grow, like $1/T$ (``High and low temperature'' below). In Backour's unbiased two-token reduction with linear logit transport and a positive constant coupling, the inverse
temperature rescales the coupling, and crossing the critical value 2 destabilizes the balanced mixture, so that one token
takes over \cite{backour2026mixture}. Steering does not appear in \eqref{eq:gain}: the vector $\alpha v$ is the same whatever the state $x$, so it has no
derivative of its own, and it changes $DF$ only by changing the quantities from which the two factors are computed. In the readout factor $\Sigma_p/T$,
steering decides which tokens compete in $p$ and temperature decides how sharply: $\Sigma_p$ is large when the probability is
spread over several tokens whose embeddings differ, and it tends to zero as the distribution concentrates on a single
token. In the network factor $J_{\tilde h_\alpha}$, steering
changes the input of every later block, and with it the attention weights, MLP activations and normalization
scales at which that block's derivative is taken, and so changes how strongly the network stretches a change of
the fed-back token; temperature changes
this factor too, because it moves the orbit along which the factor is evaluated. On the two main maps both parameters
move both factors (orbit averages along the trajectories of Figure~\ref{fig:normbounds}, power-iteration estimates).
To measure how much each parameter matters, we asked how much of the variation of these orbit averages over a map
steering strength explains on its own, as the share of the variance carried by the means over cells of equal
$\alpha$, and how much temperature explains, by the means over cells of equal $T$. For the network term
$\langle\log\|J_{\tilde h_\alpha}\|\rangle$, steering explains 7 and 33\,\% on the stripes map and the crossing
window and temperature 47 and 4\,\%; for the readout term $\langle\log(\|\Sigma_p\|/T)\rangle$, steering explains
30 and 32\,\% and temperature 21 and 27\,\%. Which of the two changes turns the loop chaotic is measured later in
this section (``Steering makes tokens compete''). From here on we drop the subscripts of $DF$ and $J_{\tilde h}$ when
$(T,\alpha)$ is fixed. Every condition that involves them, the gain condition and the threshold
$T_*$ below among them, describes a region of the $(\alpha,T)$ plane, not a range of $T$ alone.

\paragraph{A Hopfield network with a transformer inside.} If the network passed its input straight through,
$\tilde h(x)=x$, the map would be $F(x)=E^{\top}\operatorname{softmax}(Ex/T)$. This is exactly the update rule of a
modern Hopfield network whose stored patterns are the token embeddings, at inverse temperature $\beta=1/T$
\cite{ramsauer2020hopfield}. That update lowers an energy function at every step and converges to fixed points.
In \eqref{eq:gain} it is the case $J_{\tilde h}=I$, where $DF=\Sigma_p/T$ is symmetric and positive semidefinite.
The energy argument covers every map with that property. If $DF$ is symmetric and positive semidefinite everywhere,
then $F=\nabla\phi$ for a convex $\phi$ (for the network-free map, $\phi(x)=T\log\sum_t e^{e_t^{\top}x/T}$), and
convexity, $\phi(F(x))\ge\phi(x)+F(x)^{\top}(F(x)-x)$, gives for $V(x)=\tfrac12\|x\|^2-\phi(x)$
\begin{equation}
V(F(x))\le V(x)-\tfrac12\|F(x)-x\|^2 .
\end{equation}
Each step lowers this energy by at least half the squared length of the step. Along a cycle these decreases sum to
zero, so every point of the cycle is fixed. $F$ maps every state into the convex hull of the embeddings, where $V$ is
bounded below, so the steps of every orbit must shrink to zero: every accumulation point of an orbit is a fixed point,
and the strict descent of the energy rules out cycles of period above one and chaotic motion (an unstable fixed point can still have positive tangent
exponents; and the descent estimate alone does not establish convergence to a single fixed point when fixed points are not
isolated). In models with tied
embeddings, such as Qwen2.5-0.5B, every cycle and every chaotic cell
therefore depends on the network factor $J_{\tilde h}$ breaking this energy structure. With untied embeddings the
network-free map $x\mapsto E^{\top}\operatorname{softmax}(Wx/T)$ has the derivative
$\operatorname{Cov}_{t\sim p}(e_t,w_t)/T$, which need not be symmetric or positive semidefinite, so the energy argument
no longer applies to those models, and cycles become possible even without a network (two tokens whose output
vectors point at each other's input embeddings exchange at low temperature). Attention has itself been read as a Hopfield retrieval \cite{ramsauer2020hopfield}; in the soft-token
map the retrieval closes a loop around the whole network.

\input{figures/fig-readout-gain}
\paragraph{High and low temperature.} The largest Lyapunov exponent of an orbit, the average rate at which nearby
states separate, cannot exceed the long-run average of $\log\|DF\|$ along the orbit, so a positive exponent requires
that average to be positive (this gain condition is necessary, not sufficient). Both ends of the temperature axis work
against it, in different ways (Figure~\ref{fig:readoutgain}).

At high temperature $1/T$ is small, and the bound is explicit. However the probability is spread, the projections
$u^{\top}e_t$ of the embeddings on any unit vector $u$ lie in an interval no longer than the diameter
$\operatorname{diam}(E)$ of the set of embeddings, and Popoviciu's inequality bounds a variance by a quarter of the
squared range, so $\|\Sigma_p\|\le\operatorname{diam}(E)^2/4$. With $L(\alpha)=\sup_K\|J_{\tilde h_\alpha}\|$, the
network's largest gain on the set $K$ of possible states (finite because the Jacobian is continuous and $K$ is
compact), $\|DF_{T,\alpha}\|\le\operatorname{diam}(E)^2L(\alpha)/(4T)$ on $K$, and $F_{T,\alpha}$ shrinks every
distance, that is, it is a contraction of $K$, for $T>T_*(\alpha)=\tfrac14\operatorname{diam}(E)^2L(\alpha)$. Above
this temperature the loop forgets where it started: by Banach's fixed-point theorem it has a single fixed point, which attracts
every orbit, and it tends to the
mean embedding as $T\to\infty$; without tied embeddings, $\operatorname{diam}(E)^2$ becomes
$\operatorname{diam}(E)\operatorname{diam}(W)$. For affine softmax feedback, with a fixed matrix in place of the network, a recent preprint proves a sharp spectral
threshold for the uniqueness and global stability of the fixed point \cite{wang2026logitfixed}. The threshold is a curve in the
$(\alpha,T)$ plane, because the largest
gain of the network depends on the steering. The bound is rigorous, but it does not locate the measured chaos boundary, because it assumes the worst
case at every step. With the diameter of the 0.5B model's tied embeddings, $\operatorname{diam}(E)=1.2109$, computed
over all pairs of embeddings, and the largest norms of
$J_{\tilde h_\alpha}$, the Jacobian of the final normalized hidden state, met along the trajectories of 7936 cells of
the two main maps (power-iteration estimates, so lower estimates of $L(\alpha)$), $T_*(\alpha)\ge2.7\times10^{3}$ at every
steering strength sampled in these maps (64 per map), and $T_*(\alpha)\ge5.6\times10^{4}$ at $\alpha=0.06$, where the
largest norm, $1.54\times10^{5}$, was met ($\log_{10}T_*\ge3.44$ and $\ge4.75$): at least 3.1 and 4.4 decades
above the highest chaotic temperature of the unsteered sweeps below ($\log_{10}T=0.28$; \repo{tools/norm_bounds.py}).
It does not cover hard routing or a growing context.

At low temperature $1/T$ is large, but one token takes almost all the probability, and $\Sigma_p$ vanishes faster than
$1/T$ grows. At a state with a unique leading token $j$, the gain is $\|\Sigma_p\|/T=e^{-\Delta/T}(C+o(1))/T$, where
$\Delta$ is the gap between the two largest logits and $C=\lVert\sum_i(e_i-e_j)(w_i-w_j)^{\top}\rVert$, the sum
running over the runner-up tokens whose logits trail by exactly $\Delta$. As $T\to0$, $F$ tends to the greedy map,
which feeds back the embedding of the most probable token (at an exact tie, the limit averages the tied embeddings).
The limit map takes finitely many values, so every orbit of it eventually repeats (Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge}). The convergence is pointwise and in general not uniform: near a tie between tokens with different embeddings the gain is of order $1/T$.
At fixed $\alpha$ and small positive temperature, chaos is therefore excluded only on invariant sets that keep a
uniform logit gap, $\Delta\ge\Delta_0>0$ at every state of the set and for every small $T$; a gap that shrinks
with $T$ is not enough. Under that condition $\|\Sigma_p\|\le\operatorname{diam}(E)^2(V-1)e^{-\Delta_0/T}$, so
$\|DF\|\le L(\alpha)\operatorname{diam}(E)^2(V-1)e^{-\Delta_0/T}/T$, which tends to zero (with tied embeddings; untied
ones change only the constant). A chaotic set would have to return to near-ties again and again.

Chaos is therefore found in between, at temperatures where the next-token distribution is spread enough to make
$\Sigma_p$ large while $1/T$ still amplifies it, and the unsteered sweeps agree (Figure~\ref{fig:alpha0}). In the four
long sweeps (Appendix~\ref{sec:origin}) the chaotic temperatures lie in one band per model, interleaved with periodic
windows, and every band lies between $\log_{10}T=-0.20$ and $0.28$. This band is an observation; near ties, the
low-temperature argument does not exclude chaos. Below the bands, all
1547 temperatures swept have negative exponents, and all but five have a detected period; above them, up to the end
of the sweeps at $\log_{10}T=0.3$, 127 of 128 temperatures have a detected period (fixed points at 101) and one has a
negative exponent without one. These are finite sweep findings; the wider sweeps of Section~\ref{sec:sizemain} find chaos
at higher temperatures in some models, and an OLMo-2-32B sweep found three candidate cells below one nat of entropy (at $\log_{10}T=-0.42$, $-0.41$ and $-0.07$; one twin
offset). A further sweep of the unsteered 0.5B map from
$\log_{10}T=0.3$ to $1.5$ finds an attracting fixed point at all 25 temperatures. Its entropy approaches $\log V$
(11.928 against 11.931 nats at $\log_{10}T=1.5$), and its exponent falls steadily, by 2.14 per decade of temperature
at the top of the range, toward the slope $-\ln10\approx-2.30$ expected once $p$ is uniform.

\paragraph{Stability belongs to the loop, not to the step.} The gain condition is necessary, not sufficient. Along
the period-8 orbit of Table~\ref{tab:orbits}, the largest singular value of $DF$ lies between 1.2 and 21 at every
step, so every step expands some direction, and no step squeezes the state onto a single direction (none is close to rank one): the second singular value is
at least 0.15 of the first, and the second eigenvalue of $\Sigma_p$ is at least 0.23 of the first (minimum 0.236). Yet the Jacobian of the whole
cycle, the product of the eight step Jacobians, has norm between 0.059 and 0.644, depending on the phase at which the
cycle is started, against $1.1\times10^{7}$ for the product of the step norms. Its eigenvalues, which do not depend
on that phase, reproduce the multipliers of Table~\ref{tab:orbits} ($0.01104$ and $-2.96\times10^{-7}$; the table
lists moduli), and its second singular value is $1.7\times10^{-6}$ to $2\times10^{-4}$ of its first. An independent
implementation of the model reproduced the factorization (to $8.3\times10^{-14}$), the step singular values, the
signed multipliers and the cycle norms at all eight starting phases. The expansion of each step is undone by the
steps that follow, and the strongly anisotropic linear return of the stripe orbits is a property of the composed
cycle, not of any single step or of the rank of $\Sigma_p$. Nor does the contraction need the actual alignment of
the steps: with independent random (Haar) rotations inserted between the eight step Jacobians, all 16 tested products
contract, and more strongly than the actual cycle (\repo{tools/alignment_intervention.py}). Stepwise norm or
spectral summaries can be inconclusive; the ordered product of the step Jacobians captures the cycle's linear
stability. Across the maps the same holds statistically (Figure~\ref{fig:normbounds}). Along the trajectories
of 7936 cells of the two main maps (every fourth cell of the stripes map, except its top four sampled rows, 256
cells that did not run within the time limit, and every eighth of the crossing window; float32; 300 transient and 60
measured steps), the orbit averages of $\log\|DF\|$ and of
$\log(\|\Sigma_p\|/T)+\log\|J_{\tilde h}\|$ exceed zero in every cell, chaotic or periodic: their medians are 2.98 and
2.57 per step over the chaotic and the periodic cells of the stripes map for $\log\|DF\|$, against a median exponent of
0.30 in its chaotic cells (crossing window: 2.93, 2.62 and 0.17). Each norm is the stretching of one step in its
most expanding direction, but a perturbation leaves each step pointing in some other direction, which the next step
generally stretches less, so the product of single-step norms overstates the stretching of the product. Averages of
$\tfrac1k\log\|DF^k\|$ over the disjoint blocks of $k$ consecutive steps that tile the record, norms of whole blocks,
remove this overstatement inside each block. With exact norms each such average bounds the growth over the whole record, and it can
only fall when the block length doubles; here the medians fall as $k$ grows, although in 176 of the 7936 cells an
estimate rises between consecutive block lengths. They begin to separate the regimes only at $k=4$ and 5; at $k=5$ they are negative in 27 and
29\,\% of the periodic cells of the two maps (medians 0.91 and 0.80 over the chaotic cells, 0.25 and 0.22 over the
periodic ones). No estimate fell below the tangent exponent of its own 60 measured steps at any $k$ (in 13 cells a
$k=4$ or 5 estimate lies below the map's exponent, which was measured over 300 steps of a separate float64 run), and
the norms are power-iteration estimates,
which can only fall short, so the true bounds lie higher still; the four cells labeled chaotic by the maps whose $k=5$
estimate is negative have negative exponents on these shorter trajectories. With exact norms the picture sharpens
(Figure~\ref{fig:boundhierarchy}). At the first 24 of 64 cells fixed in advance (12 labeled chaotic and 12 periodic,
from both maps; float64; 32 recorded steps after 1000 transient steps from the fixed start; every norm from the dense
step Jacobian; the remaining cells did not fit the compute budget), let $P_{i,k}$ be the product of the $k$ step
Jacobians from step $i$ on. The block bounds $B_k=\tfrac1N\sum_j\log\|P_{jk,k}\|$, over the $N/k$ disjoint blocks that
tile the $N=32$ recorded steps, satisfy $B_1\ge B_2\ge B_4\ge\dots\ge B_{32}=\tfrac1{32}\log\|DF^{32}\|$ in every cell,
as submultiplicativity requires for nested blocks, and the factorization reproduces $DF$ to $2.0\times10^{-12}$. For orbits typical of an ergodic invariant measure on which $\log\|DF\|$ is integrable, the subadditive ergodic
theorem \cite{kingman1973subadditive} guarantees that
such bounds close on the largest exponent as the blocks grow, but it gives no rate and no guarantee for a finite
record; the agreement below between finite windows of the recorded orbit and the later exponent is empirical. Here
their median overshoot
of the exponent measured over the next 2000 steps falls from 2.72 per step at $k=1$ to 0.32 at $k=8$ and 0.036 at
$k=32$ over the chaotic cells (periodic cells: 2.86, 0.45 and 0.12), roughly like $1/k$ in this sample: for $k=1,2,4,\dots,32$, $k$ times
the median overshoot stays between 1.1 and 3.0 over the chaotic cells and between 2.8 and 4.0 over the periodic ones.
The sign of $B_k$ agrees with the sign of that exponent in 13 of the 24 cells for $k=1$, 2 and 4 and in 22 at
$k=16$. Averages of $\tfrac1k\log\|P_{i,k}\|$ over the overlapping windows of the record can be formed for every
length $k$, but they are finite-window diagnostics, not bounds on the whole record or on the later exponent. Their
sign agrees with that of the exponent in 23 cells for every window length from 17 to 29 steps and in all 24 at 30
steps. From 17 to 29 steps the one disagreement is a cell that the map labels periodic, with an exponent of $-0.146$
over the 2000 later steps, whose window average falls from $+0.112$ to $+0.011$ but stays positive. At 31 and 32
steps, where only two windows and one window fit, it and another periodic cell again have small positive values
(0.019 and 0.035 at 32 steps, where the single window gives $B_{32}$). A further cell, which the map labels periodic,
has an exponent of $+0.124$ over the 2000 later steps; its bounds and window averages agree with that exponent and
disagree only with the map's label. The factorized bound loses a further 2.9 per step (median) to the misalignment of
the readout and network factors. The factorized inequalities and short blocks do not locate the chaos. In these 24
cells, windows of about twenty steps of the model's own loop recover the sign of the long-run exponent, but as a
measurement along the recorded orbit, not as a forecast from the weights.

fourth cell of the stripes map except its top four sampled rows, which did not run, bottom; 300 transient and 60

\repo{tools/plot_norm_bounds.py}). Left: the orbit average of $\log\|DF\|$ per step (red: positive). Middle: the
average of $\tfrac15\log\|DF^5\|$ over blocks of five steps; black dots: cells the map labels chaotic. Right: medians of
each bound over chaotic (red) and periodic (blue) cells, the median exponent of the chaotic cells (dotted), the share of
cells with a positive bound that are chaotic (precision), and the share of periodic cells whose bound is negative.}

\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-mechanism-summary.png}
\caption{Readout and network contributions to the growth under steering: the three measurements of this section
(Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct unless marked 7B, float64). Orange: the readout factor $\Sigma_p/T$ of \eqref{eq:gain};
blue: the network factor $J_{\tilde h}$. (a) Gain split along the attractors of the block-12 map: mean logarithm per step of the relative gains of the text (gains along the tangent direction, not
operator norms), the network gain $g_{\mathrm{net}}$ (top, blue squares) and the readout gain $g_{\mathrm{read}}$
(bottom, orange circles), for chaotic cells (solid lines, filled markers; chosen by their dynamics, at any steering
strength) and for the nearly unsteered cells, $|\alpha|\le0.01$ (dashed lines; chosen by steering strength alone, whatever their dynamics, and
almost all periodic: the baseline at the same temperature), in the 18 temperature rows with at least three cells of
each kind (321 chaotic and 162 nearly unsteered cells, 6 of them in both groups); positive values expand perturbations, negative values
contract them. In the nearly unsteered cells the readout's relative gain shrinks perturbations by 0.87 to 5.49 nats
per step; in the chaotic cells it grows them, except in the three highest rows, while the network factor changes
little. (b) Share of chaotic cells (both twin exponents above $10^{-3}$ and no period up to 64) per temperature row under
residual steering at block 12, which moves both factors
(black; 331 of 5353 cells, 6.2\,\%), and under a logit bias along $Wv$, the output matrix applied to the steering
vector, with no residual steering, which at a given state changes only the readout (orange; 241 of 6837 cells,
3.5\,\%). (c) Orbit-preserving crossover: change in the finite-time growth along recorded steered orbits when only
the readout (orange, circles) or only the network Jacobian (blue, squares) is taken from the steered model; means
over the held-out chaotic cells with 95\,\% bootstrap intervals. Rows: the philosophy and gratitude directions and
a random direction of the philosophy vector's norm in the 0.5B model, 14 cells each, and the philosophy direction in
the 7B model, 10 cells. \repo{tools/plot_mechanism_summary.py}.}
\label{fig:mechsummary}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{Steering makes tokens compete.} Steering could create chaos through either factor of \eqref{eq:gain}, as
described above: it moves the logits, and with them which tokens compete in $\Sigma_p$, and it moves the hidden states,
and with them the network's response $J_{\tilde h}$. Three measurements on Qwen2.5-0.5B, steered along the philosophy
direction, tell the two apart (Figure~\ref{fig:mechsummary}; details in Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism} and
Table~\ref{tab:mechanism}). First, an accounting. One step takes the state $x$, the vector fed back as the input at the
soft-token position, through the steered transformer to its final normalized hidden state $\tilde h$ at that
position, and from $\tilde h$, through the logits and the softmax, to the next state $F(x)$, the probability-weighted
average of the token embeddings. A small change $\delta x$ of the state causes changes $\delta\tilde h$ and $\delta F$,
and each step's relative growth splits exactly into a network factor
$g_{\mathrm{net}}=(|\delta\tilde h|/|\tilde h|)/(|\delta x|/|x|)$, how much the transformer stretches a relative change
of its input, and a readout factor $g_{\mathrm{read}}=(|\delta F|/|F|)/(|\delta\tilde h|/|\tilde h|)$, how much the
softmax and the averaging of embeddings stretch it on the way out. For a tangent perturbation followed over $n$ steps,
their mean logarithms add up to its finite-time growth rate plus a boundary term,
$(\log\lVert x_0\rVert-\log\lVert x_n\rVert)/n$; both are relative gains along the orbit, not operator norms. The split
depends on how the intermediate state is measured, so only the sum is intrinsic; we compare each factor between cells
within this one convention. Over a $101\times53$ map in steering strength and temperature
(block 12; 5353 cells, 331 chaotic), the chaotic cells' readout factor exceeds that of nearly unsteered cells
($|\alpha|\le0.01$) at the same temperature by $2.58$ nats per step (95\,\% interval $[1.78,3.59]$ over 18 temperature
rows; 321 chaotic and 162 nearly unsteered cells). Their network factor differs by only $-0.07$ $[-0.23,0.12]$. With
nearly no steering the readout's relative gain shrinks perturbations strongly, by $0.87$ to $5.49$ nats per step. In
the chaotic cells it grows them, except in the three highest temperature rows ($\log_{10}T=0.175$--$0.225$), where it
still shrinks them, but less. Compared with periodic cells at the same temperature,
chaotic cells have a higher next-token entropy ($+1.62$ nats, $[1.26,1.91]$), a smaller gap between the two largest
logits ($-1.37$ in units of $T$, $[-1.98,-0.87]$) and a larger readout factor ($+1.36$, $[0.91,1.93]$), each with
$p\le10^{-4}$ in a permutation test within temperature rows: more tokens compete.

Second, a test that leaves the network unsteered. We add to the logits a bias $\beta\,b$ along $Wv$, the output
matrix applied to the steering vector (a logit-space stand-in for the steering; $b$ is centered and scaled to unit
standard deviation over the vocabulary), with no residual steering. At a given state this changes only the output
probabilities, although the orbit it produces can visit different states. On its grid, 3.5\,\% of cells were chaotic
(241 of 6837, all but one with a nonzero bias), against 6.2\,\% on the residual-steering grid (331 of 5353; the two grids share their temperature rows
but not a matched strength of intervention, so these are counts per grid, not a comparison of doses). A
change of the output probabilities alone, with the network unsteered, therefore suffices for chaotic cells in part
of the plane, mostly at $\log_{10}T\ge0.1$ (207 of the 241 cells), where the network factor itself can expand;
below, 34 of 5676 cells of the bias grid are chaotic (0.6\,\%), against 169 of 4444 (3.8\,\%) under residual
steering.

Third, a gain split compares different orbits, so steering could act simply by moving the orbit to other states. A
crossover at fixed states separates the two contributions along the recorded orbits. Along recorded steered orbits we
evaluated the steered and the unsteered model at the same states and carried four tangent dynamics. Each pairs the
steered or unsteered readout $R(p)$ with the steered or unsteered network Jacobian: arms 11, 10, 01 and 00. The
hybrid arms 10 and 01 are tangent products assembled along the recorded states, not the dynamics of a rerun hybrid
model; they apportion the growth along the recorded orbits. The frozen
validation (arm 11 within $0.02$ of the twin exponent at every cell) holds at 23 of 24 cells, all within $0.012$; at
the remaining periodic cell, which contracts at about $-12$ per step, the twin offset falls to the rounding floor. The
frozen sufficiency criterion (arm 10 within $0.1$ of arm 11 on average) fails by overshoot: steering only the readout
gives more growth than the full steered map, by $0.23$, because steering's change of the network damps it. Over 14 held-out chaotic cells, the steered readout factor with the unsteered network factor (steering only the
readout) raises the finite-time growth by $0.81$ nats per step ($[0.29,1.35]$). The steered network factor with the
unsteered readout (steering only the network response) lowers it by $0.49$ ($[-0.63,-0.35]$). The interaction is
$+0.26$ ($[0.09,0.45]$). Below $\log_{10}T=0$ the steered readout turns the contraction of the unsteered derivative
($-1.03$ nats per step) into growth ($+0.72$), more than the full steered map shows ($+0.34$). Above it, the unsteered
derivative already expands at the steered states ($+0.41$), and steering may act by bringing the orbit there. In
Qwen2.5-7B, whose embeddings are untied, the same crossover (18 cells of its steered map at block 14; arm 11 equals the
twin exponent within $10^{-4}$ at every cell) reproduces the damping. Over its 10 held-out chaotic cells, steering only
the network response lowers the growth by $1.14$ nats per step ($[-1.61,-0.64]$), and steering only the readout adds $+0.23$ ($[-0.11,0.63]$, an interval that includes zero). Eight of the ten lie at $\log_{10}T\ge0$, where the unsteered derivative already expands at
the steered states (mean $+1.33$). The crossover along two more directions of the 0.5B model gives the same
signs. For gratitude the readout effect is $+0.33$ ($[0.07,0.59]$) and the network effect $-0.14$ ($[-0.29,0.01]$). For
a random direction matched in norm to the philosophy vector, they are $+0.77$ ($[0.47,1.12]$) and $-0.09$
($[-0.22,0.06]$); 14 held-out chaotic cells each. The readout effect is positive along all three directions, and the
network effect is negative but compatible with zero along these two. In these three directions the readout effect
does not depend on the direction's meaning.

All three point the same way: along the recorded orbits of Qwen2.5-0.5B, the growth that steering adds comes mainly
from the readout, where next tokens compete; the readout's relative gain turns from shrinking to growing, while the
network factor changes little. The readout carries what steering changes; the network is what makes chaos possible
at all, since with tied embeddings the loop without it cannot cycle (``A Hopfield network with a transformer
inside'' above). At low temperature steering creates that competition at fixed states. At higher
temperature the orbit lies in states that are already competitive, consistent with steering having carried it there.
Along the philosophy direction, in both models, its change of the network's response damps the growth. These are
conditional comparisons on recorded trajectories: they do not single out the higher entropy as the cause or show that
the readout change alone would sustain the same attractor, and the readout gain stays shrinking in the three highest
temperature rows of the gain split. The readout rule holds below $\log_{10}T\approx0.1$; at higher temperature the
network factor itself can expand. Steered at block 4, the chaotic
cells at $\log_{10}T=0.15$ have a network factor of $+0.72$ and a contracting readout ($-0.35$). Along a line of the hostility-steered map (Section~\ref{sec:hostile})
at $\log_{10}T=0.12$--$0.13$ the network factor of the chaotic cells is $+0.60$ against $+0.24$ to $+0.38$ at the
neighboring periodic cells. The mechanism is not specific to
concept-labeled directions. This is consistent with the controls of Appendix~\ref{sec:origin}, in which norm-matched random directions also produce chaos in the 0.5B and 8B models, and rotating the steering direction at constant norm moves the chaotic regions but keeps the chaotic share between 13 and 24\,\% at every one of 103 angles (Figure~\ref{fig:morph}); at 70B the directions differ
(Appendix~\ref{sec:size}). What a direction does select is which tokens compete, and therefore what the attractors
write.
