\paragraph{Why low-dimensional structure can appear.} Near a bifurcation of a periodic orbit the center manifold
theorem reduces the local dynamics to the critical directions, so the normal forms of folds, flips and cusps, the
leading terms of that reduction, apply in any dimension. Near most orbits we computed the non-critical multipliers are small, from about $0.1$ at short periods to
below $10^{-13}$ along the cascade. This transverse contraction motivates scalar descriptions of whole attractors,
which need additional, global evidence; where the next multiplier is not small (up to $0.4$ along the period-5 curves
of Section~\ref{sec:organization}, and $0.28$ and $0.38$ in a high-temperature bubble of Section~\ref{sec:cascade}), such a description is only a
rough guide. Coexisting attracting orbits constrain such descriptions: in a
smooth unimodal interval map with negative Schwarzian derivative, the immediate basin of every attracting periodic
orbit contains the turning point or an endpoint, so at most one attracting periodic orbit avoids the endpoints
\cite{singer1978stable}, and coexistence calls for a second turning point, a stretch of
positive Schwarzian derivative or a second dimension (Appendix~\ref{sec:theorymore}).

\section{Methods in brief}
\label{sec:methodsbrief}
Results on the soft-token map labeled float64 are computed in float64, including the normalizations and rotary
tables that the library would otherwise compute in float32; two exceptions, float32 storage of some diagnostic step
records and a float32 final normalization in the two-GPU runs of 70B-class models before 14 October, are marked where they occur (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods} and Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}). A cell of a phase map is chaotic when the
largest finite-horizon Lyapunov exponent of a twin trajectory (offset $10^{-6}$, renormalized every step; 300
measured steps after 300 transient ones unless stated) exceeds $10^{-3}$ and no period up to 32 (48 in later zooms) is
found; at 448 checked cells automatic-differentiation tangents reproduce the twin exponents within $0.0041$, and the label is a finite-horizon diagnostic, not a proof. The twin starts from a random direction in 896 dimensions at the end of the transient, so the averaging window
includes its alignment, so the finite-window estimate of a periodic cell differs from $\ln|\mu_1|/p$
of its orbit, by amounts consistent with that transient (its contribution has not been isolated): at two adjacent cells inside the kite of Figure~\ref{fig:p6kite},
the map gives $-0.2786$ and $-0.0885$ per step and the Newton-converged period-6 orbits $-0.2821$ and $-0.0813$, offsets
of $+0.0035$ and $-0.0073$ (\repo{tools/kite_basin_check.py}). Periodic cells are classified by their period, not by
this exponent; the gray scale of the maps carries the offset. In random samples of 192 chaos-labeled cells from each of the two main float64 maps (Figures~\ref{fig:stripes} and~\ref{fig:teaser}), 176 and 143 cells were never captured by a periodic orbit in $10^5$ steps and all but one kept a positive exponent; the others were long transients that ended on periodic orbits after 600 to 97{,}500 steps \cite{grebogi1983crises}. Chaotic shares of a map are therefore finite-horizon shares of its window.
In a simplified noise-floor model, numerical noise of relative size $\sigma$ in a measured difference can produce a
spurious positive twin exponent, approximately $\max\{\lambda,\log(\sqrt2\,\sigma/\varepsilon)\}$ at offset
$\varepsilon$: below the noise level the estimate rises as the offset shrinks, while a real exponent does not depend
on the offset (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}, Figure~\ref{fig:falsechaos}). Rounding can also suppress separation or
change the sampled dynamics, so the labels are checked by comparing twin offsets and automatic-differentiation
tangents rather than by using this approximation as a one-sided guarantee.
Periodic orbits are solved by Newton's method with forward-mode derivatives; branches are continued by pseudo-arclength
continuation and fold and flip curves by minimally augmented systems \cite{govaerts2000numerical,kuznetsov2023elements};
cusps and other codimension-two points are located by bracketing sign changes along the curves; and the normal forms
of folds are computed from the network's derivatives without fitting. Appendix~\ref{sec:methods} gives the details
and the checks on maps with known answers.

\section{Chaos with periodic windows}
\label{sec:maps}
Figure~\ref{fig:stripes} shows a $256\times256$ float64 map over $\alpha\in[0.060,0.082]$ and
$\log_{10}T\in[-0.17,-0.04]$. More than half of its cells (35{,}650 of 65{,}536) meet the finite-horizon criterion, and 92\,\% of a random sample of them stay chaotic for $10^5$ steps, and periodic windows of
periods 2--32 are embedded in the chaos. Inside each window the gray darkens toward curves on which the periodic
orbit is most strongly attracting (Section~\ref{sec:organization}).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.49\linewidth]{fig-stripes-lyapunov-curves.png}\hfill
\includegraphics[width=0.49\linewidth]{fig-stripes-period-fold.png}
\caption{Soft-token map of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct steered along the philosophy direction at layer 12, $256^2$ float64
cells. Left: chaotic cells colored by the largest Lyapunov exponent, the other cells in gray by how strongly they
contract (light gray: close to losing stability; color bars as in Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}). Black dashed curves with a white edge are float64 fold curves of the 2-cycle ``is it''
(lower left, the boundary on which the stripes accumulate) and of the period-8 orbit (top). Right: periods, with the
period-8 fold curve (Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}).}
\label{fig:stripes}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} resolves the stripes' lower ends at $512\times512$ cells, of which 78{,}868 (30\,\%) meet the criterion; 74\,\% of a random sample of these stay chaotic for $10^5$ steps, and the share hardly depends on the resolution (30.0\,\% at $256\times256$), and
Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} labels the same cells by their attractor texts. Three structures stand out. The 2-cycle ``is
it'' fills the left, bounded by its fold curve, on which the stripes accumulate. A closed crescent of the period-3
loop ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe existence'' contains its period doublings and a chaotic core
(Section~\ref{sec:cascade}). Its continuation, the period-6 band ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe
question\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe existence'', crosses the stripes near $\log_{10}T=-0.14$, and where the
crescent lies on the 2-cycle's side of that fold curve, the two attractors coexist. The lower right is a chaotic
region filled with small hooked windows of ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe question'' and ``\textbackslash
n\textbackslash nThe existence'' loops.

These names are what the loops write. A period-3 orbit returns to the same soft state every three steps, and at its
three states the most probable next tokens are ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash n'' (a blank line, one token),
``The'' and `` existence'', with probabilities $0.74$, $0.36$ and $0.21$ at $\alpha=0.0642$, $\log_{10}T=-0.156$. Read
greedily, the loop writes ``The existence'' paragraph after paragraph; what it feeds back at each step, however, is the
probability-weighted average of all token embeddings, not that token. Coexistence is just as concrete. At that same
point the loop started from a state of the ``is it'' 2-cycle (taken from its attractor at $\alpha=0.0655$) stays on
the 2-cycle and writes `` is it is it\ldots'' (most probable tokens with probabilities $0.66$ and $0.29$; 300-step
exponent $-1.19$), while from the fixed start it settles on the period-3 loop (exponent $-0.13$). At $\alpha=0.0652$
the fixed start reaches the crescent's chaotic attractor, whose text alternates irregularly between ``The question''
and ``The existence'' paragraphs (exponent $+0.095$), and the 2-cycle start again writes `` is it'' ($-1.29$): one
model, one steering vector and one temperature, and the text written after the transient depends on where the loop starts
(\repo{tools/coexist_texts.py}, float64).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.62\linewidth]{fig-cross512-families.png}
\caption{The cells of Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12; $512^2$
float64 cells over $\alpha\in[0.0625,0.0775]$, $\log_{10}T\in[-0.17,-0.125]$), colored by the attractor's
leading-token loop (black: chaotic by the criterion of Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}; gray: no period up to 32 and not chaotic; bluish
gray: other periodic attractors).
Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} shows the largest Lyapunov
exponent of the same cells.}
\label{fig:cross512}
\end{figure}

Zooms of $256^2$ cells (Figure~\ref{fig:zooms}) show what the model writes in each window, and every window is a
repeating piece of text. The period-8 band repeats ``. This is there is a is a''. The neighboring bands add laps of
``is it'' (periods 10 and 12), and along the band's flip edge the period-16 loop repeats the sentence twice. The
small windows inside the chaos break the ``is X'' chain with a paragraph break and ``The'' (periods 11, 12, 13, 14).
Inside the period-8 and period-6 bands, two curves of maximal contraction meet in a sharp junction.

Top: the period-8 window ($\alpha\in[0.068,0.076]$, $\log_{10}T\in[-0.135,-0.085]$). Bottom: the period-6 hooks
($\alpha\in[0.072,0.082]$, $\log_{10}T\in[-0.085,-0.04]$), whose curved period-6 body, period-12 foot and thin leg
are analyzed in Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}.}

\end{figure}

\paragraph{What a chaotic loop writes.} The chaotic cells write text too (Table~\ref{tab:chaostexts}). Their text
reuses the words and phrases of the neighboring loops without settling into an order, and it depends on the state to
precisions far below rounding. To show the second property in words, we ran the loop at cells labeled chaotic for 1000 steps in
float64. We then recorded it together with a copy started at a relative distance of $10^{-10}$, about 600 times smaller
than the relative rounding error of float32. The copy is never reset. Its distance from the loop grows at about the
rate the cell's Lyapunov exponent sets, faster or slower over finite stretches (Figure~\ref{fig:chaostwins}). The two write the same text until the distance
has grown to between 1.27\,\% and 176\,\% of the soft token's norm, and different texts afterwards. In Qwen2.5-0.5B, two cells steered along
philosophy, with exponents 0.44 and 0.36, split at steps 44 and 51, and a cell steered along hostility, with exponent
0.35, at step 45. By then the distance had grown by 8.5 to 10.2 decades. The splits come where no token dominates. At
each of the seven splits we recorded, in this model and in Qwen2.5-1.5B below, the loop's most probable token has a
probability of at most 0.31 and leads the next by at most 0.08, and at five of them the copy writes the loop's second
choice. At step 44 of the cell with exponent 0.44, a
blank line and `` is'' have probabilities 0.139 and 0.135; at step 45 of the hostility cell, ``Yes'' and ``No'' have
0.303 and 0.298, and the copy writes ``No''.

The texts show where the chaos lives. Next to the period-3 crescent, where the exponent is only 0.10, the loop keeps
the skeleton of the period-3 text, a blank line, ``The'' and a noun, and only the noun varies, between `` existence''
and `` question'', whose probabilities come within 0.0025 of each other; there the texts split at step 151. On the
hostility plane (Section~\ref{sec:hostile}), the chaotic cell lies between windows whose loops write ``!Yes!Yes, that's
crap!Yeah'' and ``!No, that's crap!Yeah!Yes!Yes''. It repeats ``!Yes'' and inserts ``, nonsense'', ``, that'' and
``!No, that'' at irregular intervals.
In Qwen2.5-1.5B steered along philosophy, two chaotic cells (exponents 0.83 and 0.80) write short questions
separated by line breaks, ``What?'', ``What? Or'' and ``In''. Their copies split at steps 22 and 10; at step 10,
``What'' and ``In'' have probabilities 0.091 and 0.090. Finite stretches can expand much faster than the average: that
copy's distance grew a hundredfold in its first step. Without steering, at $\log_{10}T=0.657$, the next-token
distribution is nearly uniform, and in the recorded steps the most probable token never exceeds a probability of 0.001.
There the chaotic loop (exponent 0.59) writes runs of exclamation marks, in ASCII and in full width, commas, the
end-of-turn marker and the Chinese \begin{CJK}{UTF8}{gbsn}一下\end{CJK} (``a bit''), and its copy splits at step 43.

The text is fragile to the computation itself. We recorded the cell next to the crescent twice in float64 on the same
processor, once with six threads and once with four, which changes the order of floating-point sums. During the
1000-step transient the chaos had already amplified that difference, so the two recordings started from slightly
different states; they wrote the same text for 27 steps and different texts from step 28 on. At 70B the chaotic cells
of Figure~\ref{fig:z128} draw on the words of the unsteered model's fifteen-token loop (Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}):
the first eight distinct leading tokens of one chaotic cell confirmed at three twin offsets are `` I'', ``The'',
``!'', `` It'', `` infinite'', `` possibilities'', `` are'' and `` you'' (17 distinct tokens in 300 steps).

\paragraph{What the attractors look like.} The numerically observed attractors can be drawn. At the cell of strongest chaos on
the row $\log_{10}T=-0.148$ of Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} ($\alpha=0.07248$), 512 trajectories started near the fixed
start and recorded for 600 steps after 400 transient steps (float64) approach, all but 14 of them, one numerically
observed chaotic attractor; the other 14 reach a coexisting period-4 orbit. Projected on the first two principal axes
of its states (61.6\,\% of the variance; 77.4\,\% with the third), it shows thin folded filaments, colored by the most
probable next token, and successive magnifications show layers within layers (Figure~\ref{fig:strangeattractor}). The loop is a map, not a
flow: one step is one pass through the network, and a typical step moves the state by 0.30 in all 896 dimensions,
more than the mean distance (0.21) of the states from their center, so consecutive states of one trajectory usually
lie far apart on the attractor rather than along one filament. The four leading Lyapunov exponents (QR iteration, 3000 steps) are $0.274$, $-1.052$,
$-1.486$ and $-1.856$ per step, so the Lyapunov (Kaplan--Yorke) dimension estimate is $D_{\mathrm{KY}}=1.26$, a
quantity of the exponents rather than a measured fractal dimension. The correlation sum gives no dimension: its local
slope wanders between 0.42 and 0.91 over four decades of distance without settling on any decade, as for a strongly
non-uniform measure. One scalar of the output suffices to rebuild the set usefully: in delay coordinates of the
logarithm of the largest next-token probability (float32, 16 trajectories of 6000 steps), the share of false nearest
neighbors falls from 70.8\,\% with one delay coordinate and 10.4\,\% with two to 3.4\,\% with three, and points that are
nearest neighbors in the space of three delay coordinates are a median $3.7\times10^{-4}$ apart in the full state, against 0.30 for random
pairs. This supports a faithful reconstruction from the output alone; it is not a verified Takens embedding. Past the crescent's cascade ($\alpha=0.06626$; exponents $0.036$, $-1.208$, \dots;
$D_{\mathrm{KY}}=1.030$) the chaos is banded: each trajectory visits three thin bands in turn. The period-3 orbit that
lost stability in the cascade is still there, as a saddle with multiplier $-1.772$ (next $0.040$); we continued it to
this cell by Newton's method (relative residual $4.2\times10^{-13}$) and iterated a fundamental domain of its unstable
manifold 20 times under $F^6$, six steps of the map at a time. In the 12 leading principal coordinates, every one of the $100{,}600$ recorded
states of the band it reaches lies within $9.8\times10^{-6}$ of the sampled points of the manifold (median
$3.9\times10^{-7}$; the band is 0.028 wide). This supports an unstable-manifold description of the band at the sampled
resolution; it does not establish that the band equals the closure of the manifold. At the cell of the certified pair ($\alpha=0.06468$), every one of 9216 starts on a plane through the fixed
start settles on the 2-cycle (47.3\,\%) or the 3-cycle (52.7\,\%) (float32, 400 steps). The two basins interleave
in stripes, and the fixed start lies on their boundary at the resolution of the slice
(Figure~\ref{fig:pairbasins}), consistent with the switch of the attractor reached from it within
$2\times10^{-7}$ in $\alpha$ at the crescent's edge.

\paragraph{What ordinary generation writes at the same cells.} The loops' texts are not what the model writes when it
generates normally. At the four cells of the stripe row shown in this section, at three period-3 cells and at the
crescent's chaotic core, we generated 64 tokens the ordinary way: the same prefix, steering of the same strength at the
same layer, and the same temperature, but each step appends one token to a growing context (Table~\ref{tab:companion}).
Ordinary generation writes fluent prose about meaning, existence and consciousness, the topic of the steering
direction, whether the loop at that cell is periodic or chaotic. Greedy decoding writes one of three openings across
the eight cells, and samples at the cell's temperature differ from one another as sampled text does. The loops instead
recycle a few words, ``The'', ``question'', ``existence'', ``is'', ``a'' and ``there''. These are the opening words of
those replies: one sample begins ``The question is a deeply philosophical\ldots''. With only its latest output as
memory, the loop keeps returning to the start of an answer. Unsteered, greedy decoding answers ``Hello! How can I
assist you today?'' (exploratory; \repo{tools/normal_companion.py}, \repo{docs/companion/companion.json}).

\section{What the chaos does to inference and steering}
\label{sec:consequences}
The loop studied here is the expected-embedding feedback that continuous reasoning methods such as Soft Thinking use
\cite{zhang2025softthinking}, and its chaos has consequences that can be measured without any reference to dynamical
systems. We report three: whether a run can be reproduced, how the depth at which a steering vector enters sets the
chaos it causes, and what happens when two steering vectors are combined. A fourth, that a repeating text can hide a
chaotic state from anyone who reads only the output, because the output records only the most probable token, is
shown in Section~\ref{sec:hostile}.

\paragraph{How a change of batch reaches the output.} Floating-point addition is not associative: $(a+b)+c$ and
$a+(b+c)$ can differ in the last bit \cite{goldberg1991floating}. A matrix multiplication in a transformer sums thousands
of products, and GPU libraries split and order those sums according to the shape of the matrices, to keep the hardware
busy; the number of requests processed together, the batch, is part of that shape. The same request computed alone or
in a batch therefore receives results that differ at the rounding level, about $10^{-16}$ relative in float64 and about
$10^{-3}$ in bfloat16. In a single forward pass such differences are invisible. In a feedback loop they are not lost:
each output becomes the next input, so the difference between two runs is fed back and transformed by the map's
derivative at every step. On a periodic attractor the derivative contracts on average, the exponent is negative, and
the difference decays until both runs follow the same orbit and write the same text. On a chaotic attractor the
exponent $\lambda$ is positive and the difference grows like $d_1e^{\lambda t}$ from its first value $d_1$, so it
reaches a relative size of $10^{-2}$ after about $\ln(10^{-2}/d_1)/\lambda$ steps (about 100 steps for $d_1\approx
10^{-15}$ and $\lambda\approx0.3$). At most a few Lyapunov times later, at 1.27 to 176\,\% of the state's norm in the examples of
Table~\ref{tab:chaostexts} (0 to 16 steps after the distance passes $10^{-2}$), the runs write different text. This has a practical consequence.
Batch-invariant kernels, which fix the order of every reduction independently of the batch size, remove this source
of difference \cite{he2025nondeterminism}. In a periodic regime that is enough, and small differences would be forgiven
even without it. In a chaotic regime it is not: any remaining difference, from another GPU model, a library or compiler
version, the numerical precision, or a mathematically equivalent reordering of a sum, is amplified in the same way, so a
run is reproducible only if the whole computation is repeated bit for bit. Numerical differences are the disturbance;
the feedback dynamics decide whether it grows. How far it grows within a given budget also depends on its starting
size, on the horizon and on how close the run comes to a change of answer, so a periodic loop can still change an
answer when the starting difference is large. In bfloat16 the starting difference is already about $10^{-3}$, so continuous
feedback carries it into different answers within a short budget even where the float64 loop is not chaotic (below).

\paragraph{Whether rounding differences grow depends on the regime.} Language-model inference is not bitwise
reproducible: GPU kernels choose different reduction orders for different matrix shapes, so a request's result depends
on its batch layout, the set of requests that the GPU processes together with it, and on the hardware
\cite{he2025nondeterminism,yuan2025nondeterminism}. We ran the 448 labeled
cells of the horizon check (192 chaotic, 192 periodic and 64 other cells of two float64 maps; Appendix~\ref{sec:methods})
twice in lockstep from the same start. The two runs differ only in the batch: all cells in one batch of $n$ rows, or
the same rows followed by $n$ further rows whose steering vectors are those of the first $n$ in reverse order, at the
original temperatures ($2n$ rows). This changes the matrix shapes the kernels see; the first $n$ rows compute the same
cells (Figure~\ref{fig:repro}, Table~\ref{tab:repro}). In float64 the two runs first differ by about
$6\times10^{-15}$, the rounding level. At 190 of the 192 chaotic cells this difference passes a relative distance of
$10^{-2}$, after a median of 102 steps, and grows on to order one (median final distance $0.91$); at 173 of the 192
chaotic cells the most probable token of the loop's readout differs between the runs at some step of the last 500. At
182 of the 192 periodic cells the two runs stay within $10^{-2}$ for all 1500 steps, with a median largest distance of
$2.3\times10^{-12}$, and 182 of the 192 periodic cells end the run within $3\times10^{-12}$; at only 7 of the 192
periodic cells do the most probable tokens differ at any step of the last 500, so a periodic loop almost never changes
what it writes. The Lyapunov exponent
accounts for when a chaotic run diverges: the step at which the distance passes $10^{-2}$ follows
$\ln(10^{-2}/d_1)/\lambda$, where $d_1$ is the smallest nonzero distance in the run, with a median ratio of $0.91$
and a log-log correlation of $0.73$ over 190 cells. This uses the whole run and is therefore retrospective; an
independent recomputation with the first nonzero distance instead gives the same rounded values. The two chaotic
cells that never diverge have exponents of $0.013$ and $0.0013$, for which the predicted step lies beyond the run.

\paragraph{A fixed-shape control and a dose test.} Two checks, fixed in advance (\repo{docs/fs-design.md}), test this
account directly on the same 448 cells (Figure~\ref{fig:fixedshape}). When the matrix shapes do not change, the two runs
are bitwise identical over all 1500 steps in every cell, both when the same rows run twice and when the rows are
permuted, so that each cell has other neighbors and another position in the batch. The difference therefore comes
from the shapes alone: rows do not leak into one another, and the computation is deterministic. Changing the shape
again separates 190 of the 192 chaotic cells and leaves 182 of the 192 periodic ones within $10^{-2}$, as before.
With the shapes fixed, we then displaced the start of each cell by a relative amount $d$ from $10^{-15}$ to
$10^{-3}$. In the chaotic cells the displacement grows on the schedule the exponent sets. The step at which it passes
$10^{-2}$ divided by $\ln(10^{-2}/d)/\lambda$ has medians of 0.87, 1.01, 1.08, 1.10 and 1.18 for $d=10^{-15}$ to
$10^{-7}$, inside the frozen band from 0.7 to 1.3. The median step rises by 2.9 per unit of $\ln(1/d)$, against a
median $1/\lambda$ of 3.9, within the frozen 30\,\%. At $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-3}$ the linear account fails (medians
1.21 and 1.67), as it must for displacements that are no longer small. In the periodic cells a displacement of
$10^{-11}$ or less separates the runs in at most 6.3\,\% of cells. From $10^{-9}$ upward it separates 10 to 59\,\%,
presumably because displaced states cross basin boundaries into another attractor's basin (the attractors reached were
not classified). This fails a secondary criterion, which allowed at most
10\,\% up to $d=10^{-5}$.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-fixed-shape.png}
\caption{Fixed-shape control and dose test (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, the 448 cells of two float64 maps, 192 chaotic, 192 periodic and 64 with neither label, 1500
lockstep steps; \repo{tools/repro_noise.py}, \repo{tools/plot_fixed_shape.py}). (a) Largest relative distance between
paired runs per cell: with the same matrix shapes, whether the same rows run twice or the rows are permuted, it is
exactly zero in all 448 cells (plotted at $10^{-18}$); a changed shape separates the chaotic cells. (b) With the shapes
fixed, a displacement $d$ injected at the start passes $10^{-2}$ after the number of steps $\ln(10^{-2}/d)/\lambda$
predicts (chaotic cells; medians and quartiles; labels: median ratio of measured to predicted). (c) Share of
periodic cells whose displaced runs separate, presumably by crossing into another basin (the attractors reached were
not classified).}
\label{fig:fixedshape}
\end{figure}
The same holds without steering in Qwen2.5-7B, whose embeddings are untied. Over 24 chaotic and 24 periodic
temperatures of its unsteered loop, all 24 chaotic float64 runs diverge, after a median of 56 steps (the exponent again
accounts for the step, median ratio $0.95$), and at 23 of them the readout's most probable token differs at some step
of the last 500. None of the 24 periodic runs moves past $10^{-2}$; 22 end within $1.3\times10^{-13}$ of each other
and all within $2.2\times10^{-12}$. A second model family, unsteered Llama-3.1-8B, gives the same picture: all 20
chaotic temperatures diverge after a median of 68 steps (median ratio to the exponent's estimate $1.05$), all 20 differ
in their readout's most probable token in the last 500 steps, and 19 of the 20 periodic ones stay within
$10^{-2}$. At 70B the picture is unchanged. In Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct steered along hostility-vs-kindness at block 20
(float64 arithmetic on its stored bfloat16 weights, layers spread over two GPUs, 1000 steps), all 24 chaotic cells
diverge, after a median of 114.5 steps (median ratio to the exponent's estimate $0.83$, log-log correlation $0.74$),
and at 21 of them the readout's most probable token differs at some step of the last 300. None of the 24 periodic cells moves past $10^{-2}$, and all end within
$1.3\times10^{-12}$. In lower precision the rounding is
larger. In float32 every chaotic cell of both models diverges, after a median of 20 steps (0.5B) or 16 (7B). In
bfloat16, a common serving precision, the two layouts pass a distance of $10^{-2}$ after a median of one or two steps
at every cell of the two Qwen models and at 38 of the 40 Llama cells, periodic ones included. With these kernels and at
that precision, the loop was not reproducible across batch layouts in any regime we tested.

The same test at the level of answers, with a design frozen before the run, shows what this means for a task. We
asked Qwen2.5-0.5B 96 held-out two-digit additions through a short soft-thinking pipeline: for 32 steps the model is
fed the probability-weighted average of its token embeddings (the input of $F$), each new one appended to a growing
context, and a fixed answer cue then reads the number off greedily. Each problem ran alone and in a batch with three
fixed distractor problems of the same length. Nothing is padded, so the two runs compute the same quantities and differ
only in how the GPU groups its sums for the two matrix shapes, that is, by rounding. In bfloat16 the extracted answer changed with the batch layout in 56\,\% of the problems at $T=1$ and in
8\,\% at $T=10^{0.2}$. At $T=1$ the pipeline answers 23 of the 96 problems correctly alone and 22 in the batch, so
most changes are between wrong answers; at $T=10^{0.4}$, where it answers 90 of the 96 correctly in both layouts, no
answer changed (\repo{docs/pi/layout-answer-audit.json}). A forced greedy baseline with the same budget (32 greedy tokens, then the same cue) produced
identical token sequences in both layouts for every problem. The excess disagreement of soft over greedy decoding,
averaged over the two temperatures, is 32.3 percentage points (95\,\% interval $[26.5,38.1]$; sign-flip $p=10^{-4}$,
the smallest attainable). In float64 no answer changed within the 32 steps. There, the median growth of an injected
kick to the first soft token is $0.033$ nats per step at $T=1$ (range $-0.13$ to $0.24$ over six problems and three
kick sizes), and even a relative kick of $10^{-4}$ changes no answer. The bfloat16 discrepancies therefore do not
require the float64 map's chaos; they are consistent with bfloat16's rounding differences accumulating through
continuous feedback. A discrete argmax absorbs differences smaller than its margin, but not over a long feedback run in a pipeline that answers poorly (``Soft Thinking as published'', below). (The forced greedy baseline's accuracy is zero and not
informative: forced through 32 tokens, the model writes past its answer, and the cue then extracts another number.)
The same frozen test in Qwen2.5-7B, whose soft-thinking pipeline answers 51 to 64\,\% of these problems, replicates
the result. In bfloat16 the batch layout changed the soft-thinking answer in 53 of the 96 problems (55\,\%) at
$T=10^{0.2}$, the temperature at which the soft trace still matters. In 24 of these 53 problems one layout gave no
number (an invalid answer, which the frozen design counts as a value); in the other 29 (30\,\% of the problems) both
gave numbers, and they differ. The pipeline answered 49 of the 96 correctly alone and 61 in the batch; at
$T=10^{0.4}$ it answered all 96 correctly in both layouts, and no answer changed. At this precision even the forced greedy baseline
changed in 10 of the 96, the known batch-variance effect. Averaged over the frozen design's two
temperatures, $T=1$ and $10^{0.2}$, as for the 0.5B model, the excess over greedy decoding is 17.7 points
($[9.3,25.6]$, $p=2\times10^{-4}$); at $T=10^{0.2}$ alone it is 44.8 points (53 against 10 of 96), and at $T=1$, where
the soft pipeline answers none correctly and changed one answer, it is negative. The forced greedy baseline is a
computational comparator, not a useful-answer baseline: it answers 1 of the 96 correctly. In float64, at that temperature, the 7B's loop amplifies an injected perturbation
(median $+0.034$ nats per step), injected kicks change answers (2 of 18 kicks: six problems and three kick sizes), and
the rounding-level difference between the layouts changed 5\,\% of the answers within the 32 steps.
Two horizons thus matter. Within a short budget, continuous feedback in low precision makes answers depend on the
batch, and where the loop expands, even float64 rounding does. Over longer runs, a chaotic regime amplifies float64
rounding into different readout tokens, on the schedule its Lyapunov exponent sets.

An exploratory causal test locates the low-precision effect in the loop. Following a suggestion of an external review, we reran
the 0.5B pipeline at $T=1$ on 128 newly drawn additions, after a batch-1 reference run of each (exploratory; first on a
workstation GPU, where the native pipeline's answers changed with the layout in 74\,\% of the problems, against
56\,\% on the H100 of the frozen test). In the batch of four, two arms start from the reference's prompt state and
are answered by the same batch-1 decoder. In the free arm the soft phase runs on its own; in the reset arm the
target's cache and logits are replaced by the reference's before every soft step, so that each step runs in the batch
but only one at a time. In bfloat16 the answer changed in 88 of the 128 problems (69\,\%) in the free arm and in 7
(5.5\,\%) in the reset arm, a difference of 63 points (95\,\% interval $[55,72]$; 82 discordant pairs against 1, exact
McNemar $p=2\times10^{-23}$), while accuracy stayed between 23 and 25\,\% in every arm. One batched step moves the
target's logits by a median $1.5\,\%$ of their norm; in the free arm the median difference grows to $0.79$ of it by
step 32, while the reset arm's median per step stays between $1.3$ and $3.1\,\%$. In float32 the free arm's drift grows about 140-fold over the
32 steps but stays near $10^{-4}$, and 3 of the 128 answers changed (none with reset). The test replicates on an H100
and in Qwen2.5-7B. On the H100, in bfloat16, the answer changed in 60 of the 128 problems (47\,\%) in the free arm and
in 1 (0.8\,\%) with reset in the 0.5B model, and in 52 (41\,\%) against none in Qwen2.5-7B at $T=10^{0.2}$
(exact McNemar $p\le5\times10^{-16}$); in the 7B model even float32 answers changed in 33 (26\,\%) against none. In bfloat16 on the H100 most batched steps reproduce the reference bitwise (the reset arm's median step distance is
0), and the free loop amplifies the rare step that does not. No arm's
accuracy differs from the reference's by more than 5 points. Within this budget, the batch layout
changes low-precision answers because the loop carries each step's rounding forward and amplifies it, not because the
last steps alone are fragile; 128 problems per arm do not show that accuracy is unchanged. These counts compare the
extracted numbers, as the frozen test does. Until 19 October 2026 the test compared the decoded answer texts, which
also counts the same number followed by different text, and reported higher counts (102 and 14 on the workstation; 85
and 2, and 82 and 2, on the H100); the corrected and the original summaries are in \repo{docs/rr}.

\paragraph{Soft Thinking as published.} A test frozen before its data (\repo{docs/pi-matrix-confirmatory.md}) ran
Soft Thinking with its published settings on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in bfloat16. The settings are concept tokens
restricted to the 15 most probable, $T=0.6$ and 64 soft steps. The test used 256 GSM8K training problems that no
earlier run had scored, on an H100 GPU. Only the batch layout changed, between batches of 8 and of 16 prompts of equal
length without padding, and the answer changed for 21.1\,\% of the problems (54 of 256; 95\,\% interval clustered by
batch $[16.3,26.1]$). This confirms the prediction of more than 10\,\%. The batches deviated from the design in one
respect: the harness filled each group of equal-length prompts from one pool of fillers, and where that pool ran
short, both layouts received the same batch. Rebuilt from the tokenizer, 16 of the 256 problems ran in identical
batches in both layouts, and none of them changed; among the other 240 the rate is 54 of 240 (22.5\,\%). Some batches
were also short (11 of the 78 batches of 8 and 26 of the 77 of 16; \repo{docs/pi/layout-answer-audit.json}). Each secondary prediction compares another arm with this one on
the same 256 problems (paired differences of flip rates, intervals clustered by batch, exact McNemar tests):
\begin{itemize}
  \item replaying the reference run's fed-back tokens in both layouts: 5.9\,\% (difference 15.2 points,
  $[10.4,20.2]$, $p=8\times10^{-9}$); predicted lower, and it is;
  \item 16 soft steps instead of 64: 6.6\,\% (difference 14.5 points, $[10.0,19.0]$, $p=4\times10^{-7}$); predicted
  lower, and it is;
  \item ordinary greedy chain-of-thought decoding: 2.3\,\%, at 89\,\% accuracy (difference 18.8 points,
  $[13.7,23.6]$, $p=10^{-11}$); predicted lower, and it is;
  \item feeding back only the most probable token: 17.6\,\% (difference 3.5 points, $[-2.9,10.3]$, $p=0.35$);
  predicted lower, and the test cannot tell the two apart.
\end{itemize}
The three confirmed differences would survive a correction for the four tests by a wide margin, and the fourth is a
difference the test does not resolve: discrete feedback through the same pipeline flips nearly as many answers as
continuous feedback does, although the interval does not establish that the two are equal.
The full vocabulary gave 22.3\,\% (no prediction). Most changes are between wrong answers: through the soft pipeline
this model answers only 22 to 30\,\% of the problems correctly, and the layout changes whether the answer is correct
for 6.2\,\% of the problems. In float64 the same pipeline contracts. On 1312 and 1314 of the 1319 GSM8K test problems
(full vocabulary and top-15), a perturbation of $10^{-10}$ ended below its initial size, and none changed an answer.
These flips are therefore not chaos of the float64 map: the feedback carries bfloat16 rounding differences forward
until they change answers in a pipeline that answers only 22 to 30\,\% of the problems correctly, and it does so for
discrete feedback as well.

\paragraph{How the dependence builds up.} An exploratory study read the answers of the same pipeline after every
doubling of the number of soft steps, from 2 to 128. It used 200 GSM8K test problems, two temperatures (0.6 and 1.2),
and six steering strengths along the hostility direction ($\alpha$ from $-0.02$ to $0.05$). The two layouts agree on
90 to 96\,\% of the answers after 2 steps and on 51 to 70\,\% after 128, in every configuration. Among the problems
whose layouts disagree, the median first disagreement comes after 8 steps. Float64 copies of the same configurations,
started $10^{-10}$ apart, separate the two sources of disagreement.
\begin{itemize}
  \item Unsteered or weakly steered ($|\alpha|\le0.02$), the copies change the answer text of at most 1.5\,\% of the problems. The loop
  contracts, so the bfloat16 disagreements are rounding carried forward by the feedback.
  \item At $\alpha=0.05$ they change the answer text of 13.5\,\% of the problems at $T=0.6$ and 27.5\,\% at $T=1.2$, and
  the extracted number of 4.5 and 9.0\,\%. The loop itself is
  then sensitive, but accuracy has fallen to between 0.5 and 2.5\,\% in either layout.
\end{itemize}
In this model, then, the regime in which Soft Thinking is useful is the regime of accumulated rounding, not of chaos
(\repo{docs/pi/h-horizon-summary.json}).

\paragraph{Reasoning models that answer well.} Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct answers few of these problems through the soft
pipeline, so most of its flips are between wrong answers. A second test, also frozen before its data
(\repo{docs/pi-matrix2-confirmatory.md}), used reasoning models, as the Soft Thinking paper did:
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B and QwQ-32B. A row thinks in concept tokens (top 15,
$T=0.6$) until its most probable next token ends the thinking block, or until 1536 steps. It then answers by sampling
discrete tokens, with random numbers shared between the two layouts. The comparison arm thinks with discrete tokens
sampled the same way. On GSM8K problems not used before in this study the batches held 8 or 16 prompts of equal length. On the integer-answer
problems of MATH-500, each problem ran alone or as eight copies of itself. In the first run the tokenizer of
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B loaded wrongly: it dropped the spaces and newlines of every prompt, and the model thought
far less and answered 45\,\% of a check set correctly, against 83\,\% with the correct tokenizer in bfloat16 and
81\,\% in float64 (\repo{docs/pi/pm2x-llama-diag.json}). Both of its cells were rerun with the correct tokenizer,
an amendment after the data. Over the five primary cells, the batch layout changed 27.1\,\% of the soft-thinking
answers (243 of 896; 95\,\% interval $[24.7,29.5]$), confirming the prediction of more than 5\,\%; this is a weighted
summary of these cells, which mix two tasks and two layout constructions, not a general susceptibility of the models. The prediction
holds in every version of the pooling, including the original run (29.6\,\%) and the run without both cells of this
model (21.9\,\%) (\repo{docs/pi/pm2fix-summary.json}, \repo{docs/pi/pm2fix-math-summary.json}).
\begin{itemize}
  \item All three models changed their GSM8K answers with the layout alone: DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B
  for 14.5\,\% of the problems ($[10.9,18.0]$) at 88 to 90\,\% accuracy, and QwQ-32B for 19.5\,\% ($[16.4,22.3]$) at
  86 to 87\,\%, and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B for 28.9\,\% ($[23.4,34.4]$) at 74 to 77\,\%. The two layouts disagreed
  on correctness for 11.7, 14.5 and 18.0\,\% of the problems.
  \item Discrete sampled thinking also changed many answers. On GSM8K the rates were 10.2, 19.9 and 21.5\,\% for the
  same three models. Only in DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B did soft thinking change more, by 7.4 points
  ($[1.9,12.9]$; exact McNemar $p=0.029$, not corrected for the five cells), and it answered correctly 4.7 points less
  often ($[-8.6,-0.8]$; McNemar $p=0.096$). The intervals resample batches, while the exact McNemar test treats
  problems as independent pairs, so the two can disagree, as they do for this accuracy difference. Elsewhere no soft-minus-plain
  difference is significant, which does not show that the two are equal. In these pipelines the effect did not
  require continuous thinking: long sampled feedback in bfloat16 showed it too.
  \item On an A100 instead of an H100, the first model's soft rate was 11.7\,\% (difference 2.7 points,
  $[-1.6,6.6]$), a separate replication on a second GPU type; the difference is not significant, which again does not
  establish equality.
  \item Where the budget cut the reasoning short, accuracy was lower and the layout changed more answers. On MATH-500,
  where half or more of the problems reached the thinking cap, it changed 61\,\% of the answers of
  DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (44 to 48\,\% correct) and 67\,\% of those of DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (39 to
  42\,\% correct).
  \item Nearly all GSM8K changes came from long runs. Of the 161 answers that the layout changed with soft thinking,
  158 came from problems in which the thinking budget (1536 steps) or the answer budget (256 tokens) was reached in at
  least one layout, and so did all 132 changes with sampled thinking. Among problems that finished within both budgets
  in both layouts, the layout changed 3 of 480 soft answers and none of 477 sampled ones
  (\repo{docs/pi/layout-answer-audit.json}).
\end{itemize}
These runs did not use the Cold Stop rule of the Soft Thinking paper, which ends thinking once the entropy has stayed
below 0.01 nats for 256 steps. An exploratory rerun used it, with a thinking budget of 4096 steps
(\repo{docs/pi/pm2cs-design.md}, \repo{docs/pi/pm2cs-summary.json}). Cold Stop ended thinking in only 6 of the 512
soft rows and in none of the 512 sampled rows; the models nearly always ended it themselves. The layout still changed the answers: 8.6\,\% (soft) and
17.2\,\% (sampled) for DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on 128 GSM8K problems, 50.0 and 54.7\,\% for it on MATH-500, and
17.2 and 9.4\,\% for QwQ-32B on 64 GSM8K problems. The soft-minus-sampled difference changes sign between these
cells. On the same type of GPU, every row that ended thinking on its own within the original 1536 steps was reproduced
exactly in its saved switch step and answer, so the changes come from the rows the original budget cut short; the DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B GSM8K rerun
ran on a different H100 variant.

Is this chaos? We ran float64 copies of the same pipeline. Each copy's first soft token was displaced from the base
row's by a relative $10^{-10}$, and all copies shared the base row's random numbers. The three models respond differently. In
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, 216 of 256 copies ended the thinking at a distance from their base row below the
initial displacement, and 2.3\,\% changed the answer. Its layout flips are therefore mainly bfloat16
rounding carried to answers near ties, as in Qwen2.5-7B. In QwQ-32B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, more than half
of the copies ended above the initial displacement (131 of 192 and 145 of 256). Fitted over the steps where the
distance lay between ten times the displacement and $10^{-3}$, the median growth rates were 0.011 and 0.008 per step.
The displacement alone changed 13.5 and 18.4\,\% of the answers, respectively. In those models the loop's own sensitivity contributes to the answer
changes. It is a weak, finite-time instability over about a thousand thinking steps, and it acts on top of the rounding; it is
not an asymptotic chaos measurement. Some copies ended thinking before their base row (14 in R1-Llama, 23 in QwQ),
and the copies of one problem are not independent
(\repo{docs/pi/pm2f-summary.json}, \repo{docs/pi/pm2f64b-summary.json}; 128 problems for each 7--8B model and
96 for QwQ-32B).
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-repro.png}
\caption{Only the batch layout differs. The 448 cells (192 chaotic, 192 periodic, 64 with neither label) of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct (philosophy direction, layer
12) run twice from the same start, alone in a batch or followed by $n$ further rows with the steering vectors in
reverse order. (a) float64: the relative
distance between the two runs for every chaotic (orange) and periodic (blue) cell, by the cells' float64 labels, with
the medians and interquartile ranges of each label (dark) and the median of the 64 cells with neither label (gray); the
chaotic runs separate to order one; the median periodic distance stays at the rounding level, with exceptions. (b) Medians for
float32 and bfloat16 batch layouts; in bfloat16 both kinds separate within a few steps. (c) float64 chaotic cells: the step at which the distance passes $10^{-2}$ against
$\ln(10^{-2}/d_1)/\lambda$, with $d_1$ the smallest nonzero distance in the run and $\lambda$ the map exponent (a
retrospective comparison); dotted, equality.}
\label{fig:repro}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Where a steering vector enters decides how much chaos it causes.} Steering vectors are a common tool for
controlling and probing models \cite{zou2023representation,rimsky2024steering}, usually added at one layer chosen by
hand. In Qwen2.5-0.5B the philosophy direction, built with the same recipe at blocks 4, 8, 12, 16 and 20 and scaled to
each block's residual norm, makes 5.9, 8.1, 6.8, 5.0 and 2.3\,\% of the same $128\times128$ plane chaotic
(Figure~\ref{fig:layersweep}). Early steering carries the chaos down to $\log_{10}T=-0.95$ (block 8) and $-0.91$
(block 4), while at block 20 the chaotic cells lie between $\log_{10}T=-0.09$ and $0.22$, three fifths of them above
$0.1$. In the strip $|\alpha|<0.02$ every block gives 0.8 to 2.1\,\%. Llama-3.3-70B behaves the same way. Steered along its hostility direction (a contrastive valence direction; Section~\ref{sec:hostile}), it is chaotic in 8.0\,\%
of a $24\times24$ map at block 20 of 80, down to $\log_{10}T=-0.14$, against 4.3\,\% at block 35, where the chaos starts
at $\log_{10}T=0.01$, 4.3\,\% at block 50 (32 by 32 cells, a two-GPU map from before the precision fix of Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}), and 0.7\,\% at block 65, all above
$\log_{10}T=0.19$. These 70B labels are those of the map
protocol, one twin offset, and so are candidates. A logit bias, the latest possible point of entry, gives chaos mostly
above $\log_{10}T=0.1$ (Section~\ref{sec:theory}). The gain split of Section~\ref{sec:theory}, repeated along
steering lines at all five blocks of the 0.5B model, shows where the depth enters. At low temperature
($\log_{10}T\le0$) the chaotic cells of blocks 4, 8 and 12 have expanding readout factors (medians $+0.74$, $+0.46$ and
$+0.46$ nats per step over 54, 56 and 21 cells) and contracting network factors ($-0.47$ to $-0.21$). At block 16
the readout factor of such cells is only $+0.15$, and block 20 gives just 6 of them. Early injection creates token
competition at low temperature more readily; late injection mostly does not. Its chaos lives at higher temperature,
where the network factor carries it (block 20: network $+1.09$, readout $-0.78$; Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism}e).
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-layer-sweep.png}
\caption{The philosophy direction of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, built with the same recipe at each of five blocks of 24: largest
Lyapunov exponent on $128\times128$ float64 cells over the steering strength and $\log_{10}T\in[-1,0.3]$. The share of chaotic cells (finite-horizon exponent above $10^{-3}$, no period up to 48) is given above
each panel.}
\label{fig:layersweep}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Two steering vectors interact.} Steering at two blocks at once, gratitude at block 4 and hostility at block
16 of Qwen2.5-0.5B, makes 21.7\,\% of a $96\times96$ plane chaotic at $\log_{10}T=-0.1$. The grid has no row or column at
exactly zero, so we computed the single pushes separately, each with the other exactly absent and on the same grid: they
make 12.5\,\% (gratitude at block 4 alone) and 5.2\,\% (hostility at block 16 alone) of their lines chaotic. Large
chaotic regions lie where neither push alone is chaotic (Figure~\ref{fig:twoblock}): 1561 cells, 16.9\,\% of the plane
and 78\,\% of its chaotic cells, are chaotic although each push alone, at the same strength, is not, and in 1525 of them
each push alone is periodic. Over the four planes (two block orders, $\log_{10}T=\pm0.1$) this share is 8.3 to
16.9\,\% of the plane, and 7.8 to 16.5\,\% with both single pushes periodic. With the blocks exchanged (hostility at 4,
gratitude at 16) the plane is 23.4\,\% chaotic, and at $\log_{10}T=0.1$ both pairs give 33 and 42\,\%. Each plane keeps
a periodic hole around zero steering. Combining steering vectors is therefore not additive in its effect on the loop's
stability: a composition of two pushes can be chaotic where each push alone is periodic.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{fig-two-block.png}
\caption{Two steering directions at two blocks of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct: largest Lyapunov exponent on $96\times96$
float64 cells over the two strengths, at $\log_{10}T=-0.1$ (left) and $0.1$ (right). Top: gratitude at block 4,
hostility at block 16; bottom: the blocks exchanged. Dotted lines mark the zero axes; the grid does not sample them,
and the single-push controls quoted in the text were computed separately on exact zero lines.}
\label{fig:twoblock}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Does a chaotic loop predict worse answers in ordinary decoding? Not beyond the model's own uncertainty.}
Two exploratory designs found no accuracy signature of the regimes that survives controls
(Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour}). A frozen test then labeled 4000 newly drawn products of two two-digit numbers by the regime
of the unsteered Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct's own loop at $\log_{10}T=0.40$. Chaotic-loop prompts are answered less
accurately by ordinary sampling, by 7.1 points (95\,\% interval $[4.8,9.3]$), but the gap vanishes once seven
difficulty covariates, fixed before sampling, enter the fit (label coefficient $+0.15$ points, $[-1.4,1.7]$,
permutation $p=0.84$), and in a post hoc fit the first-token entropy alone absorbs nearly all of it. This fits the measurements of
Section~\ref{sec:theory}: the loop's chaotic cells have more competing next tokens than its periodic ones, and a model
unsure of its first token also answers worse. Within a prompt no association is detected either, and accuracy does not peak at the edge of
chaos: deep periodic, edge and deep chaotic prompts are answered alike, with the intervals of all three adjusted contrasts within $[-3.1,3.0]$ points. These are nulls at this precision; they do not show that the regime has no effect.

\section{Period adding written in text}
\label{sec:adding}
Between a period-2 region and the chaos lies a fan of stripes of periods $6, 8, 10, \dots$ Their orbits, numerically
validated in float64, can be read as text (Table~\ref{tab:orbits}). Each is ``. This'' followed by $k$ pairs ``is
X'', with period $2k+2$, so each stripe adds one pair. The period-2 region itself is the 2-cycle ``is it''. At the stripe centers the orbits' linear relaxation is dominated by one direction: the leading multiplier, $0.0025$ to $0.086$, exceeds the
next by more than three orders of magnitude (the next falls from $3\times10^{-6}$ at period 6 to $2\times10^{-14}$ at
period 20; Table~\ref{tab:orbits}), so near these orbits a small deviation soon lies along a single direction (the
smallest values are at the limit of float64 accuracy). This is a property of these
orbits, not of the map as a whole. The two leading multipliers of the 2-cycle, $-0.271$ and $-0.058$, differ by a
factor of only about 5 (the second modulus is about $0.037$ near its fold),
the next modulus at the period-3 flip of Section~\ref{sec:cascade} is $0.088$, and one period-6 orbit in
Appendix~\ref{sec:curves} has a complex leading pair.

\section{Mechanism: a saddle-node bottleneck}
\label{sec:mechanism}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-fold.png}
\caption{The fold behind the stripes: the 2-cycle ``is it'' at $\log_{10}T=-0.115$ (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy
direction at layer 12). (a) Continued in $\alpha$, the attracting 2-cycle (filled) and its saddle partner (open) meet
and vanish at $\alpha_c=0.06279827$. The vertical coordinate is the projection $u$ of one point of each orbit on the
fold's critical direction; orange, the normal form's fixed points $u^*=\pm\sqrt{|a|(\alpha_c-\alpha)/|b|}$. (b) The
largest multiplier of $G=F^2$ on the two orbits reaches $+1$ at the fold, following the normal form's
$1\mp2\sqrt{ab(\alpha_c-\alpha)}$ (open circles: a complex leading pair). (c) Beyond the fold the 2-cycle is gone, but
its ghost, the slow bottleneck that a saddle-node bifurcation leaves behind
\cite{strogatz1989predicted,strogatz2018nonlinear}, slows the dynamics: controlled passages from the channel's entrance $v=-U$ to its exit $v=U$ ($v$ the
oriented critical coordinate, $U=0.1/|b|$), measured on the network (markers, one shape per $\eps$) and iterated from the normal form
$v'=v+|a|\eps+|b|v^2$ (lines), take 144, 71, 34 and 14 iterates, as predicted. (d) A free trajectory at
$\eps=10^{-5}$ alternates between laminar phases in the bottleneck ($|u|<U$, shaded; 40 to 70 iterates each) and bursts
away from it, the signature of type-I intermittency. Branch residuals, fold, normal form and passages in float64; branch
multipliers in float32. \repo{tools/plot_fold.py}.}
\label{fig:fold}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{The fold.} At $\log_{10}T=-0.115$ the 2-cycle ``is it'' dies in a fold at $\alpha_c=0.06279827$ (float64
multiplier $1$ to $3\times10^{-13}$, next modulus $0.037$; Figure~\ref{fig:fold}): it meets an unstable 2-cycle, and
both vanish. In the critical coordinate $u$ of the second iterate $G=F^2$, the fold's normal form is
$u'=u+a\eps+bu^2$ with $\eps=\alpha-\alpha_c$; here $a=-4.834$ per unit $\alpha$ and $b=-25.4285$, so $ab=122.93$. Here $q$ is the critical eigenvector of $DG$ with $|q|=1$, $\ell$ the left one with $\ell^\top q=1$,
$a=\ell^\top\partial G/\partial\alpha$ and $b=\tfrac12\ell^\top D^2G[q,q]$ \cite{kuznetsov2019maps}. The normal form is
the quadratic truncation of $G$ on its center manifold: it neglects terms of order $u^3$, $\eps u$ and $\eps^2$, and it applies because the multiplier $+1$ is simple, $a$ and $b$ do not vanish, and the other multipliers lie inside
the unit circle \cite{kuznetsov2023elements}. No symmetry of the map forces a pitchfork and no persistent branch forces a
transcritical exchange, so these conditions make the point a fold. Integrated as a flow, it gives the passage from $v_{\rm in}$ to $U$ in closed form,
$n(v_{\rm in})=[\arctan(|b|U/\kappa)-\arctan(|b|v_{\rm in}/\kappa)]/\kappa$ with $\kappa=\sqrt{ab\,\eps}$; a full
passage approaches $\pi/\kappa$ only when $U\gg\sqrt{|a|\eps/|b|}$. The stable branch gives an independent estimate: extrapolating its multipliers
through $(1-\mu)^2/(4(\alpha_c-\alpha))\to ab$ yields $123.1$ with a quadratic fit ($120.6$ with a linear one). An
independent implementation of the forward map and solver recovers $\alpha_c$ to $6\times10^{-15}$ and $a$ to
$3\times10^{-12}$. Its $b$, from nested automatic differentiation, is $-25.42846$; our finite-difference value differs from it by about $2.2\times10^{-6}$ relatively, which is the finite-step bias of the difference table. The
resulting $ab=122.927$ leaves every passage prediction of Table~\ref{tab:passages} unchanged.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\input{figures/tikz-passage}
\caption{The bottleneck behind the stripes, drawn from the normal form $u'=u+a\eps+bu^2$ with the network's
coefficients ($a=-4.834$, $b=-25.4285$), in the oriented critical coordinate $v$. (a) The step of one $G$-iterate,
$\Delta v=|a|\eps+|b|v^2$, across the channel from $-U$ to $U$. Before the fold ($\eps=-10^{-5}$, dashed) the step
vanishes at two fixed points of $G$: the attracting 2-cycle (filled) and its unstable partner (open). At the fold
($\eps=0$, dotted) they merge. Beyond it ($\eps=10^{-5}$, solid) no fixed point remains, but the step stays small near
$v=0$: the ghost. (b) The 71 iterates of a full passage at $\eps=10^{-5}$, crowded where the step is smallest; the
network takes 71 as well (Table~\ref{tab:passages}). A free trajectory leaves at $U$, makes a burst, and is reinjected
slightly upstream of the middle (bar), so its passage is shorter than a full one.}
\label{fig:bottleneck}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{Controlled passages.} Figure~\ref{fig:bottleneck} shows the bottleneck that these passages cross.
In the oriented coordinate $v=\operatorname{sign}(b)\,u$ of
Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}, we start trajectories at $v=-U$ or $0$ ($U=0.1/|b|$) and count $G$-iterates until
$v>U$. At four distances from the fold, the measured counts match the iterated normal form within
one iterate (Table~\ref{tab:passages}, Figure~\ref{fig:passages}), and a displacement of the start of the same size along one random direction that leaves $u$ unchanged changes the count by
at most one (other directions and sizes were not tested).
\begin{table}[H]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rccc}
\toprule
$\eps=\alpha-\alpha_c$ & full: measured / predicted & midpoint & full + transverse kick \\
\midrule
$3\times10^{-6}$ & 144 / 144 & 73 / 74 & 143 / 144 \\
$1\times10^{-5}$ & 71 / 71 & 36 / 37 & 70 / 71 \\
$3\times10^{-5}$ & 34 / 34 & 17 / 18 & 33 / 34 \\
$1\times10^{-4}$ & 14 / 14 & 7 / 7 & 13 / 14 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Bottleneck passages ($G$-iterates). Predictions iterate $u'=u+a\eps+bu^2$ with $a,b$ computed from the
network's derivatives; no parameter is fitted.}
\label{tab:passages}
\end{table}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.49\linewidth]{fig-passages.png}\hfill
\includegraphics[width=0.49\linewidth]{fig-window-law.png}
\caption{Bottleneck passages and the window law at the fold of the 2-cycle ``is it'' (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct,
philosophy direction at layer 12, $\log_{10}T=-0.115$). Left: measured passage times against the normal-form
predictions. Right: the window law, with stripe centers plotted as $1/\sqrt{\alpha_N-\alpha_c}$ against the lap
count $N$, together with the full- and half-passage predictions.}
\label{fig:passages}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Natural reinjection and the window law.} Left to itself, a trajectory near the fold alternates between
laminar phases, in which it crawls through the bottleneck close to where the 2-cycle was and moves almost
periodically, and bursts, short excursions out of the channel after which it is reinjected into it
(Figure~\ref{fig:fold}d). This alternation is type-I intermittency \cite{pomeau1980intermittent}, whose laminar phases lengthen as
$\eps^{-1/2}$ toward the fold \cite{hirsch1982theory};
laminar is the fluid-dynamics term for the smooth stretches between turbulent bursts. Free trajectories re-enter the
channel slightly upstream of the bottleneck's middle ($v\approx-3\times10^{-4}$ to $-7\times10^{-4}$, in the same
oriented coordinate). From each measured entry point the normal form predicts the natural laminar durations: for complete phases, which stay
within $|u|<3U$ and within 0.05 of the channel in the other directions, the median signed forecast error at the four
offsets up to $\eps=10^{-4}$ lies between $-7.7\%$ and $-4.1\%$ (signed errors $-4.88$, $-4.08$, $-7.41$ and
$-7.69\%$), although single phases differ by up to 71\%; on average a natural passage lasts 0.60 to 0.67 of the normal
form's passage through the same channel from end to end. A window of
period $p$ spends $N=(p-6)/2$ iterates of $G$ in the bottleneck, one for each ``is X'' pair it writes. We read each pair as one $G$-iterate in the bottleneck, as the texts suggest (the laps were not identified
geometrically). For a channel much longer than the bottleneck a full passage takes about $\pi/\sqrt{ab\,\eps}$ iterates
\cite{hirsch1982theory,strogatz1989predicted}, so a loop whose passage is
a fraction $f$ of a full one sits at the distance $d_N=\alpha_N-\alpha_c$ from the fold for which
$d_NN^2=f^2\pi^2/(ab)$. The stripes therefore follow the $N^{-2}$ law of a saddle-node ghost
\cite{strogatz1989predicted,strogatz2018nonlinear} (log-log slope $-2.00$, below), with about a third of the
full-passage prefactor $\pi^2/(ab)=0.0803$; the two fractions agree because the prefactor scales as the square of the passage
fraction ($0.58^2\approx1/3$). The distances $d_N=\alpha_N-\alpha_c$ of stripe centers located on a float32 cut along
$\log_{10}T=-0.115$ give $d_N N^2=0.0263$--$0.0265$ for $N=4$--$7$ (log-log slope $-2.00$), compared with $\pi^2/(4ab)=0.0201$ for a half passage. Two-step return maps of $p(\text{``it''})$ (Figure~\ref{fig:returnmaps}) show the near-tangency.

Numerically estimated stripe edges along $\log_{10}T=-0.115$ (Table~\ref{tab:edges}) sharpen this picture. Each
window is bounded by a fold, where its orbit is born, and by a flip. For periods 6--12 the fold faces away from the
accumulation point. For periods 16--20 the sides are exchanged, and the period-14 window is bounded by flips on both
sides. The fold edges of periods 16--20 follow $\alpha_N-\alpha_c=C/(N+N_0)^2$ with $C=0.0266$ and $N_0=0.15$.
Their slope, a rise of $6.13$ per lap in $1/\sqrt{\alpha_N-\alpha_c}$, is $1.74$ times the full-passage slope
$\sqrt{ab}/\pi=3.53$ of the normal form. That corresponds to an effective passage of $0.58$ of a full one, which the
measured natural reinjection gives independently (Table~\ref{tab:fraction}).
\begin{table}[H]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lp{0.2\linewidth}p{0.27\linewidth}c}
\toprule
measurement & value & full-passage value & $f$ \\
\midrule
free trajectories, four distances & mean laminar phase & passage through the whole channel & 0.60--0.67 \\
window centers, $N=4$--7 & $d_NN^2=0.0263$--$0.0265$ & $\pi^2/(ab)=0.0803$ & 0.57 \\
fold edges, periods 16--20 & slope $6.13$ & $\sqrt{ab}/\pi=3.53$ (ratio 1.74) & 0.58 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Three related comparisons of the passage fraction $f$ (the free passages against the normal form's passage
through the same finite channel, the window values as effective prefactors relative to the long-channel passage; all
three share the fold and its coefficients, so they are not independent): the share of a full passage that a loop or a free
trajectory spends in the bottleneck. The fold edges' slope is the rise of $1/\sqrt{\alpha_N-\alpha_c}$ per unit of
$N$. A half passage would give $f=1/2$ and $d_NN^2=\pi^2/(4ab)=0.0201$.}
\label{tab:fraction}
\end{table}
\begin{table}[H]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rll}
\toprule
period & edge toward larger $\alpha$ & edge toward smaller $\alpha$ \\
\midrule
6 & fold $0.0790337$ & flip $0.0766947$ \\
8 & fold $0.0726585$ & flip $0.0706525$ \\
10 & fold $0.0684572$ & flip $0.0670594$ \\
12 & fold $0.0658982$ & flip $0.0651944$ \\
14 & flip $0.0645850$ & flip $0.0642958$ \\
16 & flip $0.0639284$ & fold $0.0638001$ \\

\end{tabular}
\caption{Edges of the stripe windows along $\log_{10}T=-0.115$ ($\alpha_c=0.0627983$), from the window centers of
Table~\ref{tab:orbits}. Flips: critical multiplier a real $-1$ to $3\times10^{-10}$. Folds: the multiplier nearest $+1$
extrapolated to 1 through $(1-\mu)^2$, which is linear near a fold; last converged multiplier $0.991$--$0.998$.}
\label{tab:edges}
\end{table}
We tested whether the measured reinjection predicts windows beyond the fitted range. A model with one fixed entry
point (the median measured one) and one lap offset fitted to the fold edges of periods 16--20 places the fold edges
of periods 22--30 3 to 9\,\% closer to the fold than the constant-prefactor law. Both predictions were frozen
before a float64 line of 3200 cells (spacing $10^{-7}$) located the windows. The measured edges follow the law within 3\,\% in distance from the fold through period 30 (within 1\,\% at periods 22, 24 and 28) and fall outside the model's range except
at period 30. So the law extrapolates, but one fixed reinjection point does not predict it. The measured entry
points move with $\eps$ ($-3\times10^{-4}$ to $-7\times10^{-4}$).
The exponent of the law is near 2, its prefactor empirical. Over the fold edges of periods 16 to 32 ($N=5$ to 13),
$\ln(\alpha_N-\alpha_c)$ against $\ln N$ has slope $-2.00$, while a free fit of $C/(N+N_0)^{\gamma}$ with the offset
$N_0$ also fitted is not well determined. The estimated localization scales of the edges are about $2\times10^{-5}$ to $2\times10^{-4}$ of their distances
(extrapolated locations for periods 16 to 20, and a uniform-within-cell error model for the grid boundaries of periods
22 to 32), so the misfits of 1 to 3\,\% of any such law are consistent with corrections to its asymptotic form rather than
noise: $\gamma$ runs from 2.13 to 2.45 as the smallest $N$ included rises from 5 to 8, while the fixed exponent 2 with an offset fits within 3\,\% (within 1\,\% from $N=8$), so the data do not pin the exponent to exactly 2. At these distances, however, the bottleneck is not narrow compared with
the counting window: at the fold edges of periods 16 to 32 its scale $\sqrt{|a|\eps/|b|}$ is 1.35 to 3.51 times $U$, and a full passage lasts only 9 to
23 iterates of $G$. The windows therefore lie outside the asymptotic regime in which the normal form fixes the
prefactor \cite{pomeau1980intermittent,hirsch1982theory}, and the factor of about one third is a measured value, not a
prediction. The same inversion of the bottleneck time gives the $N^{-2}$ tail of relaxation times over inputs found in
a small task-trained looped transformer \cite{kim2026phase}; here $N$ counts the laps of a periodic window.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{fig-return-maps.png}
\caption{Two-step return maps of the probability of ``it'' near the fold of the 2-cycle ``is it''
(Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12), at increasing distance from the fold.}
\label{fig:returnmaps}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A prospective test.} The same reduction, with both coefficients computed from the network's derivatives,
predicts relaxation times and spectral gaps without fitting in a small task-trained looped transformer \cite{kim2026phase}. To test the normal form at folds that were not used while the bottleneck explanation was worked out, an independent implementation located two new folds and computed their normal-form coefficients by nested automatic
differentiation. All 24 passage predictions were pushed in a frozen design before any test trajectory was run.
Target A is the 2-cycle's fold at a new temperature: $\log_{10}T=-0.140$, $\alpha_c=0.0670549$, $a=-7.055$,
$b=-24.927$. Target B is the fold of the period-8 orbit of Table~\ref{tab:orbits}, continued in temperature at
$\alpha=0.0717$: $\log_{10}T_c=-0.1033462$, $G=F^8$, $a=-11.900$ per unit $\log_{10}T$ and $b=-152.05$. Of the 24 forecasts (four distances and three entrances per target: the channel's start, its midpoint, and its start after a transverse kick), 23 fell within one iterate of the measured passages
(Table~\ref{tab:prospective}). Seven of the 24 forecasts are five iterates or shorter, where a single iterate is
20\,\% or more of the passage; of the 17 longer forecasts, 16 fell within one iterate. The pre-specified criterion, that every row pass, failed by one row: for target A,
midpoint entrance, $\eps=3\times10^{-6}$, the forecast was 61 and the measurement 59. All full-channel passages and
all passages after a transverse kick were within one iterate. The misses are not symmetric: every one of the 17
nonzero residuals of a midpoint or kicked start, here and at the two folds tested before (Table~\ref{tab:passages} and
Section~\ref{sec:hostile}), is negative, a consistent directional discrepancy across these related deterministic
cases. They share coefficients and truncation errors and are not independent draws, so we attach no sign-test
probability to them. The measured passages are consistently shorter than the iterated quadratic map, by one or two
iterates. The design also froze a secondary comparison, the continuous passage integral
$\int du/(a\eps+bu^2)$ of the same normal form, whose closed form is the arctangent usually quoted for saddle-node
bottlenecks. It came within one iterate of all 24 measurements (errors from $-0.41$ to $+0.92$) and gives 58.56 for
the failing row. At that entrance the two forms already differed by 2.4 iterates before any measurement: iterating the
quadratic map from the entrance $u_{\mathrm{in}}$ to the exit $U$ adds, to leading order for slowly varying steps, about $\tfrac12\ln[h(U)/h(u_{\mathrm{in}})]$ steps to the integral,
$h(u)=a\eps+bu^2$, most at small $\eps$ and at the midpoint entrance. Which of the two forms
the full map follows is not settled by this test, and it does not let us promote the secondary comparison after the
fact.
\begin{table}[H]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rcc}
\toprule
$\eps$ & target A ($G=F^2$): measured / predicted & target B ($G=F^8$): measured / predicted \\
\midrule
$3\times10^{-6}$ & $118/118$,\ $\mathbf{59/61}$,\ $117/118$ & $26/26$,\ $13/14$,\ $25/26$ \\

\paragraph{A second prospective test.} The first test left open which form of the normal form the full map follows,
and its shortest forecasts carried little weight. A second test, frozen before any trajectory was run, made the
continuous passage integral its primary predictor and the discrete count its secondary, and chose the distances so
that every full-channel forecast is at least 16 iterates and every midpoint forecast at least 8. Its two folds are
again new to the tests. Target C is the 2-cycle's fold at $\log_{10}T=-0.200$: $\alpha_c=0.0713690$, $a=-10.9505$, $b=-25.3300$.
Target D is the fold of a period-6 orbit, a different family, at $\log_{10}T=-0.087439$: $\alpha_c=0.0756329$,
$G=F^6$, $a=-72.695$, $b=72.237$. Distances $\eps=q^2/(ab)$ with $q=0.08$, $0.05$, $0.03$ and $0.018$ give both
targets the same forecasts (Table~\ref{tab:prospective2}). At the 2-cycle's fold the integral held in all 12 rows,
and every measured count was the forecast rounded up. At the period-6 fold the midpoint passages held as well, but
passages from the channel's entrance came in short, by 0.40 to 5.74 iterates and more so closer to the fold, so the
primary criterion failed, 18 of 24 rows (the discrete count passed 16 of 24). From the fold point to the exit the
period-6 passages follow the normal form; it is the first half of the channel that is crossed faster. Terms beyond the
quadratic normal form on that side, or the curvature of the center manifold at the entrance, are candidate causes
that this test does not resolve. Summed over both frozen tests, in which it was first the secondary and then the primary predictor, the continuous
integral came within one iterate of 42 of 48 passages; all six misses are entrance passages at the period-6 fold, short by 3.1 to 5.2\,\%. The sign hypothesis
frozen with the design, that midpoint and kicked passages do not exceed the discrete count, held in all 16 rows
(\repo{codex/confirm-fold-2}).

\section{A period-doubling cascade to chaos}
\label{sec:cascade}
The crescent of Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} is a closed region in which the period-3 loop ``\textbackslash
n\textbackslash nThe existence'' doubles inward. Along the cut $\log_{10}T=-0.14798$, the map's cells show period 3
over 25 columns, period 6 over about 14, period 12 over 4 and period 24 in one column, followed by a chaotic core. The
doubled loops repeat the same leading tokens twice and four times, with alternating probabilities. Locating the flips
along this cut in float64 (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}), in the steering coordinate $\alpha$, gives
Table~\ref{tab:cascade}. The first two ratios, $\delta_2=2.844$ and $\delta_3=2.859$ (indexed as in
Table~\ref{tab:cascade}), are far from Feigenbaum's $4.6692$, even though from the second flip on the orbits are
dominated in their linear relaxation by one direction (next modulus $0.0023$, $2\times10^{-6}$, $2\times10^{-12}$). The next ratio,
$\delta_4=3.781$, is markedly larger. The first four flip parameters agree between completed float64 computations on a
CPU and an H100 to $4\times10^{-15}$.

Continuing a just-born doubled orbit past period 48 failed: Newton's method slides onto the half-period orbit, which
is also periodic with the doubled period. We therefore also located the superstable parameters $s_p$, where the
critical multiplier vanishes in the middle of each band (Table~\ref{tab:superstable}). There the orbit attracts, so it
can be reached by forward iteration and cannot slide onto its half-period parent. Through period 768 the superstable
ratios are $3.19$, $4.02$, $4.478$, $4.625$ and $4.660$. The last three come from parameters resolved to $10^{-14}$ and
differ from Feigenbaum's value by $0.19$, $0.044$ and $0.0097$; from $4.02$ on, the distance shrinks by factors of
$3.4$, $4.3$ and $4.6$.
The flip ratios $2.84$, $2.86$, $3.78$, $4.36$, $4.60$ and $4.65$ rise in step (Figure~\ref{fig:feigenbaum}); the
last three come from runs started inside the period-96 band, which located the flips of periods 96, 192 and 384
directly. The accumulation point lies
near $\alpha_\infty\approx0.0661250$. The early superstable values are less precise. The other computed Ritz values of
the period-12 and period-24 orbits blur the root, so $s_{12}$ and $s_{24}$ are known only within brackets of
$1.24\times10^{-5}$ and $6.9\times10^{-7}$, and the ratios $3.19$ and $4.019$ have worst-case ranges $[2.895,3.338]$
and $[4.019,4.101]$. $s_6$ is less precise still, and $s_3$ was not bracketed.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.58\linewidth]{fig-feigenbaum-ratios.png}
\caption{Finite-order interval ratios of the doubling sequences: the crescent of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct (philosophy

(philosophy direction, layer 14; Figure~\ref{fig:qwen15route}), from flips. A superstable ratio spans half-bands, so
it is plotted half a level after the flip ratio of its first band. Bars: worst-case ranges from the brackets of

last superstable ratio at $-0.148$ is within 0.21\,\% of it, and the last 1.5B ratio ($4.665$) within 0.1\,\%. At $\log_{10}T=-0.156$ the first ratio, $14.9$, lies off the scale
(arrow), and the later ratios approach $\delta$ from below, the last ($4.6688$) within $0.008\,\%$ of it, and within $0.015\,\%$ over the location uncertainty of the period-384 flip. Its first two
superstable ratios carry no bars: the brackets of $s_{12}$ and $s_{24}$ allow $[3.40,29.3]$ and $[1.01,4.65]$. Each
sequence ends at the deepest level computed: the flips at $-0.148$ at period 384, the superstable points at period 768, the 1.5B route at period 256, and the cut at
$-0.156$, which we followed furthest, at the $768\to1536$ flip.
}
\label{fig:feigenbaum}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering

states projected on the unit steering direction (orange: chaotic cells, with no period up to 48). (a) The whole cut: period 3 doubles into chaos,
which ends at $\alpha=0.065278$, where the attractor jumps to a strongly contracting period-2 orbit. (b, c) Zooms in
both coordinates on the branch boxed in the panel above, showing the doublings $6\to12\to24\to48$ and
$24\to48\to96\to192$. Dashed lines: flips located by Newton's method (Table~\ref{tab:cascade156}); dotted line: the
accumulation point estimated from the last two flips with $\delta$. Under each panel, the largest Lyapunov exponent
touches zero at every flip and turns positive beyond the accumulation point. Near a flip the orbit converges slowly,
so with 3000 transient steps each fork opens slightly before its Newton flip, by $8.4\times10^{-6}$ in $\alpha$ at the
first and by at most $1.1\times10^{-6}$ at the later ones.}
\label{fig:feigtree}
\end{figure}

the other computed Ritz moduli below $10^{-20}$ from period 96 to 384; at period 768 the residual is
