Why are the early ratios so small? We first considered a crossover. Softmax at temperature $T$ is a smoothed argmax,
so the reduced one-dimensional map might have a nearly corner-like extremum, rounded only on a scale set by $T$. Early
doublings would then see the corner, where ratios tend toward 2, and later ones the smooth quadratic core. This
predicts smaller early ratios at lower temperature. We fixed that prediction before locating the flips along two
lower cuts, and it fails. At $\log_{10}T=-0.156$ the first ratio is $14.93$, five times its value at $-0.148$
(Table~\ref{tab:cascade156}). At $-0.152$ the continued period-12 solution collapsed onto its period-6 parent near
$\alpha\approx0.0657$, and the forward search found no next flip. This is compatible with a doubling bubble, but we
did not locate its reverse boundary or exclude a narrower cascade on that cut. The early ratios thus depend strongly
on where a cut crosses the crescent, and we withdraw the crossover explanation. The later ratios at $-0.156$ behave as
universality requires. The next ratios are $3.124$, $4.4988$, $4.6208$, $4.6618$, $4.6673$ and $4.6688$. Their
deviations from $\delta=4.6692$ are $-1.55$, $-0.17$, $-0.048$, $-0.0074$, $-0.0019$ and $-0.00036$; they keep one
sign, from the third on each step closes the gap by a factor of 3.5 to 6.5 (the location uncertainty of the period-384
flip, given below, leaves the last factor undetermined), and Aitken's $\Delta^2$ extrapolation of the last three
ratios gives $4.6694$. Universality of this kind holds within a class: for maps with a quadratic critical point, including suitable strongly
dissipative families of any dimension \cite{feigenbaum1979universal,collet1981period}; the constant belongs to the class,
not to period doubling as such, and differs for a critical point of higher order. Such cascades also occur in the training map of gradient descent in solvable models of deep
networks \cite{hofmann2026map}. The last ratio uses the flip of the period-768 orbit (the
$768\to1536$ flip), located with $|\mu+1|=4.1\times10^{-7}$. The penultimate flip, of the period-384 orbit,
approaches the precision floor of its multiplier, a product of 384 step Jacobians. On an H100 the computed $\mu+1$
scatters by $1.8\times10^{-5}$ about a straight line near the root; under that local linear fit, the largest residual
divided by the slope gives a location uncertainty of about $\pm3.3\times10^{-13}$, an estimate rather than a
certified bound. The workstation GPU located it at
$\alpha=0.06510347354866666$ with $|\mu+1|=4\times10^{-9}$, $9\times10^{-14}$ from the H100 estimate. Over that range the
ratio $\delta_7$ lies between $4.6672$ and $4.6674$, $\delta_8$, the last ratio, between $4.6685$ and $4.6692$, and the
extrapolated value between $4.6688$ and $4.6702$, an interval that contains $\delta$. The
superstable points along the same cut, through period 384, give the ratios $10.21$, $3.713$, $4.572$, $4.643$ and
$4.6646$. Before computing them we fixed the prediction that the last two superstable ratios at a new temperature
would lie within 5\,\% of $\delta$, in $[4.436,4.902]$. At the original readout, through period 192, the point
estimates $4.572$ and $4.643$ met it, but the bracket envelope of $4.572$ did not; extending the computation to period
384 later gave the pair $4.643$ and $4.6646$, both inside the interval. The last, $4.6646$, rests on parameters resolved to $5\times10^{-15}$ and
differs from $\delta$ by $0.1\,\%$. Over the corners of the brackets the one before lies in $[4.639,4.647]$,
limited by $s_{48}$, whose bracket is $2.4\times10^{-9}$ wide. The earlier ratio $4.572$ depends on $s_{24}$, known only
within a bracket of $5.9\times10^{-6}$ (worst-case range $[1.01,4.65]$).
Finite-order ratios also depend on the parameter coordinate, so only the trend of later ratios can
test universality \cite{feigenbaum1978quantitative,papasavvas2015gain,liu2026learningchaos}. Three claims should be kept apart here.
The map has a period-doubling cascade; its late finite-order ratios approach $\delta$ numerically, with the digits
limited by the brackets above, since the last flips lie near the float64 floor of the multiplier computation; and
Feigenbaum's theory explains such convergence for an effectively one-dimensional, unimodal return map, a hypothesis
these ratios alone do not establish for this map.

Not every doubling leads to chaos. In the high-temperature region (Figure~\ref{fig:p4}, Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}), along
$\log_{10}T=0.0319$, the period-2 loop ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe'' flips at $\alpha=0.0753534$ and its
doubled loop at $0.0736921$. These orbits are far from one-dimensional (next moduli $0.38$ and $0.28$). Toward
smaller $\alpha$ the period-8 orbit does not double again: near $\alpha\approx0.0705$ it merges back into the period-4
orbit. Along this cut it is therefore a period-doubling bubble, bounded by two flips of the period-4 orbit, not the
start of a cascade to chaos.

\toprule
 & offset & $\beta$ & amplitude ratio & doubled multiplier & $1-4\beta$ \\
\midrule

($c=6.49\times10^{3}$) & $1\times10^{-4}$ & $0.0364$ & $1.020$ & $0.8572$ & $0.8545$ \\
 & $4\times10^{-4}$ & $0.1400$ & $1.078$ & $0.513$ & $0.440$ \\
\midrule

\caption{The flip normal form where the map is far from one-dimensional: the two flips of the high-temperature bubble
(next moduli $0.38$ and $0.28$). At offsets below each flip in $\alpha$, $\beta=-\mu_p-1$ from the period-$p$ orbit's
multiplier; amplitude ratio: the doubled orbit's half-amplitude along the critical direction divided by the
prediction $\sqrt{\beta/c}$; doubled multiplier: computed against the prediction $1-4\beta$. Both coefficients are
positive (supercritical), as the stable doubled orbits require. Three of five offsets per flip are shown.}
\label{tab:flipnf}
\end{table}
The local theory still applies here (Section~\ref{sec:theory}). At each flip we computed the cubic coefficient of the
flip normal form, $\eta\mapsto-(1+\beta)\eta+c\,\eta^3$ on the critical direction, from the dense monodromy and
float64 directional derivatives (notation of Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}), $c=\ell^\top C(r,r,r)/6+\ell^\top B(r,h_2)/2$ with $(I-M)h_2=B(r,r)$
\cite{kuznetsov2023elements}. To leading order it predicts the doubled orbit without fitting: half-amplitude
$\sqrt{\beta/c}\,(1+O(\beta))$ along $r$ and multiplier $1-4\beta+O(\beta^2)$. At the smallest offset the amplitude ratios are $1.005$ and $1.003$, and the multipliers agree
within $1.5\times10^{-4}$ and $5\times10^{-5}$; the larger discrepancies at larger offsets are consistent with
higher-order corrections (Table~\ref{tab:flipnf}). These finite comparisons support the leading-order prediction; they
do not establish the order of the remainder. Figure~\ref{fig:feigtree} shows the cascade itself as a bifurcation diagram: in three nested zooms each fork sits at a flip located independently by Newton's method, and the largest exponent touches zero at every flip, as it must where a multiplier reaches modulus one, and turns
positive beyond the accumulation point. The route to chaos of Qwen2.5-1.5B (Figure~\ref{fig:qwen15route}) behaves the same way: its last ratio, $4.665$, is within $0.1\,\%$ of $\delta$ (Figure~\ref{fig:feigenbaum}).


\section{Steering toward hostility}
\label{sec:hostile}
A second steering direction tests whether the organization of the philosophy-steered map is specific to one
concept. We steer along a valence direction, the contrast of hostile statements against matched kind ones, built
in the manner of contrastive activation addition \cite{rimsky2024steering}; its cosine with the gratitude
direction is $-0.04$. Sampled self-dialogue under the same steering is a different system, with a growing context
and sampling noise; we report that study separately and follow the direction here in the deterministic map.

\paragraph{The hostility direction in the deterministic map.} Steering the soft-token map of
Section~\ref{sec:map} along the same hostility direction gives a plane with chaos (a positive largest exponent in
10\,\% of a coarse $128\times192$ float32 scan) and with periodic text loops that are hostile: ``Yes, that's crap''
(period 6), ``a lie is'' (3), ``STUP\ldots'' (3), a loop on the Chinese word for insult and its 2-cycle, and others.
They appear at $\alpha$ above about $0.035$ (the insult loop) and above $0.065$ (the rest). Around the period-6 loop
the windows form a dome (Figure~\ref{fig:hostile-cascade}), and toward higher temperature its texts gain one ``!Yes''
after another (periods $6,8,\ldots,20$). In float64 the windows of periods 12 to 20 are born in folds on their
low-temperature side and end in flips or $+1$ crossings on the other, like the stripes of Sections~\ref{sec:adding}
and~\ref{sec:mechanism}. The period-6 window ends in a fold at $\log_{10}T=-0.0991$, and the continuations of the
period-8 and period-10 windows failed to converge.

The windows accumulate on the fold of the 2-cycle ``!Yes'' at $\log_{10}T_c=-0.0462465357$ ($\alpha=0.083$; critical
multiplier $0.9999999954$, next modulus $0.050$). From period 16 on, $1/\sqrt{\log_{10}T_c-\log_{10}T_{\rm fold}}$
rises by $2.235$ per lap. The fold's normal form ($a=-1.1683$, $b=44.525$ with $\varepsilon$ in $\log_{10}T$) predicts
$\sqrt{|ab|}/\pi=2.296$ per lap for full passages through the bottleneck, within 3\,\% and without a fitted
parameter. An independent float64 forward and automatic-differentiation implementation reproduces this fold
(location to $6\times10^{-10}$ relative, a simple real $+1$ multiplier with next modulus $0.050$) and its coefficients
($ab$ to 8 parts per million). In that implementation, controlled passages from fixed entrances at $\log_{10}T$
offsets $-10^{-5}$, $-3\times10^{-5}$ and $-10^{-4}$ took 119/59, 61/31 and 27/13 iterates of $F^2$ (full
channel/from its midpoint), against prospectively frozen quadratic forecasts of 118/61, 61/32 and 27/14. Five of the
six are within one iterate; the smallest-offset midpoint missed by two, failing the frozen all-six criterion.
Finite-endpoint (arctan) forecasts, also frozen, were within $0.923$ iterates. These are controlled local transits. At
the window distances the finite-channel correction to the asymptotic passage time is large, so the agreement of the
window slope with the full-passage coefficient does not by itself show where the natural dynamics reinjects. The same
type-I intermittency mechanism as in the philosophy-steered map is thus consistent with the hostile windows'
organization, which the local fold normal form supports; the controlled passages do not identify the natural
reinjection or the global organization.

A second region, near $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.06,0.20)$, shows arms of period-7 and period-8 windows spiralling around
the insult loop. In float64 the insult fixed point doubles into a 2-cycle at $\alpha\approx0.041$ and $0.079$, and
that 2-cycle has a Neimark--Sacker bifurcation in the spiral region: at $\alpha=0.062$ its complex pair crosses the
unit circle near $\log_{10}T=0.1656$, turning $0.12$ of a revolution per step of the 2-cycle. This does not explain
the arms. The two invariant circles born at a 2-cycle's Neimark--Sacker point are exchanged by the map, so orbits
locked on them have even periods, near 16 for this rotation, whereas the arms include period 7; and of four float32
arm cells, only one kept its period in float64. In dissipative flows, windows wind in nested spirals around
periodicity hubs \cite{vitolo2011hubs}, and in the R\"ossler system the hub's shape was traced to a conjugacy to
unimodal maps in part of its parameter plane \cite{jones2012rossler}; we have not tested whether the arms here wind around such a hub. Float64 zooms
show more structure. In one arm a period-7 domain
carries period-14 crescents with period-28 cores along its lower edge, between period-1 and period-2 regions and a
chaotic band. In the cascade the windows are nested crescent arcs inside a dome bounded by the ``!Yes'' 2-cycle
region, and near $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.087,-0.069)$ the period-12 and period-14 windows bend into hooked, nested
arches. These are the map-level features that accompany shrimp (a periodic body with doubling crescents; hooked
windows).

In float64 the period-7 body is bounded by folds. At $\log_{10}T=0.1925$ the orbit exists between $\alpha=0.065966$
and $0.071188$, and at $\alpha=0.066$ it ends in a fold at $\log_{10}T=0.19263$. Below that, the multiplier passes
through $-1$ twice (bracketed in $[0.19176,0.19196]$ and $[0.18931,0.19036]$), so the period-14 crescent is a
doubling bubble. Further down, the
branch folds twice ($0.18797$ and $0.18922$, the second located by continuing its fold curve; three coexisting period-7 orbits in between) before a third flip.
All crossings are simple (next modulus below $1.5\times10^{-3}$). An S-shaped pair of folds is what a cusp of the
fold curves leaves on a nearby cut. When these boundaries are continued in the plane in float64
(Figure~\ref{fig:hubshrimp}), each of the two fold curves reverses its parameter tangent once. There, as the fold condition requires, the oriented
quadratic coefficient $b$ of the fold's normal form passes through zero; what makes such a point a cusp is that the
cubic coefficient $c$ and the unfolding determinant $\det K$ are nonzero there (the cusp test, Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}) (upper fold: $-23.9$ to $+244.6$ between
adjacent points, with $a\approx2.0$; lower fold: $+31.2$ to $-58.7$, with $a\approx0.55$) while $a$ stays away from
zero. Located along the curves, the two cusps lie at $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.067213,0.198983)$ and
$(0.066611,0.189382)$. There $b\operatorname{sign}a$ is below $1.3\times10^{-5}$ of its bracket values,
$c=-1.41\times10^{6}$ and $-5.61\times10^{5}$, and $\det K=58.2$ and $55.2$, all evaluated at the located points.
The flip curve of the period-14 bubble lies inside the body and closes around the map's period-14 cells. The cubic
coefficient is nonzero at the points that bracket both sign changes: $c\approx-1.4\times10^{6}$ to $-2.0\times10^{6}$ (step agreement $5\,\%$; the
smallest steps converge like the square of the step, and extrapolation leaves the sign) and $-5.8\times10^{5}$ to
$-5.3\times10^{5}$. This supports nondegenerate cusps inside both brackets, and $c<0$ agrees with the three
coexisting period-7 orbits, two of them stable, found between the S-shaped folds. At a fold point near the lower
cusp (where $b=31.4$) the estimated unfolding is nonsingular too ($\det K=53.5$ in the same coordinates, singular
values $262$ and $0.20$). Its local predictions at six frozen offsets matched the orbits that Newton solves seeded at
the predicted roots and the base state recovered: in number at all six and in stability at five. The miss, at the largest
offset beyond the edge of the region with three orbits, marks the range of the cubic model. The hostile period-7 body
thus has the same skeleton as the period-6 window of Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}.

Its interior is shrimp-like too. At $\log_{10}T=0.1830$, float64 bifurcation diagrams through the body
(Figure~\ref{fig:hubcascade}; 1400 cells per line) show the period-7 orbit doubling to periods 14 and 28 and entering
chaos (largest exponent up to $+0.1$) for $\alpha\approx0.0659$--$0.0673$. Windows of periods 28, 35 and 42 sit
inside the chaotic band, and the orbit then undoubles through 28 and 14 back to 7; the exponent touches zero at every
doubling. Newton's method converges at map cells to primitive orbits of periods 14, 28 and 21 (residuals
$3\times10^{-15}$, $2\times10^{-14}$ and $5\times10^{-13}$; spectral radii $0.08$, $4\times10^{-4}$ and $0.006$).
Periods 21, 35 and 42 are three, five and six times the body's period, as expected for the windows that renormalization places inside the band chaos of a period-7 cascade. Continued in temperature, the period-14 orbit flips into period 28 near
$\log_{10}T=0.1841$ and back near $0.1857$, the two ends of a doubling bubble that holds period 28 and chaos. Lower
down, its branch is S-shaped (folds near $0.1824$ and $0.18244$, with three coexisting period-14 orbits between them)
before another flip near $0.1819$. All these crossings are simple (next modulus below $4\times10^{-5}$). A $160^2$
float64 zoom of the lower body (Figure~\ref{fig:hubzoom}) shows this anatomy in the plane. The period-14 domain
encloses a period-28 ring around a chaotic core. Period-21 windows sit in the chaotic region as thin crescents with
legs, and curves of very negative exponent, where the leading multiplier is small (candidate superstable curves),
run through the windows, as superstable lines run through the windows of low-dimensional maps. In this zoom
18\,\% of cells are chaotic.

a float32 zoom (black: chaos), with the period-6 ``Yes, that's crap'' dome and its period-adding bands; dashed, the
cut at $\alpha=0.083$; dotted, the float64 fold of the ``!Yes'' 2-cycle. Right: float64 fold edges of the windows (periods

at the last window.}

bifurcation diagrams along temperature at $\alpha=0.0655$ (left) and along the steering strength at

the fixed start, projected on the steering direction (red: cells with a positive largest exponent). Bottom: largest
Lyapunov exponent, with the cells of periods 7, 14, 21, 28, 35 and 42 marked (cells of other periods are not marked).
On the right, period 7 doubles to 14 and 28, enters chaos with
windows of periods 28, 35 and 42, and undoubles back to 7. Labels come from 400 measured steps after 1000 transient

\paragraph{No one-dimensional shrimp center.} In a shrimp of a one-dimensional map, two superstable curves of the
window's orbit cross at a doubly superstable center, the head \cite{gallas1994dissecting,facanha2013endoskeletons}. We continued
one period-7 orbit over a $31\times31$ grid of the body's upper part and followed the sign of its leading real
multiplier (Figure~\ref{fig:superstable}). The curves where that sign changes between cells are candidate superstable
curves: the modulus is small along them, but a sign change between cells can also come from two real multipliers of
opposite sign exchanging dominance. They form two arcs that approach each other near
$(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.0700,0.206)$ and turn away without crossing at this resolution ($10^{-4}$ in $\alpha$,
$4.3\times10^{-4}$ in $\log_{10}T$). A finer grid of that approach (1155 cells, about 2 times finer in $\alpha$ and 6
times finer in $\log_{10}T$) shows why. There the leading multiplier is tiny (median modulus $9\times10^{-4}$) and as small as the next
one, and in 221 cells the two form a complex pair, in two wedges that flank the gap. On this sampled approach the two
computed leading multipliers exchange dominance and form a complex pair, so the sign of a leading real multiplier no
longer defines a scalar superstable curve there. Where a one-dimensional map would place its doubly superstable center,
the orbit's local linearization has two comparable directions; whether the dynamics there reduces to an effectively
two-dimensional map is not tested. Next to the body, the 7-step return maps of chaotic cells have a coarse
one-extremum shape in every phase: one interior extremum when the points are binned into 16 intervals, while finer
partitions keep extra extrema, especially in phases 4 and 5, which we do not attribute to noise. In two phases the
points split into two thin strands, an observation of the projection that does not establish a second invariant
dimension (Figure~\ref{fig:returnmapsbody}). Cobwebs on these maps show the doubling cascade and the chaos as on a
one-humped interval map (Figure~\ref{fig:cobwebs}). On the tracked branch and in the sampled region, the body is
therefore not the shrimp of a one-dimensional map. In the two-dimensional theory of Fa\c{c}anha et al.\ the head is
not generic (Section~\ref{sec:organization}); arcs that turn away give a Type-B-like geometry, but they follow the
sign of the leading real multiplier, not the trace that the theory uses, so this is a comparison, not a test.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-superstable-lines.png}
\caption{Small-multiplier structure of the hostile period-7 body (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, hostility direction at layer
12, float64). (a) Largest Lyapunov exponent of the period-7 cells, with fold (black) and flip (red, dashed) curves and
the two located cusps (gold); dark lines mark a small leading multiplier. (b) One period-7 orbit continued over the
red box of (a): sign of its leading multiplier (red positive, blue negative), dark where it is small, and the curves
where that sign changes between cells (black). These are candidate superstable curves: the modulus is small along
them, but a sign change between cells can also come from two real multipliers exchanging dominance. The two arcs
approach near $(0.0700,0.206)$ without crossing, a Type-B-like geometry. (c) Finer zoom of the cyan box of (b) (about 2 times finer in $\alpha$, 6 times in $\log_{10}T$):
logarithm of the leading multiplier's modulus. Crosses mark cells where the two leading multipliers form a complex
pair; white segments mark sign changes between cells with a real leading multiplier. Where a one-dimensional map would
have its center, the orbit's linearization has two comparable leading directions.}
\label{fig:superstable}
\end{figure}

attractor periods of a float64 zoom (white at $\alpha<0.062$: outside the zoom), the float64 fold curves of the

\label{fig:hubshrimp}

\paragraph{Chaos behind a repeating text.} The text records less than the loop feeds back. What returns at each step
is the expected embedding under the whole next-token distribution; the printed token is only that distribution's most
probable entry. A repeating text therefore says that the same token wins at each position of the repetition, not that
the distribution repeats. Along the temperature line of Figure~\ref{fig:hubcascade} (left), a longer
test (2000 measured steps after 1000 transient steps, twins at offsets $10^{-6}$ and $10^{-9}$, whose exponents agree
to within 0.0031 in every chaotic cell) finds 182 chaotic cells among 1400 between $\log_{10}T=0.176$ and $0.191$. In
65 of them the leading token repeats exactly for all 2000 steps while the state moves chaotically; in 59 of these the
period of the text is a multiple of 7, most often 7 or 28. Figure~\ref{fig:hiddenbands} shows the strongest case
($\log_{10}T=0.18995$, largest exponent 0.091 per step at both offsets). Its 2000 sampled states fall into seven
separated groups, one for each position in the seven-token text, visited in a fixed order and at least 0.098 of the state norm apart (a property of
the samples, not a proof of separated invariant sets), and each group lies inside one cell of the partition by leading
token, so the text \texttt{I!Yes\ heels$\hookleftarrow\hookleftarrow$pee$\hookleftarrow\hookleftarrow$}
repeats at every one of the 2000 steps we followed. Within each group the most probable token never changes, but
the probabilities do: at the last position of the text, the probability of the double line break, which wins there,
takes values between 0.235 and 0.254 and does not repeat over the 2000 steps. This is band chaos, the regime of a
chaotic attractor that is a finite union of pieces visited cyclically \cite{grossmann1977invariant,lorenz1980noisy}.
Over the measured horizon, the periodic sequence of leading tokens does not reveal the nonperiodic state dynamics: the
finite record of leading tokens hides the variability of the states and their positive finite-time growth. In the
terms of symbolic dynamics, this is consistent with a partition of the state space by leading token that is not
generating on this banded attractor, but a finite record cannot establish that property, which concerns infinite
itineraries. A periodic text can hide chaotic dynamics. Inside a group the sampled states lie close to a curve (a
strongly anisotropic cloud). Read once per repetition through the probability of the last token, the sampled return
relation is well approximated, on the sampled range, by a smooth map with a single quadratic maximum, the shape of the logistic map: a
polynomial of degree 8 fits it to $4\times10^{-4}$ of its range, and the fitted map's Lyapunov exponent, 0.647 per
repetition, is within 2\,\% of the full map's 0.636 per repetition (seven steps of about 0.091), measured with twin trajectories in all 896
dimensions. On their sampled ranges, the scalar approximation fits most of the cascade's cells. Rerun with the leading-token sequences saved, 62 of the 65 cells are again
chaotic behind a repeating text, and in every one of them several phases of the text (a median of ten) give
single-valued return maps; in 59 cells the best-fitting phase has a single interior stationary point (a maximum in
36, a minimum in 23). If the dynamics were exactly one-dimensional and each phase's probability a smooth invertible
coordinate on it, all single-valued phases would give the same asymptotic exponent, since exponents do not change
under such a change of coordinates. We therefore
fit each phase's map by a polynomial whose degree is chosen by cross-validation over random folds of the return-map
pairs, and take the median over the phases. It
matches the full map's exponent within 10\,\% in 51 of the 62 cells (median discrepancy 3.7\,\%; within 10\,\% in 23
of the 24 cells with seven-token text, which give 285 repetitions in 2000 steps; Figure~\ref{fig:reduction}). This
analysis was adopted after we inspected an outlier of our first, pre-specified analysis (a fixed degree of 8 at the
single best-fitting phase), which gave 49 of 62 and underfitted one cell whose maps are steep and have two turning
points; we report both. An exploratory temporal split (fit on the first 60\,\% of each record, test on the last
30\,\%) gives small value-prediction errors at the best scalar phase, chosen on the test segment itself, but in 30 of
61 cells two- or three-delay predictors improve the held-out error by 20\,\% or more, which the pre-stated rule counts
against scalar sufficiency. These results support an empirical scalar description, not a proved one-dimensional
reduction: a low-residual fit does not show that the observable is invertible, and even exact agreement of exponents
would not establish a one-dimensional invariant curve (two uncoupled logistic maps observed through one coordinate
agree in the same way). An independent implementation reproduced the strongest cell (the same leading token at all
2000 steps; exponent 0.089 against our 0.091; scalar discrepancy $+2.3\,\%$ under the first rule and $+1.5\,\%$
under the amended one) and both cross-cell counts (49 and 51 of 62). At two further cells it chose, it found good
agreement after the amendment in one (text period 10, $+0.2\,\%$, although its fitted maps have both a maximum and a
minimum) and a persistent discrepancy of about 18\,\% in the other (period 28), despite a small value error. These
are the chaotic bands that follow a period-doubling cascade, and a smooth unimodal map is the setting of Feigenbaum's
universality \cite{feigenbaum1978quantitative}; a fitted maximum on the sampled range does not by itself establish
that theorem's hypotheses, and we have shown the scalar approximation for this cascade only. A reader of the text sees a loop. Only the probabilities, or the state, reveal the
chaos.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-hidden-bands.png}
\caption{Chaos behind a repeating text, a periodic text on a chaotic orbit: the soft-token map of
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct steered along hostility at layer 12 ($\alpha=0.0655$, $\log_{10}T=0.18995$; float64, 2000 steps
after 1000 transient steps). At every step the most probable token follows the same seven-token cycle, \texttt{I},
\texttt{!}, \texttt{Yes}, \texttt{\ heels}, $\hookleftarrow\hookleftarrow$, \texttt{pee},
$\hookleftarrow\hookleftarrow$, while the largest Lyapunov exponent is 0.091 per step: the token that wins repeats,
but the probabilities behind it, which the loop feeds back, do not. (a) All states on their first two principal
components, colored and marked by their position in the text: seven separated sampled clouds. (b) The widest cloud (the last
position, whose most probable token is a double line break) on its own principal components: the sampled states lie
close to a curve. (c) The probability of that token, which stays between 0.235 and 0.254 and does not repeat over the
2000 steps, at one
repetition of the text against the next: the points fall on a single hump (black: a fitted polynomial of degree 8),
and the fitted map's Lyapunov exponent, 0.647 per repetition, is within 2\,\% of the full map's 0.636 per repetition
(seven steps of about 0.091).}
\label{fig:hiddenbands}
\end{figure}

\section{Cusps and shrimp-like windows}
\label{sec:organization}
The fold of Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} is one boundary of one window. Continuing such boundaries through the
parameter plane, and following the orbits inside them, shows how the windows of the maps are organized, and which
parts of that organization are low-dimensional. Appendices~\ref{sec:curves} and~\ref{sec:hostility} report the
continued curves and cusps of the period-4, -5 and -6 windows in full, and Section~\ref{sec:hostile} those of the
period-7 body; this section collects what they establish about the shape of the windows, after
the cascade of Section~\ref{sec:cascade} and the hostile windows of Section~\ref{sec:hostile}.

\paragraph{Fold curves with cusps.} Fold and flip curves continued in float64 with the minimally augmented systems of
Appendix~\ref{sec:methods} keep their critical multipliers at $+1$ and $-1$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}). The fold
curve of the 2-cycle ``is it'' traces the boundary of the period-2 region on which the stripes accumulate, as the
mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} requires. The fold curves of a period-6
window, of a hostile period-7 window, of a period-4 window and of a period-5 window steered at two depths bend back
on themselves. On the eleven fold curves we continued, 13 of the 17 reversals of the parameter tangent are located
cusps: the oriented quadratic coefficient of the fold's normal form changes sign there,
while the cubic coefficient stays nonzero and the two-parameter unfolding is regular. The other four, all found on 18
October, are flagged by the continuation but not located: one on the lower-left leg's own fold curve, one on an
extension of the period-5 window's second fold curve, and two on a partial trace of a fold curve of the same window
at $\log_{10}T=-0.105$. This is not a global count of cusps. Near a cusp with negative cubic coefficient, three orbits of the same period coexist between the two fold
branches, two of them stable. At a fold point near one cusp in each of two windows, the local cubic model, frozen
before solving, matched the number of orbits that Newton solves seeded at its predicted roots recovered at all twelve
parameter offsets, and their stability at eleven; the search was not exhaustive.

\paragraph{What the shrimp-like shapes are.} The maps show bodies with legs, hooks and crescents that resemble the
shrimp-shaped domains that Gallas found in the parameter plane of the H\'enon map: a main body of period $k$, followed
by domains of periods $k\cdot2^n$, with thin legs \cite{gallas1993structure}. They are made of the bifurcation curves
of periodic windows; where two windows overlap, their strips can cross in an X (Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}). A periodic
window is the region where one periodic orbit attracts. It is bounded by fold curves, where the orbit is born or dies
together with a saddle (multiplier $+1$), and flip curves, where it doubles (multiplier $-1$); beyond the flips lie
the period-doubled shells. In a one-dimensional map the multiplier is $+1$ on a fold edge and $-1$ on a flip edge, so it vanishes
in between: every path across a window from a fold edge to a flip edge crosses a superstable curve. In the soft-token
maps the dark lines of strongly negative exponent, where the leading multiplier is small, are the candidates for such
curves.
Gallas's dissection of one-dimensional shrimps names the parts of a cell (Figure~\ref{fig:anatomy}a)
\cite{gallas1994dissecting}. The superstable curves of the map's two critical points cross at the head, where the
orbit passes both critical points at once. At the end of the cell two legs, each a thin strip between a fold and a
flip curve, cross in a kite. The kite begins at a cusp, where the two legs' fold curves meet; inside it two attracting
orbits of the same period coexist, and their superstable curves cross at the tail.

Mira and coworkers sort the organizations around a cusp of a fold curve in two-dimensional maps into three kinds
\cite{mira1987chaotic,carcasses1991crossroad,mira1991crossroad}. Passing through the cusp into its wedge splits an
attracting node into a saddle and two nodes in a crossroad area, where the flip curves of the cusp's two branches
cross, as in Gallas's kite, and in a spring area, where a flip curve loops around the cusp. It splits a saddle into a
node and two saddles in a saddle area, where the attracting orbit exists only inside the wedge. The sign of the cubic
coefficient $c$ of the fold's normal form decides between the two splittings: $c<0$ for crossroad and spring areas,
$c>0$ for the saddle area (Figure~\ref{fig:cuspkinds}). Mira also noted that in two dimensions the attracting node becomes a focus, with a complex
pair of multipliers, away from the fold and flip curves. In a two-dimensional map whose Jacobian is never singular,
such as H\'enon's, no multiplier can vanish. There the curves on which the trace of the cycle's two multipliers
vanishes take the place of the superstable curves, and the head is no longer generic. Fa\c{c}anha, Oldeman and Glass
found two unfoldings: in both the crossing at the tail survives, while at the head the curves either close into a
loop (their Type~A) or approach each other and turn away (Type~B) \cite{facanha2013endoskeletons}.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-cusp-kinds.png}
\caption{The three kinds of organization around a cusp of a fold curve, computed from the quartic map of
Carcass\`es et al.\ \cite{carcasses1991crossroad}, $x\mapsto c+(1+a)x+bx^2+x^4$, in planes of $b$ and $c$ at fixed
$a$, as they drew them (gray: one attracting fixed point; blue: two). The cusp (gold) lies at the fixed point $x=t$
with $a=8t^3$, and $c_3=4t$ is the cubic coefficient of its fold normal form (written $c$ in the text). Left, $a=-1.1$:
a crossroad area; passing through the cusp splits a node into a saddle and two nodes, and the two attracting fixed
points coexist in a kite whose far corner is the crossing of the flip curves of the cusp's two branches (red dot).
Middle, $a=-2.05$: a spring area; a flip curve closes into a loop that winds once around the cusp (the loop forms at
$a=-2$, the codimension-three point $a_7$ of Carcass\`es et al.). Right, $a=+1.1$: the saddle kind; passing through
the cusp splits a saddle into a node and two saddles, and the attracting fixed point exists only inside the cusp's
wedge. Each frame contains the whole defining structure. Period-2 curves are not drawn.}
\label{fig:cuspkinds}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The skeleton of one window.} The plane steered at two depths (Section~\ref{sec:consequences}) holds a
period-5 window (Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}). A $31\times31$ grid of the window's continued orbit
(Appendix~\ref{sec:hostility}) left two anomalies, signs of folds it could not resolve, so we continued the window's
fold and flip curves in float64. One fold curve bounds the body on its right, runs over its top and along one side of
the upper and lower-right legs. Flip curves bound the body's upper-left and lower sides and the other side of each leg, so each leg lies
between a fold curve and a flip curve, as in Gallas's anatomy, and beyond the flips lie shells of periods 10 and 20.
Where the upper leg leaves the body, the fold curve has two located cusps that share a segment of the curve, and its
other two branches cross (Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}d). At the crossing the two arms are folds of two different
period-5 orbits with the same token loop (their states differ by about 4\,\%). A second fold curve, on the far left,
where the orbit dies after regaining stability beyond a doubling bubble, has two more located cusps, which also share
a segment, and its other two branches cross as well, again as folds of two different period-5 orbits (states about 9\,\%
apart; Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}a). Along the curves continued on 15 October the critical multiplier stays at
$+1$ or $-1$ to within $1.2\times10^{-7}$, and no other multiplier exceeds $0.4$ in modulus; with the curves of 18
October (their extensions toward the legs' tips and the lower-left leg's own pair) these bounds become
$1.9\times10^{-7}$ and $0.87$, the largest second multiplier ($0.86$) lying where the lower-right leg's curves reach
the bound of the continuation. No second multiplier reaches the unit circle along them; this does not exclude cusps,
which need only a nonlinear degeneracy.

\paragraph{The shrimp itself.} From the fixed start, a $512\times512$ map of the window (Figure~\ref{fig:realshrimp}a;
run HP5) shows the window's family in 59\,\% of the cells of the followed domain; in 24\,\% the fixed start reaches
another periodic attractor, mostly the coexisting period-4 attractor and its doublings (periods 4, 8, 16 and 32), in
12\,\% a largest exponent above $10^{-3}$ (chaos; at 33 cells it overrides a detected period), and in 5\,\% neither. To see the window whole, we followed its attractor from the body outward over a grid of
spacing $1.25\times10^{-4}$ in both strengths (Figure~\ref{fig:realshrimp}; \repo{tools/follow_attractor.py}). Each
cell starts from the final state of a neighboring cell that is still on the attractor and runs 250 steps, followed by
300 recorded steps with a twin. It stays on the attractor only if its orbit has a period that is a multiple of five and
the body's five-token text in at least four of its five places, or no detectable period, an exactly repeating text and
an exponent below 0.005 (a slowly converging orbit next to a fold or a flip); cells with chaos behind the same text are
recorded but not continued. (A first rule, which compared each cell only with its neighbor, leaked across the fold
into the surrounding chaos, where intermittent motion keeps the same text most of the time.) The followed attractor
fills a shrimp: a body against the fold curve and three long legs, each between a fold curve and a flip curve, reaching
$(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)=(-0.1148,0.109)$, $(-0.1252,0.0918)$ and, on the left, $\alpha_2=0.108$, with shells of periods
10, 20 and 40 beyond the flips and chaos behind the same five-token text outside them; a fourth leg, along the lower right, narrows from $5.1\times10^{-5}$ in $\alpha_1$ near the body to
$3.5\times10^{-8}$, far below the grid step, so the follow does not enter it. Along its center line, each point
started from the orbit continued along the leg's fold curve (\repo{tools/leg_centerline.py}), the attractor of
the body's family, with the body's text, is present at all 128 points from the body down to $\alpha_2=0.075$, the
bound of the continuation (period 5 at 127, period 20 at one); one leg width beyond the fold none of them keeps it,
and one leg width beyond the flip 118 of 128 carry the doubled orbit or chaos behind the same text
(Figure~\ref{fig:realshrimp}b). Of 2173 cells computed, 1720 are on the attractor: 822 with period 5, 371 with period 10, 82 with
period 20 and 11 with period 40, a few with periods 15, 30 and 50 inside chaotic bands, 240 chaotic behind the same
text and 178 converging slowly. At $\log_{10}T=-0.105$ the followed domain has the same anatomy (1760 cells on the
attractor; Figure~\ref{fig:shrimptemperature}): its period-5 body moves by $(+0.00085,+0.00236)$ in
$(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)$, and the head, continued in temperature, by about $(+0.00105,+0.00247)$ over the same step, so the
window drifts as a whole. The computed curves lie where the followed domain ends. The continuations from the body do
not reach the lower-left leg's own boundary, even when rerun with wider parameter bounds, so we marched the period-5
orbit into the leg by Newton's method, through the 68 cells by which the follow run entered it, and continued it there
(Appendix~\ref{sec:hostility}, Figure~\ref{fig:lowerleg}). The leg is a strip between a flip curve on its left and a
fold curve on its right. Every period-5 cell of the leg lies inside that strip (39 of 39), and no computed cell inside
it is lost or has another period. With these curves, 276 of the 292 pairs of neighboring cells with periods 5 and 10
straddle a flip curve to within one grid step, and 290 to within two. Of the 754 pairs in which a period-5 cell borders
a cell where the attractor was lost, 717 lie within one step of a fold curve and all 754 within two steps of a fold or
flip curve, the farthest 1.7 steps away; with the body's curves alone these counts were 251, 504 and 544. Below
$\alpha_2=0.0934$ the strip is narrower than one grid step (0.015 steps at $\alpha_2=0.080$, where the continuation
reached its bound), so the leg goes on below the followed domain, too narrow to be followed cell by cell.

\paragraph{Shrimps of a two-dimensional kind.} The period-5 window has this anatomy; only its head differs. At
every cell of the $31\times31$ continuation grid and every point of the curves continued on 15 October, the
multipliers beyond the leading pair are below $0.0032$ (on the curves of 18 October at this temperature that recorded
them, they stay below it inside the followed domain and reach $0.010$ beyond it), so on the grid the orbit's linearization has at
most two non-negligible directions. On the grid the leading multiplier never
vanishes (its modulus stays at least $0.0139$): where a one-dimensional map would be superstable, the two leading
multipliers form a complex pair, the node having become a focus, or pass each other as real numbers of opposite sign.
Between grid cells, however, a two-parameter Newton search for the point where the sum of the leading multipliers and
the sum of their pairwise products vanish converges at $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)=(-0.1184836,0.1017217)$
(\path{docs/review/p5-nilpotent.jsonl}). There the three leading multipliers have nearly equal moduli, $3.4$ to
$3.6\times10^{-3}$, as the two conditions would make exactly equal for three roots alone (they would make the roots
the cube roots of their product). The leading modulus sets the asymptotic linear contraction, a factor of about 280
per period, but a single return can still amplify a perturbation: at one phase the norm of the return map is about 65
(below). This point of small leading multipliers, which we call near-nilpotent (the leading eigenvalues of the
monodromy matrix nearly vanish, although the matrix itself does not), plays the role of the head. The nearly equal
moduli do not by themselves decide whether the return map is locally of higher rank there or nearly of rank one and strongly
nonnormal; its singular values decide it (\repo{tools/monodromy_svd.py}, checked on synthetic operators with equal
eigenvalues and different rank). At the point the second singular value of the return map is $1.8\times10^{-4}$ of the
first at one starting phase ($1.7$ to $1.8\times10^{-4}$ at four points $5\times10^{-5}$ away) and at most 0.031 at the
others, while the first, at that phase, is about 65, four orders of magnitude above the eigenvalues; in the body, away from the
head, the ratio is 0.017 to 0.23. Near its head the return map is nearly of rank one and strongly nonnormal. Its
leading rank-one part is nearly nilpotent because its left and right singular vectors are nearly orthogonal, which
shows how a large norm can coexist with small eigenvalues. That part's eigenvalue, however, is about 13 times smaller
than the full leading modulus: the remainder, whose norm is the second singular value (about $0.011$ at that phase),
exceeds the leading modulus and matters for the full spectrum. In the reduction to this rank-one part, $\lambda=\sigma_1\,(v_1\cdot u_1)$, the nonzero eigenvalue of
$\sigma_1u_1v_1^{\top}$, which stays defined where the eigenvalues collide (at the head $\lambda=-2.7\times10^{-4}$, so
the two directions are orthogonal to about $4\times10^{-6}$), the head lies on a single zero curve: on an
$11\times11$ grid of spacing $10^{-5}$ around it, $\lambda$ changes sign exactly once in every row, along one nearly
straight line through the head (Figure~\ref{fig:headrank}). Even in this reduction the head is not a crossing of two
superstable curves. Continued in temperature, over the eleven values of $\log_{10}T$ from $-0.104$ to
$-0.094$ at which the continuation passed its gates, the point moves along a nearly straight line; the leading modulus
stays between $2.4$ and $5.6\times10^{-3}$, and a fourth multiplier approaches it twice (Figure~\ref{fig:headtemperature};
\repo{tools/nilpotent_track.py}). The product $D=\mu_1\mu_2$ of the two leading multipliers also changes sign in the
window ($D<0$ at 41 of its 447 attracting cells), so the linearization restricted to the leading pair reverses
orientation in part of the window. This differs from the setting of H\'enon's map, whose two-dimensional Jacobian
determinant has a fixed sign, but it does not by itself establish noninvertibility or a two-dimensional reduction of
the full map: the pair is selected by modulus and can change identity, and where $D$ changes sign the second
multiplier is as small as those beyond the pair. The trace-zero curve around an island of negative trace (Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}c)
is closed on the grid except at one cell next to the fold curve, where it must close, because on a fold the trace is
$1$ plus the second multiplier and therefore positive: a closed trace-zero contour as in Type~A, a classification
derived for determinants of fixed sign \cite{facanha2013endoskeletons}. In the
hostile period-7 body the curves on which the leading real multiplier changes sign, candidate superstable curves of
the continued orbit, form two arcs that approach each other and turn away without crossing, a Type-B-like geometry,
where the leading multiplier becomes as small as the next (Figure~\ref{fig:superstable}; Section~\ref{sec:hostile}). The period-6 body (``A second shrimp, in the plane of the crossing window'', below) shows the
same geometry: its pseudo-superstable
lines, where the leading multiplier changes sign without vanishing, meet below the kite and approach and turn away at
the top. The same plane holds a period-doubling cascade whose cells are nested
V-shaped tongues near $\alpha_1=-0.105$, $\alpha_2=0.105$. Continued in float64 on grids over these cells, its
period-2, -4 and -8 orbits read the same way: no cell reaches superstability (the smallest leading moduli are 0.222,
0.041 and 0.034), and complex leading pairs and trace-zero curves take the place of the superstable curves. The cells
become more one-dimensional with each doubling: over their attracting cells, the median ratio of the second to the
leading multiplier is 0.59, 0.125 and 0.027. Per step, the second multiplier contracts by a nearly constant factor
(medians 0.50 to 0.57), so its share shrinks like a power of the period; the per-step medians predict 0.52, 0.146 and
0.029, within 17\,\% of the measured ratios. In the terms of the H\'enon map, whose Jacobian determinant $-b$ is
constant, so that $\mu_1\mu_2=(-b)^p$, the per-step area contraction $|\mu_1\mu_2|^{1/p}$ plays the role of $|b|$;
its medians are 0.41, 0.41 and 0.50. In the period-4 and period-8 cells the trace-zero curves run along both
arms of the V. Finer grids at its bottom resolve them: in both cells the two arms are one U-shaped trace-zero curve,
and they do not cross. In the period-8 cell the product of the two leading multipliers is positive throughout and the
third multiplier is at most $6\times10^{-5}$ of the second, so the reading is two-dimensional to that accuracy. In the
period-4 cell that product changes sign, and near the bottom of the U the second and third multipliers form a
complex pair, so the leading pair is not isolated there. Neither cell has a crossing of trace-zero curves, the
analog of a one-dimensional shrimp's head, and the period-4 cell has no near-nilpotent point at the bottom of its U
either: a two-parameter Newton search on the invariants of the leading spectrum, as for the period-5 window, converges
where its three leading multipliers have nearly equal modulus, 0.0325, and lie $120^{\circ}$ apart, ten times the
period-5 point's $3.5\times10^{-3}$ (\repo{tools/nilpotent_search.py}). Under the decision rule fixed before these
grids, both cells remain unresolved
between the loop and turn-away types (the first period-8 grid left the bottom of the V unsolved because of a defect in
the continuation, fixed for the second; \repo{docs/dss}).

The junction where the upper leg leaves the body, two cusps that share a segment of the fold curve and a crossing
of its other two branches, is the configuration that Mira et al.\ call a dovetail for critical curves with two such
adjacent cusps
\cite{mira1996chaotic}; we borrow the name, with periodic orbits in place of preimages. With the crossing, the
configuration is consistent with a plane section of a swallowtail, whose geometry predicts four orbits in the pocket
between the cusps and the crossing, none beyond the crossing and two elsewhere (Figure~\ref{fig:anatomy}b). An orbit census at 13 points fixed
before the computation found exactly these numbers at nine (Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}d): four orbits at three of
five pocket points, none at either point beyond the crossing, and two at four of five surrounding points. At three
other points it found fewer orbits, but all the attracting ones the model predicts (the counts are lower bounds); at
one point, beyond a flip curve, it also found an orbit with another token loop. At all five pocket points of the census
two attracting period-5 orbits coexist; near the upper cusp a flip curve crosses the pocket, beyond which one of them
has doubled. The pocket therefore plays the kite's part, a bistable region at the end of a leg, with two cusps and a
crossing where Gallas's kite has one cusp. In the quartic models of Figure~\ref{fig:anatomy}, both configurations are
plane sections of the swallowtail of the quartic fixed-point equation, and the window's configuration is consistent
with such a section. Lifted from the
parameter plane into the orbit states, the fold curve is smooth: its cusps are only where its shadow folds, and the
two arms that cross in the shadow lie on different sheets, as in the model (Figure~\ref{fig:foliation}). The window's second fold curve has a second dovetail. In each, the
two cusps have cubic coefficients of opposite sign, as in any section of a swallowtail: one of the saddle-area kind
($c>0$, the upper cusp in both) and one of the crossroad or spring kind ($c<0$). We do not claim the swallowtail point
itself, which needs a third parameter, and we have not traced the tail \cite{gallas1994dissecting}, determined
symbolic sequences or tested for a periodicity hub \cite{vitolo2011hubs,jones2012rossler}. With these qualifications,
the period-5 window meets the three numerical criteria we required before calling a window a shrimp: primitive
periodic orbits of the full state (float64 Newton solves), their stability (the multipliers and the followed
attractor), and the organization of their boundary (continued fold and flip curves with located cusps, matched by the
edge of the followed domain). It is a numerically verified shrimp in a two-parameter plane of the full map, of the
kind Gallas found in H\'enon's map \cite{henon1976two,gallas1993structure}. Its head resembles those of
two-dimensional maps, a closed trace-zero curve rather than crossing superstable curves, but that likeness is
geometric: no two-dimensional reduction of the dynamics is established (a closure test failed for numerical reasons;
Appendix~\ref{sec:hostility}), the product of the two leading multipliers changes sign, and none of this is proved by
interval arithmetic.

\paragraph{A second shrimp, in the plane of the crossing window.} The plane of Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}, the philosophy
direction at layer 12 against the temperature, holds a second shrimp-shaped window: the period-6 loop
``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe question\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe existence'' (Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp};
runs P6S, HR9, HR10, DSS6 and KITE). At a strongly contracting cell of the map, $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.073097,-0.15204)$,
Newton's method in float64 converges to a primitive period-6 orbit (residual $1.6\times10^{-14}$) whose leading
multipliers form a complex pair of modulus $0.016$. Followed from there through neighboring cells on the grid of
Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}, the attractor fills 4224 cells. They cover the shrimp's body and its legs, and below the body they continue through a narrow neck into a smaller,
distorted window at the lower right of Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}(c). Of these cells, period 6 holds
1946, periods 12, 24 and 48 hold 945 along the edges, other periods 35, chaos behind the repeating text in 811 (an exactly repeating text, no period up to
64 and an exponent of at least 0.005) and slow cells in 487 (the same with an exponent below 0.005; with the maps'
threshold of $10^{-3}$ the split would be 842 and 456). Continued from the body in four directions, up and down in $\alpha$ and in $T$, the period-6 orbit
ends at a fold above and on the right and at flips on the left and below. Continued in the plane with every point checked (residual at most $10^{-10}$, critical multiplier real and within
$10^{-7}$ of $\pm1$ on the body and kite curves; four points of the extended leg curves reach $2.9\times10^{-7}$), the curves outline the anatomy of Gallas's cell (Figure~\ref{fig:anatomy}a): the fold curve bounds
the body above and on the right, one flip curve bounds it on the upper left and a second along its bottom, and the
doubled shells lie beyond both. Within the window of Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}a, the edge of the followed domain lies within two grid steps of the
curves for all 232 neighboring pairs of periods 6 and 12 and all 459 pairs of a period-6 cell and a cell where the followed attractor was lost (median
distances 0.19 steps); over the whole followed domain the shares fall to 388 of 916 and 1{,}720 of 1{,}888 (medians 4.3
and 0.24 steps), because the far parts of the legs leave the traced curves, so their boundary is not resolved. A
$640\times640$ fixed-start map centered on the body (steps $4.4\times10^{-6}$ in $\alpha$ and $1.25\times10^{-5}$ in
$\log_{10}T$, about seven times finer than Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}; 66.5\,\% of its cells chaotic) shows the body
with its dark lines of strongest contraction, the flip region along its bottom and two of its legs leaving its
lower-left end, one straight down along the left flip curve and one to the lower left (Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}a).

The two flip curves cross at that end, near $(0.072912,-0.154104)$. They are flip curves of two different orbits:
Newton's method at that point (\repo{tools/kite_newton_at_R.py}), started from the orbit stored on each curve, converges
to two distinct period-6 orbits (residuals $3\times10^{-14}$ and $2\times10^{-13}$), with the same six-token text but
6.4\,\% of the mean state norm apart (the largest point distance after the best of the six cyclic alignments). Their
leading multipliers are $-0.9952$ and $-1.0005$ at this approximate crossing of the two traced curves, whose nearest
saved points lie 1.7 and 0.3 steps of the grid of Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} away. The crossing is a
pseudo-crossing of curves on two sheets of the orbit family, the configuration at the corner R of Gallas's kite, where
the flip curves of two coexisting fixed points cross \cite[Fig.~7]{gallas1994dissecting}. Inside the body near it, at
$(0.0730,-0.1539)$, the orbit of the lower flip curve is attracting (leading multiplier $-0.797$); Newton's method from
the other orbit did not converge there: the point lies just beyond that orbit's fold, on the branch C--A of the kite
(Figure~\ref{fig:p6kite}), where the orbit no longer exists. The rest of the kite
follows from continuation (Figure~\ref{fig:p6kite}; run KITE). Continued in $\alpha$ at fixed $\log_{10}T=-0.15395$ from
the first orbit's flip, the branch is S-shaped: the orbit stays attracting up to a fold at $\alpha=0.072992$, the
branch returns along the saddle to a second fold at $0.072793$, and it continues on the second orbit, which flips near
$0.07301$; at that temperature the two attracting orbits coexist for $\alpha$ between 0.072907 and 0.072992. The second
orbit, continued upward in $\log_{10}T$ from its flip at fixed $\alpha$, is attracting up to its own fold at
$-0.153657$. The fold curves traced from the two folds turn back at the same point, the kite's cusp C, located at
$(0.0730186,-0.1532803)$ (cubic coefficient $c=-1.33\times10^{4}$, unfolding determinant $\det K=-2624$, critical
multiplier within $7.7\times10^{-9}$ of $+1$, next modulus $0.00095$), on the side of R toward the body. Its two
branches meet the two flip curves at A $=(0.072991,-0.153984)$ and B $=(0.072900,-0.153707)$, completing the kite C--A--R--B of Gallas's cell. A and B are pseudo-crossings too: at the continued fold points
nearest them the orbit's other multipliers are at most 0.0022 in modulus, none near $-1$, and at the flip points nearest
them the others are at most 0.0042, none near $+1$. A fold--flip point would need one orbit with both multipliers; here,
as at R, the curves belong to different period-6 orbits. Besides C,
the fold curves have five more located cusps (Table~\ref{tab:p6cusps}): four in two pairs near the ends of the upper-left and lower-left legs, the two cusps of each pair joined by a short
segment of the fold curve and with cubic coefficients of opposite sign, the configuration of the period-5
window's upper leg; and one at the tip of a small separate window beyond a narrow neck below the body, where the orbit
is stable again after a doubling; two branches, run independently, trace that fold curve and its turn to within one
curve step.

On a $31\times31$ grid of the continued orbit over the body ($\alpha$ from 0.0724 to 0.0738, $\log_{10}T$ from $-0.1545$
to $-0.1500$; the run reached 582 of the 961 cells before its time limit, and 529 of them converged), the leading multiplier is nonzero at every solved cell. Its smallest modulus on the grid,
$0.0063$ at the cell $(0.073053,-0.15120)$ on the line of strongest contraction, belongs to a nearly imaginary
leading pair, $0.0004\pm0.0063i$, with a third
multiplier of modulus $0.0051$: three multipliers of nearly equal small modulus, the near-nilpotent signature of the
period-5 head. The dark line of strongest contraction runs from the flip crossing up the body and bends to the upper
left, and all nine cells with a complex leading pair lie on its upper part (arguments 43 to 135 degrees, moduli 0.006
to 0.038). At 289 solved cells the two largest-modulus multipliers form a real or complex-conjugate pair, and their real product
is negative at 143; at the other 240 the leading real multiplier is followed by a complex-conjugate pair, so the first
two alone define no real two-dimensional determinant, and we infer no determinant-zero curve from them. The real
leading multiplier changes sign across 45 neighboring solved pairs; only two have leading and next moduli within a
factor three at both ends, and this endpoint test does not tell a multiplier crossing zero from an exchange of
dominance. These sign changes mark the body's dark ditches (Figures~\ref{fig:p6ditches} and~\ref{fig:p6signs}), which take the place of Gallas's
superstable curves. A leading
multiplier is the largest in modulus, so it vanishes only if all multipliers do, and between the two cells of each pair it changes sign in one of two ways: it meets the next multiplier, the two leave
the real axis as a complex pair and return to it with the opposite sign, or a multiplier of the opposite sign grows
past it in modulus and becomes the leading one, a hand-over. Two lines of cells computed locally in float64
(run DITCH) show both outcomes. Across the main ditch at $\log_{10}T=-0.1530$ (21 cells, steps $7\times10^{-6}$ in
$\alpha$) the negative leading multiplier shrinks from $-0.605$ to $-0.060$, the lead passes at $\alpha=0.072976$ to a
complex pair, $0.0069\pm0.0229i$, and the pair splits into two positive multipliers, the lead rising to $+0.71$. Across
the dimmer ditch that closes the lighter region at its top, at $\alpha=0.07318$ (26 cells, steps $4\times10^{-5}$ in
$\log_{10}T$), the leading multiplier stays positive: it falls from 0.122 to a minimum of 0.064 at
$\log_{10}T=-0.15202$ and rises to 0.171, while the next stays negative and below 0.021 in modulus, a valley without a sign change. Ten times finer, on 225
cells around that minimum (steps $1.8\times10^{-6}$ in $\alpha$ and $4\times10^{-6}$ in $\log_{10}T$, run DITCHFINE on
two rented GPUs), the leading multiplier is again real and positive everywhere (0.056 to 0.075), with the same minimum
at $\alpha=0.07318$ (0.0638). It falls toward smaller $\alpha$, where the valley runs toward the main ditch, so line
A's minimum is where that line crosses the valley, not the valley's lowest point. A patch of the continued orbit over $\alpha\in[0.07300,0.07336]$ and $\log_{10}T\in[-0.15294,-0.15170]$
($21\times32$ cells, steps $1.8\times10^{-5}$ and $4\times10^{-5}$, four GPU bands, run DITCH2D; 595 cells solved, the
unsolved ones at the right edge, next to the fold) has 41 sign changes and 33 complex leading pairs: the arc closes over a
pocket of negative leading multiplier, with its peak near $(0.07326,-0.15232)$, and no sign change crosses the region
between that peak and the main ditch. The two pseudo-superstable lines thus meet below the kite's cusp C, as Gallas's
superstable curves meet in his kite, and at the top they approach and turn away without crossing, a Type-B-like
geometry \cite{facanha2013endoskeletons}. On both sides of every sign change the continued orbit is the same attracting
period-6 orbit, with the six-token text of the seed and Newton residuals below $10^{-12}$; fixed-start scans at the nearest parameters find period six at all patch and line cells but two at the patch's edge,
which are unresolved, and on the coarse grid at 44 of the 45 pairs and 6 of the 9 complex cells (the others, at the
upper left, reach a coexisting attractor of period 8, 16 or 32). These are nearby parameters, not same-parameter
full-state comparisons, so whether the fixed start reaches this very orbit was not checked at every continued point. What keeps the two lines apart was measured in run DELTA2D on four rented GPUs (\repo{tools/delta2d_analysis.py},
\repo{docs/delta2d/analysis.json}; Figure~\ref{fig:delta2d}). A $27\times37$ grid over the top of the body (steps
$10^{-5}$ in $\alpha$ and $2.5\times10^{-5}$ in $\log_{10}T$) gave 852 continued orbits, with the seed's six-token text,
Newton residuals at most $1.0\times10^{-12}$ and the three leading Ritz vectors stored at every cell; the 147 unsolved
cells lie in the corner beyond the fold. A two-dimensional block of the monodromy, initialized from the leading Ritz directions at the best-separated cell, was
tracked outward by subspace overlap, the largest mean squared cosine of the principal angles among the spans of two of
the three stored Ritz directions (so the tracked pair is not always the two locally largest). On them the
monodromy acts as a $2\times2$ block with trace $\tau$ and determinant $\delta$, and the eigenvalue of larger modulus,
or the real part of a complex pair, has the sign of $\tau$. The sign of $\tau$ matches that of the leading multiplier in
849 of the 852 cells (the other three are cells where the followed pair has mixed with a third direction); every one of
the 42 measured sign changes of a real leading multiplier lies within one cell of a sign change of $\tau$; and 60 of the
64 sign changes of $\tau$ lie within one cell of a measured sign change or a complex leading pair. The ditches are thus
the trace-zero curves of the leading block, the construction that Fa\c{c}anha, Oldeman and Glass use as the skeleton of
shrimps in two-dimensional maps \cite{facanha2013endoskeletons}. Between the main ditch and the arc, $\tau$ has a saddle
at $(0.073188,-0.152083)$ with the value 0.045 (Hessian eigenvalues $-8.0\times10^{-4}$ and $3.5\times10^{-3}$ per
squared cell, from a quadratic fit over $5\times5$ cells with residual $3.9\times10^{-4}$). Near a nondegenerate saddle
with a nonzero value the zero set splits into two separate branches, which is why the two lines approach and turn away
instead of crossing. The leading multiplier has a saddle nearby, 0.067 at $(0.073195,-0.152138)$ (Hessian eigenvalues
$-5.6\times10^{-4}$ and $2.8\times10^{-3}$), a reading fixed before the grid's data arrived. The finer patch lies just
above it, on the flank toward the main ditch, which is why the patch's lowest value sits at its corner. The run card
asked instead for a saddle of $\delta$, and that test fails: no critical point of $\delta$ passes the window test,
$\delta$ follows the steady drift of the second multiplier (from $-0.010$ to $-0.034$ across seven columns at the
saddle), and its zero set misses the arc (11 of its 32 sign changes lie within one cell of a measured sign change). The
trace was examined after the data arrived, as a disclosed amendment. In 38 cells the second and third multipliers are
comparable and the followed pair mixes with a third direction (overlap below 0.9).

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig-delta2d.png}
\caption{What keeps the two pseudo-superstable lines apart at the top of the period-6 body (run DELTA2D,
\repo{tools/plot_delta2d.py}). A two-dimensional block of the monodromy, initialized from its leading Ritz directions, was tracked from cell to
cell by subspace overlap over 852 continued orbits (float64; steps $10^{-5}$ in $\alpha$ and $2.5\times10^{-5}$ in $\log_{10}T$; gray: no orbit, beyond
the fold). (a) The trace $\tau$ of the tracked block (colors clipped at $\pm0.2$), its zero curves
(black), the measured sign changes of the real leading multiplier (white dots) and the cells with a complex leading
pair (gray squares), and the saddle of $\tau$ (gold cross, $\tau=0.045$) with the level curve through it (dashed).
(b) The determinant $\delta$, with its zero curve and the same measurements; small crosses mark cells where the followed
pair mixes with a third direction. The rectangle is the patch ten times finer around the lowest point of line A.}
\label{fig:delta2d}
\end{figure} Near the kite, where two orbits exist, curves that belong to different orbits can cross in the
parameter plane without any degeneracy, as the superstable curves of Gallas's two coexisting fixed points do at his
tail. With these qualifications the period-6 window meets the three numerical criteria along its body, with the kite of
Gallas's cell and its cusp C located (five more cusps close the ends of the legs and the small window beyond the neck):
a second shrimp-shaped window of the full map, consistent with Gallas's organization (in 896 dimensions the name
describes that organization of continued curves, not a local normal form), in the plane of
Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} itself. The far parts of its legs remain unresolved.

\begin{figure*}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig-shrimp-anatomy-pair-clean.png}
\caption{Gallas's canonical cell beside the period-6 shrimp, in the same orientation, with the shrimp's pseudo-superstable
lines. (a) Gallas's period-1 cell of Figure~\ref{fig:anatomy}a, drawn with both axes reversed, a rotation by $180^\circ$
that brings it to the orientation of (b) (gray: one attracting fixed point; blue: two; fold curves black, flip curves red
and dashed, superstable curves blue and dotted; head H, cusp C, kite C--A--R--B, tail T). (b) The period-6 shrimp: the
$640\times640$ float64 map of Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}a with the continued fold and flip curves; R (cyan diamond): the
pseudo-crossing of the two flip curves; C (gold star): the kite's cusp. Along the dark ditches the continued period-6 orbit's leading multiplier changes sign between neighboring cells or
becomes a complex pair, and the orbit is attracting throughout (the measurements are drawn in
Figure~\ref{fig:p6signs}). The multiplier never vanishes, so these dark ditches are pseudo-superstable lines; they meet below C, where the superstable curves of (a) cross at the tail T, but approach and turn away at the
top, where (a) has its head H, a Type-B-like unfolding of the one-dimensional skeleton
\cite{facanha2013endoskeletons}. In both panels the fold curve bounds the main cell
(in (b), the body) above and on the right, flip curves bound it on the left and below, and at the lower left two of its legs cross in the kite.}
\label{fig:p6ditches}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure*}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig-p6-ditches.png}
\caption{Where the period-6 orbit's leading multiplier changes sign over the body of the shrimp (float64,
\repo{tools/plot_p6_ditches.py}). (a) The map of Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}a over the body, with three measurements of
the continued orbit: the $31\times31$ grid (blue dots: midpoints of neighboring solved cells whose real leading
multipliers have opposite signs, and cells where the leading multiplier is a complex pair), a patch across the top of
the lens 1.8\,$\times10^{-5}$ by $4\times10^{-5}$ in its steps (orange dots, the same rule), and two lines (black
ticks): line A across the dim top ditch at $\alpha=0.07318$, with its lowest leading multiplier circled, and line B
across the main ditch at $\log_{10}T=-0.153$. (b) Along line A the leading multiplier (solid) dips to $0.064$ at
$\log_{10}T=-0.15202$ but stays positive, and the next modulus (dashed) stays at most $0.021$: no sign change, a
valley of the same sign between the main ditch and the arc. (c) Along line B, the control, the leading multiplier
changes sign through a complex leading pair (open marker), from $-0.605$ to $+0.710$. The orbit carries the seed's
six-token loop and is attracting at every solved cell of all three measurements.}
\label{fig:p6signs}
\end{figure*}

ends of the upper-left and lower-left legs (the lower pair's stars overlap at this scale) and the tip of the small
window beyond the neck. The fold curve through C is that of the kite (Figure~\ref{fig:p6kite}).}

times finer in $\log_{10}T$ than in Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}a (gray: periodic, darker where more contracting; colors: chaotic), with the body's two flip
curves (red, dashed), the fold curve continued from the branch of (b) (black), and the corners of Gallas's kite: R,
where the flip curves cross (two distinct orbits, one on each curve); A and B, where the fold curve meets them; C, the
located cusp where the fold curve turns back (gold star). Green: the line $\log_{10}T=-0.15395$ of the branch, thick
where the two attracting orbits coexist. (b) The branch, computed by numerical continuation of the period-6 orbit in $\alpha$ along the green line of (a):
every point is an orbit converged by Newton's method, solid where its multipliers show it attracting and dashed where
not, and the two open circles are folds, where a multiplier reaches $+1$; they make the branch an S, and the two
attracting sheets overlap in the shaded interval (\repo{tools/plot_p6_kite.py}). The vertical axis is a display
coordinate: one point of each orbit, a vector of 896 numbers, centered over the branch and projected on the direction
in which these points vary most, their first principal component. Inside the kite, the straight edge where the gray changes abruptly (near $\log_{10}T=-0.15353$) is a basin boundary
of the fixed start, not a bifurcation, where we checked it: at two neighboring cells across it both attracting
period-6 orbits exist (leading multipliers $0.18$ and $0.60$ to $0.61$), the fixed start reaches one above the edge
and the other below (\repo{tools/kite_basin_check.py}), and at one steering strength the start on the edge lies on
the stable manifold of a saddle orbit between them (Figure~\ref{fig:hooktheory}).}
\label{fig:p6kite}
