\paragraph{The hook.} A $640\times640$ map of the upper end of the kite (Figure~\ref{fig:p6hook}; cells 3.1 and 2.5
times finer than in Figure~\ref{fig:p6kite}a) resolves the hook-shaped light wedge and the striations below it. The
wedge is bounded above by the kite's fold curve, where its orbit disappears, and below by a smooth curve where the fixed
start switches to the other attracting orbit; at adjacent cells across that curve both orbits exist and attract
(multipliers $0.18$ and $0.60$ to $0.61$; pair 1). The striations are tongues bounded the same way: across the edge of
one, two other attracting period-6 orbits exist at both cells (multipliers $-0.72$ and $-0.11$; pair 2), and inside
several tongues the exponents of neighboring cells fall into two separated groups, so the fixed start's outcome
alternates from cell to cell, a structure not resolved at this grid spacing (\repo{tools/plot_p6_hook.py},
\repo{tools/kite_basin_check.py}). From a random token's embedding as the start, the same box at $128\times128$ cells shows neither
the hook nor the tongues, and only 38\,\% of its cells keep their exponent within 0.02 of the nearest cell of the fixed-start map (run HR12-RS): these edges are set
by where the fixed start lies relative to the basins, as basin boundaries should be, not by bifurcations.

\begin{figure*}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig-p6-hook.png}
\caption{The hook and the striations at the upper end of the kite (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer
12; float64). (a) A $640\times640$ map of $\alpha\in[0.07291,0.07303]$ and $\log_{10}T\in[-0.1540,-0.1533]$ (gray:
periodic, darker where more contracting; colors: chaotic), with the kite's fold curve (black) and a flip curve of the
body (red, dashed); cyan: the two pairs of adjacent cells checked by Newton continuation, on the wedge's lower edge (1)
and on a tongue's edge (2). (b) The white box in (a) at the map's own cells: tongues in which the outcome alternates
from cell to cell.}
\label{fig:p6hook}
\end{figure*}

\paragraph{Why the lower edge lies where it does.} An edge of the fixed start's outcome that is not a bifurcation is
where the start crosses the boundary between two basins, and the boundary between the basins of two attracting orbits
is typically the stable manifold of a saddle orbit between them. At $\alpha=0.072975$ we bisected the wedge's lower
edge in $\log_{10}T$ to the float64 limit, at $\log_{10}T=-0.1535299666043828$ (\repo{tools/hook_saddle_check.py}).
Started there, the loop comes close to a third period-6 orbit and stays near it: Newton's method from the most nearly
periodic state of that stretch converges (residual $2\times10^{-14}$) to a saddle with a single multiplier outside the
unit circle, $\mu_u=1.2625$, real, with the next modulus $7.8\times10^{-4}$, at relative distances
$3.5\times10^{-3}$ and $4.7\times10^{-3}$ from the two attracting orbits. A start a small distance $\delta$ off the saddle's
stable manifold comes within a distance $\propto\delta^{\chi_s/(\chi_s+\chi_u)}$ of the saddle, as for a passage near
a linear saddle with $\chi_u=\ln\mu_u$ and $\chi_s=-\ln|\mu_s|$ from its slowest stable multiplier $\mu_s$ (an
exponent of 0.97 here), and then leaves along the unstable direction, its distance growing by $\mu_u$ per period, so the time it spends near the saddle grows by
$6/\ln\mu_u=25.74$ steps for every factor $e$ by which the offset shrinks. Both predictions hold over eight decades
(Figure~\ref{fig:hooktheory}; \repo{tools/hook_saddle_exit.py}). At 17 temperatures on each side of the edge, a
distance $\delta$ from $10^{-6}$ to $10^{-14}$ in $\log_{10}T$ away, the last step within $10^{-3}$ of the saddle
grows by 25.78 and 25.75 steps per factor $e$ in $\delta$ (all points within 4.0 and 2.9 steps of the fitted lines),
the closest approach grows with $\delta$ with log-log slopes 0.986 and 0.995 (predicted 0.97), and every start below the edge ends on
the strongly contracting orbit and every start above it on the other. Together with the random start, which removes
the edge, this is strong numerical evidence that, at this steering strength, the lower edge is where the fixed start
crosses the saddle's stable manifold. Bisecting 676
jumps of the outcome along the map's columns (to $2.6\times10^{-13}$ in $\log_{10}T$; \repo{tools/hook_edge_track.py})
traces the hook's other edges (Figure~\ref{fig:hooktheory}a). The upper edge follows the kite's fold curve, 0.1 to
1.6 grid rows above a spline through the continued curve's points and more so toward the cusp. The offset comes from
the map's window (Figure~\ref{fig:hooktip}b,c; \repo{tools/hook_tip_strip.py}): half a row and one row beyond the fold,
where the weaker orbit does not exist, the exponent over the map's window (steps 300 to 600) lies between $-0.022$ and
$+0.012$ for $\alpha$ above 0.072965 and 0.072967, because the loop still lingers at the orbit that vanished there,
while over steps 3000 to 3400 every cell of the strip beyond the fold gives the strongly contracting orbit's exponent ($-0.18$ to
$-0.71$).

\begin{figure*}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig-p6-hook-theory.png}
\caption{The hook's lower edge is where the fixed start crosses the stable manifold of a saddle orbit
(Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12; float64). (a) The map of Figure~\ref{fig:p6hook}a near the
hook, with the kite's continued fold curve (gold) and the edges of the fixed start's outcome bisected along the map's
columns (cyan; brackets whose end exponents differ by at least 0.1); red star: the steering strength
$\alpha=0.072975$ of (b)--(d). (b) Distance to the saddle orbit along the loop started a distance $\delta$ in
$\log_{10}T$ below the edge: it arrives along the stable manifold within about 25 steps, lingers, and leaves along
the unstable direction; dotted: the radius $10^{-3}$ of (c). (c) The last step within $10^{-3}$ of the saddle
against $\ln(1/\delta)$, for starts below the edge (which end on the strongly contracting orbit $S$) and above it
(which end on the other, $W$); dashed: slope $6/\ln\mu_u$ from the saddle's unstable multiplier $\mu_u$. (d) Closest
approach to the saddle against $\delta$; dashed: slope $\chi_s/(\chi_s+\chi_u)=0.97$ from the saddle's multipliers.}
\label{fig:hooktheory}
\end{figure*}

\paragraph{The tip of the hook.} The two edges meet where the fixed start lands exactly on the saddle-node orbit. At
a point of the fold, the coordinate $u$ of the merged orbit along its critical direction changes over one six-step return as
$u'=u+\eta+bu^2$ to leading order, with $\eta$ the distance from the fold in the normal form's units and $\eta=0$ on
the fold (Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}; \cite{kuznetsov2023elements}); after its fast transient the fixed start lands at
$u_0=\ell^\top(x-p_0)/\ell^\top r$, with $r$ and $\ell$ the right and left eigenvectors of the multiplier $+1$ at the
orbit point $p_0$. Where $u_0$ has the sign that the quadratic term drives toward zero, the start converges onto the
saddle-node orbit, and just inside the coexistence region it reaches the weaker orbit; where $u_0$ has the other sign,
the start escapes to the strongly contracting orbit. To leading order the tip is therefore the point of the fold where $u_0$
changes sign (exactly, it is where the start lands on the strong-stable set of the saddle-node orbit, which the zero of
$u_0$ approximates near that orbit). At seven points of the continued fold curve, $u_0$ is negative at $\alpha=0.072980$ to $0.073002$, where the
start converges slowly onto the orbit (its drift fits $u'=u+bu^2$ with $b=56$ to 68), and positive at $\alpha=0.072889$
to $0.072963$, where it escapes (\repo{tools/hook_tip_check.py}). At eight fold points located between them by
Newton's method at fixed $\alpha$ (residuals at most $5\times10^{-14}$; \repo{tools/hook_tip_fold_points.py}), $u_0$ is
nearly linear in $\alpha$ and crosses zero at $\alpha=0.0729655$, the predicted tip to leading order
(Figure~\ref{fig:hooktip}a). Inside the fold, the
boundary between the two outcomes approaches this point: at offsets of 2, 1, 0.5, 0.25 and 0.125 grid rows below the
fold, the last cell that ends on the strongly contracting orbit lies at $\alpha=0.0729627$, 0.0729632, 0.0729637,
0.0729642 and 0.0729645 (long window, cells $10^{-7}$ apart), and extrapolations with exponents from $1/3$ to $1/2$
put the boundary's end on the fold at $\alpha=0.0729651$ to $0.0729657$, bracketing the prediction
(Figure~\ref{fig:hooktip}d). The normal form predicts a tangential contact, the gap between the edges growing
quadratically along the fold, which corresponds to the exponent $1/2$; these points do not resolve the exponent. In
the map's window the boundary appears up to $7\times10^{-7}$ farther from the tip, as expected where the saddle's
unstable multiplier approaches 1 near the fold and the start lingers longer.

\begin{figure*}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig-p6-hook-tip.png}
\caption{The tip of the hook (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12; float64). (a) The fixed
start's landing coordinate $u_0$ on the saddle-node orbit at points of the kite's fold curve: negative (blue) where
the start converges onto that orbit, positive (red) where it escapes; squares and inset: fold points located by
Newton's method at fixed $\alpha$, where $u_0$ crosses zero at $\alpha=0.0729655$ (vertical line, the predicted tip,
also in (b) and (c)). (b) The largest exponent over the map's window (steps 300 to 600) along rows at fixed offsets
from the fold curve (negative: beyond the fold, where the weaker orbit does not exist); the strongly contracting orbit
has exponents of $-0.18$ to $-0.71$ here, the weaker one exponents near $-0.01$; dotted: the threshold $-0.15$ between
them. (c) The same cells over steps 3000 to 3400: beyond the fold every cell ends on the strongly contracting orbit.
(d) The boundary between the two outcomes inside the fold (last cell on the strongly contracting side and first on the
weaker, long window) against the square root of the offset, with a straight-line extrapolation to the fold (which assumes the exponent
$1/2$; the points do not resolve it).}
\label{fig:hooktip}
\end{figure*}


\paragraph{Symbolic dynamics.} Jones's unimodal interpretation of selected R\"ossler return maps, which traced the
shape of a periodicity hub to a conjugacy between the R\"ossler system and unimodal maps in part of its parameter
plane \cite{jones2012rossler}, suggests an approximate
scalar coding of the six-band attractor. Where the attractor writes the body's six-token text, the loop visits six thin
bands of the state space in turn, one per token, and returns to each band every six steps. Many sampled six-step return
maps, plotted along a band's first principal axis, show a single turning point: the next visit traces a parabola against
the previous one (Figure~\ref{fig:p6returnmaps}). We code each visit by the branch it lies on, L on the increasing one
and R on the decreasing one. The branch is read from the sign of the six-step derivative along the band, which the twin
trajectory of the Lyapunov exponent provides (\repo{tools/symbolic_dynamics.py}): the twin's offset direction is carried along the
attractor, and its scalar product with its own direction six steps earlier has the sign of that derivative where the
offset is aligned with the band and the band turns by less than a right angle. The checks below support this
approximation; they do not prove it. Pooling the misplaced visits and the rarer symbol's visits over the phases where
the band is straight enough for the sign to be defined, 48 of the 58 chaotic six-band cells of a $48\times48$ map of
the window have an error ratio below 5\,\% (the largest passing ratio is 4.7\,\%, 23 cells have no misplaced visit, and a single
phase of a passing cell reaches 21.7\,\%). The other 10 cells fail the coding test: three in one column beside the body's left
flip curve and seven below the kite. In 7 of them the return map has more than one resolved turning point: it rises
and falls more than once (Figure~\ref{fig:p6returnmaps}h--k). For a $C^3$ unimodal interval map with negative
Schwarzian derivative, at most one attracting periodic orbit can have an immediate basin that avoids the interval's
endpoints \cite{singer1978stable}; the extra turning points below the kite are consistent with a scalar explanation of
the coexistence inside it, but they establish neither a global scalar reduction nor its Schwarzian sign.

A periodic orbit of period $6k$ visits each band $k$ times. Its signs, rotated to the largest sequence in the order of
kneading theory, give it a label W|X in the sense of Metropolis, Stein and Stein (MSS)
\cite{metropolis1973finite,milnor1988iterated}: W is matched to the quadratic family's superstable itinerary WC and
names the window, and X records the orbit's inferred last branch symbol. Away from a superstable parameter this is an
orbit label, not a measured itinerary of the critical point. After a majority vote over the band phases whose coding is usable (at least three) and an explicit filter that keeps
only MSS words, 44{,}577 periodic six-band cells on the $640\times640$ grid of Figure~\ref{fig:p6shrimp}a receive
23 distinct window words W (Figure~\ref{fig:words}); the filter rejects 240 further cells whose labels are not
MSS words. The body and its period doublings carry the words of the doubling cascade: the empty word (period 6), R (12),
RLR (24) and RLRRRLR (48). Windows in the surrounding chaos carry other words, among them RL (period 18), RLRR (30), and
RLRRR and RLLRL, two distinct windows of period 36 that only their words tell apart. Within a window, the label predicts
the sign of the orbit's leading multiplier, $(-1)^{n_R}$ with $n_R$ the number of R's in W|X: positive next to the
boundary where the orbit is born (a fold, or for a doubled orbit the flip curve of its parent) and negative next to its
own flip. The curve where the predicted sign changes is a candidate for the window's superstable curve. The scan
assigns C to 153 cells whose usable phases do not agree sufficiently on the last symbol; at the two selected C cells,
Newton calculations report small, nonzero complex leading pairs. These labels mark ambiguous scalar coding, not
located superstable curves. Float64 Newton solves with exact Jacobians at one cell for each label W|X with at least three coded cells in the half
of the scan then complete (the cell nearest the center of the label's largest patch: 23 cells, covering 12 of the 23
words; Table~\ref{tab:p6words}), scored by a subsequent clean-phase rule (phases whose L and R visits one threshold
separates along the band, with every $|\cos|\ge0.5$), reproduce the scan's label at 16 cells; the two C cells have
complex leading pairs, and at the remaining 5 cells, just below the kite, no phase is clean, so their four words (RLL on both sides, RLLLRLL,
RLLLRLR and RLLLRRR) remain unconfirmed; the other 11 words were not checked. At the 16 confirmed cells the full map's leading multiplier
also has the sign that the label's parity predicts; this sign rule belongs to the scalar coding and carries over to
the full map only while that multiplier stays real and dominant. These checks support selected labels, not the whole map. In total, 1{,}469 of the
46{,}046 eligible periodic six-band cells receive no word (3.2\,\%), the 240 rejected by the filter among them; 922 of them (63\,\%) lie within Euclidean
distance two grid cells of the eligible domain's edge.

\begin{figure*}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig-p6-return-maps.png}
\caption{Return maps of the six-band chaos around the period-6 shrimp (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at

the flip curves (red, dashed; multiplier $-1$) of the window's period-5 orbit, over the attractor reached from the fixed start of every cell (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}) on a wider grid (gray: outside it); gold stars: the four located cusps; magenta crosses: the self-crossings of the two fold curves; cyan: the zoom
of (b). (b) The body
over a finer grid (as Figure~\ref{fig:period5}a), with the four branches (dotted) that found the first boundary points
from a common start (circle); white: the continuation grid of (c). (c) The same curves over the trace of the continued
orbit's two leading multipliers (Figure~\ref{fig:period5}b; gray: repelling). (d) The junction of the upper leg with
the body: the fold curve has two cusps that share a segment and crosses itself (x), where its arms are folds of two
different period-5 orbits; its two traces, continued from two different boundary points (black, and orange dashed),
coincide. Circles: the 13 points of the orbit census, with the number of period-5 orbits found and, in brackets, the
number attracting (green: the count predicted before the computation; orange: not).}
\label{fig:period5folds}
\end{figure}

\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-real-shrimp.png}
\caption{The period-5 shrimp (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, gratitude at block~4 and hostility at block~16,

$2.4\times10^{-5}$ in $\alpha_1$ and $3.8\times10^{-5}$ in $\alpha_2$): cells whose period is a multiple of five, the
window's family, in gray by contraction; other periodic attractors (among them the coexisting period-4 attractor that
the fixed start reaches in parts of the legs) pale; cells whose largest exponent exceeds $10^{-3}$ by its value (this overrides a detected period at 33 cells of the
followed domain). (b) The same map with the
window's verified skeleton: the fold curves (black; multiplier $+1$) and flip curves (red, dashed; $-1$) of the period-5
orbit, including the lower-left leg's own pair (Figure~\ref{fig:lowerleg}), the located cusps (gold stars), the
self-crossings of the fold curves (magenta) and the near-nilpotent point (white circle). Cyan: the edge of the domain of
the window's attractor followed cell by cell from the body on a grid of spacing $1.25\times10^{-4}$. Blue dots: the
center line of the fourth (lower-right) leg, too thin for the grid; all 128 sampled points retain the followed family,
127 with period 5 and one with period 20. (c) The body over the trace of the continued orbit's two leading multipliers (gray: repelling): the
trace-zero curve (black) closes around an island of negative trace, the geometry of a Type~A head, and passes the near-nilpotent
point. (d) The junction of the upper leg: two cusps that share a segment of the fold curve and the crossing of its
other branches, with the number of period-5 orbits found (attracting) at 13 points fixed before the computation (green:
the count predicted by a section of a swallowtail; orange: not).}
\label{fig:realshrimp}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-shrimp-anatomy.png}
\caption{The anatomy of a shrimp's cell in two classical one-dimensional models, computed (gray: one attracting fixed
point; blue: two). (a) Gallas's canonical period-1 cell, $x\mapsto(a-x^2)^2-b$ (his eq.~(5.3), rotated and labeled as in his
Figure~7 \cite{gallas1994dissecting}). Fold curves (black; multiplier $+1$) and flip curves (red, dashed; $-1$)
bound the main cell, and the superstable curves (blue, dotted; $0$) of the map's critical points cross at the head H.
Two legs, each between a fold and a flip curve, cross in the kite C--A--R--B, which begins at the cusp C of the fold
curve; in the kite two attracting fixed points coexist, and their superstable curves cross at the tail T. The flip
curves of the two legs cross at R, which makes the kite a crossroad area in the sense of Carcass\`es et al. The
computed corners A and B agree with Gallas's to six significant digits. (b) The quartic model of Carcass\`es et
al.\ \cite{carcasses1991crossroad}, $x\mapsto c+(1+a)x+bx^2+x^4$, in the plane of $a$ and $c$ at $b=-1$, a plane they
did not draw: the fold curve has two cusps $C_1$ and $C_2$ that share a segment and crosses itself at X (a dovetail, in the
term Mira et al.\ use for critical curves with two such cusps \cite{mira1996chaotic}). Numbers: real fixed points in each region; a flip curve crosses the pocket,
and two fixed points attract only to its left. Both fixed-point equations are quartics $x^4+ux^2+vx+w=0$, whose fold
set is the swallowtail: (a) is a section at fixed $v$, with one cusp, (b) a section at fixed $u<0$, with two cusps
and a crossing. A two-dimensional map whose Jacobian determinant never vanishes, such as the H\'enon map, has no
superstable curves, since no multiplier of its periodic orbits can vanish; trace-zero curves take their place and either close around the head (Type~A) or approach each other and turn away
(Type~B) \cite{facanha2013endoskeletons}; the language model's period-5 window has the first geometry
(Figure~\ref{fig:realshrimp}c).}
\label{fig:anatomy}
\end{figure}

\section{From greedy loops to chaos: one picture}
\label{sec:picture}
The preceding sections establish the pieces: a factorized derivative, folds, cascades and windows. This section
assembles them into one account of why the loop of Qwen2.5-0.5B along the philosophy direction looks the way it does.
It answers three questions: where the periodic orbits come from, why the windows cross one another, and why a network
with 896-dimensional states shows the signatures of one-dimensional maps. Every step was checked against the full map in
float64 (\repo{docs/e2e/}).

\paragraph{At zero temperature: greedy loops.} As $T\to0$ the soft token becomes the embedding of the most probable
token (at an exact tie, the average of the tied embeddings). Wherever each visited state has a unique most probable
token, the loop thus becomes greedy decoding with a one-token memory (Section~\ref{sec:theory}), and its eventual
cycles are token cycles: the zero-temperature attractors, superstable because the limit map is locally constant away
from ties. Along this direction the greedy map has a two-token loop
`` is there'' for $\alpha$ between 0.06301 and 0.07773, and a three-token loop
``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe question'' for $\alpha$ between 0.06609 and 0.07006. At $\alpha=0.070$ and low temperature, the loop indeed settles on these: in a column of
96 temperatures from $\log_{10}T=-2$ upward, 59 end on ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe question'' and 16 on
a period-4 loop that alternates `` is it'' and `` is there''.

\paragraph{At finite temperature: compromises between competing continuations.} The attractors that organize the maps
are not greedy loops. No value of $\alpha$ between 0 and 0.12 (in steps of 0.0005) makes `` is it'' a greedy cycle: after
`` it'' greedy decoding writes ``'s'' for $\alpha<0.105$, and above that, where it writes `` is'', the next greedy
token after `` is'' is not `` it''. The 2-cycle exists only because the soft token is a mixture. After `` is'', the fed-back
vector mixes `` it'' and `` there''; after `` it'', it mixes `` is'' and ``'s''. Each step thus feeds back a weighted
blend of two continuations, and the blends close into a cycle that greedy decoding never closes. Such a compromise
lives in a band of temperature and dies at both of its ends. At $\alpha=0.070$ the 2-cycle is stable between
$\log_{10}T=-0.5147881$, where a multiplier reaches $-1$ (a flip: the competition becomes too sharp, and the blend
alternates), and $\log_{10}T=-0.1741065$, where a multiplier reaches $+1$ (a fold, the cyan curve of
Figure~\ref{fig:stripes}). The period-3 loop of the crescent, ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe existence'', is a
compromise of the same kind, between `` existence'' and `` question'' in the noun slot. It too lives in a band, between
a fold above (at $\alpha=0.065$, extrapolated to $\log_{10}T=-0.14536$ from a continuation whose last converged
multiplier was 0.956) and a flip below. It does not connect to a zero-temperature
loop.

\paragraph{What happens at the two ends.} Beyond the fold the 2-cycle is gone, but its ghost delays every trajectory
that passes near it. The number of laps $N$ spent there grows as the inverse square root of the distance to the fold,
so periodic windows of period $2N+6$ line up parallel to the fold curve and crowd toward it, with distances close to
$1/N^2$ (log-log slope $-2.00$; Section~\ref{sec:mechanism}). This gives the near-vertical fan of stripes. Below the flip, a compromise starts
alternating between its two blends, and in the crescent the alternation doubles again and again, a Feigenbaum cascade
into chaos (Section~\ref{sec:cascade}). The near-horizontal band that crosses the stripes near $\log_{10}T=-0.14$ is
the first doubling of the crescent's period-3 loop, continued to larger $\alpha$. At $\alpha=0.070$ it is bounded by
two flips, at $\log_{10}T=-0.1392937$ above and $-0.1414292$ below. The two families cross because each is bounded by
its own curves: the stripes by the distance to the fold of `` is it'', the band by the flips of the period-3 loop.

\paragraph{Why the two families cross in both orders.} Which family a map shows where they overlap is not decided by
either family's curves. In parts of the crossing the crescent's family lies over the stripes, in others a stripe lies
over the band, and the order can change from one cell to the next. Every cell of the maps starts from the same state,
the embedding of the prompt's last token, so where two attractors coexist a map shows the one whose basin contains that
state. To see what the maps hide, we recomputed the window of Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} on a $192\times256$ grid as
eight maps: one from the fixed start; three from states of the crescent's period-3 orbit, of the band's 6-cycle and of
the period-10 stripe; and four sweeps that follow each attractor across the plane as far as they can, with every column
(or row) of cells starting from the final states of the previous one, in both directions of $\alpha$ and of $T$
(Figure~\ref{fig:sheets}; \repo{docs/sheets/analysis.json}). The four starts discard 600 transient steps and measure
over the next 300; the sweeps use 200 and 200 (600 transient steps in their first column or row). An independent audit with separate code reproduced every
count below from the raw maps.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Two overlapping sheets.} We group periodic attractors by the text their loops write, not by
  continuation. Periodic attractors of the crescent's family (loops that write
  ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe \ldots'') were found at 13{,}585 of the 49{,}152 cells, and those of the
  2-cycle and its stripes (loops that write `` is \ldots'') at 36{,}787. Both exist at 9{,}675 cells, on a broad arc that
  covers the crescent and runs under the stripes (Figure~\ref{fig:sheets}b). The fixed-start map hides 32\,\% of the
  crescent family's cells and 36\,\% of the 2-cycle family's. Along each temperature row the crescent family's
  branch is continuous: at $\log_{10}T=-0.1415$ it runs from $\alpha=0.066$ to about 0.0745, under the stripes
  (Figure~\ref{fig:sheets}d; Figure~\ref{fig:sheets3d} shows the two sheets in 3D).
  \item \emph{Selection by the start.} Where the seven other maps find a periodic attractor of only one family, the
  fixed start reaches the other family at just 70 of those 31{,}042 cells (0.2\,\%). Where both families were found,
  it reaches the crescent's family at 65.4\,\% of the cells and the 2-cycle's family at 28.5\,\% (the rest are
  chaotic or unresolved). In the crescent
  zone the split is 70 to 29\,\%; across the band it is 48.5 to 48.0\,\%, with the choice alternating from cell to
  cell, which is the alternating order seen in the map. Of the map's 1{,}376 edges between periodic cells of different
  families, 557 are pure switches: each side's family was also found on the other side, and only the basin that holds
  the fixed start changes. At 662 one side's family was not found on the other side by any map, and at 157 neither
  was. Eight maps cannot find every attractor, so these are not proofs that a family ends there, and the switch count
  is a lower bound.
  \item \emph{The crescent's own edge is a switch.} At each temperature the crescent's period-3 orbit exists from its
  fold onward, but the fixed start reaches it only farther right: in a strip up to $1.1\times10^{-3}$ wide in $\alpha$
  both coexist and the map shows the 2-cycle (Figure~\ref{fig:hiddenstrip}). At $\log_{10}T=-0.148$ the map's
  existence edge, $\alpha=0.06444$, coincides with the fold found by continuation, 0.064444. The strip narrows to the
  grid spacing toward the top of the crescent. At $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.06468,-0.148)$, inside the strip,
  interval arithmetic proves that the 2-cycle and the 3-cycle coexist and that both attract (the parameters are read
  as exact binary64 rationals, and the temperature is the real number $\exp(\ln10\cdot\log_{10}T)$ for that exact
  $\log_{10}T$; Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence}).
  \item \emph{Chaos.} Almost all chaotic attractors (96\,\% of the chaotic cells) visit the crescent family's
  blank-line and ``The'' tokens, including the chaos between the stripe windows, so the chaotic regions cannot be
  assigned to the 2-cycle's side alone.
\end{itemize}
Why the fixed start lands where it does is not explained by its nearness to either attractor. At three crescent cells
random starts (single token embeddings and mixtures of the top 64) reached the 2-cycle in 82 to 90\,\% of cases, and the nearest attractor phase predicted their
destinations worse than always guessing the 2-cycle (\repo{docs/basins/basins-summary.json}). The newline that ends
the prompt happens to lie in the crescent's basin over most of the overlap, even though that basin is the smaller one
for random starts. Settle times give no sign of long transients at the switches (median 94 steps near the edges and 87
elsewhere), so these basin boundaries do not hold trajectories for long. Refined at single switches, both mechanisms appear. Along two short
transects through the band, chosen from the fixed-start map before the other maps were inspected ($\alpha=0.0708$ from
$\log_{10}T=-0.1440$ to $-0.1396$, and $\log_{10}T=-0.1396$ from $\alpha=0.0690$ to $0.0700$), we bracketed every
switch of the fixed start's family by bisection, to 1/64 of the transect spacing at three of the eight switches and to
1/4 or 1/2 of it at the other five, where the bisection met a third loop; at both ends of each bracket we ran the fixed start,
continued each end's attractor at the other end and computed the multipliers of every periodic orbit found
(\repo{tools/sheets_transect.py}). Of the eight switches, two are selections (each side's family persists on the other
side, all four orbits attracting, and only the basin that holds the fixed start changes), two are intrinsic (one side's
family does not persist across the bracket), and four remain unresolved, involving chaos or an orbit that converges too
slowly near its doubling; a calibration bracket at the crescent's edge is a selection, as expected. A classifier fixed
before the run compared continuation arms arriving from opposite sides and classed none of the first seven switches as
selections; we replaced it, before any cross-test data, with the test above, and both results are kept
(\repo{docs/research-log.md}).
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig-sheets.png}
\caption{What the fixed-start map shows, and what it hides (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12,
float64; the window of Figure~\ref{fig:cross512} on a $192\times256$ grid). (a) The attractor reached from the fixed
start (colors as in Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}; black: chaos). (b) Where periodic attractors of each family exist,
according to all eight maps of the run: four starts (the fixed start and states of the crescent's period-3 orbit, the
band's 6-cycle and the period-10 stripe) and four adiabatic sweeps; black contour: where the fixed start reaches the
crescent's family. (c) How many steps the fixed start takes to settle. (d) Cross-sections along three temperatures:
the mean probability of the blank-line token on the attractor reached in each map against $\alpha$, colored by family,
with the family the fixed start reaches shown as a ribbon at the bottom. The crescent family's branch (orange)
continues under the stripes while the fixed start switches between the two branches.}
\label{fig:sheets}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig-sheets-3d.png}
\caption{The two families as sheets. Each point is an attractor found at one cell of Figure~\ref{fig:sheets}, at a
height equal to its mean probability of the blank-line token, which separates the families: in (a), 90\,\% of the
orange points lie between 0.07 and 0.25 and 99\,\% of the blue points below 0.03 (\repo{paper/llm/figures/fig-sheets-3d.json}).
Orange: the crescent's family (loops that write ``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe \ldots''); blue: the 2-cycle and
its stripes (loops that write `` is \ldots''); gray: chaos. (a) The attractors found by the eight maps, thinned at
random to at most 9000 per map and class. (b) Only what the fixed-start map hides: periodic attractors that the seven
other maps find where the fixed start reaches the other family, chaos or no period.}
\label{fig:sheets3d}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig-hidden-strip.png}
\caption{The crescent's left edge is a switch, not its fold. (a) Along each temperature, the smallest $\alpha$ at
which the crescent's periodic family exists in any map of the run (orange) and the smallest at which the fixed start
reaches it (black); between them (purple) both exist and the map shows the 2-cycle. Star: the period-3 fold found by
continuation at $\log_{10}T=-0.148$. (b) The width of that strip; pale points: rows where the fold lies beyond the
window, so the width is a lower bound.}
\label{fig:hiddenstrip}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Why one dimension.} A single step of the map does not squeeze the state onto a single direction: it is not close to rank one. Along these orbits, the second
eigenvalue of the readout covariance $\Sigma_p$ is 0.23 to 0.87 of the first, and the second singular value of the
step derivative is 0.11 to 0.90 of the first. Composed over a cycle, however, the map is nearly one-dimensional. The
ratio of the two leading singular values is 0.081 for the 2-cycle, 0.092 for the period-3 loop and 0.26 for the
period-6 band; it falls to $3\times10^{-4}$ over twelve steps of the chaotic crescent and to $4\times10^{-9}$ in the
chaotic stripes. Each step stretches some direction, but different steps stretch different ones, and only the
direction along which the competition between two continuations repeats keeps growing over a cycle. The Lyapunov spectra say the same. The map has one exponent for each of its 896 directions, ordered from largest to smallest, and the maps give only
the largest of each cell, from the drift of a nearby twin. To find the next few, we followed four small perturbations
at once with the network's exact derivative, re-orthogonalizing them at every step so that they do not all turn
toward the fastest-growing direction (\repo{tools/lyapunov_spectrum.py}), and averaged their logarithmic growth
rates. We did this only at three representative chaotic cells, one in the crescent's chaotic core
and two among the chaotic stripes. Over 600 steps their largest exponents are $0.109$, $0.424$ and $0.239$ per step,
and the next three are strongly negative, between $-0.82$ and $-2.01$. The attractors are therefore barely more than
one-dimensional: their Kaplan--Yorke dimensions, one plus the largest exponent divided by the magnitude of
the next, are 1.13, 1.43 and 1.21. (Over 3000 steps the largest exponents are
$0.098\pm0.010$, $0.437\pm0.016$ and $0.196\pm0.014$; Section~\ref{sec:dialogue}.) These
finite spectra and the singular-value ratios support strong effective contraction in the sampled directions and
motivate scalar approximations: on the sampled range, the crescent's attractor gives a scalar return relation with a
single extremum (Figure~\ref{fig:e2e}e). The measured cascade separately provides finite evidence consistent with
Feigenbaum scaling (Section~\ref{sec:cascade}); no global one-dimensional invariant reduction is established. A scalar model built from the two
competing tokens, $q'=\sigma(h(q)/T)$, reproduces the crescent's hump (Figure~\ref{fig:e2e}e) and, at $\log_{10}T=-0.156$, places its first flip
between $\alpha=0.0646$ and 0.0648, against 0.0644767 in the full map. It does not reproduce the cascade, so the competition sets the shape, while the
rest of the network sets where the cascade ends.

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig-e2e.png}
\caption{One picture, in float64 (Qwen2.5-0.5B, philosophy direction, layer 12). (a) Leading multiplier of the
`` is it'' 2-cycle along temperature at $\alpha=0.070$. It is stable (blue, inside the gray band $|\mu|<1$) only between
the flip ($\log_{10}T=-0.5148$) and the fold ($-0.1741$). The boxes give the two blended continuations at each phase
of the cycle, with their probabilities. (b) Largest Lyapunov exponent along the row $\log_{10}T=-0.115$ of the
$256\times256$ map. Beyond the fold $\alpha_c$ of the 2-cycle, periodic windows (gray, labeled with their periods)
alternate with chaos (red). Inset: $1/\sqrt{\alpha_N-\alpha_c}$ for the fold edges of the windows against their period. For
the longer windows (periods 16 to 20) it grows linearly with the period, as the ghost predicts
(Section~\ref{sec:mechanism}). (c) Part of the crossing map, with the fold curve of
the 2-cycle (cyan). The period-6 band is bounded by the flip of the period-3 orbit above and the flip of the period-6
orbit below, located on cuts at $\alpha=0.069$, $0.070$ and $0.0715$. On the cut $\log_{10}T=-0.148$, the crescent's
period-3 orbit is born at a fold ($\alpha=0.06444$) and flips at $\alpha=0.06548$. White circles: the census cells. At the two in the overlap all
ten starts ended on the band's 6-cycle; at the third (0.0708, $-0.1396$) one of five starts ended on a period-10
loop. (d) Second over first eigenvalue of the readout covariance
$\Sigma_p$ and second over first singular value of the step derivative $DF$ at every recorded state of four periodic
orbits and two chaotic attractors, and the
same ratio for the derivative composed over one period, or over twelve steps for the chaotic cells (stars).
(e) Return map of the crescent's attractor in the noun log-odds $s$ (black), with the two-token model
$q'=\sigma(h(q)/T)$ (dashed).}
\label{fig:e2e}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The picture.} At zero temperature, away from ties, the loop writes greedy loops. As the temperature rises, the soft token
starts to mix the two continuations a token has, and the mixtures form new periodic orbits that greedy decoding does not have.
The two we continued each live between a flip, where the competition is too sharp, and a fold, where it is too weak. Their folds leave
ghosts that line up the windows, and their flips start cascades into chaos. One competition stretches while the rest
of the network contracts. This supports describing these structures by one-dimensional maps, as Feigenbaum's ratios
suggest, although no global one-dimensional reduction is established. Steering
moves which continuations compete, and so where all of this happens.

\section{Beyond the one-token loop}
\label{sec:beyond}
The soft-token map feeds back one expected embedding at a fixed prefix and position. This section summarizes what
changes when the loop keeps more context, when tokens are sampled and in other models (details in
Appendices~\ref{sec:bridge} and~\ref{sec:scale}); Sections~\ref{sec:sizemain} and~\ref{sec:huginn} then follow model
size and a second system.

\paragraph{Memory and sampling.} As $T\to0$ the soft-token map tends to a map with finitely many values that agrees,
away from ties, with greedy decoding with the same finite memory, so every orbit of the limit ends in a cycle
(Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge}). Fed back the last 8 or 16 soft tokens instead of one,
Qwen2.5-7B is chaotic at 21 and 20 of 24 temperatures, against 8 with one. In Soft Thinking's growing context, where
every soft token stays visible, an early perturbation of $10^{-10}$ grows exponentially in the same model inside its
one-token chaotic band. This does not hold in every model: in Qwen2.5-0.5B the growth moves to sharper distributions,
outside its narrow one-token chaotic band, and Qwen2.5-72B stays nearly insensitive (Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}). Sampling is different. When the expected embedding is replaced by the mean of $m$ sampled
token embeddings, the laminar phases of the bottleneck, 120 steps long in the exact map at the closest offset, last on average 10.5 steps with $m=64$ and 7.8 with $m=8$ (10.3 to 10.7 and 7.6 to 7.8 over the three offsets), and
with single samples such phases become rare, one to three per offset. The deterministic
intermittency belongs to the expected-embedding map; sampling noise turns the bottleneck into a noise-limited trap
whose strength grows with $m$.

\paragraph{Other models.} The organization recurs in Qwen2.5-1.5B (Figure~\ref{fig:qwen15}) and 7B, in OLMo-2 7B, in Llama-3.1 8B, whose
attractors we also read with sparse-autoencoder features, and in a second checkpoint of the 0.5B architecture, which
shares the organization but not the attractors (Appendix~\ref{sec:scale}). The fold law of
Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} recurs at 1.5B and 7B: windows accumulate on the fold of a short cycle, and the nearest
four resolved windows meet a criterion fixed before the lines were computed, within $0.12\,\%$ at 1.5B and $4.5\,\%$
at 7B (a correction of the period selector after the first scoring is disclosed); farther out, the 1.5B windows
spread by $10.3$ to $14.0\,\%$. 
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.49\linewidth]{fig-qwen15-zoom-lyapunov.png}\hfill
\includegraphics[width=0.49\linewidth]{fig-qwen15-zoom-families.png}
\caption{Qwen2.5-1.5B soft-token map steered along the philosophy direction at layer 14, $256^2$ float64 cells over
$\alpha\in[0.03,0.12]$, $\log_{10}T\in[-0.2,0.2]$: largest Lyapunov exponent (left, colored as in
Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}) and attractor texts (right, as in Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}). Several period-2 label regions
touch without a gap; as explained in Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}, such boundaries may be label switches of one orbit.}
\label{fig:qwen15}
\end{figure}

\section{Chaos and model size}
\label{sec:sizemain}
Is the chaos of the loop a small-model effect that larger models leave behind? We measured the unsteered map of
fifteen models: Qwen2.5 at seven sizes from 0.5B to 72B parameters, OLMo-2 at 1B, 7B, 13B and 32B, Llama-3.1 at 8B
and 70B, Llama-3.3-70B, and the mixture of experts Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, all with the paper's prefix and the settings of
Appendix~\ref{sec:methods} (1000 transient and 2000 measured steps in float64; chaotic: an exponent above $10^{-3}$ and
no detected period, which is what ``positive'' abbreviates below). The stored checkpoint values are evaluated in
float64 (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}, ``Precision''), and the 70B-class models, spread over two GPUs, were recomputed
where an earlier two-GPU computation was less precise (Appendix~\ref{sec:size}). The 7--8B
and 70B-class models had shown a steep decline of chaos with size. Before measuring the others we fixed three
predictions that extrapolate it (\repo{docs/scaling-predictions.md}): within each family, every model above 8B is
less chaotic than the family's 7--8B model (P1); the chaotic share does not increase with size above 7B (P2); and at
matched next-token entropy, both gain factors of Section~\ref{sec:theory} of the 14B and 32B models lie at or below
those of the 7B model (P3). After a review pointed out that a single prompt and a fixed temperature window could
mislead, we froze a fourth test across ten prompts (\repo{docs/prefix-scale-test.md}).

\paragraph{In a fixed temperature window, chaos seems to fade with size.} Table~\ref{tab:scale} and
Figure~\ref{fig:scale} give each model's chaotic share of the temperatures in $\log_{10}T\in[-1,0.3]$, the range
common to all sweeps, and the chaotic length of its whole temperature axis. In two families the largest models are
nearly free of chaos in this window. Qwen2.5-7B is chaotic at
12.7\,\% of the temperatures in the common range, and the 14B, 32B and 72B models at none. Llama-3.1-8B is chaotic at
19\,\%, Llama-3.1-70B, from the same release and training recipe, at 2.3\,\%, and Llama-3.3-70B, which differs from
Llama-3.1-70B only in post-training, at none. In the third family the order fails: OLMo-2-32B is the most chaotic
OLMo model, at 16.2\,\% against 6.2\,\% at 7B, 3.8\,\% at 1B and none at 13B. (The 32B model is a later release,
0325, than the 7B and 13B models, 1124, so within OLMo-2 size and release are confounded.) The frozen predictions P1
and P2 hold for Qwen2.5 and fail for OLMo-2. Below 7B we predicted no order, and none appears (Qwen2.5 0.5B, 1.5B and
3B: 1.4, 1.5 and 0\,\%).

\paragraph{What a loop without chaos writes at 70B.} Not chaotic does not mean a single repeated token. Over its 128
temperatures ($\log_{10}T$ from $-1$ to $0.3$; 1000 transient and 2000 measured steps), the unsteered Llama-3.3-70B
settles on long cycles. At low temperature, at 46 of the 49 temperatures from $\log_{10}T=-1$ to $-0.5$, it writes a
fifteen-token loop without end, \texttt{.\ It'supgrading the infinite possibilities are you'recognized protocols}
(the tokens run together as written). At intermediate temperatures period-16 variants end in
\texttt{established} (15 temperatures) or \texttt{acknowledged} (9), and a period-21 variant turns into
\texttt{\ldots\ of a fellow AI to another artificial greetings} (8). Higher temperatures give cycles of 24 to 63 steps,
and 19 temperatures have neither a period up to 64 nor a positive exponent. Llama-3.1-70B, which differs only in
post-training, mostly writes the three-token loop \texttt{\ to another AI} (31 temperatures) or the four-token loop
\texttt{.\ It'sup} (21), longer repetitions of \texttt{\ to another AI} with periods 6 to 18, and at four temperatures
the fixed point \texttt{It}, a single token repeated. Steering changes this picture (Figure~\ref{fig:z128}): along
the hostility direction, fixed points fill the plane above the chaotic band and windows of periods 3 to 13, most often
6, lie below it.

\paragraph{Across prompts, the chaos moves to higher temperature instead.} The fixed window hides where larger models
are chaotic. The frozen prompt-blocked test compares Qwen2.5-7B and -14B on ten system prompts and openers (two for
development, eight held out), over $\log_{10}T\in[-0.4,0.9]$ (48 temperatures) and then over the temperature interval
where each model's next-token entropy rises most (24 temperatures), with twins at offsets $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-7}$; a
temperature counts as chaotic only when both exponents are positive (Figure~\ref{fig:prefixscale}). It predicted that
the 14B model would be less chaotic. Both predictions fail: on all eight held-out prompts the 14B model is chaotic at
more of the 48 temperatures (9 to 17, against 0 to 7 for the 7B), and its largest exponent is lower on only three.
Every chaotic temperature of the 14B model, on all ten prompts, lies between $\log_{10}T=0.36$ and $0.90$, at and
above the temperature where its next-token distribution spreads ($0.35$--$0.65$); the 7B model's chaos lies mostly
between $0.21$ and $0.43$, around its own, lower transition ($0.21$--$0.46$). On the paper's prompt, 12 of the 48
coarse temperatures of the 14B model are chaotic, from $0.59$ to $0.90$, and the adaptive fine stage found one more
near $0.519$; the initial sweep of Table~\ref{tab:scale} ended at $0.6$ and saw only the lower edge (the table now
also includes the upper sweep). Within Qwen2.5, then, the larger model is not less chaotic: its distribution stays
sharp up to higher temperature, and its chaos sits there. The decline in the fixed window is largely a shift. A
second frozen test (\repo{docs/prefix-scale-olmo.md}; stage 1 only, four held-out prompts) asked whether the OLMo-2
order survives other prompts. It does, with small counts: OLMo-2-32B is chaotic at more of the 48 temperatures than
OLMo-2-7B on three of the four prompts (4, 5, 3 and 0 against 3, 0, 1 and 3), as predicted, and its chaos again lies
somewhat higher ($\log_{10}T=0.10$--$0.35$ against $-0.01$--$0.21$).
