\paragraph{What sets it: the gain of the loop.} The gain split of Section~\ref{sec:theory} shows which factor removes or
keeps the chaos (Figure~\ref{fig:gainscale}). We compare medians of the two factors in bins of the mean next-token
entropy on the orbit; the bins hold 1 to 17 of each model's 24 temperatures, so single medians are noisy. Within one
training recipe, from Llama-3.1-8B to Llama-3.1-70B, the readout factor changes by at most 0.3 per step in every bin
above one nat (below one nat the 8B model has a single temperature), while the network factor is lower by 0.5 to 1.3
per step between 1 and 10 nats (6--10 nats: median $-0.98$ from 3 temperatures against $+0.28$ from 4) and by only
0.18 above 10 nats: in these two checkpoints the larger model damps changes of its fed-back token. Llama-3.3-70B, which
differs from Llama-3.1-70B in post-training, has a much lower readout factor below 3 nats (1--3 nats: $-2.33$ from 7
temperatures against $-0.10$ from 6) and a similar one above 6 nats. In Qwen2.5, both factors of the 14B and 32B
models lie below the 7B's in every bin between 1 and 10 nats, as the frozen prediction P3 states (the 6--10 nat bin
only with the 7B's wider gain split, from one temperature), and both factors of
the 72B lie below the 7B's in every bin. Above 10 nats, where the distribution is nearly uniform, the 14B and 32B
models instead have the larger network factor ($+2.98$ and $+1.17$ against $+0.19$), and those are the bins of their
chaotic temperatures above $\log_{10}T=0.3$. Below 3 nats the readout factor dominates at 32B and 72B, because the
next-token distribution stays sharp up to high temperature (for the 72B, entropy at most 0.15 nats up to $T=2$). In
OLMo-2, P3 fails, and the readout factor tracks the chaos: OLMo-2-13B, with no chaotic temperature, has a lower
readout factor than OLMo-2-7B in all three bins between 1 and 10 nats ($-1.0$ to $-1.4$ against $+0.06$ to $-0.72$
per step) although its network factor is higher in two of them, whereas OLMo-2-32B, the most chaotic OLMo model, has a
higher readout factor than the 7B in all three ($+0.10$ to $+0.35$), positive as in the chaotic 7--8B models. These
are descriptive comparisons of trained checkpoints, not interventions on size or post-training: the split is a
decomposition along the evolving tangent direction, and equal entropy does not imply equal embedding covariance.
Entropy clamping tests at 72B whether forcing an intermediate entropy suffices to destabilize the loop. If its loop
were stable only because its distribution jumps from sharp to flat, holding the distribution in between should
destabilize it. We therefore choose each step's
temperature so that the next-token entropy equals a target and sweep the target instead of the temperature
(entropy-clamped decoding; the map stays deterministic, and the entropy holds to $10^{-14}$). The temperature then
depends on the state, $T=\tau(x)$ with $H(x,\tau(x))=H_\star$ for the entropy $H$, so the derivative of the clamped
map $\hat F(x)=F(x,\tau(x))$ gains a rank-one term, $D\hat F=D_xF-\partial_TF\,D_xH/\partial_TH$. We therefore take
its exponents from twins of the clamped map itself, temperature solve included, and use no fixed-temperature
derivative or gain split for it. The safeguarded solve is only piecewise smooth, but on these orbits one-step central
differences at $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-7}$ agree with those at $10^{-4}$ within $6\times10^{-6}$. Held at any of 16 targets
between 1 and 11 nats, Qwen2.5-72B has only periodic orbits, the echo of period 2 on the paper's prompt and periods 1
to 19 on a held-out prompt, with twin exponents between $-0.08$ and $-4.5$ that agree within 0.004 across offsets
$10^{-5}$ to $10^{-7}$, whereas Qwen2.5-0.5B under the same clamp is chaotic at 6.1 nats. The clamped 72B loop thus
stays stable even where its readout is spread. The clamp changes the map, so this is consistent with damping by the
network in the original map but does not identify it. The pretrained checkpoint is also mostly non-chaotic. A test
frozen before
its data (\repo{docs/prefix-scale-72b-base.md}) predicted that the pretrained base model of the same size would be
chaotic at three or more temperatures more than Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct on at least three of the four held-out prompts.
It fails on all four: the base model is chaotic at 1, 0, 0 and 0 of the 48 temperatures (and at 2 on the paper's
prompt; computed after the precision fix, with 500 transient and 1000 measured steps, half the Instruct model's
horizon), although its next-token distribution spreads over 2.5 to 5.5 times the temperature range (intermediate-entropy
breadth 0.28 to 0.61 decades against 0.11 to 0.22) and from lower temperatures on. At 72B, then, the sharpness that
post-training adds does not explain the stability, and post-training is not necessary for it; the failed prediction
does not quantify post-training's other effects. The gain split points to the network's damping of the fed-back token
(the 72B's network factor lies below the 7B's in every entropy bin), and the breadth that orders the fifteen models (Figure~\ref{fig:scale}, right) fails for this pair. The damping holds when the whole
context is kept as well: in Soft Thinking's growing context (Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge}), an early perturbation grows at
none of eleven steered cells, six of them chaotic as one-token maps, and at one of 48 unsteered temperatures on two
prompts, transiently.

\paragraph{Where the network damps.} The network factor can be resolved through the stack. Let $h_l$ be the residual
stream at the soft token's position entering block $l+1$, and $\delta h_l$ its change. The factor then splits exactly
into three terms: the growth of the change through the stack, $A_L=\log(\lVert\delta h_L\rVert/\lVert\delta x\rVert)$;
minus the growth of the residual stream itself, $N_L=\log(\lVert h_L\rVert/\lVert x\rVert)$; plus the final
normalization. The difference $A_l-N_l$ is the log of the relative change after $l$ blocks (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}).

We measured this split along the unsteered loop of fourteen models at 24 temperatures each
(Figure~\ref{fig:damping}). At 0.3 to 6 nats, the entropies where the loop's chaos occurs at low temperature, the
median network factor falls with size in two of the three families.
\begin{itemize}
  \item In Qwen2.5 it falls from $-0.12$ at 0.5B to $-1.99$ at 72B, though not monotonically ($-1.80$ at 3B, $-0.85$ at
  7B).
  \item In Llama-3 it falls from $-0.14$ at 8B to $-1.50$ and $-1.30$ at 70B.
  \item In OLMo-2 it does not fall: $+0.11$, $-0.52$, $-0.79$ and $-0.36$ from 1B to 32B. OLMo-2-32B, the family's most
  chaotic model, is the least damped of its three larger models.
\end{itemize}
Qwen2.5-72B is the only Qwen2.5 model whose network factor stays negative at nearly uniform distributions: $-1.12$ above
10 nats, against $+0.22$ to $+2.97$ for the 1.5B to 32B models, as its stability under entropy clamping suggests.

The profiles through depth show where the difference arises (Figure~\ref{fig:damping}c). Each block adds its output
to the residual stream, so the stream grows from block to block, and the change that the fed-back token makes in it
grows too. Near 1 nat, the two grow at nearly the same rate in the small models: entering the last block, the log
relative change $A_l-N_l$ is $-0.32$ (Qwen2.5-0.5B) and $-0.51$ (Llama-3.1-8B), so the change has shrunk relative to
the state only by factors of 1.4 and 1.7. In the large ones the stream grows much faster: $A_l-N_l$ is $-2.51$
(Qwen2.5-72B) and $-2.08$ (Llama-3.1-70B), factors of 12 and 8. Their blocks keep writing a growing state that
depends little on the fed-back token.

Sublayer knockouts hold one attention or MLP output at the soft token at its unperturbed value. They find no component
that does the damping: at each model's knockout temperature nearest 1 nat (entropies 0.12 to 1.61 nats), removing any
single sublayer raises the network factor by at most 0.44 nats in any of the fourteen models. The amplification
moves instead.
\begin{itemize}
  \item In the Qwen2.5 models from 1.5B to 32B, the first attention layer amplifies the change: removing it lowers the
  factor by 1.67 to 3.74 nats near 1 nat.
  \item In the 0.5B model, the first MLP does (2.40).
  \item In Qwen2.5-72B and in both 70B Llama models, no sublayer contributes more than 0.46.
\end{itemize}
The larger models' damping is therefore not added by a component. Rather, the early amplification, by the first
attention layer or MLP, that makes the change grow about as fast as the residual stream in the smaller models is
weaker or absent. These are descriptive comparisons of trained checkpoints within
three families, and the OLMo-2 exception shows that training can break the trend.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-damping.png}
\caption{Where the network damps a change of the fed-back token. Unsteered loop on the paper's prompt, 24
temperatures per model, float64; a twin at relative offset $10^{-7}$ along the evolving tangent; 500 transient and 500
measured steps. (a) Network factor against the mean next-token entropy on the orbit, for fourteen models (Qwen2.5
blue, Llama-3 red, OLMo-2 gray; darker for larger models). (b) Median network factor between 0.3 and 6 nats against the
number of parameters; bars give the range of the medians in the bins 0--1, 1--3 and 3--6 nats. (c) The log relative
change $A_l-N_l$ after $l$ blocks against relative depth, at each model's temperature nearest 1 nat. (d) The two parts
of the final relative change at that temperature: the gap after the first block, $A_1-N_1$ (solid), and the deficit
from the second block on, $(N_L-N_2)-(A_L-A_2)$, drawn with a minus sign (dashed); the increment through the second
block itself is not shown. The profiles use block inputs, so the last value is the relative change entering the last
block.}
\label{fig:damping}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Mixtures of experts.} A mixture-of-experts layer sends each token to a few of many expert networks, those
with the largest router scores, so its map is smooth only between routing boundaries. Two questions follow: whether
routing switches drive the measured exponents, and whether a model that is large in total but small per token behaves
like a small or a large dense model (Figure~\ref{fig:moe}, Appendix~\ref{sec:scale}). We followed the unsteered loops
of Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (8 of 256 experts per layer; about 3B of its 35B parameters are active per token) and OLMoE-1B-7B
(8 of 64; about 1B of 7B). Along them, a twin at relative offset $10^{-7}$ never chose different experts from the base
orbit, at any step, layer or temperature. Freezing its routing to the base orbit's left the network factor unchanged,
so the exponents measure the smooth map. The orbits do pass close to routing boundaries. At each step we recorded the
smallest gap between the eighth and ninth largest router logits over all layers; its median along a loop lies between
$1.1\times10^{-4}$ and $1.75\times10^{-2}$, and its minimum is about $3.38\times10^{-7}$. Rounding errors of bfloat16
inference (relative precision about $4\times10^{-3}$) can cross gaps this small, so in low precision routing can add
jumps that the float64 exponents do not contain.

On size, the mixture of experts behaves like a large dense model, not a small one. Two dense models of its family share
its hybrid attention: Qwen3.5-4B, about its active size, and Qwen3.5-9B. Both are chaotic. Unsteered, they are chaotic
at 19 and 10 of 128 temperatures; steered, at 15\,\% and 7\,\% of a philosophy map and at 32\,\% and 16\,\% of a
hostility map. The mixture of experts is chaotic at none of the 128 temperatures and none of the philosophy cells, and
irregular at 5\,\% of the hostility cells. Its median network factor between 0.3 and 6 nats is $-1.90$, against $+0.05$
and $-1.41$ for the two dense models and $-1.73$ for the dense Qwen2.5-32B. Within this family, chaos falls and damping
grows from 4B to 9B to the mixture of experts, which damps more than a dense model with three times its active
parameters. Routing a token through about 3B parameters therefore does not make the loop behave like a 3B model.

\paragraph{What this implies for larger models.} Size does not set this chaos in a simple way. In Qwen2.5, the 14B
model's chaos did not vanish but moved to higher temperature, to where its next-token distribution spreads; in OLMo-2
the largest model was the most chaotic; two 70B Llama checkpoints with different post-training gave different results
on the same grid;
and at 70B, chaos that passes a three-offset test remains, rarer, with and without steering (Appendix~\ref{sec:size}). The shift does not
continue to Qwen2.5-72B. A prompt-blocked test frozen before its data (\repo{docs/prefix-scale-72b.md}) predicted
that the 72B model would be chaotic at fewer of the 48 temperatures than the 14B on at least three of four held-out
prompts. It is chaotic at 0, 1, 0 and 0 of them, against 14, 9, 11 and 15, so the prediction holds on all four. These
lines ran before the precision fix (1000 transient and 2000 measured steps, twins at $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-6}$, chaotic
only when both exponents are positive); no temperature on them is positive at only one offset, and the one chaotic
temperature ($\log_{10}T=0.319$, exponents 0.35 and 0.34) is chaotic again when recomputed in float64 throughout,
with an automatic-differentiation tangent exponent of 0.34. With the paper's prompt, where it showed no robust chaos up to
$\log_{10}T=1.2$, that makes five prompts on which the 72B model is nearly free of unsteered chaos. Its next-token distribution jumps from sharp to nearly uniform within less
than the grid spacing (0.03 decades), so a narrow chaotic band at the jump would be missed by the grid. Fine lines
through the jump, computed in float64 throughout with automatic-differentiation tangent exponents, find only periodic orbits on the
paper's prompt and on three of the four held-out prompts, and on the fourth a band of chaos about 0.02 decades wide
just above the jump ($\log_{10}T=0.319$--$0.336$; exponents up to 0.40, which twins at $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-7}$ reproduce
within 0.004), at nearly uniform distributions, as the 14B model's chaos but far narrower. A fixed temperature window is therefore the wrong yardstick, and so is size alone. A model's chaos follows the
gain of its loop where its own next-token distribution is spread, no single forward pass we tested predicts it (Appendix~\ref{sec:size}), and
to find it in a larger model one has to run the loop there, across prompts. For frontier models this means two
things: their unsteered chaos may be rare, if, like the largest models here, their distributions spread over a narrow
range of temperature; and steering still drives both 70B-class models that we steered, Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B, into chaos (Appendix~\ref{sec:size}).
Resolved through the stack, the damping grows with size in Qwen2.5 and Llama-3 because in the larger models the
change that the fed-back token makes in the residual stream grows much more slowly than the stream itself
(Figure~\ref{fig:damping}). If larger models of these families
continue the trend, their unsteered loops need stronger token competition to become chaotic, which steering can still
supply; this is an extrapolation from checkpoints up to 72B, and OLMo-2 does not follow it.
Figure~\ref{fig:z128} shows it at 70B parameters: in a $128\times128$ float64 scan of Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct steered
along its hostility direction, computed after the precision fix, 20.8\,\% of the 16{,}384 cells are chaotic, in a band
threaded by periodic windows, as in the 0.5B model's maps; a test of 24 sampled cells at three twin offsets and an
automatic-differentiation tangent confirms 11 of the 12 chaotic and 5 of the 6 periodic labels (Appendix~\ref{sec:size}).
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-zoom-llama70b-z128.png}
\caption{Chaos at 70B parameters: the soft-token map of Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct steered along its hostility concept
vector at layer 50, $128\times128$ float64 cells over $\alpha\in[0.06,0.12]$, $\log_{10}T\in[0.12,0.27]$ (300 transient
and 300 measured steps; sixteen bands of eight temperatures computed separately with identical vectors and joined).
Left: largest Lyapunov exponent, gray where the cell is not chaotic and in color where it is; 3410 of the 16{,}384
cells (20.8\,\%) are chaotic (largest exponent 0.64). Right: period of the attractor reached from the fixed start
(black: chaotic; bluish gray: periods without a color of their own; light gray: neither a detected period up
to 32 nor a positive exponent, 1.7\,\% of the cells). The
chaotic band's center falls from $\log_{10}T\approx0.23$ at $\alpha=0.06$ to $\approx0.19$ at $\alpha=0.12$, and periodic
windows thread it, most often of periods 3, 5, 6 and 7 and up to 13; fixed points lie above it.}
\label{fig:z128}
\end{figure}

\section{A second system: recurrent depth}
\label{sec:huginn}
Huginn-0125 iterates a recurrent block at fixed input tokens \cite{geiping2025recurrent}. For a GSM8K-style
\cite{cobbe2021gsm8k} prompt, the native loop converges to a fixed point whose leading Jacobian pair is
$0.588\pm0.771i$ (modulus $0.970$). To probe its stability we relax the iteration to $h\leftarrow(1-\lambda)h+\lambda F(h)$: $\lambda=1$ is the model's own
loop, $\lambda<1$ takes shorter steps and $\lambda>1$ overshoots, and at the fixed point the relaxed loop has
multipliers $1-\lambda+\lambda\mu$, so $\lambda$ acts as a bifurcation parameter we can set. For this prompt this
predicts that the pair crosses the unit circle at
$\lambda_c=2(1-\operatorname{Re}\mu)/|1-\mu|^2=1.0775$, transversally and away from the strong resonances (at
0.156 of a revolution per step), consistent with a Neimark--Sacker bifurcation. Below it the latent state
decays; above it the state settles on a closed curve. A fit of the squared amplitude gives $\lambda_c=1.0778$, with
$A/\sqrt{\lambda-\lambda_c}=0.52$--$0.54$. A stable circle that grows like the square root of the distance is
consistent with a supercritical Neimark--Sacker bifurcation; we did not compute its first Lyapunov coefficient, so its
nondegeneracy is not established. Causal attention makes the loop block lower-triangular in
token positions. The first position to oscillate is the ``.'' of the prompt, and all later positions follow. On the
invariant circle the motion turns by $0.156$ of a revolution per step, matching the argument of the critical pair.
The rotation number changes smoothly, by about $10^{-4}$ over $\lambda\in[1.079,1.092]$, and no return within 60
steps was found at $10^{-3}$ of the radius. This is consistent with quasi-periodic motion, although locking in narrow
high-order tongues cannot be excluded. From $\lambda\approx1.094$ the circle deforms or a second frequency appears,
near where a second complex pair destabilizes the fixed point ($\lambda=1.0814$). Fixed-point and Jacobian stability
of looped models has been studied before \cite{yang2026stars}; what this adds is the predicted crossing, its amplitude
law and the rotation number.

\paragraph{The spectrum predicts the relaxation response.} With the full instruction template, the GSM8K prompts'
fixed points look different: the leading multipliers surround the origin, a real $0.914$, complex pairs of modulus
$0.85$--$0.89$ at many angles and negative real values near $-0.8$, with no near-critical pair. Because the prefill
Jacobian is block lower-triangular in positions, its spectrum is the union of per-position blocks, and template
positions contribute identical eigenvalues to every prompt. Such a spectrum makes three predictions before any task is
scored. First, within the computed spectral estimates of these prompts, no fixed relaxation can speed convergence
much. With leading multipliers spread around the origin at many angles, any $\lambda$ that shrinks one group enlarges
another, and the best $\lambda^*=1.03$--$1.06$ lowers the rate from $0.914$ to only $0.908$--$0.911$. (A finite
computed spectrum does not show that no other polynomial scheme could do better; Anderson acceleration, tried below,
did not.) Second, under-relaxation slows convergence ($\lambda=0.55$: predicted $0.953$, measured $0.951$ on one
prompt). Third, over-relaxation destabilizes the negative modes first, at
$\lambda_c=2\operatorname{Re}(1-\mu)/|1-\mu|^2=1.074$--$1.126$ (five prompts), in a flip or a Neimark--Sacker
bifurcation close to $-1$. The approach to it is visible: on the prompt with $\lambda_c=1.074$, the measured rate at
$\lambda=1.05$ is $0.955$ against a predicted $0.956$ (native $0.864$). Elsewhere the spectral radius bounds the rate
measured over the accessible window from above, by up to $0.05$, because the slowest modes carry little amplitude
there. Independently, \citet{wang2026taps} report a Huginn GSM8K drop from $19.41\,\%$ to
$8.34\,\%$ at a constant step of $1.2$; at $0.8$ GSM8K is $19.79\,\%$, while their four-task mean falls slightly.
Their trajectory-based scheduling analysis does not compute the prompt-specific spectral thresholds studied here.

\paragraph{Does the task notice? (development data).} On ARC-Challenge \cite{clark2018arc} questions scored by
log-likelihood, where one prefill per answer choice is exactly the analyzed recurrence, the spectra again predict
convergence. Rates agree to $0.002$--$0.006$ where the slowest mode dominates, and beyond each question's $\lambda_c$
($1.075$--$1.149$ for four questions) the residual stops decaying. For 6 of 30 development questions the native fixed
point is itself unstable ($\rho(1)=1.009$--$1.273$; the fixed point, located with a relaxed transient, has a real
multiplier beyond $-1$ or a complex pair outside the circle). The native loop then oscillates (relative amplitude
$2\times10^{-2}$ for one of them), and relaxation to $0.55$ or $0.75$ restores convergence to $10^{-6}$. For the
other 24, no unstable mode was detected in the computed spectral estimates up to $\lambda=1.020$--$1.149$ (median
$1.095$); partial Ritz spectra cannot exclude an undetected unstable mode. Question by question, an
estimated spectral feature ranks the question's residual. This feature, $\hat\rho_q(\lambda)$, is the largest
$|1-\lambda+\lambda\mu|$ over the computed Ritz values of the question's gold-answer prefill, and its Spearman
correlation with the residual is $0.73$ at $\lambda=1$ and $0.75$ at $1.10$ over the 30 questions (development
data). In this historical analysis the residual was the all-choice aggregate entering the 32nd recurrence call, so
this is a predictive association across related maps and precisions, not a same-state contraction identity. It is
consistent with the latent orbits that \citet{geiping2025recurrent} observed on some tokens of harder questions, here
traced to the fixed point's spectrum. The item-level part of the frozen test below uses matched gold-row token IDs and
a per-row residual with an explicit clock.

Accuracy, however, tolerates the instability (300 training questions, 32 steps): it is $0.367$ native, $0.387$ at
$\lambda=1.10$ and $0.373$ at $1.15$, where the latent state oscillates with relative size $0.08$ and $0.23$. Only at
$1.25$ (size $0.68$) does it fall, to $0.300$. Yet the oscillation does reach the answers. Reading every step from 24
to 32 on 100 questions, the margin between the correct choice and its best rival moves by $0.11$ nats per step
natively, $0.37$ at $1.10$ and $0.95$ at $1.15$, in alternating directions, and the chosen answer changes within these
nine steps for 12\,\%, 16\,\% and 33\,\% of the questions. The changes go both ways, so the accuracy at any one step
stays the same on average. Under-relaxation instead costs 4--5 points at 8--16 steps and catches up by 32 (a slower
transient), and Anderson acceleration neither speeds the transient nor gains accuracy. Testing the accuracy
consequence therefore needs a task in which such step-to-step changes can compound.

GSM8K generation is such a task. With eight chain-of-thought examples in the lm-evaluation-harness format, greedy
decoding and 32 recurrence steps per token (development problems 0--119, every arm on the same problems and initial
states), accuracy is $0.375$ natively, $0.425$ at $\lambda=1.05$ and at $1.10$, $0.29$ at $1.15$ (17 problems lost and
7 gained, one-sided McNemar $p=0.03$) and $0.16$ at $1.20$ (29 lost, 3 gained, $p=10^{-6}$). Under-relaxation to
$0.8$ costs nothing at 32 steps ($0.41$). The break lies between $1.10$ and $1.15$, at or just above the spectral
thresholds ($\lambda_c=1.074$--$1.126$ for the GSM8K prompts; median upper stability limit $1.095$ for the ARC
development questions). Generation breaks earlier than log-likelihood scoring, consistent with answer changes that
compound over tokens. The collapse at $1.2$ is the one \citet{wang2026taps} reported.

A frozen test on problems not read before it was frozen confirms this. Its design and analysis were pushed before any test item was read,
after two rounds of independent review. On 200 GSM8K test problems, accuracy is $0.330$ natively, $0.395$ at
$\lambda=1.05$, $0.255$ at $1.15$ and $0.095$ at $1.20$. The two pre-stated predictions pass: accuracy declines at $1.20$ ($-23.5$
points; 50 problems lost and 3 gained, exact one-sided McNemar $p=3\times10^{-12}$), and $1.05$ loses no more than
four points (difference $+6.5$, one-sided 95\,\% lower bound $+2.5$). At $1.15$ accuracy falls by $7.5$ points
($p=0.01$, reported descriptively). Development and test agree closely (Figure~\ref{fig:huginn-gsm8k}).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-huginn-gsm8k.png}
\caption{Huginn-0125 chain-of-thought accuracy on GSM8K against the relaxation strength $\lambda$ (eight examples,
greedy decoding, 32 recurrence steps per token): development problems (blue) and the frozen test on held-out problems
(red; design and analysis fixed before any test problem was read). Gray: the spectral thresholds
$\lambda_c=1.074$--$1.126$ computed from the fixed points of five development prompts. Left: accuracy with Wilson
95\,\% intervals. Right: paired difference from the native loop with paired-bootstrap 95\,\% intervals.}
\label{fig:huginn-gsm8k}
\end{figure}
The same frozen design tested the item-level prediction on 64 held-out ARC-Challenge questions. The first 16
spectra preceded the read-out generation; the remaining ones were completed afterward under a recorded amendment, with
the producer attesting that read-out outcomes had not yet been inspected, and duplicate copies were resolved by a rule
fixed in advance. Every question passed the admission gates (fixed-point residual at most
$2.6\times10^{-6}$; identical gold-row token IDs in every arm); for 13 questions one of the three Arnoldi sets was
partial, which the design admits for an estimated feature. The pre-stated statistic, the mean of the within-arm
Spearman correlations between $\hat\rho_q(\lambda)$ and the gold row's residual entering the 32nd update, is $0.701$
(95\,\% bootstrap interval $[0.575,0.780]$; $0.68$, $0.73$, $0.73$ and $0.67$ at $\lambda=1$, $1.05$, $1.08$ and
$1.10$); the frozen primary gate, the interval's lower end above $0.3$, is met. On unseen questions, the fixed
point's estimated spectrum therefore ranks each question by the size of the gold row's residual entering update 32;
a residual is not in general a distance to the fixed point. Descriptively, the share of questions with a
detected unstable mode rises from $8\,\%$ natively to $34$, $69$ and $84\,\%$ at $\lambda=1.05$, $1.08$ and $1.10$.
An independent implementation reproduced both parts of the frozen test from the saved data.

\section{Two loops in conversation}
\label{sec:dialogue}
Two instances of a language model left to talk with each other drift into recurring states
\cite{ko2026attractor}. The best-known case is the ``spiritual bliss'' attractor of two Claude Opus 4 instances, whose
open-ended conversations end in repeated exchanges of gratitude and silence \cite{anthropic2025claude4}. The soft-token map poses the deterministic version of the
question: can two loops that are each chaotic on their own lock each other into a periodic orbit? Agents A and B are
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct with the conversation prompt of a sampled self-dialogue study that we report separately,
each with its own steering strength along the
philosophy direction at layer 12. They write one reply together, alternating soft tokens, $x_{n+1}=F_A(x_n)$ and
$x_{n+2}=F_B(x_{n+1})$, so one exchange is the map $G=F_B\circ F_A$. Equal agents reproduce the single loop bitwise in
float64, so on a map over $(\alpha_A,\alpha_B)$ the diagonal holds each agent writing alone
(Figure~\ref{fig:dialogue}).

At $\log_{10}T=-0.10$, over $\alpha_A,\alpha_B\in[0.060,0.082]$ ($48\times48$ cells), 27 of the 48 agents are chaotic
alone and 21 periodic. Of the 702 pairs whose two agents are both chaotic alone, 422 are periodic together; of the 420
pairs of two periodic agents, 91 are chaotic together. Writing together favors order (70\,\% of pairs are periodic,
against 44\,\% of single agents), though some chaos is inherited: pairs of chaotic agents are periodic less often than
pairs in general. The exponent map is plaid, as if each agent contributed its own stability, and where one agent is
weakly steered ($\alpha\lesssim0.064$) and the other is not, the pair settles into a fixed two-token exchange. Twelve
entrained pairs spread over the map all converge under Newton's method to attracting primitive orbits of $G$
(residuals at most $2\times10^{-13}$, leading multipliers $0.0008$ to $0.79$ in modulus, exchange periods 1, 3, 5 and
6). Their texts show how two chaotic writers share a loop. At period 1, A writes ``is'' and B ``it'', the single loop's
2-cycle split between two writers. At period 5, B answers ``is'' every time while A cycles through ``This'',
``there'', ``it'' and ``a''. That alternating two chaotic maps can produce order is known for quadratic maps
\cite{almeida2005chaos}; here it happens in a language model's own dynamics.

Two different models behave alike. Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B share a tokenizer, so a message can be passed as a
distribution over the shared vocabulary and embedded by each model with its own matrix. Unsteered, the 1.5B writing
with itself is chaotic at 41 of 131 temperatures ($\log_{10}T$ from $0.29$ to $0.80$, exponents $0.21$ to $1.76$ per
exchange), the 0.5B at one. Writing together they are periodic at 39 of those 41 temperatures, mostly in a fixed
two-token exchange: the stable partner sets the rhythm. A dialogue framing behaves differently. With each one-token
message placed in a user turn and the first token of the reply as the answer, every map we computed is periodic, for
one agent alone and for every pair. The reply is decided by the replier's own prompt and steering, and the partner's
message barely moves it; mutual locking of the kind seen in long conversations would need longer messages than this
frame provides.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-dialogue.png}
\caption{Two loops writing one text. (a) Agents A and B (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at layer 12,
strengths $\alpha_A$ and $\alpha_B$) alternate soft tokens at $\log_{10}T=-0.10$: period of the exchange map
$G=F_B\circ F_A$ per cell (black: chaotic). The dotted diagonal holds each agent alone; white crosses mark pairs that
are periodic although both agents alone are chaotic, and circles the twelve such pairs verified by Newton's method.
(b) The smaller twin exponent of $G$ per exchange on the same cells. (c) Two different models, unsteered: the smaller
twin exponent per exchange of Qwen2.5-0.5B and of Qwen2.5-1.5B each writing with itself, and of the two writing
together; dots mark periodic cells.}
\label{fig:dialogue}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Synchronization of coupled loops.} Alternating writers are one way to couple two loops. The classical way
to couple identical chaotic maps is to mix their outputs: each copy of the loop feeds back a fraction $1-c$ of its own
soft token and a fraction $c$ of the other's,
\begin{equation}
x_A'=(1-c)F(x_A)+cF(x_B),\qquad x_B'=(1-c)F(x_B)+cF(x_A).
\end{equation}
The state where both copies agree, $x_A=x_B$, is then invariant, and on it each copy runs the single loop. The difference $u=x_A-x_B$, which points across the synchronized state (transverse to it), evolves exactly as
$u'=(1-2c)\,[F(x_A)-F(x_B)]$, which near synchrony linearizes to $u'=(1-2c)\,DF\,u$. At each step the loop's
derivative stretches the difference, on average by the factor $e^{\lambda_1}$, where $\lambda_1$ is the leading
Lyapunov exponent of the single loop, and the mixing multiplies it by $1-2c$. A small difference therefore grows on
average at the rate $\lambda_\perp=\lambda_1+\ln|1-2c|$ per step, the transverse Lyapunov exponent. Without coupling, $\lambda_\perp=\lambda_1>0$ and the copies drift apart. As $c$ rises from 0 toward $1/2$, the
factor $1-2c$ falls from 1 toward 0, and $\lambda_\perp$ falls with it. It crosses zero at the critical coupling
\begin{equation}
c^*=\tfrac12\bigl(1-e^{-\lambda_1}\bigr),
\label{eq:cstar}
\end{equation}
where the mixing's contraction per step, $1-2c^*=e^{-\lambda_1}$, exactly cancels the loop's average stretching per
step, $e^{\lambda_1}$. For couplings between $c^*$ and $1/2$ (and, by the symmetry $c\to1-c$, up to $1-c^*$) typical transverse
perturbations decay along the synchronized orbit: the transverse-stability condition for our symmetric output coupling, derived above; it follows the
Lyapunov-stability framework of Fujisaka and Yamada \cite{fujisaka1983stability}, and \cite{pecora1990synchronization}
treats synchronization of chaotic systems more broadly. This local, typical-orbit condition need not imply synchronization from every start: other
invariant sets inside the attractor may be transversely unstable at the same coupling, and it says nothing about which
attractor a pair reaches. Near
$c^*$ the theory predicts on-off intermittency: long synchronized stretches broken by bursts, with stretch lengths
distributed as a power law of exponent $-3/2$ \cite{platt1993onoff,heagy1994characterization}. Above $c^*$, bursts can still occur, which is called attractor bubbling \cite{ashwin1994bubbling}: $c^*$ describes a
typical orbit, but a chaotic attractor also contains unstable periodic orbits, and along some of them the loop
stretches differences more than on average. While the synchronized pair moves near such an orbit, a tiny difference
can grow into a burst before the coupling pulls the copies back together.

We tested this on three chaotic cells of Qwen2.5-0.5B's loop (philosophy direction, layer 12, float64), with
$\lambda_1=0.098$, 0.437 and 0.196 per step over 3000 steps, so that $c^*=0.046$, 0.177 and 0.089
(Figure~\ref{fig:sync}; \repo{docs/sync/sync-summary.json}). Both copies are the loop of the rest of the paper,
with the same chat prefix, steering direction and cell. They run as two rows of one batch, and at every step the
program mixes the two new soft tokens as above before feeding them back. Copy~A started on the attractor after a
1000-step transient and copy~B at a relative distance $10^{-3}$ from it in a random direction. Pairs ran 3000 steps at
couplings from 0 to 0.30 in steps of 0.01 and 10,000 steps at eight couplings near each threshold, and a pair counted as
synchronized when $|x_A-x_B|/|x_A|$ stayed below $10^{-8}$ over its last 500 steps. For the transverse exponent, copy~B started at relative distance $10^{-7}$ from copy~A and was reset to that distance
after every step for 2000 steps. Far starts put copy~B 500 steps further along the attractor (2000 steps, couplings in
steps of 0.02). Other prefixes, models and directions, larger ensembles of starts, asymmetric or delayed coupling and
changes of precision were not tested. The difference's growth rate, measured directly on the coupled map at seven
couplings from 0 to 0.30 in each cell, approximately follows $\lambda_1+\ln|1-2c|$ from $+0.44$ to $-0.82$: the
largest discrepancy is 0.051 per step, up to four times the twins' own standard errors, which do not include the
uncertainty of $\lambda_1$. Whether pairs locked together depended on the protocol (Table~\ref{tab:sync}). In the first
cell the copies synchronized between $c=0.045$ and 0.05 in 10,000-step runs, consistent with $c^*$. In the second they
synchronized between 0.18 and 0.19 when ``synchronized'' means a difference below $10^{-6}$, consistent with $c^*$; on
the stricter criterion of $10^{-8}$ they had not synchronized by 0.19. Just below its predicted threshold, at $c=0.16$
to 0.17, the difference rose from below $10^{-8}$ to above $10^{-3}$ 12 to 14 times in 10,000 steps, and the synchronized stretches had a
length distribution whose rough log-binned slopes lay between about $-1.8$ and $-1.5$, close to $-3/2$; these
descriptive fits, from the few runs with enough stretches, do not establish a power law.
In the third cell the copies synchronized only well above $c^*$, between 0.15 and 0.16 in 3000-step runs and between
0.12 and 0.15 in 10,000-step runs on the $10^{-8}$ criterion (Table~\ref{tab:sync}). At $c=0.105$ to 0.115, where the
difference should shrink on average (predicted transverse exponents $-0.040$ to $-0.065$), it still burst from the
rounding level ($10^{-14}$) to above $10^{-3}$, 5 to 10 times in 10,000 steps, consistent with attractor bubbling; the
candidate transversely unstable orbits were not computed. No noise or mismatch was added. Two identical inputs in one
batch give outputs that differ by up to $9\times10^{-16}$ relative, a plausible seed of the bursts whose causal role was
not isolated. Synchronized copies write the same text (the most probable token at each step), and their texts sample
the single loop's attractor. Below $c^*$, many pairs
instead locked onto periodic orbits of the joint state (periods 6 to 12, verified to close within $2.2\times10^{-13}$),
on which the two copies write different or staggered texts. In the first cell, some starts far from the attractor synchronized onto a second attractor of the single loop, an
attracting 2-cycle ``it is'', and two far-start pairs at $c=0.14$ and 0.20, above that cell's $c^*$, settled on
periodic orbits of the joint state without synchronizing.

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Synchronization thresholds of the coupled copies for each protocol: the largest tested coupling that did not
synchronize and the smallest from which all larger tested couplings did, $(c_{\rm lo},c_{\rm hi}]$
(\repo{docs/sync/sync-summary.json}). Near starts: copy~B at relative distance $10^{-3}$ from copy~A; far starts:
copy~B 500 steps further along the attractor. Criterion: the relative difference stays below $10^{-8}$ or $10^{-6}$
throughout the last 500 steps. $^{*}$A smaller tested coupling also synchronized. $^{\dagger}$Synchronized pairs here include pairs locked onto a coexisting period-2 orbit of the single loop.}
\label{tab:sync}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
start, steps, criterion & cell 1 & cell 2 & cell 3 \\
\midrule
$\lambda_1$ (per step) & 0.098 & 0.437 & 0.196 \\
predicted $c^*$ & 0.046 & 0.177 & 0.089 \\
near, 3000, $10^{-8}$ & $(0.04,0.05]$ & $(0.22,0.23]^{*}$ & $(0.15,0.16]^{*}$ \\
near, 3000, $10^{-6}$ & $(0.04,0.05]$ & $(0.19,0.20]$ & $(0.13,0.14]$ \\
near, 10,000, $10^{-8}$ & $(0.045,0.05]$ & none up to 0.19 & $(0.12,0.15]$ \\
near, 10,000, $10^{-6}$ & $(0.045,0.05]$ & $(0.18,0.19]$ & $(0.12,0.15]^{*}$ \\
far, 2000, $10^{-8}$ & $(0.20,0.22]^{*\dagger}$ & $(0.18,0.20]$ & $(0.14,0.16]$ \\
far, 2000, $10^{-6}$ & $(0.20,0.22]^{*\dagger}$ & $(0.18,0.20]$ & $(0.10,0.12]$ \\

\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The number that measures the loop's chaos, its largest Lyapunov exponent, thus also sets how fast the difference
between two coupled copies grows: the measured growth rate approximately followed the prediction at every coupling,
within 0.051 per step. The predicted threshold for a stable synchronized state rises with the chaos, from 0.046 in the
weakest to 0.177 in the strongest, and in the common near-start protocol the observed thresholds rank the same way: the
more chaotic each copy is alone, the more of the other's output it must take in. The threshold concerns a typical
orbit, however, and synchrony in practice depends on the observation time, the tolerance, the start and the attractor
reached. Near the threshold the copies alternated between long synchronized stretches and bursts; in one cell bursts
from the rounding level continued above the threshold; and from some starts the pair locked onto a coexisting periodic
orbit. Weak coupling, instead of synchrony, often produced order, a periodic exchange on which the copies write
different texts.

The coupled-agents check of Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour} differs in one essential way: there the contexts grow, so the
two agents are not two copies of a fixed map, and at $c=0.25$ they barely synchronized although $c=0.25$ exceeds $c^*$
for all three cells here.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/fig-sync.png}
\caption{Synchronization of two coupled copies of the soft-token loop (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction,
layer 12, float64). (a) Mean relative difference between the copies in the second half of the run against the
coupling $c$, for three chaotic cells; dashed, the predicted threshold $c^*=(1-e^{-\lambda_1})/2$. (b) The difference's
growth rate measured on the coupled map against the prediction $\lambda_1+\ln|1-2c|$ (bars: two standard errors).
(c) On-off intermittency just below the threshold of the second cell ($c=0.165$). (d) Bursts above the predicted
threshold in the third cell ($c=0.115$), as in attractor bubbling.}
\label{fig:sync}
\end{figure}

\end{tabular}
\end{table*}
\paragraph{What theory allows, and what is new.} Chaos and periodic windows occur in many nonlinear feedback systems:
random recurrent networks become chaotic above a critical gain \cite{sompolinsky1988chaos,doyon1994bifurcations},
and periodic windows, shrimp-shaped domains and doubling cascades are found in low-dimensional maps and in
experiments \cite{feigenbaum1978quantitative,gallas1993structure,stoop2010real}. In our map temperature plays the role
of an inverse gain and the steering vector that of an input. The factorization of Section~\ref{sec:theory} makes this
correspondence concrete, but it remains an analogy, not a transfer of the random-network theorems
(Appendix~\ref{sec:theorymore}). These results motivate looking for such structure in a language model's feedback
map; they do not imply it. This paper shows that the models we studied realize it, with concrete texts as
attractors, so that a bifurcation changes what the model writes, and that several prospective quantitative
predictions succeeded; the failed all-row criteria and the unresolved cases are reported with them.
\paragraph{Is steering the origin of the chaos?} No. Without steering ($\alpha=0$) the map is the model's own feedback
map, and temperature alone gives chaos with embedded windows in most dense models we swept (in eleven of fourteen on
regular grids). Steering widens the chaotic regions, but not through the concept it names: in the 0.5B model
norm-matched random directions are as chaotic as the concept directions, and in Llama-3.1 8B sparse-autoencoder
feature, contrastive and random directions all are, with direction-dependent prevalence. What the direction selects is
the text of the attractors (Appendix~\ref{sec:origin}).

forms. The numbers are given in the text.}
\label{tab:signatures}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.15\linewidth}p{0.29\linewidth}p{0.38\linewidth}p{0.12\linewidth}@{}}

\paragraph{What is and is not claimed.} Table~\ref{tab:signatures} lists, for each bifurcation, crossing and
boundary named here, what it requires and what we show. The objects studied here are invariant sets and bifurcations of an exactly
defined smooth map built from a frozen model's own computation. The answer-level reproducibility tests use two Qwen2.5
models (0.5B and 7B), one task family and a soft-thinking pipeline without the top-$k$ filtering of the published
method, and their bfloat16 effect does not require the float64 map's chaos: in float64 no answer changed in the 0.5B
model, and 5\,\% changed in the 7B model, whose loop expands at the temperature tested (Section~\ref{sec:consequences}). The mechanism of
Section~\ref{sec:theory} is measured along three directions in a 0.5B model and one in a 7B model, and at high
temperature the network factor can carry the instability. The loop's regime does not mark the accuracy of ordinary
sampled answers beyond the model's first-token uncertainty, and when slight steering moved a prompt's loop into chaos no change of that accuracy was detected ($+0.36$ points,
$[-0.36,1.08]$; Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour}). We do not claim that ordinary sampled generation is
chaotic in this sense (Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge}), nor that any window is a certified shrimp. The period-5 window is a numerically verified shrimp
(Section~\ref{sec:organization}): its attractor, followed cell by cell, fills a body with legs and doubling shells bounded
by continued fold and flip curves with located cusps, with a closed trace-zero curve and two dovetails; none of it is
proved, no two-dimensional reduction of its dynamics is established, and its head is not that of a one-dimensional
shrimp. The period-6 and period-7 windows have fold-and-flip skeletons with
located cusps, and in the hostile period-7 body the candidate superstable arcs (sign changes of the leading real
multiplier) avoid each other where a head would be. Symbolic
sequences are determined for the period-6 window only (paragraph ``Symbolic dynamics''); we have not traced tails,
tested for a periodicity hub \cite{vitolo2011hubs,jones2012rossler}, or shown that a two-dimensional map reproduces the
windows' nonlinear dynamics. The cusps are float64
computations validated by step-size checks, not proofs (Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}).

\paragraph{Limitations.} The detailed orbit studies cover Qwen2.5 at 0.5B and 1.5B, with three float64 orbits at 7B;
OLMo-2 7B and Llama-3.1 8B have maps but no continued orbits. The size comparison rests on regular temperature grids and mostly one twin
offset per label, and part of the 70B-class maps predate a precision fix and were rechecked only in samples. The maps
use one prompt, apart from 22 variants of the 0.5B standard plane (more in the size tests), and steering uses contrastive vectors, checked against random and
sparse-autoencoder directions in two models. Chaos labels are finite-horizon estimates: a longer horizon check kept 86
and 92 of 96 chaotic labels in two maps, near the accumulation of a cascade transient chaos makes labels less reliable (on one line only 135 of 235 chaotic labels held), the chaotic cells are numerically validated (twin exponents agree with automatic-differentiation tangents, across precisions and horizons) but not yet established by a computer-assisted proof, and three periodic orbits have such proofs: the period-8 orbit and a coexisting period-2 and period-3 pair. The Feigenbaum ratios are
finite-order values. All orbit, bifurcation and chaos results concern the soft-token map, not sampled generation with
a growing context. Appendix~\ref{sec:limits} gives the details.

\paragraph{Steering strength is a bifurcation parameter.} In the soft-token map, small changes of the steering
strength switch the model between distinct repeating texts, chaos and coexisting attractors. Near a fold curve,
behavior becomes intermittent, with laminar phases whose duration the fold's normal form predicts in advance. This argues for treating
an activation-steering coefficient in a continuous-feedback loop like any control parameter of a nonlinear system; whether the same holds for ordinary generation is not tested here. Its bifurcation set should be
characterized before an operating point is chosen, history dependence should be expected near boundaries, and a
monotone, smooth dose response should not be assumed. In a test fixed before its data, the history of a steering
schedule did change a decision with right and wrong answers: after a brief excursion to stronger permissive steering
and back, Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, thinking in soft tokens, wrongly allowed 13 percentage points more of the prohibited
access requests at the same final strength (Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour}). There the memory most plausibly lies in
the growing context, not in coexisting attractors of a fixed-size state. The block at which a vector enters matters as much as its
strength. In a 0.5B and a 70B model, earlier injection gives more chaos and brings it to lower temperature, and two
vectors at two blocks create chaos where neither does alone (Section~\ref{sec:consequences}). Outputs that fall into verbatim repetition at high steering
doses are naturally treated as quality failures. In the present framework such loops are candidate attracting
periodic orbits, and the dose at which they appear is a boundary that can be mapped like the others. The
deterministic map is where such sets can be defined precisely and computed numerically. Whether its boundaries
predict boundaries of sampled behavior is open. The bridge of Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge} shows that sampling noise
shortens the laminar phases of the bottleneck, by an amount that depends on how many samples are averaged.

\paragraph{What this suggests for interpretability and alignment.} Our results come from open models of 0.5B to
72B parameters and the soft-token loop, but they suggest three things about models whose outputs feed back into their inputs, as in
self-dialogue, agent loops and pipelines of model calls. First, where the loop is chaotic, a perturbation of
$10^{-6}$ grows to order one, while the loop stays in that regime, within roughly 15 to 50 steps at the largest exponents we measured (those of the
unsteered 7B and 8B models, 0.30 to 0.88 per step), and more slowly at smaller ones. Even a complete mechanistic account of what a single forward pass does would then not predict a long trajectory:
that would require knowing the state to far more digits than any interpretability description, which summarizes the
state by a few features, can supply. Stepwise summaries can be
inconclusive: every step of the period-8 orbit expands some direction while the cycle contracts, so the ordered
product of the step Jacobians, not a summary of each step, captures the loop's stability
(Section~\ref{sec:theory}). What can remain predictable are the invariant sets, their
statistics and the boundaries between them. This extends the analysis of trained recurrent networks through their
fixed points \cite{sussillo2013opening,maheswaranathan2019reverse} to the periodic orbits, chaos and bifurcations of a
pretrained transformer. Second, feature dictionaries say which features an attractor occupies, but the boundaries
between attractors, which decide what a small change does, are properties of the map's derivatives. That would hold even if most units have one interpretable job, as a recent
essay argues for Qwen3-32B \cite{cammarata_clean}; in its pruning tests, too, the answers that change are mostly
those the network was already unsure of, much as the loop's chaotic cells are those where next tokens compete. Single neurons are no exception: in the 0.5B model, steering
along the output direction of the neuron most specific to philosophy makes 9.9\,\% of the map chaotic, its index
neighbor 5.1\,\% and a random neuron 12.9\,\%, against 6.8 to 12.5\,\% along the concept and Gaussian directions
(Appendix~\ref{sec:origin}). In our 8B controls,
a sparse-autoencoder feature direction, contrastive and random directions all drove the map into chaos (27 to 39\,\%
of cells); at 70B the hostility-related directions gave more chaotic cells than the random ones (4.3 and 3.5\,\%
against 1.1 and 0.2\,\%, two-GPU maps from before the precision fix; Appendix~\ref{sec:size}). Along recorded orbits, the growth that steering adds comes mainly from the readout, where next tokens compete
(Section~\ref{sec:theory}); this is not specific to concept-labeled directions, but a direction's geometry and its
meaning can be correlated. Text can
also hide the dynamics: the emitted text records only the most probable token, and in the hostile cascade a
seven-token text repeats exactly while the probabilities that drive the loop do not repeat over the run and the state
underneath is chaotic (Figure~\ref{fig:hiddenbands}), so a monitor that reads only the emitted text would call a chaotic regime
periodic.
Third, conclusions drawn at one operating point need not transfer: which loop a
model enters depends on how much context it keeps (Table~\ref{tab:memory}), on the path by which a boundary is
approached, and on the sampling noise (Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge}). Treating a looped model as a dynamical system, with
regimes and boundaries to be mapped, rather than as a function to be evaluated point by point, seems to us the
appropriate stance for both interpretability and safety evaluation. The reproducibility tests of
Section~\ref{sec:consequences} make this concrete: in a chaotic regime the batch a request happens to share can change,
after a predictable number of steps, what the loop writes, while in a periodic regime it almost never does (in the
0.5B model, the text changed in 173 of 192 chaotic loops and in 7 of 192 periodic ones). In low precision, continuous feedback makes even short-budget
answers depend on the batch, far more often than greedy decoding does; over a long run in a pipeline that answers poorly, discrete feedback does too. These findings motivate testing whether interventions in continuous-feedback
systems, including steering for safety, alter finite-horizon sensitivity or task-relevant decisions as well as
immediate outputs; such tests would complement research on how latent-reasoning architectures affect chain-of-thought
monitorability \cite{korbak2025monitorability}. We tested no safety property: dynamical instability is neither
misalignment nor evidence of it.

\paragraph{Other inference loops.} Any inference scheme that feeds a network's output or internal state back as its
input defines an iterated map, but feedback by itself implies neither convergence nor chaos. The energy-descent
argument for the isolated tied readout (Section~\ref{sec:theory}) does not establish convergence of either complete
feedback operator studied here. In the soft-token loop we find fixed points, cycles and chaos. In the recurrent-depth
model Huginn (Section~\ref{sec:huginn}) the loop's derivative is the block's Jacobian, without the readout factor;
there we observe convergence and oscillatory instability, predicted from the block's multipliers, and found no chaotic
regime. These results motivate evaluating the dynamics of looped and recurrent-depth language models
\cite{zhu2025ouro,bae2025mor} jointly with their number of iterations; they do not establish a general relation
between stability and reasoning accuracy, especially at the short horizons of deployed loops, where no attractor need
be reached. Agentic loops that sample discrete tokens and tool outputs have no derivative at all; only the
zero-temperature limit and the sensitivity measurements of this paper bear on them.

\paragraph{Is the chaos useful?} The chaos we map is not a source of new content. The chaotic cells of
Table~\ref{tab:transcripts} recombine the phrases of the neighboring loops in an order that did not settle over the recorded horizon: the
trajectory wanders among the ghosts of nearby periodic orbits, which gives variety in sequence but not in substance.
The periodic loops are the familiar degeneration of decoding into repetition
\cite{holtzman2019degeneration,xu2022loop}. Neither regime is good text, and the diversity of ordinary generation
comes from sampling and a growing context, which wash out much of this structure (Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge}). What
the chaos does matter for is predictability and control. Positive finite-horizon exponents limit open-loop
prediction of a trajectory at fixed state precision (our experiments do not establish mixing or limits on feedback
stabilization); a stable attractor is predictable, but it can also hold a model in a state it should leave, such as the hostile loops of Section~\ref{sec:hostile}. Whether regimes
near the boundary between order and chaos are especially useful, as has been argued for computation in dynamical
systems and recurrent networks \cite{langton1990computation,bertschinger2004realtime} and shown for signal propagation
in deep networks at initialization \cite{poole2016exponential,schoenholz2017deep}, is open for language models. We
tested three versions, and none shows an advantage at the edge. Prompts whose own loop sits at the edge
($|\lambda|\le0.1$) are answered no better by ordinary sampling than prompts whose loop is deeply periodic or deeply
chaotic (Section~\ref{sec:consequences}). Used as a reservoir, the loop remembers a weak injected input best in short
stable cycles. Its linear memory capacity falls at every period doubling toward the onset of chaos, does not rise as
the exponent approaches zero within a period, and is nearly erased by chaos (Figure~\ref{fig:reservoir}). In Huginn,
whose reasoning is itself a latent loop, a float32 pilot on 40 development problems, separate from the tests of
Section~\ref{sec:huginn}, recorded the decay rate of every generated token's recurrence. Accuracy holds while that
recurrence is comfortably stable and falls as it approaches its edge: it was $0.475$ natively and $0.525$ at relaxation
$1.05$, where the token recurrence is nearly as stable as natively (median decay rate $0.921$ against $0.912$), and
fell to $0.375$ at $1.15$, where the recurrence is near critical ($0.981$, with about half of the tokens no longer
converging). With 40 problems these differences are indicative only.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-reservoir.png}
\caption{Memory capacity of the soft-token loop used as a reservoir (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction at
layer 12, float64). A weak random input enters the map, $x_{n+1}=F(x_n+\epsilon|x|u_nr)$, and linear readouts of the
state reconstruct the past inputs; the capacity is the sum of their held-out $R^2$ over 41 delays. (a) Along the
doubling cascade at $\log_{10}T=-0.148$: capacity for two input sizes, the input-free twin exponent (gray), the flips
(red) and the onset of chaos (dotted). (b) The 448 cells of the horizon check (192 chaotic, 192 periodic, 64 with neither label) against the map exponent, with
bin means and bootstrap 95\,\% intervals.}
\label{fig:reservoir}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Which findings might reach frontier models.} The parts that rest only on structure every smooth
softmax-readout language model with a fixed prefix shares should carry over: the factorization of the loop's
derivative, contraction at sufficiently high temperature, greedy token cycles at zero temperature wherever the most
probable token is unique, and the role of a
steering coefficient as a bifurcation parameter. The attractor texts, the positions of windows, folds and cusps, and
every number we report belong to particular weights; a second checkpoint of the same architecture already has
different attractors (Appendix~\ref{sec:scale}). Size gives no guarantee of stability (Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}), hard
routing in mixture-of-experts models falls outside the smooth theory of Section~\ref{sec:theory} (no expert switched
along our float64 orbits, but in low precision routing can), and deployed
inference samples tokens, in low precision, over long contexts; an 8-bit weight quantization that changes next-token
distributions by only 0.003 nats changes the attractor class or period of one start in seven
(Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour}). Closed models permit none of these computations, which
need the input embeddings, full next-token distributions and derivatives; for them the method and the questions transfer, not the numbers.

\paragraph{Conclusion.} A language model that reads its own soft output is a smooth dynamical system, and it behaves as
one. Temperature and steering carry it from repeating text through period-doubling cascades into chaos with a positive
Lyapunov exponent. Its periodic windows open and close in folds and flips whose normal forms, computed from the
network's own derivatives, forecast how long trajectories linger near a vanished orbit; its cascades approach
Feigenbaum's constant; and its parameter plane holds shrimp-shaped windows organized as in the low-dimensional maps
where such windows were first found. Two coupled copies drift apart at approximately the rate the loop's Lyapunov exponent predicts.
None of this rests on a simplified model of the network: the orbits, multipliers and normal forms are those of the full
transformer in float64, and three of its periodic orbits are proved to exist with interval arithmetic. The
factorization of the loop's derivative into temperature, the covariance of the next-token distribution and the
network's Jacobian holds for every smooth softmax-readout model read through a fixed prefix, and chaos with embedded
windows appears in all three model families we scanned. The organization is therefore not a quirk of one network,
although the attractors' texts and every number we report belong to particular weights. For models whose outputs feed
back into their inputs, Lyapunov exponents, continuation and normal forms are a working method, not an analogy.

\section*{Code and data availability}
The code, the designs of the tests fixed in advance with their audits, the run cards, the failed attempts and
hash-bound records of every computation are in the project repository, \url{https://github.com/tdj28/neural-shrimp};
the links in this paper point to its tag \texttt{arxiv-v1}. For the interval-arithmetic certificates the repository
holds the verification code, the exact map and input specifications, the release records and hashes of the native
evidence. The complete native evidence of both computer-assisted proofs (center definitions, interval enclosures, derivative
shards, audits and the failed attempts), with the verification code, release records and checksums, is archived at
Zenodo, \href{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23488416}{doi:10.5281/zenodo.23488416}. Bulky raw trajectories and model
states are kept outside the repository, which holds their checksums; they are available from the author.

\section*{Ethics and responsible use}
This study involved no human participants, private conversations or personal data. The access-control requests and
their users in Appendix~\ref{sec:behaviour} are synthetic, and the benchmark problems are public; institutional review
was therefore not applicable. The released models and benchmarks were used under the terms of their public licenses.
Some experiments steer models toward hostile or permissive behavior; they measure the stability of iterated inference
and release no harmful outputs. The results concern the auditability and control of iterative pipelines: near a
bifurcation, a small change of a steering strength or of the numerical environment can change what such a pipeline
does.

\section*{Use of AI assistance}
This work was carried out with AI coding and research agents (Anthropic's Claude Code and OpenAI's Codex) working
under the author's direction. The agents wrote and ran the code, executed the experiments, checked results against
the raw data and drafted text; other AI systems made independent read-only checks of the results and the
manuscript. Frozen designs, run cards, failed attempts, audits and hash-bound records of every run are kept in the
project repository. The author, whose doctoral research in nonlinear dynamics included work on a periodicity hub of the
R\"ossler attractor \cite{jones2012rossler}, directed the study, reviewed the work and takes full responsibility for
its content.
