\appendix
\section{Related work in detail}
\label{sec:related}
Language-model generation has been studied as a dynamical system at several levels, and
the closest studies differ from ours in what they compute. Lyapunov-like quantities have been used to measure
sensitivity to small perturbations in reasoning traces \cite{li2025quasilyapunov,jaca2026chaos} and in token rings
resampled by a model's own conditionals, with a test that separates the construction from the model
\cite{verazuniga2026selffeeding}, but these studies
measure chaos-like sensitivity in generation without computing orbits or bifurcations. Output statistics change
abruptly with sampling temperature, and several studies analyze the changes as phase transitions or criticality
\cite{arnold2024phase,nakaishi2024phase,sun2025phase,ruan2026criticality}. These analyses concern output statistics;
they are not bifurcation analyses of a defined map. Closest to ours in subject, a study of eleven models sorts sampled
continuations into classes from frozen and periodic to intermittent and noisy across temperature, fits a noisy effective map to one
hidden-state coordinate that predicts repetition, and steers along it \cite{johnson2026temperature}; that map is
fitted to sampled trajectories, and no orbits, multipliers or bifurcation curves of the model's own map are computed. Repetition loops are a known failure of
decoding \cite{holtzman2019degeneration,xu2022loop}, successive paraphrasing converges to attractor cycles
\cite{wang2025attractor}, and multi-turn conversations between models settle into attractor states
\cite{ko2026attractor}. These attractors are behavioral: they are observed in generated text rather than computed as
invariant sets of a defined map. Feeding a model its own output during training degrades it over generations
\cite{shumailov2024collapse}; we study the inference-time loop of a model with fixed weights. The attractors of
recurrent networks have been related to the difficulty of training them \cite{ribeiro2020attractors}, and fixed points
with their linearizations have long been used to reverse-engineer trained recurrent networks
\cite{sussillo2013opening,maheswaranathan2019reverse}. Equilibrium, looped and recurrent-depth models iterate a block to a fixed point or for
many steps, and their stability has been analyzed through Jacobians and fixed points
\cite{bai2019deq,bai2021stabilizing,geiping2025recurrent,zhu2025ouro,tomihari2025recurrent,yang2026stars,prairie2026parcae,buehlmaier2026layernorm,labovich2026stability,movahedi2026fixedpoint},
and recent analyses of looped reasoners follow their internal dynamics, their settling, marginal or drifting regimes
and slowly decaying oscillatory modes \cite{blayney2026mechanistic,viakhirev2026thinkshallow,park2026loopedearlier};
these studies examine fixed-point stability, finite-depth dynamics and nonstationary regimes. Here we continue
full-state periodic branches and classify their bifurcations in specified frozen-model feedback maps. A minimal recurrent self-attention with a repulsive value map
becomes chaotic \cite{gao2026repulsive}, but it is a constructed model, not a trained one. A separate line predicts the answers of looped models from the geometry of
score margins along completed finite-depth trajectories \cite{luo2026prediction}, without fixed points or Jacobians. The closest methodological precedent is a derivative-based fold analysis of a small task-trained
looped transformer, whose passage and spectral-gap predictions are unfitted \cite{kim2026phase}. That model was
trained for a task, whereas ours is a released pretrained language model, and our analysis also differs in its
objects: the fold of a primitive periodic orbit, the full-channel passage of a two-step map, and the accumulation law
of the windows, whose prefactor is set by the global reinjection. Expected-embedding
feedback itself is used in training \cite{zeng2025ponderlm}, solved for as equilibria in non-autoregressive decoding
\cite{zheng2023deqnar}, and, training-free, in pretrained models
\cite{zhang2025softthinking,wu2025singlethreaded}, next to continuous latent reasoning \cite{hao2024coconut}; Backour shows that the softmax readout and autoregressive feedback form a dynamical system that either amplifies
small differences until one component of a mixture dominates or contracts different mixtures together, with
pretrained rollouts mostly on the amplifying side \cite{backour2026mixture}; that analysis concerns the fate of
mixtures, while ours computes the invariant sets, bifurcations and normal forms of the full released map and
their consequences for reproducibility, and whether
they exploit the superposition the mixtures could carry has been questioned \cite{rizvimartel2026illusion}.
Newer methods vary what is fed back. CoTS keeps the sampled token and mixes in the expected embedding in
proportion to the competition between the two leading candidates \cite{yu2026cots}, the near-tie that makes our
loop's readout expand (Section~\ref{sec:theory}); since at least half of each input is the discrete token, our
orbits do not carry over to it directly. LaTER feeds back a linear projection of the final hidden state
\cite{li2026later}, and a trained projector lets a model roll out in a learned embedding space without its
output head \cite{koriagin2026nohead}. For such hidden-state loops the derivative of Section~\ref{sec:theory}
takes a different form, without the readout covariance, while the tools used here (orbits, multipliers,
continuation and normal forms) apply unchanged. Fixing
the prefix and the position turns that feedback into the autonomous map we analyze.

In neural systems more broadly, saddle-node ghosts produce quantitative delays in task-trained recurrent networks
\cite{dinc2026ghost}. Finite-order Feigenbaum ratios have been measured in neural rate and mass models
\cite{battaglia2007temporal,papasavvas2015gain} and, through period 128, in a transformer trained on logistic-map
trajectories \cite{liu2026learningchaos}. Learned networks reproduce period-doubling routes \cite{kim2021teaching},
and so does gradient descent itself \cite{kalra2025universal}. Normal forms and continuation have been applied to
recurrent neural maps, including their quasi-periodic orbits \cite{marichal2015multiple}, and shrimp-shaped windows and periodicity staircases appear in
neuron models \cite{gomez2014universal,stenzinger2025shrimp,caixeta2025staircase}. Our second control parameter comes
from activation steering along contrastive directions \cite{zou2023representation,zhang2025steering}. The
dynamical-systems tools are standard: numerical continuation and normal forms
\cite{kuznetsov2023elements,govaerts2000numerical}, for maps in particular \cite{kuznetsov2019maps}, type-I intermittency \cite{pomeau1980intermittent}, shrimps and
hubs \cite{gallas1993structure,facanha2013endoskeletons,vitolo2011hubs,jones2012rossler}, and period-doubling universality
\cite{feigenbaum1978quantitative}.

\section{Methods}
\label{sec:methods}
\paragraph{Precision.} Every result on the soft-token map labeled float64 is computed in float64 arithmetic
throughout: embeddings, activations, attention keys and values (the prefix cache), normalizations (patched, since the
library computes RMS normalization in float32 by default), rotary tables, logits, the softmax and the fed-back token.
The released checkpoints store their weights in bfloat16, and a bfloat16 number is exactly a float64 number, so
converting the weights is exact. For large models the decoder's linear weights stay in their stored bfloat16 and are
converted to float64 at each use, which gives the same map as converting them once (bitwise on Qwen2.5-0.5B). The
designated float64 computations use the patched normalization and rotary paths; some diagnostic traces, such as the
step records of the gain split, are serialized in float32, as recorded in their artifacts. The dense checkpoints store bfloat16 weights (checked against their safetensors
metadata), and the mixture of experts stores some tensors in float32, which we restore exactly; float64 does not make
the weights more precise than stored. One exception concerns the 70B-class models, which are spread over two GPUs. In
the two-GPU runs made before the fix described here, the final RMS normalization was computed in float32: the hooks
that move tensors between the GPUs keep a layer's original code, so our float64 version of that layer was bypassed.
This makes the computed map rough at about $10^{-8}$ of the state (its fixed points wander by $10^{-8}$, against
$10^{-15}$ on one GPU) and can matter for exponents measured with perturbations of $10^{-6}$ and below. The affected
runs are the 70B-class sweeps and their plateau tests, the first Qwen2.5-72B prompt test, the 70B steering maps and
the first zoom, the gain splits of the 70B-class models, and the start-state test, which spread all fourteen models
over two GPUs; Appendix~\ref{sec:size} says which results we recomputed. Derivatives are automatic-differentiation tangents of
this float64 computation: free of finite-difference truncation, but computed in floating point. We call a result
exact only when it comes from interval or rational arithmetic, as in the proofs of Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence}.

\paragraph{Statistical units.} The cells of a parameter map are evaluations of one deterministic function at
neighboring parameters, not independent experiments, and neighboring cells are strongly correlated. Their counts and
shares therefore describe the area of a region on a chosen grid, not a prevalence over models or prompts. Comparisons
between chaotic and periodic cells use temperature rows as units: intervals come from bootstraps over rows, and
permutation $p$-values within rows are descriptive. Where a random sample of cells is drawn, its seed and sampling
frame are recorded with the run. In tests on problems the problem is the unit, paired across conditions; where
problems share a batch, intervals are clustered by batch, and McNemar tests, which treat problems as independent, are
reported alongside them. Batch-level randomization tests, which flip the sign of each batch's summed paired
difference, give the same conclusions for every contrast of the Soft Thinking and reasoning-model tests
(\repo{tools/cluster_tests.py}, \repo{docs/cluster/}). Soft Thinking's contrasts with replay, greedy decoding and
16 soft steps all have batch-level $p\le5\times10^{-6}$, the resolution of 200\,000 draws, over 77 batches. In
DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, soft thinking changes 7.4 points more answers than plain reasoning (batch level
$p=0.033$, against $0.029$ for McNemar's test, over 16 batches) and answers correctly 4.7 points less often (batch
level $p=0.063$, against $0.096$). Every interval names its resampling unit. A null result is stated together with the size of
effect its interval excludes; effects smaller than that remain compatible with the data.

\paragraph{Independent implementations.} Where an independent implementation reproduced a result, a second
implementation of the map (and, where stated, of the solver or the normal-form computation), written separately by the
second coding agent from the model's published architecture (\path{codex/crosscheck} in the repository), recomputed it.
The two implementations share the stored weights, the tokenizer and the specification of the map, not code; hardware and
library versions were not varied on purpose. The second implementation, a manual float64 forward pass of the stored
checkpoint that uses neither our tools nor the Transformers library, also reproduces the chaos: with the scans' protocol
(fixed start, 300 transient steps, twin offset $10^{-6}$), its 300-step exponents at two chaotic cells of
Figure~\ref{fig:cross512}, $(0.065201,-0.155998)$ and $(0.075475,-0.133454)$, are $0.098$ and $0.354$, against
$0.113$ and $0.359$ in the scan ($0.106$ and $0.332$ over 1000 steps), and at the certified period-8 orbit it gives
$-0.564$ per step over 1000 steps ($-0.579$ over 300), the value $\ln(0.0110)/8$ that the orbit's leading multiplier
predicts (\repo{tools/independent_chaos_check.py}).

\paragraph{Phase maps.} Each cell of a phase map is one parameter pair. Unless stated otherwise, we discard 300
transient steps and measure over the next 300. The largest Lyapunov exponent comes from a twin trajectory at
offset $10^{-6}$ that is renormalized to that offset after every step, Benettin's method with a finite-difference
tangent \cite{benettin1980lyapunovI,benettin1980lyapunovII}; it estimates over a finite horizon the exponent that the
multiplicative ergodic theorem defines as a limit \cite{oseledets1968multiplicative}. At the 448 random cells of the
horizon check (Appendix~\ref{sec:limits}), an automatic-differentiation tangent vector carried by forward-mode differentiation
reproduces the twin exponents at offsets $10^{-6}$ and $10^{-9}$ within 0.0041 (median 0.0004), with the same 178
chaotic labels, and at six chaotic cells the four leading exponents from QR iteration include exactly one positive one
(0.06 to 0.42; the next is $-0.64$ to $-1.10$). The period is the smallest $q\le32$ (48 in the later zooms) for which the last state returns
to within $10^{-8}\max(1,\lVert x\rVert)$ of the state $q$ steps earlier ($10^{-5}$ to $10^{-4}$ in float32 maps), and
the attractor is identified by its leading-token loop, up to rotation. We call a cell chaotic when this
finite-horizon exponent exceeds $10^{-3}$ and no period is found; the label is a finite-horizon diagnostic, not a
proof of chaos. The threshold matters little: raising it to $5\times10^{-3}$ lowers the chaotic share of the map of
Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} from 30.1 to 29.8\,\%. We call what the trajectory from a cell's fixed start settles on the cell's attractor. The label is
operational: it does not show that the set attracts initial conditions of positive measure, as Milnor's definition
requires \cite{milnor1985concept}, and a long chaotic transient can carry a chaotic label \cite{grebogi1983crises}. A
loop is at the edge of chaos when $|\lambda|\le0.1$. Maps draw the chaotic cells by this criterion: in color by their exponent where a map shows the exponent, in
black where it shows periods or attractor text. Two figures color every cell whose exponent exceeds $10^{-3}$, also
where a period is detected, as their captions state: the prompt maps (Figure~\ref{fig:prompts}) and the followed
domain of the period-5 window (Figure~\ref{fig:realshrimp}). Panels whose legend reads `Lyapunov $>$ 0.005' follow an older
convention that marks every cell whose exponent exceeds $0.005$, whether or not a period was found; on the five maps
we redrew with the criterion, the two differ in at most $0.56\,\%$ of a map's cells. A $256^2$ map takes about 100 minutes on one H100 GPU in float64. Every cell starts from the same
state, so where attractors coexist, a map shows the one whose basin contains that state. Labels are text, not
dynamical identities: one continuous orbit changes its label where two tokens tie for the maximum. We therefore call
two labeled regions different attractors only when the contraction rate jumps between them or when float64 orbits of
both exist at one parameter value.

\paragraph{Resolving the network factor through the stack.} Hooks read, at the soft token's position, the residual
stream entering each block, the outputs of its attention and MLP sublayers, and the final hidden state before and
after the final normalization. They do so for the base row and for a twin at relative offset $10^{-7}$ along the
evolving tangent direction. The split of the network factor into $A_L-N_L$ and the final normalization is exact.

In the pre-normalization architectures of Qwen2.5, Qwen3.5, Llama-3 and OLMoE, the residual stream adds the sublayer
outputs, $\delta h_L=\delta x+\sum_s\delta o_s$, and this sum closes to within $4\times10^{-7}$ relative in every
model and temperature. OLMo-2 normalizes each sublayer's output before adding it, so the sum does not close there (relative
errors of 0.9 to 150). For OLMo-2 we therefore use only quantities that do not depend on it: the factor itself, $A_l$,
$N_l$ and the knockouts.

A knockout holds one sublayer's output at the soft token at its value in the base row while the input carries the
twin's change, which blocks the change's path through that sublayer. The knockout rows reproduce the plain twin's
network factor within $5.1\times10^{-7}$.

For the mixtures of experts, a third row receives the twin's input but uses the base row's experts in every layer,
weighted by its own router probabilities, renormalized over them where the router renormalizes its top-8 weights
(Qwen3.5) and not where it does not (OLMoE). Each
step also records whether the twin's set of experts differs from the base row's in any layer, and the base row's
smallest gap between the eighth and ninth largest router logits over all layers. Qwen3.5's linear-attention layers
carry recurrent states, which are restored after every step, as in the soft-token loop.

\paragraph{False chaos and real chaos.} A twin exponent measures how fast the difference between two trajectories
grows, so anything that adds a difference of its own can imitate growth. Suppose that both rows receive independent
noise of relative size $\sigma$ at every step, from rounding or from the float32 normalization described above, so
that their difference receives noise of root-mean-square size $\sqrt2\,\sigma$. We use a simplified model: a
difference survives along one fixed leading direction, growing by a constant factor $e^{\lambda}$ per step, while the
other directions contract within a step; the noise component along the leading direction is neglected, and the norm
of the rest is replaced by its root-mean-square value. Let $c_t$ be the cosine of the angle between the renormalized
twin offset and the leading direction, and $r=\sqrt2\,\sigma/\varepsilon$ the noise relative to the offset
$\varepsilon$. Without these simplifications one step gives
$c_{t+1}^2=(e^{\lambda}c_t+\eta_\parallel)^2/[(e^{\lambda}c_t+\eta_\parallel)^2+\|\eta_\perp\|^2]$, where
$\eta_\parallel$ and $\eta_\perp$ are the components of the difference noise, in units of $\varepsilon$, along and
across the leading direction; the model sets $\eta_\parallel=0$ and $\|\eta_\perp\|=r$, so that
$c_{t+1}^2=e^{2\lambda}c_t^2/(e^{2\lambda}c_t^2+r^2)$. While $r<e^{\lambda}$, the angle settles at
$c^2=1-r^2e^{-2\lambda}$, and the twin grows by $e^{\lambda}$ per step. Otherwise $c\to0$, and the twin grows by $r$
per step, whatever the dynamics. In the model the twin therefore measures $\max\{\lambda,\log r\}$, and for the
network we use this as an approximation:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_{\mathrm{meas}}\approx\max\bigl\{\lambda,\ \log(\sqrt2\,\sigma/\varepsilon)\bigr\}.
  \label{eq:falsechaos}
\end{equation}
Above the offset $\sqrt2\,\sigma e^{-\lambda}$ the model gives the true exponent. Below it, a noise term takes over,
rising by one nat for every factor $e$ by which the offset shrinks. In the model, noise can thus make a contracting
orbit look chaotic when the offset lies below $\sqrt2\,\sigma$, but it cannot make a chaotic orbit look stable. That
one-sided conclusion holds only in the model: rounding can also suppress separation, and noise can change the sampled
dynamics or move a trajectory into another basin (below), so we compare offsets and tangent estimates rather than use
the approximation as a one-sided guarantee.

The model assumes a single fixed leading direction with constant amplification, contraction within one step and no
noise along the leading direction, so we test it in the network
(Figure~\ref{fig:falsechaos}). The test uses Qwen2.5-0.5B in float64 at three cells of the philosophy-steered map: a
chaotic cell, the weakly contracting period-3 loop of the coexistence example (Section~\ref{sec:maps}), and a strongly
contracting orbit; each runs 400 transient and 800 measured steps. In clean float64 the exponents do not depend on the
offset over eight decades: $+0.092$ to $+0.099$, $-0.123$ to $-0.128$, and $-0.843$ to $-0.862$ for offsets from
$10^{-12}$ to $10^{-4}$.

We then add Gaussian noise with $\sigma=10^{-10}$, $10^{-8}$ or $10^{-6}$ to every row's next soft token.
\begin{itemize}
  \item Where the noise dominates, all three cells give the same false exponent, which Eq.~\eqref{eq:falsechaos}
  matches within 0.006: for example $4.951$ to $4.953$ against $\log(100\sqrt2)=4.952$ at $\sigma=10^{-10}$ and
  $\varepsilon=10^{-12}$.
  \item Where the offset lies well above the crossover, the prediction matches within 0.047.
  \item In the crossover itself, two offsets per cell and noise level, the measured exponent lies above the prediction
  by up to 0.43, because noise injected one step earlier has not yet contracted.
  \item Noise lowered no chaotic estimate below 0.087 (clean 0.092 to 0.099). It lowered the contracting estimates by at
  most 0.048, which makes them look more stable, not less.
\end{itemize}
A free twin that is never renormalized shows the difference directly (Figure~\ref{fig:falsechaos}d). Started at
$10^{-12}$, the chaotic twin grows exponentially until its distance saturates near $10^{-1}$ after about 240 steps.
Under $\sigma=10^{-8}$, the period-3 twin instead jumps to a plateau at about $2\times10^{-8}$ (the model predicts
$3\times10^{-8}$) and stays there.

The float32 final normalization, computed as in the early two-GPU runs and without added noise, reproduces the defect.
\begin{itemize}
  \item At offsets from $10^{-10}$ to $10^{-8}$, both contracting orbits show positive exponents, up to 2.03 for the
  period-3 loop.
  \item At $10^{-12}$ and $10^{-11}$, rounding makes the twin and base rows identical in 454 to 771 of the 800 steps,
  leaving too few measurable steps to read.
  \item From $10^{-6}$ up, the contracting orbits read $-0.177$ to $-0.123$ and $-0.853$ to $-0.783$ (clean $-0.124$ and
  $-0.848$), and the chaotic one $0.059$ to $0.078$ (clean $0.094$).
\end{itemize}
The last shift is of another kind. The defect changes the map itself, by about $10^{-8}$ at every step, not only the
measurement, and the chaotic exponent of the changed map is lower, though still positive. The spurious Qwen2.5-72B
window of Appendix~\ref{sec:size} carries the signature of Eq.~\eqref{eq:falsechaos}. Its exponent was negative at
$10^{-5}$, near zero at $10^{-6}$ and positive at $10^{-7}$, and its orbits are periodic when computed in float64
throughout.

Hence the rule we apply: a chaotic label needs an exponent that does not depend on the offset. We check this with
three offsets, or with an automatic-differentiation tangent vector, which needs no offset. Two limits remain.
\begin{itemize}
  \item The argument concerns noise in a measured difference. Noise acting on the dynamics can change which attractor a
  trajectory follows, and in some maps it lowers a positive exponent, the phenomenon of noise-induced order
  \cite{matsumoto1983noise}; see also \cite{crutchfield1982fluctuations}. Small per-step errors can accumulate, or be
  amplified near weak stability and in nonnormal dynamics; a constant error $d$ moves the fixed point of
  $x'=\rho x+d$ by $d/(1-\rho)$, which grows without bound as $\rho\to1$. Whether such errors change the
  attractor depends on the response and on the basin geometry; the per-step error alone neither bounds the
  displacement nor excludes a basin switch.
  \item Far below the rounding resolution, two rows can become identical. A naive estimator would then report infinite
  contraction, a false negative; ours reports the step as unmeasurable. The offsets of our maps lie far above the
  resolution of their arithmetic.
\end{itemize}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-false-chaos.png}
\caption{False chaos and real chaos in a twin test (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, float64, philosophy direction at block 12;
400 transient and 800 measured steps). The twin exponent is the mean logarithmic growth of a second row renormalized
to the relative offset $\varepsilon$ after every step. (a) A chaotic cell ($\alpha=0.0652$, $\log_{10}T=-0.156$): clean
float64 (black) and Gaussian noise of relative size $\sigma$ added to every row's next soft token (colors); dashed,
Eq.~\eqref{eq:falsechaos} with the clean exponent. (b) The same for a weakly contracting period-3 loop ($\alpha=0.0642$,
$\log_{10}T=-0.156$; circles) and a strongly contracting orbit ($\alpha=0$, $\log_{10}T=0.18$; squares): below the offset
$\sqrt2\,\sigma$ both read as chaotic. (c) The float32 final normalization of the early two-GPU runs, without added
noise: false positive exponents near float32 resolution. Open markers mark offsets at which the twin and base rows
became identical in more than half of the steps; dotted lines mark the clean exponents. (d) A free twin, started at
$10^{-12}$ and never renormalized: real chaos grows; under noise the contracting loop's twin jumps to the noise level
and stays there; in clean float64 it stays near $10^{-14}$, the rounding floor of the map.}
\label{fig:falsechaos}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{How a fold or flip curve is found.} Every curve in this paper is found in the same five steps, all on the
full map in float64.
(i) \emph{An orbit.} At one cell inside a window the loop is iterated from the fixed start until it settles; the
period $p$ is the smallest return time of the full state, $\lVert F^p(x)-x\rVert$ small, and Newton's method on
$F^p(x)=x$ with forward-mode Jacobians through the network refines the orbit to residuals near $10^{-14}$.
(ii) \emph{A branch.} The orbit is continued in one parameter ($\log_{10}T$ at fixed $\alpha$, or $\alpha$ at fixed
$T$) by pseudo-arclength steps, and at every point all multipliers are computed from the monodromy matrix
$M=DF^p$. A fold shows up as a turning point of the branch, where the orbit meets a saddle and the branch turns back
(a multiplier passes $+1$); a flip shows up as a real multiplier crossing $-1$, where the orbit doubles.
(iii) \emph{A curve.} From the boundary point found on the branch, the bifurcation itself is continued in the plane of
the two parameters. The unknowns are the orbit point and the two parameters; the equations are $F^p(x)-x=0$ and one
scalar test function $g=0$, the last component of the solution of the bordered linear system with matrix
$[[M\mp I,v],[w^\top,0]]$, which vanishes exactly when $M$ has the multiplier $\pm1$ (upper sign: fold)
\cite{govaerts2000numerical,kuznetsov2023elements}. Newton's method with Jacobian-vector products through the network
corrects each pseudo-arclength step.
(iv) \emph{A gate at every point.} A point is drawn only if its residual is at most $10^{-10}$ and the critical
multiplier of the dense monodromy is real and within $10^{-7}$ of $\pm1$; rejected points are counted and never
drawn.
(v) \emph{Cusps and the check against the map.} Where a fold curve's direction in the plane reverses, a cusp candidate
is located by the sign change of the oriented quadratic coefficient of the fold normal form, and accepted only if the
cubic coefficient and the unfolding determinant are nonzero. Finally, where the attractor followed cell by cell ends,
a computed curve must be near (the boundary gates of the figures). For the 0.5B model one curve point takes about 50\,s
on one H100 GPU.

\paragraph{Periodic orbits.} We solve $F^p(x)=x$ by Newton's method with forward-mode Jacobians. The multipliers are
the eigenvalues of the monodromy matrix, the ordered product of the step Jacobians along the orbit, which plays the
role of the Floquet matrix of a flow \cite{kuznetsov2023elements}; where the dense matrix is not float64, we refine them by a short Arnoldi
factorization with float64 Jacobian-vector products.

\paragraph{Continuation.} Periodic branches are continued in one parameter by pseudo-arclength continuation, and fold
and flip curves in two parameters with minimally augmented systems \cite{govaerts2000numerical,kuznetsov2023elements}.
The test function $g$ of the bordered system $[[M\mp I,v],[w^\top,0]]$ is obtained by GMRES (generalized minimal
residual iterations) with Jacobian-vector products, and its gradient by reverse-mode differentiation through a
Jacobian-vector product. When the parameter tangent reverses, we flag a cusp candidate and test it with the normal
form. To locate a codimension-two point on a continued curve (\repo{tools/cusp_locate.py}), we bracket a sign
change between two curve points, then retrace the curve from the left end with steps of a quarter of the bracket.
We do this for three rounds and finish with a secant step. The function whose sign changes is the oriented quadratic
coefficient $b\operatorname{sign}a$ for a cusp, $|\mu_2|-1$ for a second multiplier $\mu_2$ leaving the unit
circle, and the projected parameter tangent for a turn of the curve. At a cusp we then evaluate $c$ and the unfolding
matrix at the located point. Elsewhere, the multipliers and the angle between their eigenvectors type the point: a
double multiplier whose eigenvectors coincide is a Jordan block. On maps with known answers the tool recovers a
cusp to $10^{-6}$ (with $c=-1$ and $\det K=1$) and a fold-flip point.

\paragraph{Normal form and passages.} Consider a fold of the two-step map $G=F^2$, with right and left eigenvectors
$r$ and $\ell$ normalized by $\|r\|=1$ and $\ell^\top r=1$. Along the critical direction the reduced map is
$u'=u+a\eps+bu^2$, with $a=\ell^\top\partial_\alpha G$ and $b=\tfrac12\ell^\top D^2G[r,r]$. We compute $b$ by
central differences of float64 Jacobian-vector products over a step-size plateau (later cross-checks use nested
automatic differentiation), and $a$ includes the steering's effect on the prefix. The signed coefficients are kept in
the original eigenvector gauge, with $\eps$ measured in the free parameter ($\alpha$ or $\log_{10}T$, whose units
apply to $a$). On maps with known normal forms the code recovers $ab$, the cubic coefficient $c$ with its
center-manifold term and the two-parameter unfolding matrix, and it traces the analytic fold and flip curves of the
H\'enon map (\texttt{tests/test\_bifurcation\_curves.py}). For passages we set $v=\operatorname{sign}(b)\,u$ and take the ghost side
$p-p_c=\operatorname{sign}(ab)\,\epsilon$ with $\epsilon>0$, so that $v'=v+|a|\epsilon+|b|v^2$ to quadratic order. A
full passage starts at $v=-U$ and a midpoint passage at $v=0$; both end at the first strict crossing $v>U$, with
$U=0.1/|b|$. We compare passage counts with the iterated quadratic map and with the arctangent formula
\cite{pomeau1980intermittent}.

\paragraph{Period-doubling cascade.} Along a cut in one parameter, we locate the flip of each period-$p$ orbit as the
root of $\operatorname{Re}\mu_c+1$, where $\mu_c$ is the multiplier with the smallest real part, using the Illinois
variant of regula falsi with warm-started orbits. All but one or two multipliers are small, so Newton's method on
$F^p(x)-x$ needs the monodromy only on a six-dimensional dominant subspace. We obtain that subspace by subspace
iteration with forward-mode Jacobian-vector products chained along the orbit, so no dense Jacobian is formed,
and the residual is always the exact float64 $F^p(x)-x$. Each root is classified before it is accepted. The critical
multiplier must be real and equal to $-1$, with a Ritz residual below $10^{-6}$, and the other computed Ritz values
(six in all, estimates of the dominant spectrum) must lie inside the unit circle. We then reach the period-$2p$ orbit
by forward iteration inside the next band. On the logistic map embedded in six dimensions, the procedure reproduces
the first four flip parameters to $10^{-9}$. This classifies the crossing, not the bifurcation: the cascade flips of
Section~\ref{sec:cascade} and the flip curves of Section~\ref{sec:organization} are located where a real multiplier
crosses $-1$ and confirmed by the doubled orbits beyond them. Flip coefficients were computed only at the two flips of
the high-temperature bubble (Table~\ref{tab:flipnf}), so the other flips are not classified as super- or subcritical. Nor were their crossing speeds computed: a generic
flip also needs a nonzero crossing speed of the multiplier and a nonzero cubic coefficient, so the cascade's flips are
identified by a simple real multiplier at $-1$ and the doubled orbit beyond each.

\FloatBarrier
\section{Theory background, network contraction and the dimension of the chaos}
\label{sec:theorymore}
\input{figures/tikz-reduction}
\paragraph{Why the structures of low-dimensional maps appear.} The map acts on a space of hundreds to thousands of
dimensions, yet two reductions make low-dimensional theory apply (Figure~\ref{fig:tworeductions}). Near a bifurcation of a periodic orbit the center
manifold theorem reduces the local dynamics to the critical directions, whatever the size of the other multipliers,
as long as they stay off the unit circle; the normal forms are the leading terms of this local reduction
\cite{kuznetsov2023elements}. Our normal forms of folds, flips, cusps and 1:1 resonances rest on this
reduction alone. A scalar description of a whole attractor needs additional, global evidence. The small non-critical
multipliers of the periodic orbits we computed show strong transverse contraction near those orbits and motivate that
description: they range from about $0.1$ at short periods to below $10^{-13}$ along the cascade
(Sections~\ref{sec:mechanism} and~\ref{sec:cascade} and Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}), and along the cascade at $\log_{10}T=-0.156$ the next modulus
falls from $0.12$ at the first flip to $0.045$, $7\times10^{-5}$, $4\times10^{-7}$ and $2\times10^{-13}$ at the next
four. Temperature, through the gain of \eqref{eq:gain}, and steering act as two parameters. One-dimensional families of
maps with a quadratic extremum can exhibit fold and flip curves, nondegenerate cusps, period-doubling cascades whose
interval ratios tend to Feigenbaum's $\delta$, and saddle-node windows that accumulate on a fold with
$d_N\propto N^{-2}$ (Figure~\ref{fig:atlas} sketches the objects we locate); we test these predictions on specific branches and parameter sequences in Sections~\ref{sec:mechanism} to~\ref{sec:organization}. The organization of shrimps needs
more, either a second turning point of the reduced map or a second dimension
\cite{gallas1993structure,jones2012rossler}. We test the consequences where we state them: the multipliers of each
orbit, the interval ratios, and the normal-form predictions of passage times and coexisting orbits. Where the second
multiplier is not small, as in the high-temperature bubble of Section~\ref{sec:cascade} (next moduli $0.28$ and
$0.38$) and along the period-5 curves of Section~\ref{sec:organization} (up to $0.39$), the global reduction is only a rough guide, while the local normal forms still apply.

\paragraph{Coexisting attractors constrain one-dimensional descriptions.} A classical theorem says what a
one-dimensional reduction cannot do. If an interval map is three times differentiable, unimodal and has negative
Schwarzian derivative, $Sf=f'''/f'-\tfrac32(f''/f')^2<0$, the immediate basin of every attracting periodic orbit
contains the turning point or an endpoint of the interval \cite{singer1978stable}; if basins reaching an endpoint are
excluded, at most one attracting periodic orbit remains. The soft-token map has coexisting attracting orbits in several
places. Near each located cusp with
$c<0$, two attracting orbits of the same period coexist with a saddle between them (periods 4 and 6, Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}; period 7, Section~\ref{sec:hostile}). Inside the hostile period-7 body, the period-7 loop
coexists with a fixed point, which the fixed start reaches at isolated cells. Such coexistence constrains a scalar
interpretation: unless one attractor's basin reaches an end of the scalar interval, the effective one-dimensional
dynamics cannot be a single unimodal map with negative Schwarzian derivative, and needs a second turning point, a
stretch of positive Schwarzian derivative, or a second dimension. A second turning point is also what a doubly
superstable shrimp center requires.

\input{figures/tikz-atlas}

\paragraph{What theory allows.} Chaos and periodic windows occur in many nonlinear feedback
systems. In a specified ensemble of random recurrent networks with asymmetric couplings, chaos sets in above a
critical gain, sharply in the large-network limit, and finite networks pass through limit cycles of increasing
complexity on the way \cite{sompolinsky1988chaos}; how the transition looks depends on the architecture and the
transfer function \cite{kadmon2015transition}. Discrete-time random networks, $x(t+1)=\tanh(gJx(t))$, iterate a map
as our loop does: their fixed point loses stability when the gain times the spectral radius of the couplings exceeds
one, the first bifurcation of large networks is a Neimark--Sacker bifurcation, and chaos follows by a quasi-periodic
route, through a torus and frequency locking, over a range of gain that narrows as the network grows
\cite{doyon1994bifurcations}. Our largest model narrows differently: Qwen2.5-72B passes from a confident echo to a
nearly uniform fixed point within 0.03 decades of temperature, and its chaos is confined to narrow bands, whereas in
random networks chaos is generic above the transition. External input can suppress that chaos
\cite{rajan2010stimulus}, and deep networks at initialization show an order-to-chaos transition in signal propagation
\cite{poole2016exponential,schoenholz2017deep}. In our map, temperature plays the role of an inverse gain and the
steering vector that of an input. Section~\ref{sec:theory} makes the correspondence concrete through an exact
chain-rule factorization, with the softmax derivative $(\operatorname{diag}p-pp^{\top})/T$ in the place of the slope
of the transfer function; the identification with the random-network transition remains an analogy, not a transfer
of that ensemble's theorem. Feigenbaum scaling holds for a class of one-dimensional maps with a quadratic maximum
\cite{feigenbaum1978quantitative}, and periodic windows, shrimp-shaped domains with their cusps and spirals, and
doubling cascades are found in many dissipative two-parameter systems
\cite{gallas1993structure,bonatto2008periodicity,vitolo2011hubs,facanha2013endoskeletons} and in experiments
\cite{stoop2010real}; intermittency near folds is classical \cite{pomeau1980intermittent}.

\paragraph{Contraction and expansion in the network.} Analyses of deep attention identify contracting tendencies:
pure self-attention collapses token representations toward a common vector with depth \cite{dong2021attention}, and
idealized attention dynamics form clusters \cite{geshkovski2023emergence}. Residual connections and MLPs counteract
the collapse \cite{dong2021attention}. Which tendency wins along an orbit is a property of the trained weights that
only computation settles. In every dense model we mapped, both outcomes occur: the same map has negative largest
exponents in some regions of its parameter plane and positive ones in others.

\paragraph{How many dimensions does the chaos have?} Near a periodic orbit, the multipliers measure how many
directions the dynamics keeps (Section~\ref{sec:theory}). For chaotic attractors the corresponding measure is the
Kaplan--Yorke dimension \cite{kaplan1979chaotic}, $D_{\mathrm{KY}}=j+\sum_{i\le j}\lambda_i/|\lambda_{j+1}|$ with $j$ the
largest index at which the partial sum of the ordered Lyapunov exponents is still nonnegative; with one positive
exponent and $\lambda_1+\lambda_2<0$, $D_{\mathrm{KY}}=1+\lambda_1/|\lambda_2|$. We estimated the four leading
exponents by QR iteration (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}; 1000 transient and 2000 measured steps, float64) at 35 completed
cells: 28 chosen by percentile of their largest exponent before any of their spectra were seen (of 30 requested), and
seven from the earlier horizon check, six chaotic and one periodic control. At this horizon 31 have a positive leading
estimate and none has two positive estimates. These are finite-time estimates, not asymptotic dimensions. They grow
with temperature. Along the hostile temperature line, where we fit a scalar return relation (Section~\ref{sec:hostile}), eight chaotic cells have $D_{\mathrm{KY}}=1.008$ to $1.16$, consistent with nearly
one-dimensional chaos. At fourteen chaotic cells of the
headline maps ($\log_{10}T$ from $-0.17$ to $-0.04$) it is $1.03$ to $1.65$, rising with $\lambda_1$. In the
high-temperature region ($\log_{10}T$ from $-0.02$ to $0.13$), nine chaotic cells have $D_{\mathrm{KY}}=1.37$ to
$2.02$; at two of them the sum of the first two finite-time estimates is nonnegative, which indicates noncontracting
two-dimensional area at this horizon, although only one estimated exponent is positive. A scalar return
map describes the low-temperature chaos well and the high-temperature chaos poorly, consistent with the large second
multipliers of the high-temperature bubble of Section~\ref{sec:cascade}. The number of competing tokens does not
set the dimension. The readout's
trace-to-leading-eigenvalue ratio, $\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_p/\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_p)$ averaged along the orbit (with
$\lambda_{\max}$ estimated by eight power iterations), counts the directions in which the readout amplifies comparably.
$D_{\mathrm{KY}}$ never exceeds it and rises with it within each group of cells (Spearman $0.72$ over 17 philosophy
cells and $0.69$ over 8 hostile cells, computed when the data came in and not independently reproduced; both are
confounded with temperature). Across groups it does not predict the dimension: the hostile cells have ratios of about
16 to 19 and next-token entropies of about 7 to 9 nats, yet $D_{\mathrm{KY}}\le1.16$. The readout supplies many competing directions; the network decides how many
of them expand.
\section{Behavioral tests in detail}
\label{sec:behaviour}
We looked for a behavioral signature of the regimes outside the loop. Two exploratory designs found no accuracy
signature that survives controls. First, ordinary sampled answers of Qwen2.5-0.5B under steering at the strengths of chaotic and periodic map
cells (30 matched pairs; arithmetic, open questions, short programs) showed no measurable accuracy contrast: at those
strengths, steering every position turns every reply into philosophy-flavoured text, so accuracy sits at the floor in
both zones (3 against 4 correct of 4500 arithmetic answers). Of nine secondary contrasts of entropy and reply length,
one (open questions, next-token entropy, $p=0.016$) passes $0.05$ uncorrected, not after a correction for nine tests,
and the entropy contrasts change sign across tasks. Second, labeling each of 200 prompts by the regime of its own unsteered loop in Qwen2.5-7B, prompts with
a chaotic loop were answered far less accurately (by 26 to 37 points at three temperatures). The chaotic labels fell
mostly on the harder task, and in a linear model with task, greedy correctness and the sampled answers' entropy, the
label's coefficient is $-3.7$ to $-4.6$ points (permutation $p$ from $0.07$ to $0.20$). These are associations: the
entropy is measured during sampling, so the adjustment does not identify a causal effect of the regime.

A frozen test addresses the second design. Its design, covariates and analysis were pushed before any data. It uses
4000 newly drawn products of two two-digit numbers and the unsteered Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $\log_{10}T=0.40$: 1484 prompts
have a chaotic loop and 2509 a periodic one (seven with other labels are excluded). Chaotic-loop prompts are again answered less accurately by ordinary
sampling, by 7.1 points (95\,\% interval $[4.8,9.3]$; 10 samples per prompt). The gap vanishes once seven difficulty
covariates, all fixed before sampling, enter the fit. Beyond them the label's coefficient is $+0.15$ points
($[-1.4,1.7]$, permutation $p=0.84$), and the label adds no held-out predictive value. The first-token entropy alone
absorbs nearly all of the gap (post hoc: $-0.8$ points remain). This fits the measurements of
Section~\ref{sec:theory}. The loop's chaotic cells have more competing next tokens than its periodic ones, and a model
unsure of its first token also answers worse. Beyond that uncertainty, no association of the loop's regime with accuracy is detected at
this precision; this does not show that the regime has no effect.

Within a prompt no association is detected either. We steered the first 1000 of these prompts slightly, at block 14
along philosophy, hostility-vs-kindness and a random direction. The strength, 1\,\% of the residual norm, is the largest
at which greedy accuracy stayed within 5 points of the unsteered model on the first 200 of these prompts (so the
calibration overlaps the test prompts); on the 1000 prompts it is 0.87 to 0.89, against 0.89 unsteered. Under these
pushes the loop's label differs between conditions for 508 of the 1000 prompts. With prompt and condition fixed
effects (3992 prompt-condition rows), in an analysis fixed before the data, a chaotic label is associated with a change
of $+0.36$ points in the accuracy of the prompt's ordinary sampled answers (95\,\% interval $[-0.36,1.08]$;
permutation within prompts, $p=0.34$). This does not identify a causal effect of entering chaos: the pushes can act on
the answers through other paths as well.

Nor does accuracy peak at the edge of chaos. Adjusted for the covariates, three groups of prompts (3603 of the 4000;
the rest have intermediate exponents) are answered alike:
\begin{itemize}
  \item deep periodic ($\lambda\le-0.3$, 1961 prompts): $+0.3$ points;
  \item edge ($|\lambda|\le0.1$, 242 prompts): $-0.1$ points;
  \item deep chaotic ($\lambda\ge0.3$, 1400 prompts): $+0.3$ points.
\end{itemize}
All three intervals lie within $[-3.1,3.0]$. The edge contrast is $-0.4$ points ($[-3.5,2.6]$), and the curvature in
$\lambda$ is positive, the opposite of an edge optimum. Post hoc, the curvature comes from the most strongly
contracting tenth of prompts ($\lambda\le-3.42$), answered 5.3 points ($[3.0,7.6]$) better than the covariates
predict. That is a lead for a future test, not a finding.

\paragraph{Sensitivity without control.} A loop whose answers change with rounding differences raises a further
question: can a deliberate perturbation of one fed-back token steer the answer to a chosen wrong value? An exploratory
test asked this of the Qwen2.5-0.5B soft-thinking pipeline on two-digit additions, in float32, with 16 soft steps, at
temperatures 0.3 and 1.0, on 16 development and 64 held-out problems. The target was
the correct sum plus or minus 10. A 20-step projected gradient ascent on the target's log-probability perturbed the first
soft token by one token norm, and random search over 45 directions with at least as much computation served as the
comparison. On the held-out problems, the early perturbations reached the target in 5 of 101 problem-temperature pairs
that the unperturbed pipeline answered correctly, and random search in none: a difference of 4.2 points (95\,\%
interval $[0.8,8.3]$), below the advantage of 10 points that the design required. The same optimization applied to the
last soft token before the answer cue reached the target in 14 of the 101, so in this pipeline the loop gives less
leverage early than late. Random early perturbations of that size changed about a third of the answers (one random direction: 34 of the 101
eligible pairs), almost never to
the target. Here the loop's sensitivity comes without selective control. The test covers temperatures at which the loop
contracts for most problems (on the development problems 10 and 38\,\% of twins expanded, with median rates of
$-0.131$ and $-0.058$ per step), where the pipeline still answers correctly, and the doses and the optimizer were
chosen on the development problems (\repo{docs/pi/c12-1-summary.json}).

\paragraph{Taming and using the sensitivity.} Two exploratory tests with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in bfloat16 (512
unused GSM8K training problems, 16 at each of 32 prompt lengths, fixed before any outcome; soft thinking with the top 15 tokens at $T=0.6$ for 64 steps) asked whether the theory gives a
remedy, and whether the sensitivity can be put to use. The factorization of Section~\ref{sec:theory} isolates
the readout gain, which along recorded orbits of the 0.5B model carries most of the growth that steering adds. Lowering a step's temperature until a proxy of the readout gain fell below a cap (the cap could not be met on about
1\,\% of steps, at exact ties) reduced the
share of answers that the batch layout changed from 27.7 to about 23\,\% (by 4.1 to 4.7 points at three caps), and
accuracy did not fall. Lowering the temperature to 0.5 at every step, a control added after the first result, gained
as much accuracy, and the caps' extra reduction of flips relative to it (2.7 to 3.3 points) was not significant. So
the remedy is modest and not shown to be specific to the readout. Second, a plurality vote over eight runs whose first
soft token was displaced by a relative $10^{-3}$ showed no detectable accuracy gain over one run ($+0.2$ points,
$[-1.0,1.4]$), although one displacement changed 20.2\,\% of the answers on average; sampled self-consistency with eight samples gained 5.6
points ($[3.5,8.1]$). The perturbed runs differ, but rarely toward the correct answer. Problems whose perturbed runs
disagreed were also the ones answered correctly least often (\repo{docs/pi/e2-summary.json},
\repo{docs/pi/e3-summary.json}).

\paragraph{Weight quantization and the attractors.} Deployed models are often quantized, and quantization is usually
checked one step at a time, by how close the quantized model's next-token distributions stay to the original's. An
exploratory test asked whether that check certifies the closed loop. We quantized every linear weight of the decoder
blocks of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, either in 8-bit blocks (as the Q8\_0 format of llama.cpp) or in symmetric 4-bit
blocks, and kept the arithmetic in float64. The unsteered loop then ran on 48 held-out prompts, at 8 temperatures over
$\log_{10}T\in[-0.5,0.5]$, from 2 starts each. A frozen random logit bias, scaled so that its one-step divergence
matches the 8-bit variant's, served as an unstructured control.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{8-bit.} The one-step distributions stay close: on the reference's attractor states the mean
  Kullback--Leibler divergence is 0.0028 nats, and the most probable token agrees in 94.5\,\% of states. The share
  of starts that end on a periodic orbit of period at most 8 shows no detectable change ($+1.2$ points, 95\,\% interval
  $[-1.9,4.2]$). Yet 14.1\,\% of the starts end in a different class or period, as do 15.0\,\% under the random
  bias of the same one-step size. These changes concentrate at the temperatures where the attractors change.
  \item \emph{4-bit.} The share rises by 6.8 points ($[2.6,11.0]$), mostly because the edge of a chaotic band
  turns periodic. Its one-step distributions, however, are far from the original's: the mean divergence is 0.83 nats,
  and the most probable token agrees in only 52\,\% of states.
\end{itemize}
In this model, then, a close one-step check was consistent with unchanged statistics of the closed loop but did not
fix the attractor that an
individual run reaches. We found no effect of quantization beyond that of unstructured noise of the same one-step size
(\repo{docs/pi/c12-2-summary.json}).

\paragraph{Chaos onset and repetition in sampled text.} Does the temperature at which a prompt's loop turns chaotic
predict the temperature at which ordinary sampled text stops repeating itself verbatim? An exploratory test with
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct on 24 held-out prompts did not detect the proposed positive association. The Spearman
correlation is $-0.23$ (95\,\% interval $[-0.60,0.25]$, permutation $p=0.28$). This is not evidence of no association.
One sensitivity analysis (orbits of period up to 32, clustered by system prompt) gives an interval that excludes zero
on the negative side, $[-0.62,-0.18]$. At matched temperature, the repetition rates of chaotic and periodic cells
differed by 0.003 (interval $[-0.013,0.023]$), with both regimes present at only 8 of the 24 temperatures. In 13 of
the 15 prompts where both onsets were observed, sampled text stopped repeating below the loop's chaos onset. The
median gap over these 15 is 0.42 decades of temperature. The soft-token loop and sampled decoding with a growing
context are different systems, and this test did not find a bridge between them
(\repo{docs/pi/e5-heldout-analysis.json}).

\paragraph{Coupled agents (behavioral check).} Two Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct agents solving the same multiplication
problem each fed back a fraction $1-c$ of their own concept token and $c$ of the other's, while their contexts grew
(64 problems, 8 pairs each; float32; exploratory). At $c=0.25$ wrong-answer agreement rose by only 1.6 points
($[-0.6,3.9]$) and accuracy fell by 5.5 points, as it did with replayed messages. At $c=0.5$ mixing gives the two agents
equal next inputs, although their growing contexts differ. They then agreed on 96\,\% of answers, including
41.0\,\% of pairs that gave the same wrong number, and accuracy was 12.5 points lower than for independent agents
(\repo{docs/pi/c12-3-summary.json}).

\paragraph{Steering history and a policy decision.} Coexisting attractors suggest that the history of a steering
schedule can matter, not only its present value. An exploratory test asked this of a decision with right and wrong
answers. Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, thinking in soft tokens, judged synthetic access-control requests against a supplied
permission table, answering ALLOW or DENY, while a permissive-versus-restrictive direction steered it at layer 18.
Every run ended with 32 steps at the same steering strength ($\alpha=0.025$). Some runs got there directly; others
first made a brief excursion to $\alpha=0.04$ and back. In the exploratory run, the excursion raised the share of
prohibited requests wrongly allowed from 49.5 to 70.8\,\%, a difference of 21.4 points (95\,\% interval over 24
held-out instances $[15.6,26.6]$; \repo{docs/pi/c12-4-summary.json}).

We then repeated the test as a confirmatory one. The design, the analysis and a new set of 48 instances (drawn from
a new pool of users) were fixed and pushed before any data (\repo{docs/pi-c12-4-confirmatory.md}), with the
prediction that the excursion would raise false allowing by at least 10 points, with an interval excluding zero. The
prediction held, with a smaller effect: 13.0 points ($[7.2,18.8]$ over 24 prohibited instances) after the 32 steps at
the final strength, and 15.1 points ($[10.4,19.8]$) after 64 steps. A second permissive direction, built the same way
from differently worded statements (cosine 0.77 with the first), gave 19.3 points ($[12.5,26.1]$). A restrictive
excursion raised false denials of permitted requests by 23.4 points ($[17.1,29.7]$). An excursion of the same size
along a random direction showed no detectable effect ($-3.1$ points, $[-8.9,2.1]$), and an excursion of zero size
changed none of the 384 decisions (\repo{docs/pi/c12-4c-summary.json}). The present steering strength therefore
does not describe the steered model's behavior here. The effect persists after the steering values match. Because what the loop wrote during the excursion stays in its
growing context, a memory of the context is the plausible mechanism, although these tests do not isolate it; this is
history dependence of the protocol, not a demonstration of hysteresis in a fixed-size state. One model and one schedule were tested, in float32, with two wordings of one
direction.

\paragraph{Does the hidden state change answers?} In band chaos the loop writes an exactly repeating text while its
state moves chaotically: the same token wins at each position, but the probabilities behind it keep changing
(Section~\ref{sec:hostile}). An exploratory test asked whether states with the same visible
history answer a follow-up question differently. We took 16 states at one phase of the text from each of 92 held-out
band-chaos cells; within a cell, all 16 had identical leading tokens over the preceding 70 steps. Each state was read
through the steered prefix, followed by one of three queries chosen on development cells: a yes-or-no question about
hostility, a one-word mood, and a hostility rating from 1 to 10. Each answer was decoded greedily. No pair of states
gave different answers in any cell for any query (a one-sided 95\,\% bound of 3.21\,\% of cells), although the
probability of a fixed answer token differed at six significant digits in 98 to 99\,\% of the pairs. The hidden
differences therefore reach the readout, but here they stay within the margins of the greedy choices, and only the
wording of the yes-or-no answer changed, in 9 of the 92 cells. The visible text is not a complete record of the state.
For these readouts that is a limit on what can be observed, not a change in behavior
(\repo{docs/pi/c12-5-summary.json}).

\section{Bifurcation curves and cusps}
\label{sec:curves}
\paragraph{Period-8 stripe.} Continued for 80 points with a float64 test function, the fold curve of the period-8
orbit keeps its critical multiplier within $1\pm6\times10^{-6}$, follows the stripe's upper edge and caps its top near
$\log_{10}T=-0.065$ (Figure~\ref{fig:stripes}). At $\alpha=0.0717$ the stripe lies between a flip at
$\log_{10}T=-0.128955$ and the fold at $-0.1033462$. Continued downward, the flip curve keeps its multiplier at $-1$
for 115 points to $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.0776,-0.402)$, with two reversals of its parameter tangent near
$(0.0750,-0.219)$ and $(0.0746,-0.206)$, and the fold curve keeps its multiplier at $+1$ for 120 points to
$(0.0758,-0.200)$. Located by bisection along the curve (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}), the two reversals of the flip
curve, at $(0.074955,-0.219336)$ and $(0.074608,-0.206007)$, carry no second multiplier near the unit circle (the
next ones are $3\times10^{-7}$ and $1\times10^{-5}$). On a flip curve the parameter tangent can vanish only where a
second multiplier reaches $+1$, so both are tight but smooth turns of the curve in the parameter plane, not
codimension-two points.

\paragraph{The accumulation boundary.} The fold curve of the 2-cycle ``is it'', continued from
$(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.0628,-0.115)$ in both directions (38 and 126 points, critical multiplier $1$ to $10^{-7}$, no
cusp), traces the boundary of the period-2 region in Figures~\ref{fig:stripes} and~\ref{fig:cross512}. The stripe fan
accumulates on this boundary, as the mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} requires. Further along one direction,
outside the plotted window, another multiplier leaves the unit circle (moduli up to 11). Located along the curve,
this second real multiplier reaches $+1$ at $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.035628,-0.341724)$, where the eigenvectors of
the two multipliers at $+1$ coincide to $3\times10^{-4}$ degrees: a double multiplier $+1$ with a Jordan block,
the 1:1 resonance of maps (the analog of a Bogdanov--Takens point).

\paragraph{A period-6 window with shrimp-like anatomy.} The hooks of Figure~\ref{fig:zooms} (bottom) belong to
the period-6 loop ``. -\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe is a''. Float64 orbits (Newton residuals
$10^{-15}$--$10^{-13}$) show that its curved body and its thin leg are one window: the multipliers are $-0.061$ and
$+0.048$ at the body, $-0.564$ lower down and $-0.645$ on the leg. The foot is its period-doubled loop (period 12,
leading multiplier $-0.156$). Inside the body lies a different 6-cycle, ``.The\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe is
a'', whose leading multipliers are a complex pair, $-0.031\pm0.152i$. One-parameter branches from the body end in
folds in every direction: at $\log_{10}T=-0.0558$ and $-0.0721$ in temperature, at $\alpha=0.08074$ in steering, and,
toward smaller $\alpha$, in two folds only $10^{-5}$ apart. Such a close pair is the signature of a nearby cusp. A
flip at $\alpha\approx0.0797$ then opens the period-12 foot. The body is not effectively one-dimensional either. At two body cells the leading
multiplier is a complex pair, $-0.020\pm0.264i$, or has a second multiplier of comparable size ($-0.261$ against
$0.099$). Below the body, where the window's strip appears to cross a period-5 strip near
$(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.0773,-0.0774)$, both orbits continued from their own strips are unstable at the crossing
(leading multipliers $-1.88$ and $-6.59$). The crossing is therefore neither a center nor a point where two attractors
coexist.

Fold curves that bound a window and meet in cusps, with flip curves inside, form the codimension-two organization of
a shrimp \cite{facanha2013endoskeletons} (complex dynamics of small recurrent networks has been studied in the same
spirit \cite{pasemann2002complex}). In the continued curves (Figure~\ref{fig:z3curves}), a single fold arc bounds the
body on its outer side and a flip arc on its inner side, and the leg lies between them where they converge. The outer
fold arc extends far to the left as a thin arm and ends in a cusp at $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.069106,-0.039750)$.
The fold curve through the close fold pair has a second cusp inside the body, at $(0.080097,-0.058344)$, from
which two fold branches descend.

At both reversals the quadratic coefficient $b$ changes sign between consecutive curve points while $a$ stays away
from zero (Table~\ref{tab:z3cusps}). That alone is not evidence of a cusp: along any fold curve the parameter tangent
reverses exactly where $b$ vanishes. The test's content is the nondegeneracy at that point.
Because the signs of $a$ and $b$ both flip with the orientation of the critical eigenvector, the test uses
$b\operatorname{sign}a$. A cusp is nondegenerate when the cubic coefficient $c$ of the normal form
$u'=u+a\varepsilon+bu^2+cu^3$ is nonzero where $b$ vanishes. We compute $c$ with the second-order center-manifold
term, $c=\ell^\top C(r,r,r)/6+\ell^\top B(r,h_2)/2$ with $(I-M)h_2=B(r,r)-2br$ and $\ell^\top h_2=0$, where $M$ is
the monodromy matrix and $B$ and $C$ are the second and third derivatives of its map, obtained by central differences
of float64 Jacobian-vector products. (The code reproduces $c$ to $10^{-6}$ on a map with a known reduction.) At the two
curve points that bracket each sign change, $c$ is nonzero far beyond its step-size uncertainty and has the same sign,
and $b$ changes sign with nonzero slope between them. This supports a nondegenerate cusp inside each bracket. Located along each fold curve (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}),
$b\operatorname{sign}a$ falls to below $5\times10^{-6}$ of its bracket values at $(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.069106,-0.039750)$
on the outer arc and at $(0.080097,-0.058344)$ inside the body. At these same points $c=-1.51\times10^{4}$ and
$-2.38\times10^{5}$, and the unfolding matrix $K$ defined below is regular ($\det K=2416$ and $14.0$): two
numerically located cusps. (Location uses the small-step oriented coefficient $b\operatorname{sign}a$; the unfolding
coefficients use a separate finite-difference ladder, and the selected plateau of $c$ spreads by at most 4.8\,\% over
adjacent steps.) The
negative sign of $c$ means that near each cusp, two stable period-6 orbits coexist with a saddle between the fold
branches.

A generic codimension-two cusp also needs the two parameters to unfold it independently. Near the cusp the reduced
map is $u'=u+\beta_1+\beta_2u+bu^2+cu^3$, where $\beta_1$ and $\beta_2$ vanish at the cusp and depend on the
parameters. The unfolding is regular when $K=\partial(\beta_1,\beta_2)/\partial(\log_{10}T,10\alpha)$ has rank two
($\det K\neq0$). We first estimated $K$ at a fold point near the outer-arc cusp (where $b\operatorname{sign}a=14.7$):
$\det K=2737$ (singular values $1630$ and $1.7$), so the estimated unfolding is nonsingular there, as it is at the
located cusp ($\det K=2416$). With these derivatives, the local cubic model predicted the number of period-6 orbits and their
stability at six parameter offsets, written to disk before any orbit was solved. Newton solves started from the
predicted roots and from the base state recovered the predicted number of distinct orbits, with the predicted
stability, at all six. The search was seeded, not exhaustive: orbits that these starts do not reach would be missed.

is a float32 phase map of $480\times400$ cells (largest exponent from 300 steps after 300 transient steps; chaotic cells colored by the exponent and the other cells in gray by theirs, on one color bar). Where it overlaps the float64

\paragraph{A period-4 window with five cusps on one fold curve.} At higher temperature, $\log_{10}T\approx0$--$0.1$, periodic orbits
are no longer effectively one-dimensional (second multipliers $0.06$--$0.09$). At $\alpha=0.06484$ the temperature
branch of the period-4 orbit ``$\backslash$n$\backslash$n - $\backslash$n$\backslash$n The'' is S-shaped, with folds
at $\log_{10}T=0.0640$ and $0.0664$. When that fold is continued in the plane, its parameter tangent reverses at three
points; at the first two, the quadratic coefficient $b$ changes sign while $a$ stays away from zero,
and the cubic coefficient $c$ (defined above) is nonzero (Table~\ref{tab:cusps}). Located along the curve, these two cusps lie at
$(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.062959,0.059958)$ and $(0.065574,0.074447)$. There $b\operatorname{sign}a$ is below
$1.1\times10^{-5}$ of its bracket values, $c=-1.02\times10^{4}$ and $+1.37\times10^{5}$, and $\det K=-42.0$ and $35.5$. Further traces of the same fold curve,
started from other points of the period-4 branches, retrace it to within $3\times10^{-7}$, and one of them finds two
more reversals near $\log_{10}T\approx0.012$. All five reversals are located cusps. The other three lie at
$(\alpha,\log_{10}T)=(0.083082,0.108633)$, $(0.074072,0.011831)$ and $(0.073220,0.012759)$. There $c$ is
$-9.7\times10^{4}$, $-5.7\times10^{3}$ and $+1.7\times10^{3}$, and $\det K$ is $25.3$, $-188.5$ and $68.5$. The last
two cusps, close together, have opposite signs of $c$. Three period-4 orbits
coexist between the two fold branches, and near the first cusp ($c<0$) two of them are stable
(Figure~\ref{fig:p4}).
\begin{table}[H]

 & cusp 1 & cusp 2 \\

\centering

\caption{Period map of the high-temperature region of the Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct map (philosophy direction, layer
12; $192^2$ float64 cells) with float64 fold curves (cyan, long dashes) and flip curves (magenta, short dashes) of the
period-4 orbit. The yellow circle is the first cusp; the second lies just above the window. The flip curves run along
the boundary between period 4 and period 8. The fold curves run inside the period-4 region, where three period-4
orbits coexist (near the first cusp, two of them stable). In this map a C-shaped band of the doubled loop
``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe'' (period 4) inside the period-2 region
``\textbackslash n\textbackslash nThe'' contains period 8 and an irregular core. Along the cut of
Section~\ref{sec:cascade} the period-8 orbit merges back into period 4 (a doubling bubble), so this band is not shown
to be a second cascade.
Continued from all its fold points, the region's boundary consists of an upper fold curve, with the five cusps of
the text, and a separate lower fold curve, nowhere within $2\times10^{-3}$ of it in the traced ranges; the lower curve
carries the codimension-two points described below. Within the traced ranges the curves do not join, so the region is
not shown to be one shrimp-organized component.}
\label{fig:p4}
\end{figure}
Along the lower fold curve a second real multiplier reaches $+1$ twice, at $\alpha=0.062483$, $\log_{10}T=0.004955$
and at $\alpha=0.062499$, $\log_{10}T=-0.001780$. Both points are 1:1 resonances, with the eigenvectors of the double multiplier parallel
to $3\times10^{-4}$ degrees. Close by, at $\alpha=0.062522$, $\log_{10}T=-0.002667$, a multiplier reaches $-1$: a fold-flip point. We
did not continue the curves that emanate from these points.

\paragraph{Evidence level of the cusps.} We estimated the rank condition of the unfolding at
a fold point near one cusp of the period-6 window and one of the hostile period-7 body (Section~\ref{sec:hostile}; determinants $2737$ and $53.5$), and there the local model's
predictions at twelve frozen offsets matched the orbits recovered by Newton solves seeded at the predicted roots and
the base state, in number at all twelve and in stability at eleven; the search was not exhaustive. At the thirteen
located cusps themselves (including the four of the period-5 window of Appendix~\ref{sec:hostility}), $|\det K|$ lies
between $14$ and $2416$. These are float64
computations validated by step-size checks (rung 1), not computer-assisted proofs. At the two continuation points
that bracket the lower cusp of the hostile body, an independent implementation of the model reproduced the
normal-form coefficients, including the sign change of $b$: $|b|$ within $1.2\,\%$ and $c$ to two figures, with the
opposite orientation of the null vector, which flips the signs of $a$ and $b$ together.

\section{Steering toward hostility: further results}
\label{sec:hostility}
This appendix gives further results on the hostility direction of Section~\ref{sec:hostile}: the details of the shrimp-like shapes, the period-5 window of a plane steered at two depths, its orbit census and a closure test.

\paragraph{Details of the shrimp-like shapes.} Section~\ref{sec:organization} summarizes the anatomy (Figure~\ref{fig:anatomy}); three details belong here. In higher dimensions a window can also end where a
complex pair crosses the unit circle, and the leading multiplier can change sign through a complex pair, as it does in
parts of our windows; beyond some of the flip curves we followed lie the doubling sequences of
Section~\ref{sec:cascade}, but a flip need not start a complete cascade. The period-6 hook's orbit never comes close
to superstable: its leading multiplier stays above $0.028$ in modulus. And the X-shaped crossing of a period-5 and a
period-6 strip (Figure~\ref{fig:zooms}, bottom) is not one window's anatomy but two windows overlapping, and at the
crossing both of their orbits are unstable (Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}). The shapes in these one-block planes are
therefore the two-parameter unfolding of windows whose return maps have one coarse extremum: shrimp-like in body,
legs and internal cascade, but without a one-dimensional shrimp center on the tracked branches.

\paragraph{Two turning points from two steering depths.} Steering at two depths gives the band dynamics a second
turning point. We drew 1584 chaotic cells (200 from each of the four two-block planes of Figure~\ref{fig:twoblock},
150 from each one-block map of Figure~\ref{fig:layersweep} and the 34 of the hostile line of
Figure~\ref{fig:hubcascade}), counted the turning points
of each band's return map and inspected every strong candidate by eye, since a projection can fold a single hump. Three
cells, all in two-block planes at $\log_{10}T=-0.1$, have smooth, single-valued return maps with two or more turning
points (Figure~\ref{fig:bimodal}); the other strong candidates were point sets, split bands or folded projections, or
were not reproduced. In one cell (hostility at block~4, gratitude at block~16), every one-dimensional phase of a
20-band attractor is N-shaped: a bimodal map. A $128\times128$ zoom around another, whose map has three maxima, shows
a period-5 window with a body, legs, a period-10 and period-20 border, and patches where the fixed start settles on
period~4 (Figure~\ref{fig:period5}a). We continued the window's period-5 orbit over a $31\times31$ grid of its body:
608 cells solved, all with the same token loop, 447 of them attracting. In 61 of these 447 cells, the nearest cell of
the zoom (at most $2.0\times10^{-5}$ away in $\alpha_1$ and $3.2\times10^{-5}$ in $\alpha_2$) settles on period~4, so
attractors of periods 4 and 5 coexist at grid scale, which a single unimodal map with negative Schwarzian derivative
allows only if a basin reaches an end of its interval (Section~\ref{sec:theory}).

The window still has no one-dimensional center. The leading multiplier of the continued orbit never comes near zero:
its modulus is at least $0.0139$ in all 608 cells. Where a one-dimensional map would be superstable, it meets the
second multiplier, and the two form a complex pair (91 cells, in narrow strips) or, where their product is negative
(41 other cells), pass each other as real numbers of opposite sign (Figure~\ref{fig:period5}c). Per step, the second
direction contracts by a median factor of $0.44$ and the first by $0.76$ over the attracting cells, so near these
strips the orbit's linearization has two comparable leading directions. The curves on which the trace $\mu_1+\mu_2$ changes sign take the place of
superstable curves, and they do not cross: no $2\times2$ block of attracting cells has the alternating signs of a
crossing (Figure~\ref{fig:period5}b). One isolated cell holds a repelling orbit with the same token loop
($\mu_1=1.65$), and near the top of the grid one column of Newton solves failed above cells whose leading multiplier
rises to $0.88$; both are explained by the window's fold curve (next paragraph). A second turning point
in the band maps therefore does not give this window a one-dimensional shrimp center. Like the hostile body, it is a window whose
orbit has two comparable leading directions near the strips where a one-dimensional map would be superstable; Section~\ref{sec:organization} shows that it
is a numerically verified shrimp. Its head, the near-nilpotent point, persists wherever we continued it in temperature
(Figure~\ref{fig:headtemperature}). At $\log_{10}T=-0.105$,
grids of the continued orbit over the tip of the band where the leading multiplier is positive show the same avoided
crossing: the band narrows between two strips of complex leading pairs, of modulus $0.097$ to $0.109$, which come within
one cell of each other ($5\times10^{-5}$ in $\alpha_1$) without crossing (\repo{docs/research-log.md}, runs P5Z).
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-head-temperature.png}
\caption{The head of the period-5 shrimp continued in temperature: the near-nilpotent point, where the sum of the
leading multipliers and the sum of their pairwise products vanish (tools/nilpotent\_track.py; float64, steps of 0.001 in
$\log_{10}T$). (a) Moduli of the five leading multipliers at the point ($\mu_2$ and $\mu_3$ form a complex pair of equal
modulus; hollow: the row failed its gate, $|c_1|>10^{-7}$). The defining conditions would put the three leading multipliers on
one modulus, the cube root of their product, if they were the only multipliers; the computed moduli are nearly equal,
and a fourth multiplier approaches them twice. (b) The point's
position in the plane, colored by $\log_{10}T$.}

\end{figure}

started at three phases, in the body far from the head, at the head (the near-nilpotent point), at four points

the modulus of the leading eigenvalue (triangles). (b) The reduction to the rank-one part,

\begin{figure}[tbp]

block~16, float64; \repo{tools/plot_shrimp_temperature.py}). (a) The domain of its attractor at $\log_{10}T=-0.100$,
followed cell by cell and colored by period (black: chaos behind the same five-token text; dark gray: no period found
and an exponent below 0.005; light gray: the attractor was lost), with the fold (black) and flip (red, dashed) curves. (b)
The same at $\log_{10}T=-0.105$, with the curves continued at that temperature (a partial trace of a further fold
curve, with two flagged reversals, is not drawn). (c) The outlines of the period-5 body
(solid) and of the whole followed domain (dotted) at both temperatures, and the path of the head (the near-nilpotent
point) between $\log_{10}T=-0.104$ and $-0.094$, colored by temperature.}
