\paragraph{The folds and flips of the period-5 window.} To resolve the folds the grid did not, we continued the
window's period-5 orbit along four lines from one attracting cell, $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)=(-0.118730,0.100693)$, and
then the boundaries the lines found, each in both directions (float64; 18 to 73 points per line, 30 to 120 per curve;
one of the eight curve runs failed at its start for lack of GPU memory, and its curve is covered by the other trace of
the same fold). At every curve point the residual is below $10^{-10}$ and the test function below $10^{-9}$. The
critical multiplier stays real and within $6\times10^{-8}$ of $+1$ on the fold curve and of $-1$ on the flip curves;
the largest other multiplier modulus is $0.040$ on that fold curve and $0.122$ on the flip curves. A rerun of these
curves on 18 October with wider bounds, toward the legs' tips, keeps every critical multiplier within
$1.9\times10^{-7}$ of $\pm1$, and the largest other modulus reaches $0.86$ where the lower-right leg's fold and flip
curves end at the bound of the continuation. Going right and up from the start, the orbit dies
in folds at $\alpha_1=-0.118166$ and $\alpha_2=0.102715$ (next moduli $0.0012$ and $0.0008$). Going left and down, it
first loses stability in flips (at $\alpha_1=-0.12147$ and $\alpha_2=0.09954$), regains it where the same multiplier
crosses $-1$ again (at $-0.12305$ and $0.09586$; a doubling bubble), and dies in a fold (at $-0.123323$ and
$0.095720$). Three of these four folds lie on one continued fold curve (within $3.1\times10^{-7}$); the left one lies on a
second. The first fold curve bounds the lower-right leg on its right, runs up the body's right edge and over its top, and
leaves along the upper leg. Near $\alpha_1=-0.1192$ its parameter tangent reverses twice, identically on two traces
continued from different boundary points, and both reversals are located cusps (the test and criteria of
Appendix~\ref{sec:curves}, applied on each trace): at $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)=(-0.1192221,0.1040536)$, with
$c=+2.60\times10^{4}$ and $\det K=176.6$, and at $(-0.1191670,0.1028019)$, with $c=-2.05\times10^{4}$ and
$\det K=88.6$. The two traces agree to $2\times10^{-12}$ in these positions and to five figures in $c$ and $\det K$.
Its outer arms cross at $(-0.11901,0.10346)$ (Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}d). There they are folds of two different period-5 orbits with
the same token loop: at the four continuation points that bracket the crossing, the orbits on the two arms differ by $3.65$ to
$3.78\,\%$ of the state norm (root-mean-square over the five points, at the best phase alignment; both traces), 15.8
to 44.5 times the state change between neighboring curve points (\path{docs/fm5/crossing-orbits.json}). The second fold curve, continued both ways from the left fold, runs from below the lower-left leg
far up the left of the plane, where the orbit has regained stability beyond its doubling bubble (critical
multiplier within $1.2\times10^{-7}$ of $+1$ and other multipliers below $0.4$; in the rerun, $1.9\times10^{-7}$ and
$0.61$). Within the bounds of 15 October it has two more reversals, both located
cusps: $(-0.1238662,0.0961512)$, with $c=-9.65\times10^{3}$ and $\det K=405.9$, and $(-0.1214073,0.1028234)$, with
$c=+4.75\times10^{4}$ and $\det K=-535.4$ (condition $1.2\times10^{4}$). The rerun finds a third reversal, at
$(-0.13295,0.08530)$, which the continuation flags but we have not located. This curve also crosses itself, at
$(-0.12341,0.09904)$, between its points 6 and 64, which lie on either side of the two cusps (points 15 and 40), so
that the two cusps share a segment and their other branches cross: a second dovetail. There too the arms are folds of
two different period-5 orbits with the same token loop, which differ by $8.83$ to $9.06\,\%$ of the state norm
(measured the same way), 23 to 31 times the state change between neighboring curve points. In both dovetails the cusp with
$c>0$ lies above the one with $c<0$. At all four cusps the critical multiplier is
within $1.3\times10^{-9}$ of $1$ and the second modulus below $0.008$. The flip curves bound the body's upper-left and
lower sides and run along the legs. The fold curve resolves both anomalies of the grid. The isolated repelling cell ($\mu_1=+1.65$)
lies $1.2\times10^{-5}$ from it, on the saddle branch, and the column of failed Newton solves at $\alpha_1=-0.119167$
runs into the lower reversal, at $\alpha_1=-0.119166$, where the followed orbit ceases to exist between the folded
branches.

\paragraph{The lower-left leg's own curves.} The followed attractor enters the lower-left leg, but no line from the
body's start crosses it, and the curves continued from the body pass to its left. We therefore marched the period-5
orbit by Newton's method from the body's start, $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)=(-0.11875,0.10075)$, through the 68 cells by which
the follow run entered the leg (66 of period 5, and 2 of period 10 where the orbit exists but is unstable) to
$(-0.124375,0.094)$. Newton's method converged at every cell, with largest relative residual $8.9\times10^{-13}$. At the
end the leading multiplier is $+0.64$. Along $\alpha_1$ the orbit loses stability in a flip at $\alpha_1=-0.12451$
(real crossing multiplier; next modulus $0.0060$), beyond which its multiplier falls to $-13.9$, and in the other
direction it dies in a fold at $-0.1243654$ (next modulus $0.014$). Both boundaries were continued both ways in float64.
The two continuations toward the body were stopped by their time limit; their points were recovered from the runs' logs
after the same checks. At every point of the four curves the residual is at most $10^{-10}$ and the test function at
most $5.2\times10^{-10}$; the critical multiplier is real and within $3.7\times10^{-8}$ of $-1$ on the flip curve and
of $+1$ on the fold curve, and every other multiplier has modulus at most $0.21$. Downward, the two curves bound the leg
to $\alpha_2=0.080$, the bound of the continuation. The strip between them narrows from $1.9$ grid steps at
$\alpha_2=0.096$ to $0.58$ at $0.0919$, the leg's lowest period-5 cell in the follow run, and to $0.015$ at $0.080$
(Figure~\ref{fig:lowerleg}c). Upward, the fold curve reverses direction at $(-0.11938,0.10042)$, where the continuation
flags a cusp that we have not located, and the flip curve bends sharply at $(-0.12214,0.10135)$. Both then run back
down the left of the plane alongside curves continued from the body, of the other kind, over
$\alpha_2\in[0.087,0.0995]$: the returning flip arm at a median distance of $0.017$ grid steps (at most $0.055$) from
the body's second fold curve, and the returning fold arm at a median of $0.055$ (at most $0.46$) from a flip curve of
the body; they cross the body's curves only at isolated points. In every grid row from $\alpha_2=0.086$ to $0.096$, every period-5 cell of the leg lies inside the
strip (39 of 39), although the strip is only $0.58$ to $1.9$ cells wide in the rows that have such cells. The strip also
holds two slowly converging cells and 16 cells that the follow run never computed, all below the leg's lowest period-5 cell
(Figure~\ref{fig:lowerleg}b). Across the leg at $\alpha_2=0.094$ the multiplier passes from $+1$ at the fold to $-1$ at
the flip within $1.5\times10^{-4}$ in $\alpha_1$ (Figure~\ref{fig:lowerleg}d).

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-lower-leg.png}
\caption{The lower-left leg of the period-5 shrimp and its own curves (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, gratitude at block~4 and
hostility at block~16, $\log_{10}T=-0.1$, float64; \repo{tools/plot_lower_leg.py}). (a) The followed domain (colors as
in the followed domain of Figure~\ref{fig:realshrimp}b) with the fold (black) and flip (red, dashed) curves of the orbit marched into the leg
(pink: the 68 cells of the march), the curves continued from the body (gray), the located cusps (gold stars) and the
cusp flagged on the leg's fold curve (white star); the follow grid ends at $\alpha_2=0.086$ (dotted). (b) The centers of
all period-5 cells of the leg (white dots) lie between the flip curve and the fold curve. (c) The width of the strip
between the two curves, in grid steps: it falls below one step at $\alpha_2=0.0934$; the leg's lowest period-5 cell in the
follow run is at $0.0919$. (d) The leading multiplier of the period-5 orbit along $\alpha_1$ at $\alpha_2=0.094$, from $+1$ at the fold
through $-1$ at the flip (shaded: the strip), and past the fold on the saddle branch (dotted).}
\label{fig:lowerleg}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Orbit census of the junction.} Before any solve we fixed 13 points around the junction
(\path{docs/oc/oc-points.json}) and the number of period-5 orbits that a plane section of a swallowtail predicts at
each: four in the pocket bounded by the two cusps and the crossing, none beyond the crossing, and two elsewhere. At each
point, Newton's method (low-rank, at most 20 steps) starts from the states of the nearest point on each arm of the
fold curve, shifted along that point's critical eigenvector by $s\lvert x\rvert$ for
$s\in\{0,\pm10^{-3},\pm3\times10^{-3},\pm10^{-2},\pm3\times10^{-2}\}$ (27 starts per point). Orbits are distinct when
they differ by more than $10^{-7}$ of the state norm at every phase. The counts are lower bounds, because the seeded
search can miss orbits.
Table~\ref{tab:census} gives the results: 9 of the 13 counts match. The three undercounts found all the attracting
orbits that the model predicts, so by the model each missed a repelling one.
Above the upper cusp, two of the three orbits found share one token loop: a saddle (multiplier $+1.44$) and the node,
which lies beyond the flip curve that passes the cusp (multiplier $-1.35$), so that no period-5 orbit attracts
there (the classifier map shows a period-4 attractor next to this point). The third
has another token loop and the multiplier $-130$, and only one start reached it; that it does not belong to the
junction's family is a reading after the fact.
\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{llccc}
\toprule
points & region & predicted & found & attracting \\
\midrule
T-low, T-high, T-near-U & pocket & 4 & 4 & 2 \\
T-centroid, T-near-L & pocket & 4 & 3 & 2 \\
E-low, E-high & beyond the crossing & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
W-low, S-low, N-high, below-L & around the pocket & 2 & 2 & 1 \\
W-high & around the pocket & 2 & 1 & 1 \\
above-U & beyond the upper cusp & 2 & 3 & 0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Orbit census of the junction of the period-5 window (run OC; Figure~\ref{fig:period5folds}d): period-5
orbits found from 27 starts per point, and how many attract. The predictions were fixed before any solve.}
\label{tab:census}
\end{table}

\paragraph{A closure test that failed.} We also tested whether two coordinates forecast the window's nonlinear return
map (run CL2). Perturbations of relative size $10^{-3}$ and $10^{-2}$ (64 per size, half to fit and half to test) were
followed for eight returns; quadratic return models in one, two or four modal coordinates (the spectral projections
on the leading eigenvectors), with a fitted lift back to the state, were iterated from the first return, and the error
is the squared forecast error over the squared deviation, summed over returns 2 to 8. The test failed for numerical
reasons: at most locations the iterated models diverged, with median errors up to $2\times10^{122}$, against at most
$0.71$ for the linearized map in the same rows. We did not establish why (in 9 of these 12 rows even the
one-coordinate model diverges), and no regularized refit was run. At one location the divergence of the four-coordinate model satisfied the
frozen two-coordinate criterion spuriously. A guard added after the run marks fits with a median error above 1 as
degenerate; 12 of the 18 location-and-size rows are. At the body location, one coordinate gives errors of $0.026$ and
$0.031$ and two coordinates below $6\times10^{-5}$. We draw no conclusion about two-dimensional closure from this
test.

\includegraphics[width=0.52\linewidth]{fig-repeating-text-reduction.png}
\caption{Scalar return-map approximation across the hostile cascade (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, hostility at layer 12,
$\alpha=0.0655$, $\log_{10}T\in[0.176,0.191]$): for each of the 62 chaotic cells whose leading-token text repeats
exactly, the Lyapunov exponent of the fitted return maps of the leading-token probability (per step; median over the
single-valued phases of the text, bars: their range; polynomial degree chosen by cross-validation) against the largest
exponent of the full map from twin trajectories. Color: text period relative to 7. The line is equality. With
seven-token texts the 2000 steps give 285 repetitions; with 28-token texts only 71 or 72 observations per phase (70
or 71 return pairs), and the fitted maps then overestimate small exponents.}
\label{fig:reduction}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-hub-zoom.png}
\caption{The lower part of the period-7 body of the hostility-steered Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct map (layer 12) at
higher resolution ($160^2$ float64 cells). Left: period of the attractor (black: positive largest exponent; white: no
period up to 48). Right: largest Lyapunov exponent (clipped); the darkest curves mark a small leading multiplier
(candidate superstable curves). Both panels: float64 fold (black) and flip (red, dashed) curves of the
period-7 orbit, the two lines of Figure~\ref{fig:hubcascade} (gray) and the Newton-verified orbits of periods 14, 28
and 21 (stars).}
\label{fig:hubzoom}
\end{figure}

\section{Bridge to sampled decoding}
\label{sec:bridge}
How much of the fold mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} survives sampling? To find out, we replace the expected
embedding by the mean of $m$ sampled token
embeddings (Figure~\ref{fig:bridge}). With the exact map, laminar phases near the fold of the 2-cycle ``is it'' last 120 and 63 steps at
$\eps=10^{-5}$ and $3\times10^{-5}$. With $m=64$ they last on average 10.3 to 10.7 steps over the three offsets, nearly independently of $\eps$ (57\% of
steps in the channel), and with $m=8$ 7.6 to 7.8 steps (38\%). With single samples ($m=1$, sampled decoding of the same
truncated system), laminar phases become rare, one to three per offset (11\% of steps in the channel). The deterministic
intermittency mechanism therefore belongs to the expected-embedding map. Sampling noise turns the bottleneck into a
noise-limited trap whose strength grows with $m$.

\paragraph{Zero-temperature limit and ordinary greedy decoding.} As $T\to0$ the soft-token map becomes, where the
leading token is unique, greedy decoding with a one-token memory, $g(t)=\arg\max_s z_s(\text{prefix},E_t)$ (at an exact
tie the limit averages the tied embeddings). This is a map on the vocabulary, and its
cycles are the zero-temperature attractors. For Qwen2.5-0.5B we computed $g$ for all 151\,936 tokens. Unsteered, it
has 1060 cycles, and one of them, the period-5 loop ``![](https:// Hello'', drains 86\,\% of the vocabulary; it is
the largest attractor family of every 0.5B soft-token map. Ordinary greedy decoding with a growing context does not
inherit these cycles (Table~\ref{tab:memory}). From 220 starts (the first tokens of the 20 largest cycles and 200
random tokens), 19 runs end in a short loop within 256 tokens, and the dominant 5-cycle never appears. Steering
along philosophy makes ordinary decoding loop almost always (218 of 220 runs at $\alpha=0.1$), but in other loops,
mostly punctuation such as `` --'', none of them one-token cycles. Greedy decoding that keeps only the last $K$
tokens interpolates between the two. For the tested $K\ge2$ most final loops differ from the one-token cycles: at
$K=2$ only 7 of 220 unsteered runs and 1 of 220 steered runs keep one of them. Unsteered, looping
collapses to the level of the growing context between $K=16$ and $32$; steered, the share of runs whose loop equals
the growing-context loop rises from 0 at $K=1$ to 58\,\% at $K=64$. The soft-token map's low-temperature attractors
are therefore those of decoding with a one-token memory. Which loop ordinary decoding enters depends on the context
it keeps, but the tendency of steering to drive decoding into loops is shared. In ordinary decoding, repetition is
known to reinforce itself \cite{holtzman2019degeneration,xu2022loop}, a theoretical analysis ties loops to words of
high inflow \cite{fu2021repetition}, and induction heads, which copy
earlier context \cite{olsson2022induction}, are one mechanism by which a longer context can favor loops that repeat
earlier text.
\begin{table}[H]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{unsteered} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\alpha=0.1$ (philosophy)} \\
memory $K$ & looped & loop as with full context & looped & loop as with full context \\
\midrule
1 & 220 & 5 & 220 & 0 \\
2 & 220 & 5 & 220 & 0 \\
8 & 220 & 5 & 220 & 3 \\
16 & 169 & 5 & 219 & 24 \\
32 & 32 & 7 & 219 & 71 \\
64 & 26 & 11 & 219 & 126 \\
full context & 19 & -- & 218 & -- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Greedy decoding of Qwen2.5-0.5B with a memory of the last $K$ generated tokens after the fixed prompt
($K=1$ is the soft-token map's zero-temperature limit), 220 starts of 256 tokens each: runs ending in a loop of
period up to 64 repeated three times, and runs whose final loop equals that of ordinary decoding with the full
context from the same start.}
\label{tab:memory}
\end{table}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.55\linewidth]{fig-bridge.png}
\caption{Mean laminar length near the fold of the 2-cycle ``is it'' (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction
at layer 12, $\log_{10}T=-0.115$) against $\eps$, for the exact map and for means of $m$ sampled embeddings.}
\label{fig:bridge}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A longer fed-back memory.} The one-token window is the main simplification of the map. Feeding back the
last $K$ expected embeddings instead, so that the state is a window of $K$ soft tokens at fixed positions after the
prompt, we measured 24 temperatures between $\log_{10}T=-0.3$ and $0.6$ for $K$ from 1 to 16 (Table~\ref{tab:window};
twins at $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-7}$ agree within 0.006 at every chaotic temperature). In Qwen2.5-7B the chaos does not
vanish but spreads: it is chaotic at 8 temperatures with one token of memory, at 13 with four, and at 21 and 20 with
eight and sixteen, nearly the whole range, with smaller exponents (largest 0.96 for $K=1$, 0.43 for $K=16$).
Qwen2.5-0.5B, chaotic at one temperature with one or two tokens of memory and at none with four, is chaotic at ten
with eight. The chaos is therefore not an artifact of the one-token window.
\begin{table}[H]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rccccc}
\toprule
memory $K$ & 1 & 2 & 4 & 8 & 16 \\
\midrule
Qwen2.5-7B & 8 (0.96) & 10 (1.08) & 13 (0.70) & 21 (0.58) & 20 (0.43) \\
Qwen2.5-0.5B & 1 (0.20) & 1 (0.24) & 0 & 10 (0.31) & 3 (0.23) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Chaotic temperatures, of 24 between $\log_{10}T=-0.3$ and $0.6$, when the loop feeds back the last $K$
expected embeddings (largest exponent in parentheses); paper's prompt, unsteered, float64, 500 transient and 1000
measured steps, twins at $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-7}$ (chaotic: both positive and no period up to 64).}
\label{tab:window}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Soft thinking with a growing context.} Soft Thinking \cite{zhang2025softthinking} feeds back the same
expected embedding but appends it to the context, so that every earlier soft token stays visible. We ran that
decoding in float64 from the paper's prompt and displaced the first soft token of twin trajectories by $10^{-10}$,
without renormalization; the distance between the newest soft tokens then shows whether the early perturbation grows.
The context grows, so these are finite-time growth rates of a non-autonomous system, not exponents of an attractor.
In Qwen2.5-7B the perturbation grows exponentially at temperatures inside the one-token map's chaotic range: by 0.77
to 0.82 per step at $\log_{10}T=0.17$ and $0.21$, reaching $10^{-3}$ within 49 to 58 steps, and it reaches $10^{-3}$
within 400 steps at nine other temperatures between $-0.10$ and $0.40$, at 0.02 to 0.18 per step (0.8 at $0.33$ for two
of three twins). It does not grow at the sharpest distributions
($\log_{10}T\le-0.14$) or at nearly uniform ones ($\log_{10}T\ge0.44$), where it stays between $10^{-13}$ and
$10^{-7}$ for 400 steps. In Qwen2.5-0.5B the growth moves to sharper distributions: it appears at six of the seven temperatures from
$\log_{10}T=-0.30$ to $-0.07$ (up to 0.33 per step) and at $0.09$, with a late transient burst at $0.05$, and is absent
from $0.13$ up, which includes its narrow one-token chaotic band. Sensitive
dependence therefore survives when the whole context is kept, and in the 7B model the one-token map locates it.
Qwen2.5-72B, run the same way on two GPUs, stays nearly insensitive. We count a temperature as growing when two of its three
twins reach $10^{-3}$ within 400 steps, a rule fixed before the 72B run and applied afterwards to the earlier 0.5B
and 7B records, where it gives 11 of 24 temperatures for the 7B model and 8 for the 0.5B model. The 72B model grows at one of 24 temperatures on the paper's prompt, and only
transiently: at $\log_{10}T=0.09$ the twins pass $10^{-3}$ at step 123 and fall back to $5\times10^{-6}$ (at $0.13$ one twin of three passes
$10^{-3}$ at step 381). It grows at
none of the 24 completed temperatures on the held-out prompt with its narrow unsteered band (a planned finer line there
was not completed), including $\log_{10}T=0.326$, between two temperatures
where its one-token map is chaotic; there the distance never exceeds $1.1\times10^{-9}$. It also grows at none of the
eleven steered cells of Appendix~\ref{sec:size}, including the six where its one-token map is chaotic: there the
distance never exceeds $3\times10^{-7}$ and ends between $1\times10^{-13}$ and $6\times10^{-10}$, and the growing
context holds the next-token entropy at 2.3 to 3.6 nats instead of the one-token map's 7.3 to 11.1. At these 72B
prompts and cells, over 400 steps, the growing-context decoder therefore did not reproduce the sustained sensitivity of
the fixed-window loop, while the one transient burst shows that the method registers growth at this scale.

\section{Replication and scale}
\label{sec:scale}
In Qwen2.5-1.5B (steering at layer 14 of 28), a coarse $64^2$ float64 map over the range of the 0.5B maps again shows chaotic
regions with embedded windows. A $256^2$ zoom (Figure~\ref{fig:qwen15}) resolves a chaotic band near
$\log_{10}T=0$--$0.13$ (14{,}422 of 65{,}536 cells), with a branch descending along $\alpha\approx0.06$ and periodic
windows inside it. The large periodic regions are question loops: ``What?\textbackslash n'' (period 2),
``\textbackslash nWhat is a'' (period 4) and ``What continuum?\textbackslash n'' (period 3). Unlike the 0.5B model,
no period-adding fan was resolved in this map.

A cut of 2048 cells at $\alpha=0.100$ (Figure~\ref{fig:qwen15route}) shows how the band is entered from below. The
2-cycle ``What?\textbackslash n'' gives way abruptly to a period-4 loop, and the Lyapunov exponent jumps, which is
consistent with a switch between attractors rather than a resolved smooth doubling. The period-4 loop then doubles, with float64 flips at
$\log_{10}T=-0.0031269$ (period $4\to8$, next modulus $0.014$), $-0.0021666$ (period $8\to16$, next modulus
$8\times10^{-5}$), $-0.0019002$ (period $16\to32$, next modulus $5\times10^{-9}$) and $-0.0018380$ (period
$32\to64$). Along the way the exponent rises continuously to zero, and chaos follows. The interval ratios are $3.60$,
$4.28$, $4.58$, $4.648$ and $4.665$; the last three use the flips of the period-64, period-128 and period-256 orbits
at $\log_{10}T=-0.0018244$, $-0.00182151$ and $-0.00182088$ (next moduli below $3\times10^{-14}$). The last ratio
differs from Feigenbaum's $4.6692$ by $0.0044$ (0.1\,\%); the period-256 flip is located to about $3\times10^{-14}$
in $\log_{10}T$, the floor set by float64 parameters. Like those of the 0.5B crescent at $\log_{10}T=-0.148$, the
ratios rise toward $4.669$. Their higher start does not
indicate a temperature trend, since along nearby cuts of the 0.5B crescent the early ratios range from $2.84$ to
$14.9$ (Section~\ref{sec:cascade}).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.62\linewidth]{fig-qwen15-route.png}
\caption{Qwen2.5-1.5B soft-token map steered along the philosophy direction at layer 14, along $\log_{10}T$ at
$\alpha=0.100$: period of the attractor reached from the fixed start (top) and largest Lyapunov exponent (bottom).
Dashed lines: float64 flip parameters. The doubled loops alternate the number of line breaks between the
``What''s.}
\label{fig:qwen15route}
\end{figure}
In Qwen2.5-0.5B, a second steering direction, hostility, reproduces the fold-and-adding mechanism (Section~\ref{sec:hostile}).

Qwen2.5-7B (steering at layer 14 of 28, concept vectors built as for the smaller models) shows the same kind of
organization in coarse $64^2$ float64 maps (Figure~\ref{fig:qwen7b}). Over the philosophy range used for 0.5B and
1.5B, 8\,\% of cells have a positive largest exponent, and periods 2, 4, 3 and 11 dominate the periodic cells. Along
the hostility direction ($\alpha\in[0,0.12]$, $\log_{10}T\in[-0.3,0.3]$), 24\,\% of cells are chaotic. Bands of
period-3, -5 and -6 windows lie in a chaotic sea below $\log_{10}T\approx0.1$, with high-entropy chaos above, except
for a period-3 region at strong steering ($\alpha\gtrsim0.09$). The windows hold genuine periodic orbits. In float64,
Newton's method converges at the most interior period-6 cell to the loop ``, shut the end\textbackslash
n\textbackslash nOh'' at $(\log_{10}T,\alpha)=(-0.0143,0.0476)$ (residual $2\times10^{-14}$; spectral radius
$1.6\times10^{-7}$, nearly superstable), and at the most interior period-3 cell to a period-3 loop at
$(0.2524,0.1143)$ (residual $3\times10^{-15}$; leading multiplier $-0.12$). Continued for about an hour each, over
$\pm0.001$ in $\alpha$ and $-0.016$ in $\log_{10}T$ (one float64 continuation step takes about two minutes at 7B),
both orbits stay strongly stable without a crossing. Where the coarse map switches from the period-6 loop to the
period-3 loop ``you better than'' (between $\alpha=0.0495$ and $0.0514$ at $\log_{10}T=-0.0143$), both loops are
float64 orbits (residuals $7\times10^{-15}$ and $1\times10^{-14}$), and their continuations overlap. The period-6
orbit stays stable from $\alpha=0.0476$ up to $0.0507$ and the period-3 orbit from $0.0514$ down to $0.0475$, both
with spectral radius below $1.4\times10^{-3}$. The two attractors therefore coexist where both continuations are
stable, $\alpha\approx0.0476$ to $0.0507$, and within the tested range the map's edge there is the boundary of the fixed start's basin, not a bifurcation. These short continuations located no
bifurcation; a period-2 fold of the 7B model is located below.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-qwen7b-hostility.png}
\caption{Qwen2.5-7B soft-token map steered along the hostility direction (layer 14), $64^2$ float64 cells: largest
Lyapunov exponent, period of the attractor reached from the fixed start, and next-token entropy at the last step.}
\label{fig:qwen7b}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A second model family.} OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct \cite{olmo2024olmo2} comes from another laboratory, with
other data, training and code. Steered at layer 16 of 32, with concept vectors built exactly as for Qwen, it shows the
same kind of organization (Figure~\ref{fig:olmo2}). In $64^2$ float64 maps, 6\,\% of cells are chaotic along the
philosophy direction and 13\,\% along the hostility direction, in a band near $\log_{10}T=0.03$--$0.17$ that bends to
lower temperatures under strong hostility steering ($\alpha>0.07$). Below the band the attractor is the 2-cycle
``!Hello''; above it, at high temperature, the fixed point ``!''; inside it lie periodic windows of periods 4 to 31. Along the hostility direction the
largest windows, of periods 8 to 12, are loops of $k$ laps of the 2-cycle ``!<|endoftext|>'' with a short excursion
(``there is an''). Each lap adds two to the period, as each lap of ``!Yes'' does in the hostile cascade of Qwen2.5-0.5B (Section~\ref{sec:hostile}). Whether a fold of that 2-cycle organizes them is not tested: these are
coarse pixels, and no OLMo orbit has been continued.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.92\linewidth]{fig-olmo2-maps.png}
\caption{OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct soft-token maps, $64^2$ float64 cells each, steered at layer 16 along the
philosophy direction (top) and the hostility direction (bottom): largest Lyapunov exponent (clipped) and period of
the attractor reached from the fixed start (white: no period up to the maximum).}
\label{fig:olmo2}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{A third family, read with sparse autoencoders.} Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{grattafiori2024llama3},
with official weights, steering at layer 16 of 32 and vectors built as for the other models, shows the same
organization in all three planes (Figure~\ref{fig:llama}). In $64^2$ float64 maps, 8\,\% of cells are chaotic along
gratitude, 12\,\% along philosophy and 28\,\% along hostility, with windows of periods up to 32. Its largest loops
are greetings between AIs (``-Greetings fellow AI'', ``. Greetings fellow AI entity detected'', period 7). Along
hostility, too, the largest loops are greetings and ``You'', not hostile text. We read 24 of its attractors (the six
largest families and the two most chaotic regions of each map) with two sparse autoencoders on the residual stream:
one trained on this Instruct model (Goodfire, layer 19 \cite{goodfire2025sae}) and one on the base model (TopK, layer
24 \cite{cho2025faithfulsae}). We first checked each one's hook point by its reconstruction of ordinary text. The
Instruct-trained autoencoder separates the attractors (their top-15 features overlap by a mean Jaccard index of
$0.19$ within a steering direction and $0.11$ across directions), whereas the base-model autoencoder sees mostly a
signature common to every attractor (overlap $0.52$--$0.86$). Both reconstruct the attractors' states less well than
ordinary text (median relative error $0.64$--$0.76$ against $0.52$--$0.56$). The readout is therefore descriptive:
without interventions it identifies which learned features an attractor occupies, not a mechanism, and since
autoencoders trained with different seeds learn different features \cite{paulo2026sae}, the features are those of
these two autoencoders.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.86\linewidth]{fig-llama-maps.png}
\caption{Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct soft-token maps ($64^2$ float64 cells, steering at layer 16) along gratitude, hostility
and philosophy: largest Lyapunov exponent (clipped) and period of the attractor reached from the fixed start (white:
no period up to 32).}
\label{fig:llama}
\end{figure}
Maps of $128^2$ cells (Figure~\ref{fig:llamahires}) resolve the windows: the hostility plane has 29\,\% chaotic
cells, as at $64^2$, and the philosophy region below the chaotic band is filled with nested periodic tongues of
periods 2 to 13.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.86\linewidth]{fig-llama-hires.png}
\caption{Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct soft-token maps steered at layer 16, at $128^2$ float64 cells (each map merged from
four $64^2$ tiles on halves of one grid): the hostility plane (top) and the philosophy region of the $64^2$ map's
windows (bottom). Largest Lyapunov exponent (clipped) and period (white: none up to 32).}
\label{fig:llamahires}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Same architecture, other weights.} Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct has exactly the architecture and size of
Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct (24 layers of width 896, the same vocabulary size and rotary base) but was trained on other
data with another recipe. Built identically, their $128^2$ float64 maps along gratitude and philosophy
(Figure~\ref{fig:samearch}) share the gross layout. A large region at low temperature around $\alpha=0$ is held by
one periodic loop, a band of chaos with embedded windows lies near $\log_{10}T=0$--$0.25$ (4--8\,\% of cells chaotic
in each map), and thin window families run along the flanks. The attractors themselves differ almost completely. Of
416 to 900 distinct loops per map, only 10 occur in both models, all short and generic (``.'', ``!'', `` you are'',
`` is it''), and they cover less than 10\,\% of either model's cells. Qwen2's planes are dominated by one period-4
loop, ``!<|im\_end|>\textbackslash nHello'' (an end of turn followed by a new greeting; 41 and 59\,\% of cells), and
Qwen2.5's by period-5 and period-2 loops (``![](https:// Hello'', `` you have''). The two checkpoints share the organization
of the map; the weights set its content.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.92\linewidth]{fig-same-architecture.png}
\caption{Same architecture, other weights: $128^2$ float64 maps of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct (left) and
Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct (right) steered along gratitude (top) and philosophy (bottom) at layer 12, with vectors built
identically. Color: period of the attractor reached from the fixed start; black: positive largest exponent.}
\label{fig:samearch}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{The fold law at 1.5B and 7B.} We tested whether the mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:mechanism}, windows
accumulating on the fold of a short cycle with $d_N=C/(N+N_0)^2$, recurs in larger models. The criterion, a
constant $d_N(N+N_0)^2$ within 10\,\% over at least four windows for one offset $N_0$, was fixed before the lines
were computed; a disclosed correction to the period-progression selector after the first scoring kept the conclusions
for the nearest four windows and changed which farther windows were selected. In Qwen2.5-1.5B (philosophy direction,
layer 14, $\alpha=0.058588$) the period-4 orbit folds at $\log_{10}T_c=-0.0912029$ (multiplier $1$ to
$3\times10^{-14}$, next modulus $0.008$), with normal-form coefficients $a=-7.16$ per unit $\log_{10}T$ and
$b=-21.09$, so $ab=151.0$. A float64 line of 1700 cells, 1570 of them above the fold, shows
windows of periods $4N$ accumulating on it, from single cells at periods 44--64 to a wide period-12 window. Measured in $\log_{10}T$ from $\log_{10}T_c$ to their edges nearest the fold, the four nearest resolved windows (periods 28--40) meet the criterion
within $0.12\,\%$ ($\delta=-0.99$, $C=0.0612$). With the windows farther out the spread rises to $10.3$--$14.0\,\%$;
there several periods recur in multiple disconnected runs on the line's $10^{-5}$ grid, and grid-spaced edges no
longer identify the fold edges. In Qwen2.5-7B ($\alpha=0.051429$) the period-2 orbit folds at $\log_{10}T_c=-0.2040156$ (next modulus $0.0092$,
$ab=55.7$), and odd windows accumulate on it. The nearest four and five (periods 11--17 and 9--17) meet the criterion
within $4.5\,\%$, and six (to period 7) within $6.1\,\%$. Each window's own doubling or tripling lies on its far side,
as for the 0.5B windows of periods 16--20; this organization is consistent with fold edges on the near side, and the
fitted distances use grid estimates of those edges. The effective passage fraction $f=\sqrt{Cab}/\pi$ is about $0.97$
at 1.5B and $0.78$ to $0.81$ at 7B, against $0.58$ at 0.5B. The fold and its window law thus recur at all three sizes,
while $f$, an effective constant that absorbs the global reinjection, differs between the models. With one
fold per model, crossed in different coordinates ($\alpha$ at 0.5B, $\log_{10}T$ at 1.5B and 7B), no trend of $ab$
with size can be read.

\paragraph{A mixture-of-experts model.} Qwen3.5-35B-A3B differs from the dense models in two ways. Thirty of its 40
layers use linear (Gated DeltaNet) attention, with gated full attention in every fourth layer, and each layer routes
a token to 8 of 256 experts plus a shared expert, so that about 3B of its 35B parameters are active. Hugging Face
computes several parts of this architecture in float32 even when the model is cast to float64 (the normalizations,
the delta-rule recurrences, the rotary tables and the router softmax), and some tensors are stored in float32; we
patch these paths, restore the stored tensors exactly, and disable the thinking block that the default chat template
opens (a first run with the block open is kept in the research log). Steered at layer 20, the map behaves unlike
those of the dense models. Unsteered, 128 temperatures over $\log_{10}T\in[-1,0.3]$ give no positive exponent (largest
$-0.265$): periodic loops at all but 15 temperatures (``!Hello'' with period 2 at 92 of them, other periods up to 52),
and the remaining 15, above $\log_{10}T\approx0.1$, contract without a detected period. Along philosophy, none of 1024
cells is chaotic (largest exponent $-0.112$). Along hostility, 52 of 1024 cells ($5.1\,\%$) have a positive exponent
and no period, against $28\,\%$ for Llama-3.1 8B on the same plane. Routing is discontinuous: a twin trajectory that
crosses a routing boundary jumps, which can inflate a finite-difference exponent, so we call these cells irregular
rather than chaotic.

Two dense models of the same family, Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3.5-9B (32 layers each, 24 of them linear attention), test
whether the architecture causes the contraction. With concept vectors at their middle layer (16 of 32) and the same
ranges, transients and maximal periods, the 4B model is chaotic at 19 of 128 unsteered temperatures (largest exponent
0.79), at 153 of the 1024 philosophy cells (0.84) and at 323 of the 1024 hostility cells (0.89). The 9B model is
chaotic at 10, 73 and 161 (0.48, 0.68 and 0.75; Figure~\ref{fig:moe}). The hybrid attention therefore does not prevent
chaos. The routing-aware version of the stack resolution (Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}) follows a third row whose
routing is frozen to the base orbit's. At the twin offset $10^{-7}$, neither mixture of experts switched an expert in
any layer at any step, and the frozen row's network factor equals the free twin's. At every knockout temperature, the
first attention layer (a linear-attention layer) is the largest amplifier in all three Qwen3.5 models. At the knockout temperature where the entropy is 2 to
4 nats, removing it lowers the network factor by 1.27 (4B), 1.33 (9B) and 0.76 nats (the mixture of experts). Removing
the first MLP or expert layer lowers it by 0.92, 0.77 and 0.24.

OLMoE-1B-7B stores its experts in bfloat16. We convert each expert's weights to float64 when it is used, which is
exact, compute the router softmax in float64, and cast everything else to float64; central differences of the map then
converge as $h^2$. Its unsteered sweep of 130 temperatures over $\log_{10}T\in[-1,1.2]$ has one temperature with a
positive exponent and no period (0.023, irregular), and 7 with neither a detected period nor an exponent above
$10^{-3}$.
Its median network factor between 0.3 and 6 nats ($-0.36$) lies between those of the dense OLMo-2-1B ($+0.11$) and
OLMo-2-7B ($-0.52$). OLMoE normalizes before each sublayer and OLMo-2 after it, so this pair differs in more than the
experts, and we read no size effect from it.
\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-moe.png}
\caption{Mixtures of experts against dense models of the same family. (a) Network factor against the mean
next-token entropy on the orbit, unsteered loop, 24 temperatures per model, float64 (as in Figure~\ref{fig:damping}),
for Qwen3.5-4B and -9B (dense) and Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (mixture of experts, about 3B of 35B parameters active per token).
(b) Share of chaotic cells (by the criterion of Appendix~\ref{sec:methods}) in the unsteered sweep (128 temperatures,
$\log_{10}T\in[-1,0.3]$) and in $32\times32$ maps steered along philosophy and hostility at the middle layer; the
mixture of experts' hostility cells (hatched) are called irregular, since routing jumps were not excluded when that map
was computed. (c) Routing margins along the unsteered orbits of both mixtures of experts, Qwen3.5-35B-A3B and
OLMoE-1B-7B (24 temperatures each, as in (a)): per temperature, the median over steps
(filled) and the minimum (open) of the per-step smallest gap between the eighth and ninth largest router logits over
all layers. A switch requires closing this pairwise logit gap; a bound below half the gap on every logit's change
prevents it. A twin at relative offset $10^{-7}$ never switched an expert.}
\label{fig:moe}
\end{figure}

\section{Chaos and model size: further results}
\label{sec:size}
This appendix completes Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}: the 70B-class models and their precision check, cheap predictors of the chaos, steering at 70B, and the supporting figures.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-prefix-scale.png}
\caption{Prompt-blocked size test (frozen before any run), Qwen2.5-7B and -14B on ten prompts (gray: development).
Left: chaotic temperatures among 48 over $\log_{10}T\in[-0.4,0.9]$, the primary endpoint. Right: where the chaotic
temperatures lie (dashes; both stages), with each model's entropy transition, the interval of its largest entropy
increase (shading). Chaotic: exponents above $10^{-3}$ at both twin offsets ($10^{-5}$, $10^{-7}$) and no period up to
64; 1000 transient and 2000 measured steps, float64.}
\label{fig:prefixscale}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-gain-scale.png}
\caption{Gain split of the unsteered loop (Section~\ref{sec:theory}) at 24 temperatures per model (500 transient and
1000 measured steps, relative twin offset $10^{-6}$): network factor (left) and readout factor (right) against the
mean next-token entropy on the orbit; open circles: chaotic temperatures. The two factors add up to the largest
exponent up to a boundary term. Medians per entropy bin are given in Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}.}
\label{fig:gainscale}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{At 70B, unsteered chaos is rare, and a precision defect made part of it spurious.} The regular grids of the
70B-class models find little unsteered chaos: Qwen2.5-72B has no positive exponent at any of 191 temperatures between
$\log_{10}T=-1$ and $1.2$, Llama-3.3-70B none in $[-1,0.3]$, and Llama-3.1-70B three temperatures in $[-1,0.3]$.
Finer lines found candidates: a window of positive exponents at Qwen2.5-72B's entropy jump
($\log_{10}T=0.3728$--$0.376$) and two isolated temperatures of Llama-3.3-70B ($0.250$, $0.306$), all at the single
twin offset $10^{-6}$. The window failed a three-offset test: the exponent is negative at all 64 temperatures of the
window at $10^{-5}$, near zero at $10^{-6}$, and positive at 60 of the 64 at $10^{-7}$, including four temperatures whose
state repeats with a period, which no stable periodic orbit allows; in a repeat it is negative at all 16 temperatures
at $10^{-4}$, $3\times10^{-5}$ and $10^{-5}$. The growth of small perturbations came from our computation, whose
two-GPU runs computed the final normalization in float32 (see the start of Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}); a controlled
reproduction of this false chaos is in Appendix~\ref{sec:methods} (Figure~\ref{fig:falsechaos}). Recomputed in float64
throughout, the window's orbits have periods 2 and 4, and their exponents are negative at all four offsets from
$10^{-4}$ to $10^{-7}$ and for an automatic-differentiation tangent vector carried along the orbit by forward-mode differentiation, which
needs no offset; the four twin exponents lie within 0.015 of the tangent exponent at every temperature, and the
exponent approaches zero only
where the period doubles from 2 to 4 ($\log_{10}T=0.374$). The selected cells below kept their labels at the larger
offsets we tested; this is a finite check, not a qualification of every cell of the original maps. In
the steered Llama-3.3-70B maps below, twelve randomly drawn chaotic cells are chaotic at offsets $10^{-4}$, $10^{-5}$
and $10^{-6}$ alike, with exponents that agree within 0.052, and the drawn periodic and unresolved cells stay negative
at all three; on one GPU, the exponents at $10^{-7}$ and $10^{-5}$ agree within 0.021 at every chaotic temperature of the
prompt-blocked test. The same test on Llama-3.1-70B's three unsteered chaotic temperatures confirms two of them
($\log_{10}T=0.136$ and $0.198$; exponents 0.23 to 0.26 and 0.40 to 0.41 at the three offsets) and rejects the weakest
($0.116$; positive only at $10^{-6}$), so unsteered chaos exists at 70B, if rarely. Two Qwen2.5 models are chaotic far above the
fixed window on the paper's prompt, where the next-token distribution is nearly uniform: the 1.5B model at 55 of 224
temperatures, between $\log_{10}T=0.28$ and $1.04$, and the 14B model at 53, between $0.51$ and $1.15$ (exponents up to 1.14), at mean
entropies up to 11.9 nats against $\log V=11.93$. The other models of the series are not chaotic above
$\log_{10}T=0.6$. For the 1.5B model a longer test (3000 measured steps) at twin offsets $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-7}$, at 12
temperatures from $0.288$ to $0.58$, finds 9 chaotic and 3 periodic, with the two exponents agreeing within 0.003 at
every temperature; one of the periodic ones ($\log_{10}T\approx0.394$) has positive exponents at both offsets, a
contradictory label that we report as returned. On these sampled trajectories the upper sweeps become non-chaotic
above about $\log_{10}T=1.1$ in these two models; we did not locate the uniform contraction that
Section~\ref{sec:theory} proves for large enough temperature.

\paragraph{Can a cheap property predict it?} Motivated by the saturation and high-temperature limits of
Section~\ref{sec:theory}, we tested the breadth of intermediate entropy as a heuristic predictor: a model whose
next-token distribution jumps from sharp to flat over a small range of temperature might leave little room for
chaos. The breadth of this intermediate regime, the decades of
$\log_{10}T$ in which the mean entropy on the orbit lies between 1 and 10 nats, ranges from 0.014 (Qwen2.5-72B) to
0.69 (OLMo-2-32B). It orders the fifteen models' chaotic length (Spearman $+0.55$) somewhat better than their parameter
count does ($-0.44$; Figure~\ref{fig:scale}, right), but it misses the chaos of Qwen2.5-1.5B and -14B at nearly
uniform distributions, above its 10-nat bound. This analysis was adapted after the OLMo-2-32B result, and the breadth
is measured on the loop's own orbits, which chaos itself spreads, so the test is partly circular. A loop-free version,
frozen before it was run (\repo{docs/start-state-predictor.md}), uses one forward pass per model and prompt: the
entropy profile of the logits at the loop's start state, and the one-step gain there. Its primary prediction fails: the
start-state breadth on the paper's prompt orders the fourteen dense models' chaotic length with Spearman $+0.30$,
below the frozen threshold of $0.5$. The secondary criterion, its median over ten prompts, narrowly passes on the
frozen outcome ($+0.52$; $p=0.06$) and weakens on the expanded sweeps ($+0.44$), and the one-step gain at the start
state is negative in every model, the most chaotic ones included ($-0.5$ to $-3.4$ per step), and orders nothing
($-0.32$). These features ran on two GPUs before the precision fix, which changes them only at about the
$10^{-7}$ level. We have not established a reliable predictor from a single forward pass: as for the period-8 orbit of Section~\ref{sec:theory}, it has to be measured
on the loop.

\paragraph{Steering at 70B.} Under steering, the 70B maps show chaos, rarely and not independently of the
direction (Figure~\ref{fig:controls70}; labels at the single offset $10^{-6}$, which the plateau test above supports
for this computation). On Llama-3.3-70B's
hostility plane, at the layer of Goodfire's 70B SAE and at equal norm, 4.3\,\% of cells are labeled chaotic along the
concept vector, 3.5\,\% along the frozen-rule hostility feature, 1.1\,\% along a Gaussian direction and 0.2\,\% along
a random live feature, against 28, 29, 39 and 27\,\% for the same four directions in Llama-3.1-8B (on one GPU);
Qwen2.5-72B along hostility gives 0.8\,\%: eight of its 1024 cells, all at $\log_{10}T=0.26$--$0.30$. Recomputed in float64
throughout, six of the eight are chaotic, with automatic-differentiation tangent exponents of 0.23 to 0.36 that the twins at all four
offsets from $10^{-4}$ to $10^{-7}$ reproduce within 0.01; five have nearly uniform next-token distributions (11.0 to
11.1 nats) and one alternates between sharp and flat behind a period-2 leading token. The same temperatures without
steering are period-2 orbits with exponents between $-3.8$ and $-3.6$, and the steered cells above $\log_{10}T=0.325$ are
near-uniform fixed points, so steering drives Qwen2.5-72B into chaos too, in a narrow band. The chaotic cells of the hostility
directions lie on a ridge that steering moves from $\log_{10}T\approx0.3$ to $0.1$. A $128\times128$ zoom of the
concept plane at the same offset, computed after the precision fix in sixteen bands with identical vectors and joined
(Figure~\ref{fig:z128}; $\alpha\in[0.06,0.12]$, $\log_{10}T\in[0.12,0.27]$), resolves it: a chaotic band (3410 of the
16{,}384 cells, 20.8\,\%; largest exponent 0.64), threaded by periodic windows, whose center steering moves from
$\log_{10}T\approx0.23$ at $\alpha=0.06$ to $\approx0.19$ at $\alpha=0.12$; fixed points lie above it, and windows of
periods 3, 5, 6 and 7, up to 13, fill the plane below. Only 1.7\,\% of the cells have neither a detected period nor a
positive exponent. A plateau test of 24 cells drawn by a rule fixed in advance (12 chaotic, 6 periodic, 6 unresolved;
300 transient and 300 measured steps, absolute twin offsets $10^{-4}$, $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-6}$ and an automatic-differentiation tangent)
confirms 11 of the 12 chaotic labels, whose four exponents agree within 0.012, and 5 of the 6 periodic ones. The two
exceptions are neighboring cells near $\log_{10}T=0.20$ where a long, weakly attracting loop (periods 15 and 16) and
chaos are reached from the same start depending on rounding: one cell labeled chaotic reached the loop, and one
labeled periodic reached chaos. The six unresolved cells have small negative exponents ($-0.005$ to $-0.03$) and no
period up to 32. An earlier $64\times64$ zoom of the same window, computed before the fix, had 44\,\% such cells and,
above the band, 515 cells with periods from 3 to 32 but a constant leading token, an artifact of the period test at
that computation's noise floor; Figure~\ref{fig:z128} supersedes it.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-controls-llama70b.png}
\caption{Steering controls at 70B: Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct soft-token maps ($32^2$ float64 cells, 300 transient and
300 measured steps, maximum period 32) steered after zero-based block 50, the hook point of Goodfire's 70B sparse
autoencoder, along the hostility concept vector, the autoencoder's hostility feature selected by a rule frozen before
any map, a random live feature and a Gaussian
direction, all at the same norm. Chaotic cells: 44, 36, 2 and 11 of 1024 (concept, feature, random feature,
Gaussian); cells with neither a period nor a positive exponent: 468, 358, 223 and 424.}
\label{fig:controls70}
\end{figure}
\section{Unsteered chaos and steering controls}
\label{sec:origin}
Steering adds a fixed vector to the residual stream, so chaos along
a steering axis could belong to the push rather than to the model. At $\alpha=0$, however, the map is the unsteered
model's own feedback map, with temperature as its only parameter. The $\alpha=0$ columns of the hostility maps contain
cells with positive finite-horizon exponents in Llama-3.1 8B (26 of 64 temperatures), Qwen2.5-7B (15 of 64) and
OLMo-2 7B (11 of 64), while the near-zero columns of the 0.5B and 1.5B maps contain one such cell each (of 128 and
64). A longer float64 sweep of the unsteered 0.5B map (512 temperatures over $\log_{10}T\in[-1,0.3]$, 1000 transient
and 2000 measured steps) finds a narrow chaotic band at $\log_{10}T\approx0.15$--$0.21$ (7 temperatures, exponents up
to $0.28$), with a period-5 loop at 437 of the 512 temperatures. The same long sweep finds 96 chaotic temperatures in
Llama-3.1 8B, between $\log_{10}T=-0.20$ and $0.24$ (exponents up to $0.66$), interleaved with periodic windows of
periods 2 to 52; within $\log_{10}T\in[-0.3,0.3]$ its chaotic share, 41\,\%, equals that of the coarse column.
Qwen2.5-7B has 65 chaotic temperatures between $\log_{10}T=-0.07$ and $0.28$ (exponents up to $0.74$), and OLMo-2 7B
has 32 between $0.04$ and $0.14$ (up to $0.30$). Steering is therefore not needed to reach chaotic regimes in any of
these four models; where it does create chaos, the growth it adds along recorded orbits comes mainly from the readout, where next
tokens compete (Section~\ref{sec:theory}). Temperature alone produces chaos with embedded windows, broadly in the 7B and 8B models and in
a narrow band in the 0.5B model (Figure~\ref{fig:alpha0}). Across the fifteen models of Section~\ref{sec:sizemain}, the
regular-grid sweeps find unsteered chaos in eleven of the fourteen dense models (in Llama-3.1-70B at two
temperatures that pass a three-offset test), but not in Qwen2.5-72B, Llama-3.3-70B, OLMo-2-13B or the mixture of
experts; finer lines found candidates in the first two, and the Qwen2.5-72B window, recomputed in float64
throughout, is periodic.

Steering does widen the chaotic regions, but not as a property of the concept: along norm-matched random directions,
the 0.5B maps are as chaotic as along the concept directions (Figure~\ref{fig:controls}). On the same $128^2$ grid,
7.6, 8.3 and 12.5\,\% of cells are chaotic for three Gaussian directions with the philosophy vector's norm, against
6.8\,\% for philosophy and 7.5\,\% for gratitude. In all five maps the chaotic share rises from about 1\,\% at
$|\alpha|<0.01$ to between 5 and 22\,\% at larger $|\alpha|$, and a chaotic region at high temperature, with windows
reaching down, recurs. In this model, chaos under steering is therefore a generic response to perturbing the residual
stream at this norm. Rotating the steering direction makes the same point continuously: along the great circle from
one Gaussian direction to another, $90^{\circ}$ away, at constant norm, the chaotic regions move and change shape, but
the chaotic share stays between 13 and 24\,\% at every one of 103 angles (Figure~\ref{fig:morph}; a video of the
rotation is in the supplementary material). Single neurons behave the same way. Steering along the output direction (Figure~\ref{fig:neuronsteer})
of one MLP neuron of the steered layer (its column of the down-projection, rescaled to the same norm; adding it is the same as raising that one neuron's activation by a fixed amount for every token of the input: by 37.4 at $|\alpha|=0.12$ for the neuron most specific to philosophy, whose mean activation on the philosophy texts is 0.27) gives 9.9\,\%
chaotic cells for the neuron most specific to philosophy on held-out texts, chosen like the autoencoder feature below
by a rule fixed before any map, 5.1\,\% for its index neighbor and 12.9\,\% for a random neuron
(Figure~\ref{fig:neurons}); near $\alpha=0$ the share is 0.4 to 1.2\,\%. The direction does select the texts. The largest attractor family in all five maps is the
unsteered period-5 loop, but beyond it only 26 to 56\,\% of one map's cells carry an attractor text that appears
anywhere in another map. Llama-3.1 8B agrees. On its hostility plane, two norm-matched random directions at the same
layer give 26 and 17\,\% chaotic cells against 28\,\% along the concept direction; all three carry 34 to 44\,\% near
$\alpha=0$, and the random directions thin out at large $|\alpha|$. At layer 19, where a sparse autoencoder trained on
this model reads the residual stream \cite{goodfire2025sae}, the concept vector rebuilt there gives 28\,\%. The
decoder direction of the autoencoder feature most specific to hostility on held-out texts (chosen by a rule fixed
before any map; cosine $0.10$ with the concept vector) gives 29\,\%, a random live feature 27\,\% and a Gaussian
direction 39\,\%, all at the same norm. In this model, chaos therefore occurs along autoencoder-feature,
contrastive and random directions alike, with direction-dependent prevalence; at 70B the directions differ more
(Appendix~\ref{sec:size}).
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.74\linewidth]{fig-alpha0.png}
\caption{The unsteered soft-token map ($\alpha=0$) along temperature, top to bottom: Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct,
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct and
the mixture of experts Qwen3.5-35B-A3B. Largest Lyapunov exponent (clipped at $-1.5$) from float64 sweeps of 512
temperatures (128 for the 70B-class and mixture-of-experts models; 1000 transient and 2000 measured steps). Red: no
period up to 64 and an exponent above $10^{-3}$; black crosses: neither a detected period nor a positive exponent
(unresolved); other points have a detected period, colored by $\log_2$ of the period. Table~\ref{tab:scale}
summarizes these and the wider sweeps of the other models.}
\label{fig:alpha0}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-controls-q05.png}
\caption{Steering controls in Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct ($128^2$ float64 cells, steering at layer 12, one grid and one
set of settings): largest Lyapunov exponent along the philosophy and gratitude directions and along three Gaussian
directions rescaled to the philosophy vector's norm. Titles give the chaotic share (finite-horizon exponent above $10^{-3}$, no period up
to 48); color gives the exponent, clipped at $\pm0.5$.}
\label{fig:controls}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{\input{figures/tikz-neuron-steering}}
\caption{Steering along one MLP neuron, in decoder layer 12 of Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct. Each token has its own
residual stream, one 896-dimensional vector per token, and its own pass through the same MLP; attention, with its causal
mask, is the only step that mixes tokens. Inside every MLP box (bottom, for one token with residual vector $x$), the
4864 neurons write $\sum_i h_i w_i$ into the stream, neuron $i$ along its own column $w_i$ of the down-projection. The
steering strength $\alpha$ is a scalar we set (negative values lower the activation), and
$312=\lVert v_{\mathrm{phil}}\rVert/\lVert w_{832}\rVert=188.99/0.606$ is a fixed scale for neuron 832. Raising
$h_{832}$ by $312\alpha$ for every token is the same as adding $\alpha v$, with $v=312\,w_{832}$, to the layer's output,
which is how we implement it: $\sum_i h_i w_i+312\alpha\,w_{832}=\sum_{i\neq832}h_i w_i+(h_{832}+312\alpha)\,w_{832}$.
At $|\alpha|=0.12$ the increase is 37.4, against a mean activation of 0.27 on the philosophy texts; for neurons 833 and
4137 the scale is 329 and 346. The tokens shown are only an example: in the loop, the input is the fixed prompt followed
by the fed-back soft token.}
\label{fig:neuronsteer}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig-controls-neurons-q05.png}
\caption{Steering along single MLP neurons in Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct (same grid, settings and color scale as
Figure~\ref{fig:controls}): the output directions of the layer-12 neuron most specific to philosophy on held-out texts
(832), of its index neighbor (833) and of a random neuron (4137), each rescaled to the philosophy vector's norm (cosines
with it 0.013, $-0.028$ and 0.022). Titles give the chaotic share.}
\label{fig:neurons}
\end{figure}
\paragraph{The prompt.} Every 0.5B result above uses one prompt, so we mapped (run PV, \repo{tools/pv_summary.py}) the standard plane ($\alpha$ from
$-0.12$ to $0.12$, $\log_{10}T$ from $-1$ to $0.3$; $128\times128$ float64 cells, philosophy direction at layer 12) under
22 variants (Figure~\ref{fig:prompts}): each of its two parts alone, and neither (Qwen's default system prompt, which the
chat template inserts when none is given); the system prompt's words in two random orders, the user message's two
tokens reversed, and both changes together; an opposite system prompt; happy and sad versions of the system prompt and of the user message; the
same texts without chat markup, a system prompt three times as long and a two-turn conversation; three prompts that are
a single random vector, with no text at all (a Gaussian direction at the median embedding norm, the embedding of a
random token, and a Dirichlet(0.1) mixture of the embeddings of 64 random tokens); and the base prompt with the loop started from three random vectors.
Every prompt we tested gives chaos with periodic windows: 5.9 to 14.9\,\% of the plane is
chaotic (7.0\,\% for the base prompt), with largest exponents of 0.62 to 0.85 per step (here chaotic means a largest exponent above $10^{-3}$ alone; this
finite-window indicator overlaps a detected period at 3 to 18 cells per map). Removing the user message doubles the chaotic share, and even a prompt that is one random vector gives
8 to 10\,\%. In every prompt we tested, chaos and periodic windows appear, but the prompt decides where they lie: the
chaotic cells of each of the other 18 prompts overlap those of the base prompt by 1 to 37\,\%
(Jaccard index). For the three random starts, the chaos/non-chaos threshold labels agree with the base run at
98.9 to 99.0\,\% of the cells, while the periods detected within the run agree at only 86.5 to 93.1\,\%, so within
this horizon the orbit reached can depend on the start. Across the 19 prompts there is
nonetheless a common structure (Figure~\ref{fig:promptfreq}): 48.3\,\% of the cells are chaotic for at
least one prompt but only 3.0\,\% for half of them or more, and these hot spots lie at moderate
steering of either sign in a band of temperature, where the mean fraction of chaotic cells per row peaks at 38\,\% near $\log_{10}T=0.15$, against 0.8\,\% below
$\log_{10}T=-0.5$. The three random-vector prompts move the band to colder temperatures: their mean profile peaks
near $\log_{10}T=0.03$, that of the text prompts near $0.16$.

\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig-prompt-variants.png}
\caption{Every prompt we tested gives chaos, at places that depend on the prompt (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, philosophy direction
at layer 12, float64;
$128\times128$ cells over $\alpha\in[-0.12,0.12]$ and $\log_{10}T\in[-1,0.3]$). (a) The chaotic share of the plane for
each of the 22 variants (19 prefixes, and the base prefix with the loop started from three random vectors), grouped by
the kind of change; dotted: the base prompt (the system prompt ``You are an AI talking
with another AI\ldots'' and the user message ``Hello!''). (b) Eight of the maps (colors: the largest exponent where it exceeds $10^{-3}$; gray: the other cells, darker where
more contracting, among them a few unresolved nonperiodic cells). The exact texts and token identities of the prompts are in
the project repository (\repo{docs/prompt-variants}).}
\label{fig:prompts}
\end{figure*}

\caption{Where the chaos is found across prompts. (a) For each cell of the plane of Figure~\ref{fig:prompts}, the
fraction of the 19 prompt variants (the random-start runs left out) in which it is chaotic. (b) The mean

\section{Limitations in detail}
\label{sec:limits}
The detailed orbit studies cover one model family (Qwen2.5, 0.5B and 1.5B), with coarse
maps and three float64 orbits at 7B; the two other families (OLMo-2 7B and Llama-3.1 8B) have maps, including
$128^2$ refinements for Llama, but no continued orbits. The size comparison of Section~\ref{sec:sizemain} uses regular
temperature grids that can miss narrow windows, a window that missed most of the larger models' chaos, and mostly
one twin offset per label. The two-GPU runs of the 70B-class models computed their final normalization in float32
until we fixed it; their labels rest on offsets of $10^{-6}$ or more, and the cells we rechecked, the Qwen2.5-72B jump
window and steered candidates in float64 throughout and samples of the Llama maps at three offsets, behave as stated,
but the rest of those maps were not recomputed. Neither were the four coarse lines of the frozen 72B test; after the
fix we computed only fine lines through their entropy jumps, one of which passes through their single chaotic
temperature.
The maps use one prompt, apart from the 22 prompt variants of the 0.5B standard plane (paragraph ``The prompt''
above; Section~\ref{sec:sizemain} adds ten for Qwen2.5-7B and -14B, and four for Qwen2.5-72B, its base
model, and OLMo-2-7B and -32B), and steering uses contrastive vectors, checked against norm-matched random and sparse-autoencoder
directions in two models. The soft-token loop is one inference scheme: Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge} extends it to memories of up to sixteen soft tokens and to the growing context of Soft Thinking, but sampled decoding of discrete tokens is a different system. The evidence consists of numerical
validation, cross-checks and prospectively frozen passage tests (including failed all-row criteria), with
computer-assisted proofs for only three orbits at two parameter points (the period-8 orbit and a coexisting period-2
and period-3 pair). Chaos labels are finite-horizon estimates (300
measured steps after 300 to 500 transient steps), and the chaotic cells are numerically validated (twin exponents agree with automatic-differentiation tangents, across precisions and horizons) but not yet established by a computer-assisted proof. A computer-assisted proof of chaos, such as a horseshoe verified by covering
relations, would need enclosures of the map over boxes many orders of magnitude larger than the period-8 proof's
(radius $2^{-640}$). That proof is unaffected, but its ball arithmetic does not scale to large boxes: in a separate
test, a box of radius $2^{-80}$ already gave enclosures too wide to show that an RMS normalization at layer 3
divides by a positive number (the enclosures overestimate through the layers), so a proof of chaos needs other
verified methods. A horizon check retested
random cells of two float64 maps (Figures~\ref{fig:teaser} and~\ref{fig:stripes}) with 1000 transient and 2000
measured steps at two twin offsets: 86 and 92 of 96 cells labeled chaotic stayed chaotic (all but one of the rest
settled on periodic orbits), all 96 periodic cells in each map stayed periodic, and 29 and 27 of 32 cells with neither
label settled on periodic orbits (18 and 20 of them with periods up to 32, the maps' maximum, the others with periods
up to 64). For the cells that stayed chaotic, the map's 300-step exponent differs from the 2000-step exponent by a
median of 0.020 and 0.030 (90th percentiles 0.064 and 0.096, maxima 0.23 and 0.30), and the 2000-step exponents at
offsets $10^{-6}$ and $10^{-9}$ agree within $3\times10^{-3}$. The chaotic counts do not hinge on the threshold:
raising it from $10^{-3}$ to $0.01$, $0.02$ or $0.05$ leaves 77{,}646, 76{,}761 or 73{,}437 of the 78{,}868 chaotic
cells of Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}. Near the accumulation of a cascade the labels are less reliable, because orbits there converge
slowly or wander chaotically before they settle (transient chaos \cite{grebogi1983crises,lai2011transient}): on
the hostile line of Figure~\ref{fig:hubcascade} (left), 135 of the 235 cells labeled chaotic from 400 measured steps
stayed chaotic and 91 were periodic. The Feigenbaum ratios are
finite-order values that rise toward $\delta$ (the last is within 1\,\%); the limit itself is not computed. All
orbit, bifurcation and chaos results concern the soft-token map, not sampled generation with a growing context. With
sampling, the bridge of Appendix~\ref{sec:bridge} tests only whether the bottleneck's laminar phases survive the noise;
its other tests are deterministic (greedy decoding, longer fed-back memories and Soft Thinking's growing context).

\section{Evidence and reproducibility}
\label{sec:evidence}
We grade claims on an evidence ladder. Rung 1 means numerically validated (tolerances, residuals, convergence,
precision varied); rung 2, cross-checked by an independent estimate, implementation or hardware; rung 3, a
computer-assisted proof.

\paragraph{Search scale.} The soft-token results come from an exploratory search. An initial census of the raw
data at 00:19 UTC on 14 October 2026 counted 34 distinct maps (987\,308 cells, 27 maps in float64) in six models,
each with one prompt and one of three steering directions or none, plus one partial map. Of these, 29 maps and the
partial one appear here; the five omitted are early discovery or side maps. Later additions are not in that count:
the steering-control maps of Appendix~\ref{sec:origin}, the mixture-of-experts maps, and the sweeps, lines and maps of the models of Section~\ref{sec:sizemain} and Appendix~\ref{sec:size}.
The labeled maps contain about 2600 periodic families of at least six cells, counted per map. We singled out about
26 windows or families for float64 work: 20 are reported in detail, 2 only as map features and 4 not at all (an
orbit whose continuation was abandoned, a cell that proved aperiodic, a band whose float32 orbit did not converge and
a pilot that found no cusp within a bounded search). Of 54 Newton orbit attempts, 7 failed. All nine tangent
reversals on continued fold curves (seven found by 14 October, and two on traces of a period-4 fold curve continued on
15 October) are numerically located cusps; the two reversals of a continued flip curve are smooth turns. The four
reversals on the two fold curves of the period-5 window found on 15 October (below) are located cusps as well. Four
further reversals found on 18 October, one on the lower-left leg's own fold curve, one on the extension of the
window's second fold curve and two on a partial trace of a fold curve of the window at $\log_{10}T=-0.105$, are flagged
by the continuation but not located. Two of the five
regions we flagged as shrimp candidates are reported as shrimp-like. On 15 October, the turning-point search of the
two-block planes (not counted in Table~\ref{tab:search}) led to two more windows: a period-5 window, reported with its continued orbit and its continued fold and flip curves in
Appendix~\ref{sec:hostility}, and a period-6 window that was mapped but not continued. One further
language model (Ouro-1.4B) was explored and is not reported; its apparent chaos was a long transient. Every window
was chosen after the maps were inspected. In the census of Table~\ref{tab:search}, only the passage forecasts, the
cusp-unfolding offsets and Huginn's relaxation test were fixed before the computations that test them; tests frozen
later, up to the fixed-shape control of 18 October, are identified as such where they are reported.
